Integrating the Wigner Distribution on subsets of the phase space, a Survey
Nicolas Lerner

Abstract
. We review several properties of integrals of the Wigner distribution on subsets of the phase space. Along our way, we provide a theoretical proof of the invalidity of Flandrin’s conjecture, a fact already proven via numerical arguments in our joint paper [ 6 ] with B. Delourme and T. Duyckaerts. We use also the J.G. Wood & A.J. Bracken paper [ 55 ] , for which we offer a mathematical perspective. We review thoroughly the case of subsets of the plane whose boundary is a conic curve and show that Mehler’s formula can be helpful in the analysis of these cases, including for the higher dimensional case investigated in the paper [ 39 ] by E. Lieb and Y. Ostrover. Using the Feichtinger algebra, we show that, generically in the Baire sense, the Wigner distribution of a pulse in L 2 ​ ( ℝ n ) L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}) does not belong to L 1 ​ ( ℝ 2 ​ n ) L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}) , providing as a byproduct a large class of examples of subsets of the phase space ℝ 2 ​ n \mathbb{R}^{2n} on which the integral of the Wigner distribution is infinite. We study as well the case of convex polygons of the plane, with a rather weak estimate depending on the number of vertices, but independent of the area of the polygon.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35P05, 42B20, 47G10, 47G30, 47N70, 94A12

July 3, 2026,
\currenttime

Foreword

As indicated by the title of this article, this paper is a survey of properties of integrals of the Wigner distribution on subsets of the phase space. Since it is quite lengthy, we wish in this foreword to describe the content of this paper, browsing through the table of contents, expecting that the reader will find some organization with the way this article is written. In particular, we shall point here what is original in our survey (to the best of our knowledge) and what was well-known beforehand. There is no doubt that the fifty-five articles quoted in the references list are a small part of the literature on the topic and could be probably extended tenfold: we expect nevertheless that our choice of references will be enough to cover the most important contributions.

Section 1 is
Preliminaries and Definitions
and is very classical. We have used
J. Leray’s book
[
31
]

and other Lecture Notes of this author at the
Collège de France
such as
[
30
]
,
L. Hörmander’s
four-volume treatise,
The Analysis of Linear Partial Differential operators
and in particular Volume III,
as well as K. Gröchenig’s
[
16
]

Foundations of time-frequency analysis
,
along with G.B. Folland’s
[
15
]
,
A. Unterberger’s
[
50
]
and N. Lerner’s
[
33
]
.
Some details are given, in particular on positive quantizations, but that section is far from being self-contained, which is probably unavoidable: the link of properties of the Wigner distribution and of the Weyl quantization of classical Hamiltonians is easy to obtain but turns out to be an important piece of information for our purpose.

Section 2 is stressing the link
Quantization of radial functions - Mehler’s formula
and is also very classical: here also the link aforementioned is easy to get but gives some simplifications in the formulas providing the quantization of radial Hamiltonians: in one dimension for the configuration space (phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2}
), we are reduced to check simple integrals related to the Laguerre polynomials, following P. Flandrin’s method in his 1988 article
[
13
]
.

Section 3 is dealing with
Conics with eccentricity
\mathbf{<1}
.

The result for the disc in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is due to P. Flandrin and the result for the Euclidean ball in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
to E. Lieb & Y. Ostrover in
[
39
]
. Using Mehler’s formula simplifies a little bit the presentation, but leaves open the case of anisotropic ellipsoids for which we formulate a conjecture.

Section 4 is dealing with
Epigraphs of Parabolas.

The results obtained in that section follow easily from Section 3 but nevertheless the precise diagonalization proven there seems to be new.
We formulate also a conjecture on anisotropic paraboloids which is closely related to the conjecture in Section 3.

Section 5 is concerned with
Conics with eccentricity
\mathbf{>1}
.

Many of the results in that section are contained in the paper

[
55
]

by J.G. Wood and A.J. Bracken; however since the latter article contains some formal calculations, using for instance test functions which do not belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we have made a mathematically sound presentation. As certainly the most important contribution of this work, we provide a “theoretical”
disproof of Flandrin’s conjecture on integrals of the Wigner distribution on convex subsets of the phase space: we find in particular some
a>0
and some function
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with norm 1 such that

\iint_{[0,a]^{2}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi>1,

where
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is the Wigner distribution of
u
.
This fact was already proven in
our joint paper
[
6
]

with B. Delourme and T. Duyckaerts, using a rigorous numerical argument.

Section 6 is entitled
Unboundedness is Baire generic
and most of its content
is included in Chapter 12 of K. Gröchenig’s book
[
16
]
,

Foundations of time-frequency analysis
.
Using the Feichtinger algebra,
we show that, generically in the Baire sense, the Wigner distribution of a pulse in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

does not belong to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, providing as a byproduct a large class of examples of subsets of the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
on which the integral of the Wigner distribution is infinite.
We raise a couple of questions, in particular whether we can find a pulse
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that

E_{+}(u)=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)>0\}\quad\text{is connected.}

Section 7 is
Convex polygons in the plane
: we study there the sets defined by the intersection of
N
half-spaces in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the integrals of the Wigner distribution on these sets. We start with convex cones (
N=2
) for which a complete result is known and we go on with triangles (
N=3
) for which we find an upper bound: the integral of
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
on a triangle of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
for a normalized pulse in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is bounded above by a universal constant. We show also that
the integral of
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
on a convex polygon with
N
sides of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
for a normalized pulse in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
is bounded above by a universal constant
\times\sqrt{N}
.
We raise a couple of questions: in particular it seems possible that the behaviour of convex subsets of the plane is such that there exists a constant
\alpha>1
such that

\forall C\text{ convex subset of the plane $\mathbb{R}^{2}$},\quad\forall u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\text{ with $\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1$,}\\
\text{we have\quad}\iint_{C}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\alpha.

That would be a weak version of Flandrin’s conjecture: the original Flandrin’s conjecture was the above statement with
\alpha=1
, which is untrue, but that does not rule out the existence of a number
\alpha>1
such that the above estimate holds true.

Section 8 is entitled
Open questions and Conjectures
: we review in that section the various conjectures that we meet along the text of the article, estimating the importance and difficulty of the various questions. Section 9 is an Appendix containing only classical material, hopefully helping the reader by improving the self-containedness of this paper.

1.
Preliminaries & Definitions

1.1.
The Wigner Distribution

Let
u,v
be given functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The function
\Omega
, defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

(1.1.1)

\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni(z,x)\mapsto u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(x-\frac{z}{2})=\Omega(u,v)(x,z),

belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
from the identity

(1.1.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\Omega(u,v)(x,z)|^{2}dxdz=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

We have also

(1.1.3)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\Omega(x,z)|dz\leq 2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

We may then give the following definition.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
u,v
be given functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We define the joint Wigner distribution

\mathcal{W}(u,v)

as the partial Fourier transform
1
1

1

For
f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
,
we define its Fourier transform by

\hat{f}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}e^{-2i\pi x\cdot\xi}f(x)dx
and we obtain the inversion formula

f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{N}}e^{2i\pi x\cdot\xi}\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi
. Both formulas can be extended to tempered distributions:
for
T\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
, we define the tempered distribution
\hat{T}
by

(1.1.4)

\langle\hat{T},\phi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\langle T,\hat{\phi}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

Note also that with this normalization, it is natural to introduce the operators
D_{x}^{\alpha}
defined for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{N}

by

(1.1.5)

D_{x}^{\alpha}u=D_{x_{1}}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots D_{x_{N}}^{\alpha_{n}}u,\ D_{x_{j}}u=\frac{\partial u}{2i\pi\partial x_{j}},\text{ so that }\widehat{D_{x}^{\alpha}u}=\xi^{\alpha}\hat{u}(\xi),\quad\text{with $\xi^{\alpha}=\xi_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\dots\xi_{N}^{\alpha_{N}}$}.

with respect to
z
of the function
\Omega
defined in
(
1.1.1
).
We have for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\xi}
, using (
1.1.3
),

(1.1.6)

\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(x-\frac{z}{2})dz.

The Wigner distribution of
u
is defined as
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
.

N.B
.

By inverse Fourier transformation we get, in a weak sense,

(1.1.7)

u(x_{1})\otimes\bar{v}(x_{2})=\int\mathcal{W}(u,v)(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)\ e^{2i\pi(x_{1}-x_{2})\cdot\xi}d\xi.

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
u,v
be given functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. The function

\mathcal{W}(u,v)
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and we have

(1.1.8)

\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

We have also

(1.1.9)

\overline{\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)}=\mathcal{W}(v,u)(x,\xi),

so that

\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is real-valued.

Proof.

Note that the function
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and satisfies
(
1.1.8
)
from (
1.1.2
) and the definition of
\mathcal{W}
as the partial Fourier transform of
\Omega
.
Property (
1.1.9
) is immediate and entails that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is real-valued.
∎

Remark 1.3
.

We note also that the real-valued function
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
can take negative values,
choosing for instance
u_{1}(x)=xe^{-\pi x^{2}}
on the real line, we get

\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{1})(x,\xi)=2^{1/2}e^{-2\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}\bigl(x^{2}+\xi^{2}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\bigr).

In fact the real-valued function
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
will take negative values unless
u
is a Gaussian function, thanks to a Theorem due to E. Lieb (see
[
37
]

and the books
[
16
]
and
[
41
]
).
As a matter of fact, this range of
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
intersecting
\mathbb{R}_{-}
for most “pulses”
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

makes rather weird
the qualification of
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
as a “quasi-probability”
(anyhow the emphasis must be on
quasi
, not on
probability
).

Remark 1.4
.

We have also by Fourier inversion formula, say for
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(1.1.10)

u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{u}(x-\frac{z}{2})=\Omega(x,z)=\int\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)e^{2i\pi z\cdot\xi}d\xi,

so that, with
z=2x=y
,
we get the Reconstruction Formula,

(1.1.11)

u(y)\bar{u}(0)=\int\mathcal{W}(u,u)(\frac{y}{2},\xi)e^{2i\pi y\cdot\xi}d\xi,

as well as

(1.1.12)

|u(x)|^{2}=\int\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)d\xi,\quad|\hat{u}(\xi)|^{2}=\int\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dx,

the former formula following from (
1.1.10
) and the latter from

(1.1.13)

\int\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dx=\iint e^{-2i\pi z\xi}u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{u}(x-\frac{z}{2})dzdx\\
=\iint e^{-2i\pi\xi(x_{1}-x_{2})}u(x_{1})\bar{u}(x_{2})dx_{1}dx_{2}=|\hat{u}(\xi)|^{2}.

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
u
be a function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which is even or odd. Then
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is an even function.

Proof.

Using the notation

(1.1.14)

\check{u}(x)=u(-x),

we check

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(u,v)

\displaystyle(-x,-\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2i\pi z\cdot\xi}u(-x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(-x-\frac{z}{2})dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\check{u}(x-\frac{z}{2})\check{\bar{v}}(x+\frac{z}{2})dz=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\check{u}(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{\check{v}}(x-\frac{z}{2})dz

\displaystyle=\mathcal{W}(\check{u},\check{v})(x,\xi),

so that if
\check{u}=\pm u
, we get

\mathcal{W}(u,u)(-x,-\xi)=\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)
.
∎

N.B
.

This lemma is a very particular case of the symplectic covariance property displayed below in
(
1.2.74
).

N.B
.

In
Part 1 of Volume IV in the collected works

[
54
]
of Eugene P. Wigner, we find
the first occurrence of what will be called later on the
Wigner distribution
along with a physicist point of view.

It turns out that most of the properties of the Wigner distribution (in particular Lemma
1.5
) are inherited
from its links with the Weyl quantization introduced by H. Weyl in 1926 in the first edition of
[
53
]
and our next remarks are devised to stress that link.

1.2.
Weyl quantization, Composition formulas, Positive quantizations

1.2.1.
Weyl quantization

The main goal of Hermann Weyl in his seminal paper
[
53
]
was to give a simple formula, also providing symplectic covariance, ensuring that real-valued Hamiltonians
a(x,\xi)
get quantized by formally self-adjoint operators. The standard way of dealing with differential operators does not achieve that goal since for instance the standard quantization of the Hamiltonian

x\xi
(indeed real-valued) is the operator
xD_{x}
, which is not symmetric (
D_{x}
is defined in (
1.1.5
)); H. Weyl’s choice in that case was

x\xi\text{ \it should be quantized by the operator\hskip 10.22217pt}\frac{1}{2}(xD_{x}+D_{x}x),\quad\text{\it(indeed symmetric),}

and more generally, say for

a\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the quantization of the Hamiltonian
a(x,\xi)
, denoted by
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
,
should be
given by the formula

(1.2.1)

({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u)(x)=\iint e^{2i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}a\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2},\xi\bigr)u(y)dyd\xi.

For
v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we may consider

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iiint a(x,\xi)e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(x-\frac{z}{2})dzdxd\xi\\
=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}a(x,\xi)\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)dxd\xi,

and the latter formula allows us to give the following definition.

Definition 1.6
.

Let
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. We define the Weyl quantization
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
of the Hamiltonian
a
, by the formula

(1.2.2)

({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u)(x)=\iint e^{2i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}a\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2},\xi\bigr)u(y)dyd\xi,

to be understood weakly as

(1.2.3)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,\bar{v}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle a,\mathcal{W}(u,v)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

We note that the sesquilinear mapping

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni(u,v)\mapsto\mathcal{W}(u,v)\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),

is continuous so that the above bracket of duality
\langle a,\mathcal{W}(u,v)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}

makes sense.
We note as well that a temperate distribution
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
gets quantized
by a continuous operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
from

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

into

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
This very general framework is not really useful since we want to compose our operators
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}
.
A first step in this direction is to look for sufficient conditions
ensuring that the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Moreover, for
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
b
a polynomial in
\mathbb{C}[x,\xi]
,
we have the composition formula,

(1.2.4)

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}

\displaystyle={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\sharp b)},

(1.2.5)

\displaystyle(a\sharp b)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{1}{(4i\pi)^{k}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=k}\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}a)(x,\xi)(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}b)(x,\xi),

which involves here a finite sum.
This follows from (2.1.26) in
[
33
]

where several generalizations can be found (see in particular in that reference the integral formula (2.1.18) which can be given a meaning for quite general classes of symbols).
As a consequence of (
1.2.5
), we get that

(1.2.6)

\displaystyle(a\sharp b)=\sum_{k\geq 0}\omega_{k}(a,b),\quad\omega_{0}(a,b)=ab,\quad\omega_{1}(a,b)=\frac{1}{4i\pi}\left\{a,b\right\},

(1.2.7)

\displaystyle\left\{a,b\right\}=\partial_{\xi}a\cdot\partial_{x}b-\partial_{x}a\partial_{\xi}b,

where
\left\{a,b\right\}
is called the
Poisson bracket
of
a
and
b
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
a
be a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Then we have

(1.2.8)

\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq\min\bigl(2^{n}\|a\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},\|\hat{a}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\bigr).

Proof.

In fact we have from (
1.2.3
),
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iiint a(x,\xi)u(2x-y)\bar{v}(y)e^{-4i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}2^{n}dydxd\xi,

so that defining for
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
the operator
\sigma_{x,\xi}
by

(1.2.9)

(\sigma_{x,\xi}u)(y)=u(2x-y)e^{-4i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi},

we see that the operator
\sigma_{x,\xi}
(called
phase symmetry
, also known as the
Grossman-Royer operator
) is unitary and self-adjoint
2
2

2

Indeed we have

(1.2.10)

(\sigma_{x,\xi}^{2}u)(y)=(\sigma_{x,\xi}u)(2x-y)e^{-4i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}\\
=u(2x-(2x-y))e^{-4i\pi(x-(2x-y))\cdot\xi}e^{-4i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}=u(y),\ \text{so that}\ \sigma_{x,\xi}^{2}=\operatorname{Id}.

We have

\langle\sigma_{x,\xi}^{*}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle u,\sigma_{x,\xi}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

=\overline{\mathcal{W}(v,u)(x,\xi)}={W(u,v)(x,\xi)}={\langle\sigma_{x,\xi}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}},

proving that
\sigma_{x,\xi}^{*}=\sigma_{x,\xi}
.

and

(1.2.11)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}=2^{n}\iint a(x,\xi)\sigma_{x,\xi}dxd\xi,

proving the first estimate of the proposition.
As a consequence of (
1.2.11
),
we obtain that

(1.2.12)

\left({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\right)^{*}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{a})},\quad\text{so that for $a$ real-valued, $({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)})^{*}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}$.}

To prove the second estimate, we introduce the so-called ambiguity function
\mathcal{A}(u,v)
as the inverse Fourier transform of the Wigner function
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
, so that for
u,v
in the Schwartz class, we have

(\mathcal{A}(u,v))(\eta,y)=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)e^{2i\pi(x\cdot\eta+\xi\cdot y)}dxd\xi,

i.e.

(1.2.13)

(\mathcal{A}(u,v))(\eta,y)=\int u(x+\frac{y}{2})\bar{v}(x-\frac{y}{2})e^{2i\pi x\cdot\eta}dx,

which reads as well as

(1.2.14)

(\mathcal{A}(u,v))(\eta,y)=\int u(\frac{y}{2}+\frac{z}{2})\ \overline{\check{v}}(\frac{y}{2}-\frac{z}{2})\ e^{2i\pi z\cdot\frac{\eta}{2}}dz\ 2^{-n}\\
=\mathcal{W}(u,\check{v})(\frac{y}{2},-\frac{\eta}{2})2^{-n}.

N.B
.

The ambiguity function is called the Fourier-Wigner transform in G.B. Folland’s book

[
15
]
.

Remark 1.8
.

With
\Omega(u,v)
defined by (
1.1.1
), we have

(1.2.15)

\mathcal{W}(u,v)=\mathcal{F}_{2}\bigl(\Omega(u,v)\bigr),

where
\mathcal{F}_{2}
stands for
the Fourier transformation with respect to the second variable. Taking the Fourier transform with respect to the second variable in the previous formula gives, with
\mathcal{F}_{j}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}
)
standing for the Fourier transform with respect to the
j^{\text{th}}
variable
(resp. all variables),

\mathcal{F}_{2}{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{C}_{2}{\Omega},\quad\mathcal{F}{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{F}_{1}{\mathcal{C}_{2}{\Omega}},\quad\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}{\mathcal{W}}=\mathcal{F}_{1}{\mathcal{C}_{1}{\Omega}},

where
\mathcal{C}

(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{1}
or
\mathcal{C}_{2}
)
stands for the “check” operator
\mathcal{C}
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}

given by (
1.1.14
)
(resp. with respect to the first or second variable),
the latter formula being
(
1.2.13
).

Applying Plancherel formula on (
1.2.3
),
we get

(1.2.16)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle\hat{a},\mathcal{A}(u,v)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

We note that a consequence of (
1.2.5
) is that for a linear form
L(x,\xi)
,
we have

L\sharp L=L^{2},\quad\text{and more generally}\quad L^{\sharp N}=L^{N}.

As a result, considering for
(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, the linear form
L_{\eta,y}
defined by

(1.2.17)

L_{\eta,y}(x,\xi)=x\cdot\eta+\xi\cdot y,

we see that

(1.2.18)

\mathcal{A}(u,v)(\eta,y)=\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi(x\cdot\eta+\xi\cdot y)})}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and thus we get Hermann Weyl’s original formula

(1.2.19)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}=\iint\widehat{a}(\eta,y)e^{i{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\eta,y})}}dyd\eta,

which implies the second estimate in the proposition.
∎

Proposition 1.9
.

Let
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. The distribution kernel
k_{a}(x,y)
of the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
is

(1.2.20)

k_{a}(x,y)=\widehat{a}^{[2]}(\frac{x+y}{2},y-x),

where
a^{[2]}
stands for the Fourier transform of
a
with respect to the second variable.
Let
k\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
be the distribution kernel of a continuous operator
A
from
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
into
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then the Weyl
symbol
a

of
A
is

(1.2.21)

a(x,\xi)=\int e^{-2\pi it\cdot\xi}k(x+\frac{t}{2},x-\frac{t}{2})dt,

where the integral sign means that we take the Fourier transform with respect to
t

of the distribution
k(x+\frac{t}{2},x-\frac{t}{2})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}

(see (
1.1.4
) in footnote
1
for the definition of the Fourier transformation on tempered distributions).

Proof.

With
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have defined
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
via Formula (
1.2.3
)
and using Remark
1.8
, we get

\displaystyle\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,\bar{v}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=\langle a(x,\xi),\widehat{\Omega}^{[2]}(x,\xi)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{a}^{[2]}(t,z),u(t+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(t-\frac{z}{2})\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{a}^{[2]}(\frac{x+y}{2},y-x),u(y)\bar{v}(x)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

proving (
1.2.20
). As a consequence, we find that

k_{a}(x+\frac{t}{2},x-\frac{t}{2})={\widehat{a}^{[2]}}(x,-t),

and by Fourier inversion, this entails

(1.2.22)

a(x,\xi)=\mathcal{F}\text{ourier}_{t}\bigl(k_{a}(x+\frac{t}{2},x-\frac{t}{2})\bigr)(\xi)=\int e^{-2\pi it\cdot\xi}k_{a}(x+\frac{t}{2},x-\frac{t}{2})dt,

where the integral sign means that we perform a Fourier transformation with respect to the variable
t
.
∎

A particular case of Segal’s formula (see e.g. Theorem 2.1.2 in
[
33
]
)
is with
F
standing for the Fourier transformation on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(1.2.23)

F^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}F={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a(\xi,-x))}.

1.2.2.
The symplectic group

We define the canonical symplectic form
\sigma

on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

(1.2.24)

\langle\sigma X,Y\rangle=\bigl[X,Y\bigr]=\xi\cdot y-\eta\cdot x,\quad\text{with $X=(x,\xi),Y=(y,\eta)$.}

The symplectic group
3
3

3

This is obviously a group
since for
S_{1},S_{2}\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
, the last equation in (
1.2.25
) implies that
|\det S|=1
and

[S_{1}S_{2}^{-1}X,S_{1}S_{2}^{-1}Y]=[S_{2}^{-1}X,S_{2}^{-1}Y]=[X,Y],

since

[S_{2}^{-1}X,S_{2}^{-1}Y]=[S_{2}S_{2}^{-1}X,S_{2}S_{2}^{-1}Y]=[X,Y].

We shall prove below that the determinant of a symplectic mapping is actually 1.

\text{\sl Sp}(n,\mathbb{R})
is the subgroup of
S\in\text{\sl Gl}(2n,\mathbb{R})

such that

(1.2.25)

\forall X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\quad[SX,SY]=[X,Y],\qquad\text{i.e.\quad}S^{*}\sigma S=\sigma,

where
S^{*}
is the transpose and

(1.2.26)

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

It is easy to prove directly from (
1.2.25
) that

Sp(1,\mathbb{R})=Sl(2,\mathbb{R}).

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
n
be an integer
\geq 1
.
The group

Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
is included in
Sl(2n,\mathbb{R})

and generated by the following mappings

(1.2.27)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
A&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle,\ \text{where $A$ is a $n\times n$ symmetric matrix,}

(1.2.28)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}B^{-1}&0\\
0&B^{*}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle,\quad B\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R}),

(1.2.29)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle,\ \text{where $C$ is a $n\times n$ symmetric matrix.}

For
A,B,C
as above, the mapping

(1.2.30)

\Xi_{A,B,C}=\begin{pmatrix}B^{-1}&-B^{-1}C\\
AB^{-1}&B^{*}-AB^{-1}C\end{pmatrix}\\
=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
A&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B^{-1}&0\\
0&B^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

belongs to
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
.

Moreover, we define on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
the generating function
S

of the symplectic mapping

\Xi_{A,B,C}

by the identity

(1.2.31)

S(x,\eta)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\langle Ax,x\rangle+2\langle Bx,\eta\rangle+\langle C\eta,\eta\rangle\bigr)\ \text{so that}{\hskip 15.0pt}\Xi\Bigl(\frac{\partial S}{\partial\eta}\oplus\eta\Bigr)=x\oplus\frac{\partial S}{\partial x}.

For a symplectic mapping
\Xi
, to be of the form (
1.2.30
)
is equivalent
to the assumption that the mapping

x\mapsto\pi_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}}\Xi(x\oplus 0)

is invertible from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
;
moreover, if this mapping is not invertible,
the symplectic mapping
\Xi
is the product of two mappings of the type

\Xi_{A,B,C}
.

Proof.

The expression of
\Xi
above
as well as (
1.2.31
)
follow from a simple direct computation
left to the reader.
The inclusion of the symplectic group in the special linear group
follows from the statement
on the generators.
We consider now
\Xi
in
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
: we have

(1.2.32)

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{where
$P,Q,R,S,$
are $n\times n$
matrices.
}

The equation

(1.2.33)

{\Xi^{*}}\sigma\Xi=\sigma

is satisfied
with

\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix},

which means

(1.2.34)

{P^{*}}R={({P^{*}}R)}^{*},\quad{Q^{*}}S={({Q^{*}S})}^{*},\quad{P^{*}}S-{R^{*}}Q=I_{n}.

We can note also that the mapping
\Xi\mapsto\Xi^{*}
is an isomorphism
of
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})

since

\Xi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
means

\Xi^{*}\sigma\Xi=\sigma\Longrightarrow\Xi^{-1}\sigma^{-1}(\Xi^{*})^{-1}=\sigma^{-1}\Longrightarrow\Xi^{-1}(-\sigma^{-1})(\Xi^{*})^{-1}=(-\sigma^{-1}),

and since

(-\sigma^{-1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix},

we get that

\Xi^{*}\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
.
As a result,

(1.2.35)

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R}),

is also equivalent to

(1.2.36)

PQ^{*}={(PQ^{*})}^{*},\quad RS^{*}={(RS^{*})}^{*},\quad PS^{*}-QR^{*}=I_{n}.

Let us assume that
the mapping
P
is invertible,
which is the assumption in the last statement of the theorem.
We define then the mappings

A,B,C
by

\displaystyle A=RP^{-1},\quad B=P^{-1},\quad C=-P^{-1}Q,

\displaystyle\quad\text{\footnotesize so that we have}\quad A^{*}={P^{*}}^{-1}R^{*}PP^{-1}={P^{*}}^{-1}P^{*}RP^{-1}=RP^{-1}=A,

\displaystyle\quad\text{\footnotesize as well as}\ C^{*}=-Q^{*}{P^{*}}^{-1}=-P^{-1}PQ^{*}{P^{*}}^{-1}=-P^{-1}QP^{*}{P^{*}}^{-1}=-P^{-1}Q=C,

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize and}\quad P=B^{-1},\quad R=AB^{-1},\quad Q=-B^{-1}C,

\displaystyle S={P^{*}}^{-1}(I_{n}+R^{*}Q)=B^{*}(I_{n}-{B^{*}}^{-1}A^{*}B^{-1}C)=B^{*}-AB^{-1}C.

We have thus proven that any symplectic matrix

\Xi
as above such that

P
is invertible
is indeed given
by the product appearing in Theorem

1.10
.

Let us now consider the case where a symplectic mapping
\Xi
(given by (
1.2.35
))
is such that
\det P=0
;
writing

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\ker P\oplus N

we have that
P
is an isomorphism from
N
onto
\operatorname{ran}P
.
Let
B_{1}\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})
such that
B_{1}P
is the identity on
N
4
4

4

This is indeed possible: choosing a supplement space
M
for
P(N)
, we have

\mathbb{R}^{n}=\underbracket{\ker P}_{\text{dim }p}\oplus\underbracket{N}_{\text{dim }n-p}=\underbracket{P(N)}_{\text{dim }n-p}\oplus\underbracket{M}_{\text{dim }p},

and we can define
B_{1}
on
P(N)
by
B_{1}(Px)=x

(without ambiguity since for
x_{1},x_{2}\in N
with
Px_{1}=Px_{2}
we get

x_{1}-x_{2}\in\ker P\cap N=\{0\}
) and
{B_{1}}_{|M}:M\rightarrow\ker P

can be chosen as an isomorphism, so that

B_{1}(P(N))+B_{1}(M)=N+\ker P,\ \text{which implies }\operatorname{rank}B_{1}=n.

.
We have

(1.2.37)

\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&0\\
0&{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}P&B_{1}Q\\
{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}R&{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}S\end{pmatrix}.

If
p=\dim(\ker P)
, we have for the
n\times n
matrix
B_{1}P
the following block decomposition

(1.2.38)

B_{1}P=\begin{pmatrix}0_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&I_{n-p}\end{pmatrix},

where
0_{r,s}
stands for a
r\times s
matrix with only 0 as an entry.
On the other hand, we know from
(
1.2.34
)
that the mapping

(B_{1}P)^{*}{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}R=P^{*}R

is symmetric.
Writing

{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}R=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{R}_{p,p}&\tilde{R}_{p,n-p}\\
\tilde{R}_{n-p,p}&\tilde{R}_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix},

where
\tilde{R}_{r,s}
stands for a
r\times s
matrix,
this gives the symmetry of

\begin{pmatrix}0_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&I_{n-p}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{R}_{p,p}&\tilde{R}_{p,n-p}\\
\tilde{R}_{n-p,p}&\tilde{R}_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
\tilde{R}_{n-p,p}&\tilde{R}_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix},

implying that

\tilde{R}_{n-p,p}=0
.
The symplectic matrix (
1.2.37
) is thus equal to

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&I_{n-p}\end{pmatrix}&B_{1}Q\\
\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{R}_{p,p}&\tilde{R}_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&\tilde{R}_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix}&{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}S\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{where $B_{1}Q$ and ${B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}S$
are $n\times n$ blocks.}

The invertibility of (
1.2.37
) implies that
\tilde{R}_{p,p}
is invertible.
We consider now the
n\times n
symmetric matrix

C=\begin{pmatrix}I_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&0_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix},

and the symplectic mapping

(1.2.39)

\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}&0\\
0&{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{1}P&B_{1}Q\\
{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}R&{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}S\end{pmatrix},

which is a symplectic mapping
\begin{pmatrix}P^{\prime}&Q^{\prime}\\
R^{\prime}&S^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

with

P^{\prime}=B_{1}P+C{B_{1}^{*}}^{-1}R\\
=\begin{pmatrix}0_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&I_{n-p}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}I_{p,p}&0_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&0_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{R}_{p,p}&\tilde{R}_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&\tilde{R}_{n-p,n-p}\end{pmatrix}\\
=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{R}_{p,p}&\tilde{R}_{p,n-p}\\
0_{n-p,p}&\tilde{I}_{n-p}\end{pmatrix},

which is an invertible mapping.
From the equation (
1.2.39
)
and the first part of our discussion, we get that

\begin{pmatrix}P^{\prime}&Q^{\prime}\\
R^{\prime}&S^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
A^{\prime}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime-1}&0\\
0&B^{\prime*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C^{\prime}\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix},

with
A^{\prime},C^{\prime}
symmetric and
B^{\prime}
invertible
and

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}{B_{1}}^{-1}&0\\
0&{B_{1}^{*}}\end{pmatrix}\ \begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
A^{\prime}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime-1}&0\\
0&B^{\prime*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C^{\prime}\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix},

proving that
the
\Xi_{A,B,C}

generate the symplectic group
and more precisely
that every
\Xi
in the symplectic group is the product of at most two mappings of type

\Xi_{A,B,C}
.
The proof of
Theorem

1.10

is complete.
∎

Corollary 1.11
.

We have
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})\subset Sl(2n,\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Indeed the symplectic mappings (
1.2.27
), (
1.2.28
) and (
1.2.29
)
do have determinants equal to 1 and since Theorem
1.10
implies that they generate the symplectic group, this proves
the sought result.
∎

Remark 1.12
.

Of course for
n\geq 2
,
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
is a proper subgroup of
Sl(2n,\mathbb{R})
. Indeed
the following matrix

\mathtt{M}=\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}

has determinant 1 but fails to be symplectic: using Notation (
1.2.32
), we see that the first and the third equation are satisfied, which is not the case for the second one.

N.B
.

Since the matrix
-I_{2n}
belongs to
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
((
1.2.25
) holds trivially), we find that
S\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
is equivalent to

-S\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
.

Claim 1.13
.

The symplectic group is also generated by the mappings

(1.2.40)

\displaystyle\text{(i)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(B^{-1}x,B^{*}\xi),\quad B\in\text{\sl Gl}(n,\mathbb{R}),

(1.2.41)

\displaystyle\text{(ii)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(\xi,-x),

(1.2.42)

\displaystyle\text{(iii)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(x,\xi+Ax),\quad A\in\text{\sl Sym}(n,\mathbb{R}).

Another set of generators of the symplectic group is given by the mappings

(1.2.43)

\displaystyle\text{(j)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(B^{-1}x,B^{*}\xi),\quad B\in\text{\sl Gl}(n,\mathbb{R}),

(1.2.44)

\displaystyle\text{(jj)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(\xi,-x),

(1.2.45)

\displaystyle\text{(jjj)\hskip 10.22217pt}(x,\xi)\mapsto(x-C\xi,\xi),\quad C\in\text{\sl Sym}(n,\mathbb{R}).

Indeed, we have for
C^{*}=C
a real symmetric
n\times n
matrix

\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}}_{\sigma^{-1}}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}}_{\sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
C&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Remark 1.14
.

The symplectic matrix

(1.2.46)

\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}=2^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}2^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\Xi_{-I_{n},2^{1/2}I_{n},-I_{n}}^{2},

is not of the form

\Xi_{A,B,C}
but is the square of such a matrix.
It is also the case of all the mappings

(x_{k},\xi_{k})\mapsto(\xi_{k},-x_{k})

with the other coordinates fixed.
Similarly the symplectic matrix

(1.2.47)

\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{n}\\
I_{n}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-I_{n}\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
I_{n}&I_{n}\end{pmatrix},

is not of the form

\Xi_{A,B,C}
but is the product

\Xi_{0,I,I}\Xi_{I,I,0}
.

1.2.3.
The metaplectic group

Proposition 1.15
.

Let
A,B,C
be as in Theorem
1.10
,
and let
S
be the generating function of
\Xi_{A,B,C}
(cf. (
1.2.31
)).
We define the operator
M_{A,B,C}
on
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by

(1.2.48)

(M_{A,B,C}v)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2i\pi S(x,\eta)}\hat{v}(\eta)d\eta(\det B)^{1/2},

where
(\det B)^{1/2}
is a square root of
\det B
.
This operator is an automorphism of

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

which is unitary on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
and such that, for all
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,

(1.2.49)

M_{A,B,C}^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}M_{A,B,C}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\circ\Xi_{A,B,C})},

where
\Xi_{A,B,C}
is defined in Theorem
1.10
.

N.B
.

We have for
A,B,C
as above,

(1.2.50)

\displaystyle(M_{A,I,0}v)(x)

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi\langle Ax,x\rangle}v(x),

(1.2.51)

\displaystyle(M_{0,B,0}v)(x)

\displaystyle=(\det B)^{1/2}v(Bx),

(1.2.52)

\displaystyle(M_{0,I,C}v)(x)

\displaystyle=\bigl(e^{i\pi\langle CD_{x},D_{x}\rangle}v\bigr)(x),

three operators which are obviously automorphisms of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and of

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as well as unitary operators in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Formula (
1.2.49
) is easily checked for each operator (
1.2.50
),
(
1.2.51
)
and (
1.2.52
). Since
\Xi_{A,B,C}=\Xi_{A,I,0}\ \Xi_{0,B,0}\ \Xi_{0,I,C}
and

(1.2.53)

M_{A,B,C}=M_{A,I,0}M_{0,B,0}M_{0,I,C},

we get (
1.2.49
) and the proposition.
∎

Remark 1.16
.

We define

(1.2.54)

m(B)=\frac{\arg(\det B)}{\pi}\\
=\begin{cases}\frac{k2\pi}{\pi}=2k\in\{0,2\}\mod 4\quad\text{for }\det B>0,\\
\frac{k2\pi+\pi}{\pi}=2k+1\in\{1,3\}\mod 4\quad\text{for }\det B<0,\end{cases}

so that

(1.2.55)

\det B=|\det B|e^{i\pi m(B)},\quad(\det B)^{1/2}\in|\det B|^{1/2}\{e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}m(B)},e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}(m(B)+2)}\}.

We shall consider
m(B)
as an element of
\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
, so that the function
m(B)\mapsto e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}m(B)}
is well-defined. For
A,B,C
as in Proposition
1.15
, we may define

(1.2.56)

\bigl(M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}v\bigr)(x)=e^{\frac{i\pi m(B)}{2}}|\det B|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{i\pi(Ax^{2}+2Bx\cdot\eta+C\eta^{2})}\hat{v}(\eta)d\eta,

but
most of the time, we shall omit the super-script
m(B)
when we do not want to distinguish between the two roots of
\det B
.
7
7

7

We note in particular that we have

M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}=\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\ M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{2\}}=-\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and also with the notation (
1.2.9
),

M_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n\}}=e^{\frac{i\pi n}{2}}\sigma_{0},\ M_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n+2\}}=-e^{\frac{i\pi n}{2}}\sigma_{0}.

More generally, we have

\text{for $\det B>0$, }M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}=-M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{2\}},\qquad\text{for $\det B<0$, }M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{1\}}=-M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{3\}}.

We note also that for
B\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})
, we have

(1.2.57)

m(B^{*})=m(B)=m(B^{-1}),

since
\det B=\det B^{*}
and

\det(B^{-1})=(\det B)^{-1}

so that

\arg(\det B)=\arg(\det B^{-1}).

Moreover we have for
B\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})
,

\det(-B)=(-1)^{n}\det B,\quad\arg(\det(-B))=\begin{cases}\arg(\det B)&\text{if $n$ is even,}\\
\arg(\det B)+\pi&\text{if $n$ is odd,}\end{cases}

so that

(1.2.58)

m(-B)=n+m(B).

Examples
.

Let us start with a one-dimensional example: in Remark
1.14
, we have seen in particular that

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}=\left\{2^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}\right\}^{2},\quad 2^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
-1&1\end{pmatrix}=\Xi_{-1,2^{1/2},-1},

where we have used
(
1.2.30
) to get the second equation.
We have also with the notations of Theorem
1.10
,

(M_{-1,2^{1/2},-1}v)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2i\pi\frac{1}{2}(-x^{2}+2^{3/2}x\eta-\eta^{2})}\hat{v}(\eta)d\eta 2^{1/4},

so that the kernel
k_{1}(x,y)
of the operator
M_{-1,2^{1/2},-1}
is

k_{1}(x,y)=2^{1/4}\int e^{i\pi(-x^{2}+2^{3/2}x\eta-\eta^{2})}e^{-2i\pi y\eta}d\eta\underbracket{=}_{\text{use \eqref{foimga}}}2^{1/4}e^{-i\pi/4}e^{i\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})}e^{-2^{3/2}i\pi xy},

so that the kernel
k_{2}

of the operator
(M_{-1,2^{1/2},-1})^{2}
is (using again (
9.1.8
)),

k_{2}(x,y)=\int k_{1}(x,z)k_{1}(z,y)dz\\
=2^{1/2}e^{-i\pi/2}e^{i\pi(x^{2}+y^{2})}\int e^{2i\pi z^{2}}e^{-2i\pi z2^{1/2}(x+y)}dz=e^{-i\pi/4}e^{-2i\pi xy},

so that

(1.2.59)

(M_{-1,2^{1/2},-1})^{2}=e^{-i\pi/4}\mathcal{F}_{1},

with
\mathcal{F}_{1}
standing for the 1d Fourier transformation.
We get similarly that in
n
dimensions,

(1.2.60)

(M_{-I_{n},2^{1/2}I_{n},-I_{n}})^{2}=e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F},

with
\mathcal{F}
standing for the Fourier transformation.
Similar expressions can be obtained for
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, the Fourier transformation with respect to the variable
x_{k}
in
n
dimensions,
k\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
with

(1.2.61)

(M_{A_{k},B_{k},C_{k}})^{2}=e^{-i\pi/4}\mathcal{F}_{k},

where
B_{k}
is the
n\times n
diagonal matrix with diagonal entries equal to
1
except for the
k
th equal to
2^{1/2}
, the
n\times n
diagonal
matrices
A_{k}=C_{k}
with diagonal entries equal to 0, except for the
k
th equal to
-1
.

Definition 1.17
.

The metaplectic group
Mp(n)

is defined as the subgroup of the group of unitary operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
generated by

(1.2.62)

\displaystyle M_{A,I,0},\text{\footnotesize where $A$ is a $n\times n$ symmetric matrix, cf. \eqref{mety01}},

(1.2.63)

\displaystyle M_{0,B,0},\text{\footnotesize with $B\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})$, with $(\det B)^{\frac{1}{2}}=|\det B|^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{i\pi m(B)}{2}}$, cf. \eqref{maslov},
\eqref{mety02},
}

(1.2.64)

\displaystyle M_{0,I,C},\text{\footnotesize where $C$ is a $n\times n$ symmetric matrix, cf. \eqref{mety03}}.

Claim 1.18
.

If
M
belongs to
Mp(n)
, then
-M
belongs to
Mp(n)
.

Proof.

According to Footnote
7
on page
7
, we have
M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{2\}}=-M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}=-\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

so that
-\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
belongs to
Mp(n)
, proving the claim.
∎

Proposition 1.19
.

The metaplectic group
Mp(n)

is generated by

(1.2.65)

\displaystyle M_{A,I,0},\text{\footnotesize where $A$ is a $n\times n$ symmetric matrix, cf. \eqref{mety01}},

(1.2.66)

\displaystyle M_{0,B,0},\text{\footnotesize with $B\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})$, with $(\det B)^{\frac{1}{2}}=|\det B|^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{i\pi m(B)}{2}}$, cf. \eqref{maslov},
\eqref{mety02},
}

(1.2.67)

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}\mathcal{F},\text{\footnotesize where $\mathcal{F}$ is the Fourier transformation.}

Proof.

We check for
C
symmetric
n\times n
matrix,

\bigl(M_{C,I,0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}(e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}v)\bigr)(\eta)=e^{-i\pi n/4}e^{i\pi C\eta^{2}}\hat{v}(\eta),

so that

e^{i\pi n/4}\bigl(\mathcal{F}^{-1}(e^{-i\pi n/4}e^{i\pi C\eta^{2}}\hat{v}(\eta))\bigr)(x)=\int e^{2i\pi x\eta}e^{i\pi C\eta^{2}}\hat{v}(\eta)d\eta=(M_{0,I,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}v)(x),\qquad

yielding

e^{i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}^{-1}M_{0,I,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}=M_{0,I,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}},

so that the group generated by (
1.2.65
), (
1.2.66
), (
1.2.67
)
contains
(
1.2.62
), (
1.2.63
), (
1.2.64
) and thus contains
Mp(n)
.
Moreover (
1.2.60
) shows that (
1.2.67
) is included in
Mp(n)

so that the group generated by (
1.2.65
), (
1.2.66
), (
1.2.67
) is included in
Mp(n)
, proving the proposition.
∎

Remark 1.20
.

According to (
9.1.6
) in our Appendix and to Footnote
7
on page
7
, we find

(1.2.68)

(e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F})^{*}=e^{i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\sigma_{0}=e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}e^{i\pi n/2}\sigma_{0}=e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}M_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n\}}.

As a consequence,

e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F},e^{-i\pi n/2}\sigma_{0},e^{i\pi n/2}\sigma_{0}

belong to the metaplectic group.

Lemma 1.21
.

For
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we define the linear form
L_{Y}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
by

(1.2.69)

L_{Y}(X)=\langle\sigma Y,X\rangle=[Y,X].

For any
M\in Mp(n)
there exists a unique
\chi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
such that

(1.2.70)

\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\quad M^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})}M={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\chi^{-1}Y})}.

Proof.

Indeed, thanks to (
1.2.49
) and Definition
1.17
, we can find
\chi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
such that

\displaystyle M^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})}M={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y}\circ\chi)}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\chi^{-1}Y})},\quad\text{since}

\displaystyle(L_{Y}\circ\chi)(X)=\langle\sigma Y,\chi X\rangle=\langle\chi^{*}\sigma\chi\chi^{-1}Y,X\rangle=\langle\sigma\chi^{-1}Y,X\rangle=L_{\chi^{-1}Y}(X).

Moreover, if
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
are such that for all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

0={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\chi_{2}^{-1}Y}-L_{\chi_{1}^{-1}Y})}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{(\chi_{2}^{-1}-\chi_{1}^{-1})Y})},

we get
L_{(\chi_{2}^{-1}-\chi_{1}^{-1})Y}=0
, implying

\forall Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\ (\chi_{2}^{-1}-\chi_{1}^{-1})Y=0,

i.e.

\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}
.∎

We can thus define a mapping

(1.2.71)

\Psi:Mp(n)\rightarrow Sp(n,\mathbb{R}),\quad\text{with $\Psi(M)=\chi$ satisfying
\eqref{hg54rr}.}

In particular we have from (
1.2.49
) in Proposition
1.15
and (
1.2.60
) that

(1.2.72)

\Psi(M_{A,B,C})=\Xi_{A,B,C},\qquad\Psi\bigl(e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}\mathcal{F}\bigr)=\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Theorem 1.22
.

The mapping
\Psi
defined in (
1.2.71
) is a surjective homomorphism of groups with kernel
\{\pm\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\}
.

Proof.

This mapping is an homomorphism of groups: if
M_{1},M_{2}
belong to
Mp(n)
, we have with
\chi_{j}=\Psi(M_{j})
,

(M_{1}M_{2})^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})}M_{1}M_{2}=M_{2}^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\chi_{1}^{-1}Y})}M_{2}\\
={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{\chi_{2}^{-1}\chi_{1}^{-1}Y})}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{(\chi_{1}\circ\chi_{2})^{-1}Y})},

proving that

\Psi(M_{1}M_{2})=\Psi(M_{1})\Psi(M_{2}).

Moreover the homomorphism
\Psi
is onto, thanks to (
1.2.49
) and Theorem
1.10
.
The kernel of
\Psi
is made with
M\in Mp(n)
such that for all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

M^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})}M={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})},\quad\text{i.e.}\quad\bigl[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})},M\bigr]=0,

so that, thanks to (
1.2.5
), (
1.2.6
), if
\mu(x,\xi)
is the Weyl symbol of
M

(
M
is an endomorphism of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and thus has a distribution kernel as well as a Weyl symbol
via Formula (
1.2.22
)), we get for all
(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

0=\left\{\eta\cdot x-y\cdot\xi,\mu(x,\xi)\right\}\quad\text{so that $d\mu=0$,}

and
\mu
is a constant so that
M=c\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, necessarily with
|c|=1
(since
M
is unitary).
Applying Theorem
9.17
gives
c\in\{\pm 1\}
, concluding the proof.∎

N.B
.

The proof of Theorem
9.17
is relegated in our Appendix, and requires some effort.

Corollary 1.23
.

For
\chi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
,
the fiber
\Psi^{-1}\{\chi\}
contains exactly two metaplectic transformations and more precisely

\Psi^{-1}\{\chi\}=\{M,-M\},

where
M
is a metaplectic transformation.

Proof.

This corollary is an immediate consequence of Theorem
1.22
.
∎

Theorem 1.24

(Symplectic covariance of the Weyl calculus)
.

Let
a
be in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and let
\chi
be in
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
. Then for a metaplectic operator
M
such that
\Psi(M)=\chi
, we have

(1.2.73)

M^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}M={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\circ\chi)}.

For
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

we have

(1.2.74)

\mathcal{W}\left(Mu,Mv\right)=\mathcal{W}(u,v)\circ\chi^{-1},

where
\mathcal{W}
is the Wigner distribution given in (
1.1.6
).

Proof.

The first property follows immediately from (
1.2.49
) and Definition
1.17

whereas (
1.2.74
) is a consequence of (
1.2.3
) and (
1.2.73
).
∎

We note also that for
Y=(y,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, the symmetry
S_{Y}
is defined by

S_{Y}(X)=2Y-X
and is quantized by the phase symmetry
\sigma_{Y}
as defined by (
1.2.9
)
with the formula

(1.2.75)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\circ S_{Y})}=\sigma_{Y}^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\sigma_{Y}=\sigma_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\sigma_{Y}.

Similarly,
the translation
T_{Y}
is defined on the phase space by
T_{Y}(X)=X+Y
and is quantized by the
phase translation

\tau_{Y}
,

(1.2.76)

(\tau_{(y,\eta)}u)(x)=u(x-y)e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{y}{2})\cdot\eta},

and we have

(1.2.77)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\circ T_{Y})}=\tau_{Y}^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\tau_{Y}=\tau_{-Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\tau_{Y}.

Remark 1.25
.

Property (
1.2.74
) can be extended to the affine symplectic group and we have with the phase translations defined in (
1.2.76
),

(1.2.78)

\forall(X,Y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n},\qquad\mathcal{W}\left(\tau_{Y}u,\tau_{Y}v\right)(X)=\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X-Y).

We shall define the
affine group
Mp_{a}(n)
as the group of unitary transformations of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

generated by transformations (
1.2.50
), (
1.2.51
), (
1.2.52
)
and phase translations given by (
1.2.76
).

N.B
.

More information on the metaplectic group
is given in J. Leray’s book
[
31
]
, the same author’s articles

[
32
]
,
[
30
]
,
as well as A. Weil’s
paper
[
52
]

(see also V.S. Buslaev’s article

[
5
]
,
Chapter 9 in K. Gröchenig’s book
[
16
]
,
H. Reiter’s Lecture Notes
[
43
]
).

Theorem 1 in E. Lieb’s classical article
[
37
]
gives a more precise version of
(
1.2.80
), (
1.2.81
) and (
1.2.82
) below.

Theorem 1.26
.

Let
u,v
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
is a uniformly continuous function belonging to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and using the definitions (
1.2.76
), (
1.2.9
) for the phase translations and phase symmetry, we have

(1.2.79)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)=2^{n}\langle\sigma_{X}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=2^{n}\langle\tau_{X}^{*}u,\tau_{X}\check{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\hskip 190.0pt=2^{n}\langle\sigma_{0}\tau_{-2X}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(1.2.80)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

(1.2.81)

\displaystyle\forall p\in[1,+{\infty}],\quad\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq 2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

More generally, for
q\geq 2
and
r\in[q^{\prime},q]
, we have
8
8

8

We use the standard notation: for
p\in[1,+{\infty}]
we define
p^{\prime}
by the equality
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1.

(1.2.82)

\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq 2^{\frac{n(q-2)}{q}}\|u\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Moreover, we have

(1.2.83)

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni X,|X|\rightarrow+{\infty}\end{subarray}}\Bigl[{\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)}\Bigr]=0.

Proof.

We have with
\check{v}(x)=v(-x)=(\sigma_{0}v)(x)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)=2^{n}\int u(x+z)\bar{v}(x-z)e^{-4i\pi z\xi}dz

\displaystyle=2^{n}\int u(z-(-x))e^{2i\pi(z-\frac{-x}{2})(-\xi)}\bar{\check{v}}(z-x)e^{-2i\pi(z-\frac{x}{2})\xi}e^{-4i\pi z\xi+2i\pi(z-\frac{-x}{2})\xi+2i\pi(z-\frac{x}{2})\xi}dz

\displaystyle=2^{n}\int(\tau_{(-x,-\xi)}u)(z)\overline{(\tau_{(x,\xi)}\check{v})(z)}dz=2^{n}\langle\tau_{(x,\xi)}^{*}u,\tau_{(x,\xi)}\check{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

or for short

(1.2.84)

\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)=2^{n}\langle\tau_{X}^{*}u,\tau_{X}\check{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

As a consequence we find from (
1.2.11
) that

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,v\rangle=\int a(X)2^{n}\langle\sigma_{0}\tau_{2X}^{*}u,v\rangle dX,

and since

(\sigma_{x,\xi}u)(y)=u(2x-y)e^{-4i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi},

we can verify directly that

(1.2.85)

\sigma_{0}\tau_{-2X}=\sigma_{X}.

Indeed, composing the translation of vector
-2X
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with the symmetry with respect to 0,
we have

Y\mapsto Y-2X\mapsto 2X-Y=Y^{\prime},\qquad\frac{1}{2}(Y+Y^{\prime})=X,

that is the symmetry with respect to
X
.
Quantifying this equality, we use

(\tau_{(-2x,-2\xi)}u)(z)=u(z+2x)e^{2i\pi(z-\frac{-2x}{2})(-2\xi)}=u(z+2x)e^{-4i\pi(z+x)\xi},

so that we obtain

\sigma_{0}(\tau_{(-2x,-2\xi)}u)(z)=u(-z+2x)e^{-4i\pi(-z+x)\xi}=(\sigma_{x,\xi}u)(z),

which proves (
1.2.85
) and thus (
1.2.79
).
Formula (
1.2.80
) is already proven in (
1.1.8
) and (
1.2.81
)
follows from (
1.2.79
), Hölder’s
inequality and the fact that
\tau_{X}
is an endomorphism of
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

with norm 1 (cf. the expression (
1.2.76
)).
To prove (
1.2.82
) we note that
from the expression (
1.2.15
), the Hausdorff-Young’s inequality implies

(1.2.86)

\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{q}\otimes L^{q}}\leq\|\Omega(u,v)\|_{L^{q}\otimes L^{q^{\prime}}}\leq\||u|^{q^{\prime}}\ast|v|^{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{q/q^{\prime}}}^{1/q^{\prime}}2^{n\frac{q-2}{q}},

and since
Young’s inequality
9
9

9

For
p,q,r\in[1,+{\infty}]
with
\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{q^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}
, we have

(1.2.87)

\|f\ast g\|_{L^{r}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}}.

gives

\||u|^{q^{\prime}}\ast|v|^{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{q/q^{\prime}}}\leq\||u|^{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{a/q^{\prime}}}\||v|^{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{b/q^{\prime}}},

a,b\geq q^{\prime}
with

1-\frac{q^{\prime}}{q}=1-\frac{q^{\prime}}{a}+1-\frac{q^{\prime}}{b},\quad\text{i.e.\quad}q^{\prime}\bigl(\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}\bigr)=1+\frac{q^{\prime}}{q},\ \text{that is \ }\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}=1,

so that

\||u|^{q^{\prime}}\ast|v|^{q^{\prime}}\|_{L^{q/q^{\prime}}}\leq\|{u}\|_{L^{a}}^{q^{\prime}}\|{v}\|_{L^{b}}^{q^{\prime}},

in such a way that (
1.2.86
) yields

\|\mathcal{W}(u,v)\|_{L^{q}\otimes L^{q}}\leq 2^{n\frac{q-2}{q}}\|{u}\|_{L^{a}}\|{v}\|_{L^{b}},\quad a,b\geq q^{\prime},\quad\frac{1}{a}+\frac{1}{b}=1,

which is
(
1.2.82
) .
We are left with the proof of uniform continuity of
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
.
We have for
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

\mathcal{W}(u,v)(Y)-\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)=2^{n}\langle(\sigma_{Y}-\sigma_{X})u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and since
\sigma_{Y}^{2}=\operatorname{Id}
(see the footnote
2
on page
2
), we find

\mathcal{W}(u,v)(Y)-\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)=2^{n}\langle(\sigma_{Y}\sigma_{X}-\operatorname{Id})\sigma_{X}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
=2^{n}\langle\sigma_{X}u,(\sigma_{X}\sigma_{Y}-\operatorname{Id})v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

According to Formula (2.1.16)
in
[
33
]
, we have

(1.2.88)

\sigma_{X}\sigma_{Y}=\tau_{2X-2Y}e^{4i\pi[Y,X]},

and this implies

(1.2.89)

|\mathcal{W}(u,v)(Y)-\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)|\leq 2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\tau_{2(X-Y)}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

We have from (
1.2.75
),

\tau_{z,\zeta}v(x)-v(x)=v(x-z)e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{z}{2})\zeta}-v(x)\\
=\bigl(v(x-z)-v(x)\bigr)e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{z}{2})\zeta}+v(x)\bigl(e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{z}{2})\zeta}-1\bigr),

and thus

\|\tau_{Z}v-v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\left(\int|v(x-z)-v(x)|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}+\left(\int|v(x)|^{2}|e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{z}{2})\zeta}-1|^{2}dx\right)^{1/2}.

We have the classical result, due to the density in
L^{2}
of continuous compactly supported functions,

\lim_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\ni z\rightarrow 0}\int|v(x-z)-v(x)|^{2}dx=0,

and moreover the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem implies

\lim_{(z,\zeta)\rightarrow(0,0)}\int\underbrace{|v(x)|^{2}}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\underbrace{|e^{2i\pi(x-\frac{z}{2})\zeta}-1|^{2}}_{\leq 4}dx=0,

so that

\lim_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni Z\rightarrow 0}\|\tau_{Z}v-v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0
.
As a consequence (
1.2.89
) implies the uniform continuity of
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
.
Moreover, we have, for
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{W}(u,v)=\mathcal{W}(u-\phi,v)+\mathcal{W}(\phi,v-\psi)+\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi),

so that

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq\int|(u-\phi)(x+\frac{z}{2})||v(x-\frac{z}{2})|dz

\displaystyle\hskip 58.0pt+\iint|(v-\psi)(x-\frac{z}{2})||\phi(x+\frac{z}{2})|dz+|\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)|

\displaystyle\leq 2^{n}\|u-\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+2^{n}\|v-\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\hskip 237.0pt+|\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)|.

We choose now sequences
(\phi_{k}),(\psi_{k})
of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

converging respectively in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
towards
u,v
.
We obtain for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(1.2.90)

|\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)|\leq 2^{n}\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+2^{n}\|v-\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
+|\mathcal{W}(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})(x,\xi)|,

so that using that
\mathcal{W}(\phi_{k},\psi_{k})
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, we get

\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni X,|X|\rightarrow+{\infty}\end{subarray}}\Bigl[|\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)|\Bigr]\\
\leq 2^{n}\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+2^{n}\|v-\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and thus, taking the limit when
k\rightarrow+{\infty}
, we obtain

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni X,|X|\rightarrow+{\infty}\end{subarray}}\Bigl[|\mathcal{W}(u,v)(X)|\Bigr]=0,

completing the proof of Theorem
1.26
.
∎

Remark 1.27
.

Let
u
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be an even function. We then have

(1.2.91)

\mathcal{W}(u,u)(0,0)=2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}=\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

On the other hand if
u
is odd we have

(1.2.92)

\mathcal{W}(u,u)(0,0)=-2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}=-\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

showing that for odd functions the minimum of the Wigner distribution is negative (we assume
u\not=0
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) and attained at 0.
Let us check for instance the (odd) function
u_{1}
of Remark
1.3
.
We have

2\|u_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=2\int x^{2}e^{-2\pi x^{2}}dx=4\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{t}{2\pi}e^{-t}(2\pi)^{-1/2}\frac{1}{2}t^{-1/2}dt\\
=\frac{2\Gamma(3/2)}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}=\frac{\Gamma(1/2)}{(2\pi)^{3/2}}=\frac{1}{2^{3/2}\pi}=-\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{1})(0,0).

1.2.4.
On weak versions of the Wigner distribution

Let
u,v
be in the space
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

of tempered distributions. Then we can define as above the tempered distribution

\Omega(u,v)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
:
we set

(1.2.93)

\langle\Omega(u,v)(x,z),\Phi(x,z)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\\
=\langle u(x_{1})\otimes\bar{v}(x_{2}),\Phi(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},x_{1}-x_{2})\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

and then we define the Wigner distribution
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
as the Fourier
transform with respect to
z
of
\Omega(u,v)
, meaning that

(1.2.94)

\langle\mathcal{W}(u,v),\Psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\langle\Omega(u,v),\mathcal{F}_{2}\Psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

where

(\mathcal{F}_{2}\Psi)(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\Psi(x,z)dz.

Of course
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
is only a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and we have the inversion formula, using the notations of Remark
1.8
,

(1.2.95)

\Omega(u,v)=\mathcal{F}_{2}\mathcal{C}_{2}\mathcal{W}(u,v).

The above remarks show that there is no difficulty to extend the definition of the joint Wigner distribution
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
to the case where
u,v
are both tempered distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Some properties are surviving from the
L^{2}
theory, in particular the inversion formula, but one should be reasonably cautious at avoiding to write brackets of duality as integrals.
Theorem 2 in
[
37
]
gives a more complete version of the following result.

Theorem 1.28
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. Then
u
belongs to

L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
p\in[1,+{\infty}]

and we have

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|u\|_{L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

N.B
.

We refer the reader to our Section

6.3
and in particular to
our
Theorem
6.18
on page
6.18

showing that the set of
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
is meager.

Proof.

Thanks to Theorem
1.26
, we have
\mathcal{W}(u,u)\in L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
for all
p\in[1,+{\infty}]

and we have in a weak sense,

u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{u}(x-\frac{z}{2})=\int e^{2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)d\xi,

so that

(1.2.96)

u(x_{1})\bar{u}(x_{2})=\int e^{2i\pi(x_{1}-x_{2})\cdot\xi}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)d\xi,

and thus

\int|u(x_{1})||u(x_{2})|dx_{1}\leq\iint\bigl|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)\bigr|d\xi dx_{1}=2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

i.e.

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|u\|_{L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq 2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

proving the lemma.
∎

1.2.5.
Composition Formulas

The following lemma is classical (see e.g
[
19
]
,
[
46
]
); however
we shall provide a proof for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 1.29
.

Let
u,v,f,g
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then

(1.2.97)

\langle u,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle f,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)\mathcal{W}(f,g)(x,\xi)dxd\xi.

In other words, the Weyl symbol of the rank-one operator

u\mapsto\langle u,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}f

is
\mathcal{W}(f,g)
.
In particular if
f=g
is a unit vector in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we find that

\mathcal{W}(f,f)
is the Weyl symbol of the orthogonal projection onto
\mathbb{C}f
.

Proof.

Both functions
\mathcal{W}(u,v),\mathcal{W}(f,g)
belong to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, so that the integral on the right-hand-side of (
1.2.97
)
actually makes sense. Also

\mathcal{W}(u,v)

is the partial Fourier transform with respect to the variable
z

of
(x,z)\mapsto u(x+z/2)\bar{v}(x-z/2)
, thus applying Plancherel formula
10
10

10

We refer of course to the formula

\langle\hat{u},\hat{v}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

when using the
complex
Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Note however that Formula (
1.1.4
) is using the
real
duality between

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
so that to check,
with
\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
standing for the anti-dual of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})

(i.e. continuous anti-linear forms on
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
), we have also

(1.2.98)

\langle\widehat{T},\widehat{\phi}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\langle\widehat{T},\overline{\widehat{\phi}}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\langle T,\widehat{\overline{\widehat{\phi}}}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}=\langle T,{\overline{\phi}}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{{}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}\\
=\langle T,{{\phi}}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}^{N}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{N})}.

we obtain that

\iint\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)\mathcal{W}(f,g)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\\
=\iint u(x+z/2)\bar{v}(x-z/2)f(x-z/2)\bar{g}(x+z/2)dxdz\\
=\langle u,g\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle f,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

The last property follows from (
1.2.3
).
∎

Using Section 2.1.5 in
[
33
]
,
we obtain that
for
a,b\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(Y)b(Z)2^{2n}\sigma_{Y}\sigma_{Z}dYdZ.

We get

(1.2.99)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\sharp b)},

with

(1.2.100)

\displaystyle\ (a\sharp b)(X)

\displaystyle=2^{2n}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-4i\pi[X-Y,X-Z]}a(Y)b(Z)dYdZ

(1.2.101)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-2i\pi\langle\Xi,Z\rangle}a\bigl(X+\frac{\sigma^{-1}\Xi}{2}\bigr)b(Z+X)d\Xi dZ,

(1.2.102)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{2i\pi\langle X,\Xi\rangle}a\bigl(X+\frac{\sigma^{-1}\Xi}{2}\bigr)\widehat{b}(\Xi)d\Xi,

where
[\cdot,\cdot]
is the symplectic form (
1.2.24
) and
\sigma
is (
1.2.26
).
Formula (
1.2.101
) is interesting since very close to the group
J^{t}
defined in Formula (4.1.14) of

[
33
]
.

Lemma 1.30
.

Let
u_{0},u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Then we have for all
X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

(1.2.103)

|\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}||\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{3})(X)|\leq 2^{n}\bigl(|\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})|\ast|\mathcal{W}(\check{u}_{1},u_{3})|\bigr)(X).

Proof.

According to Lemma
1.29
,
we have for
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})}){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{3})})v={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})})\bigl(\langle v,u_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}u_{1}\bigr)\\
=\langle v,u_{3}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}u_{0}\\
=\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{3}))}v,

so that with the notation (
1.2.99
), we get

(1.2.104)

\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})\sharp\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{3})=\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{3}),

and using (
1.2.102
), we get

(1.2.105)

\bigl(\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})\sharp\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{3})\bigr)(x,\xi)\\
=\iint e^{2i\pi(x\cdot\eta+\xi\cdot y)}\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})\bigl(x-\frac{y}{2},\xi+\frac{\eta}{2}\bigr)\overbrace{\mathcal{F}\bigl(\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{3})\bigr)(\eta,y)}^{\mathcal{A}(u_{1},u_{3})(-\eta,-y)}dyd\eta,

where
\mathcal{F}
stands for the Fourier transformation and
\mathcal{A}
for the ambiguity function
(cf. (
1.2.13
)).
With Formula (
1.2.14
), we obtain

(1.2.106)

\bigl(\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})\sharp\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{3})\bigr)(x,\xi)\\
=\iint e^{4i\pi(-x\cdot\eta+\xi\cdot y)}\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})(x-y,\xi-\eta)\mathcal{W}(\check{u}_{1},u_{3})(y,\eta)dyd\eta 2^{n},

yielding from (
1.2.104
) for any
X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

(1.2.107)

\langle u_{1},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}}\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{3})(X)\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{4i\pi[X,Y]}\mathcal{W}(u_{0},u_{2})(X-Y)\mathcal{W}(\check{u}_{1},u_{3})(Y)dY2^{n},

which implies the lemma.
∎

1.2.6.
L^{2}
-boundedness

Theorem 1.31
.

Let
a
be a semi-classical symbol on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
i.e. a smooth function of
(x,\xi)
depending on
h\in(0,1]
such that

(1.2.108)

\forall l\in\mathbb{N},\quad p_{l}(a)=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},h\in(0,1]\\
|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq l\end{subarray}}|(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\text{a})(x,\xi,h)|h^{-\frac{|\alpha|+|\beta|}{2}}<+{\infty}.

Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a(x,\xi,h))}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and such that

(1.2.109)

\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a(x,\xi,h))}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq c_{n}p_{\ell_{n}}(a),

where
c_{n}
and
\ell_{n}
depend only on
n
.

Proof.

Theorem 1.2 in A. Boulkhemair’s article
[
3
]
is providing that result (and more)
with
\ell_{n}=[n/2]+1
.
Note also that Theorem 1.1.4 in
[
33
]
is providing an elementary proof
of the above result for the ordinary quantization of
a
given by

(1.2.110)

({\text{Op}_{0}(a)}u)(x)=\int e^{2i\pi x\cdot\xi}a(x,\xi,h)\hat{u}(\xi)d\xi\\
=\iint e^{2i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}a(x,\xi,h)u(y)dyd\xi.

∎

N.B
.

Formula (
1.2.102
) appears as

(1.2.111)

(a\sharp b)(X)=\Bigl({\text{Op}_{0}\bigl(a\bigl(X-\frac{\sigma\Xi}{2}\bigr)}b\Bigr)(X),

where
{\text{Op}_{0}(\cdot)}
stands for the ordinary quantization in
2n
dimensions.

The following classical result is a consequence of Theorem
1.31
.

Theorem 1.32
.

Let
C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
be the set of bounded smooth complex-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that all derivatives are bounded and
let
a
be in
C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and the
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
norm of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
is bounded above by a fixed semi-norm of
a
in the Fréchet space
C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

1.2.7.
On the Heisenberg Uncertainty Relations

Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
. We have, using the notations (
1.1.5
),

(1.2.112)

2\operatorname{Re}\langle D_{x}u,ixu\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle[D_{x},ix]u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

implying in particular

(1.2.113)

\|D_{x}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|xu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\geq\frac{1}{4\pi}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

which is an equality for
u(x)=e^{-\pi x^{2}}
; moreover we infer also from
(
1.2.112
) that

(1.2.114)

\displaystyle\langle\pi(D_{x}^{2}+x^{2})u,u\rangle

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

and for

(1.2.115)

q_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}),\quad 0\leq\mu_{1}\leq\dots\leq\mu_{n},

the inequality

(1.2.116)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(\pi q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\geq\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{defined as}\\
\operatorname{trace}_{+}(q_{\mu})\end{subarray}},

which is an equality for
u(x)=e^{-\pi|x|^{2}}
. Note that the above (optimal)
inequality can be reformulated as

(1.2.117)

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\pi q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\geq\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{trace}_{+}(q_{\mu}).

Note also that with the symplectic matrix
\sigma
defined by (
1.2.26
),
the so-called fundamental matrix of
q_{\mu}
is defined by

(1.2.118)

F_{q_{\mu}}=\sigma^{-1}Q_{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-I\\
I&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}M&0\\
0&M\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-M\\
M&0\end{pmatrix},\\
\text{with\quad}M=\operatorname{diag}(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}),

so that

(1.2.119)

\operatorname{Spectrum}F_{q_{\mu}}=\{\pm i\mu_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n},\quad\operatorname{trace}_{+}(q_{\mu})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\text{ eigenvalue of $F_{q_{\mu}}$}\\
\text{with }\operatorname{Im}\lambda>0\end{subarray}}\lambda/i.

With the notations

(1.2.120)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C_{j}=D_{x_{j}}+ix_{j},&\text{ creation operators},\\
C_{j}^{*}=D_{x_{j}}-ix_{j},&\text{annihilation operators},\end{cases}

we see that

\pi[C_{j}^{*},C_{j}]=\pi[D_{x_{j}}-ix_{j},D_{x_{j}}+ix_{j}]=I,

and

(1.2.121)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu})}=\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}C_{j}C_{j}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{trace}_{+}(q_{\mu}),

which provides another proof of (
1.2.116
).

Lemma 1.33

(Quantum Mechanics must deal with unbounded operators
11
11

11

Thus QM must involve infinite dimensional Hilbert spaces and unbounded operators on them.
)
.

Let
\mathbb{H}
be a Hilbert space and let
J,K\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})
; then the commutator
[J,K]\not=\operatorname{Id}
.

Proof.

Let
J,K
be bounded operators with
[J,K]=\operatorname{Id}
. Then for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have

(1.2.122)

[J,K^{N}]=NK^{N-1}.

Indeed, this is true for
N=1
and if it holds for some
N\geq 1
, we find that

[J,K^{N+1}]=JK^{N}K-K^{N+1}J=[J,K^{N}]K+K^{N}JK-K^{N+1}J\\
=[J,K^{N}]K+K^{N}(JK-KJ)=[J,K^{N}]K+K^{N}=(N+1)K^{N},\quad\text{qed}.

Note that (
1.2.122
) implies that for all
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we have
K^{N}\not=0
: of course
K\not=0
since
[J,K]=\operatorname{Id}
and
if we had
K^{N}=0
for some
N\geq 2
,
(
1.2.122
) would imply
K^{N-1}=0

and eventually
K=0
.
As a consequence, we get from (
1.2.122
) that for all
N\geq 2
,

N\|K^{N-1}\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}\leq 2\|J\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}\|K^{N}\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}\leq 2\|J\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}\|K\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}\|K^{N-1}\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})},

implying since
\|K^{N-1}\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})}>0
,
that

\forall N\geq 2,\quad N\leq 2\|J\|\|K\|,

which is impossible and proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 1.34

(Hardy’s inequality: the study of non-self-adjoint operators may be useful to determine lowerbounds of self-adjoint operators)
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N},n\geq 3
;
let
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\nabla u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),|x|^{-1}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Then we have

(1.2.123)

\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\geq\bigl(\frac{n-2}{2}\bigr)^{2}\||x|^{-1}{u}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}.

Proof.

We write first

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\|(D_{x_{j}}-i\phi_{j})u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle|D|^{2}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\langle|\phi|^{2}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}-\frac{1}{2\pi}\langle(\operatorname{div}\phi)u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

so that with

\phi(x)=\frac{\nu x}{2\pi|x|^{2}},

we get the operator inequality

|D|^{2}+\frac{\nu^{2}}{4\pi^{2}|x|^{2}}\geq\frac{\nu(n-2)}{4\pi^{2}|x|^{2}},\quad\text{ so that }\quad{-\Delta}\geq|x|^{-2}\underbrace{\nu(n-2-\nu)}_{\text{largest at }\nu=(n-2)/2},

proving the lemma.
∎

N.B
.

A modern approach to the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle should certainly begin with reading C. Fefferman’s article
[
8
]
as well as E. Lieb’s book
[
38
]
.

1.2.8.
Non-negative quantizations formulas

Lemma 1.35
.

Let
\chi
be an even function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
norm equal to 1. We define

(1.2.124)

\Gamma_{\chi}=\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi.

Then the function
\Gamma_{\chi}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, is real-valued even and is such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX=1.

Let
u
be in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then the convolution

\mathcal{W}(u,u)\ast\Gamma_{\chi}
is non-negative.
As a result, the operator with Weyl symbol
a\ast\Gamma_{\chi}
is a non-negative operator whenever
a
is a non-negative function.

Proof.

According to the book
[
33
]
, the composition formula (
1.2.100
)
is bilinear continuous from
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{2}
into
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and we have also

\overline{a\sharp b}=\bar{b}\sharp\bar{a}.

so that
\Gamma_{\chi}
is indeed real-valued. Moreover, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX=2^{2n}\iiint_{(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{3}}e^{-4i\pi[X-Y,Y-Z]}\bar{\chi}(Y)\chi(Z)dYdZdX\\
=\int|\chi(Y)|^{2}dY=1,

and

\Gamma_{\chi}(-X)=2^{2n}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-4i\pi[-X-Y,-X-Z]}\bar{\chi}(Y)\chi(Z)dYdZ\\
=2^{2n}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-4i\pi[-X+Y,-X+Z]}\bar{\chi}(Y)\chi(Z)dYdZ=\Gamma_{\chi}(X).

We have also

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathcal{W}

\displaystyle(u,u)\ast\Gamma_{\chi}\bigr)(Y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(Y-X)\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(Y+X)\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(T_{Y}(X))\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(\tau_{-Y}u,\tau_{-Y}u)(X)\Gamma_{\chi}(X)dX=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(\tau_{-Y}u,\tau_{-Y}u)(X)(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)(X)dX

\displaystyle=\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)}\tau_{-Y}u,\tau_{-Y}u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\geq 0,

proving the first statement of non-negativity. Let
a
be a non-negative function, say in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
; we have

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\ast\Gamma_{\chi})}

\displaystyle=2^{n}\iint a(Y)\Gamma_{\chi}(X-Y)\sigma_{X}dYdX

\displaystyle=\int a(Y)\int(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)(X-Y)2^{n}\sigma_{X}dXdY

\displaystyle=\int a(Y)\int(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)(T_{-Y}(X))2^{n}\sigma_{X}dXdY=\int a(Y)\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)}\tau_{-Y}dY

\displaystyle=\int a(Y)\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}dY

\displaystyle=\int a(Y)\underbrace{\bigl[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]^{*}\bigl[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]}_{\text{non-negative operator}}dY\geq 0,

if
a(Y)\geq 0
for all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and this concludes the proof.∎

We can write as well

(1.2.125)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\ast\Gamma_{\chi})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(Y)\bigl[\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]^{*}\bigl[\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]dY=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(Y)\Sigma_{\chi}(Y)dY,

with

(1.2.126)

\Sigma_{\chi}(Y)=\bigl[\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]^{*}\bigl[\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}\tau_{-Y}\bigr]\\
=\bigl({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}\bigr)^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}.

Remark 1.36
.

The Gaussian case in the previous lemma gives rise to the standard non-negativity properties of coherent states. In fact choosing
\chi(X)=2^{n}e^{-2\pi|X|^{2}}
, we see that
\chi
is even, belongs to the Schwartz space and

\|\chi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}^{2}=2^{2n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{-4\pi|X|^{2}}dX=2^{2n}4^{-2n/2}=1.

We have also
12
12

12

Proposition 4.1.1 in
[
33
]
is useful to compute the Fourier transform of Gaussian functions and is a notable asset of the Fourier normalization given in Footnote
1
page
1
.

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\chi}(X)=2^{4n}\iint_{(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{2}}e^{-4i\pi[X-Y,X-Z]}e^{-2\pi(|Y|^{2}+|Z|^{2})}dYdZ

\displaystyle=2^{3n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{4i\pi[Y,X]}e^{-2\pi(|X+Y|^{2}+|Y|^{2})}dY=2^{3n}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{4i\pi[Y,X]}e^{-2\pi(|Y+\frac{X}{2}|^{2}+{|Y-\frac{X}{2}|}^{2})}dY

\displaystyle=2^{3n}e^{-\pi|X|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}e^{4i\pi[Y,X]}e^{-4\pi|Y|^{2}}dY=2^{3n}e^{-\pi|X|^{2}}4^{-n}e^{-\pi|X|^{2}}=\chi(X).

In that case we find that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}
is a rank-one orthogonal projection on the fundamental state
\Psi_{0}
of the Harmonic Oscillator
\pi(|D_{x}|^{2}+|x|^{2})
.
According to (
9.1.31
)
the one-dimensional
k
-th Hermite function is

(1.2.127)

\psi_{k}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{k}\sqrt{k!}}2^{1/4}e^{\pi x^{2}}\left(\frac{d}{\sqrt{\pi}dx}\right)^{k}(e^{-2\pi x^{2}}),

so that

\Psi_{0}(x)=2^{n/4}e^{-\pi|x|^{2}}.

We calculate

\Gamma(x,\xi)=\mathcal{W}(\Psi_{0},\Psi_{0})(x,\xi)=2^{n/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\pi(|x+z/2|^{2}+|x-z/2|^{2})}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}dz\\
=2^{n/2}e^{-2\pi|x|^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\pi z^{2}/2}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}dz=2^{n}e^{-2\pi|x|^{2}}e^{-2\pi|\xi|^{2}}=\chi(x,\xi).

The anti-Wick quantization of a symbol
a
is defined as (see e.g. M. Shubin’s book
[
47
]
)

(1.2.128)

{\text{Op}_{\rm aw}(\text{\tt a})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\text{\tt a}(Y)\Sigma_{Y}dY,

where

\Sigma_{Y}
is the rank-one orthogonal projection given
by

(1.2.129)

\Sigma_{y,\eta}u=\langle u,\tau_{y,\eta}\Psi_{0}\rangle\tau_{y,\eta}\Psi_{0}.

Remark 1.37
.

It is interesting to notice that to produce non-negativity of the operator with Weyl symbol
\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi}
when
a
is a non-negative function, we do not use the non-negativity of
\Gamma_{\chi}
as a function, which by the way does not always hold (except in the Gaussian cases),
but we use the fact that the quantization of
\Gamma_{\chi}
is non-negative, as it is defined as
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)}=({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)})^{*}\ {\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi)}
.

Remark 1.38
.

Another important remark
is concerned with the Taylor expansion of
\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi}
: we have

\displaystyle(\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi})(X)

\displaystyle=\int\text{\tt a}(X-Y)\Gamma_{\chi}(Y)dY=\int\text{\tt a}(X+Y)\Gamma_{\chi}(Y)dY

\displaystyle=\int\Bigl(\text{\tt a}(X)+\text{\tt a}^{\prime}(X)Y+\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\text{\tt a}^{\prime\prime}(X+\theta Y)Y^{2}\Bigr)\Gamma_{\chi}(Y)dY

\displaystyle=\text{\tt a}(X)+\int\!\!\!\int_{0}^{1}(1-\theta)\text{\tt a}^{\prime\prime}(X+\theta Y)Y^{2}\Gamma_{\chi}(Y)dY.

As a result the difference
(\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi})-\text{\tt a}
depends only on the second derivative of
a
.
If for instance
a
is a semi-classical symbol, i.e. a smooth function of
(x,\xi)
depending on
h\in(0,1]
such that

(1.2.130)

\forall(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\times\mathbb{N}^{n},\quad\sup_{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},h\in(0,1]}|(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\text{\tt a})(x,\xi,h)|h^{-\frac{|\alpha|+|\beta|}{2}}<+{\infty}.

then the difference

{\text{Op}_{\rm aw}(\text{\tt a})}-{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a})}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with an
O(h)

operator-norm, so that if
a
happens also to be non-negative,
we find
