{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a})}=\underbrace{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a})}-{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi})}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}O(h)\\
\text{as an operator,}\\
\text{cf. Theorem \ref {thm.117jhg}
}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a}\ast\Gamma_{\chi})}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\geq 0\\
\text{as an operator}\end{subarray}},

and we obtain a version of the so-called Sharp Gårding Inequality,

(1.2.131)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\text{\tt a})}+Ch\geq 0\quad\text{(as an operator)}.

Theorem 1.39
.

Let
\chi
be an even function in the Schwartz space
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

norm equal to 1 and let
\Gamma_{\chi}
be given by (
1.2.124
).
For

a\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, we define

(1.2.132)

{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a\ast\Gamma_{\chi})}.

Then
{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}
is a bounded operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we have

(1.2.133)

\|{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\leq\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

Moreover, if
a
is valued in some interval
J
of the real line, we have
the operator inequalities

(1.2.134)

\inf J\leq{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}\leq\sup J.

In particular if
a(x,\xi)\geq 0
for all
(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we have the operator-inequality

{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}\geq 0
.

N.B
.

The non-negativity of the anti-Wick quantization (
1.2.128
) and its avatars Husimi (
[
25
]
), Coherent States,
Gabor wavelets (see e.g.
[
11
]
),
are particular cases of the above theorem. More information on this topic is available in Section 2.4 of the book
[
33
]
. Another remark is that this result can easily be extended to matrix-valued symbols as in Remark 2 page 79 of L. Hörmander’s
[
24
]
and even to symbols valued in
\mathcal{B}(H)
, where
H
is a Hilbert space.

Proof.

We start with Formulas (
1.2.125
), (
1.2.126
), entailing

{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(Y)\Sigma_{\chi}(Y)dY,

with

\Sigma_{\chi}(Y)=\bigl[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}\bigr]^{*}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}=\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)}\tau_{-Y}.

We note that

{\text{Op}(\chi,1)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\tau_{Y}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\bar{\chi}\sharp\chi)}\tau_{-Y}dY,

so has Weyl symbol

X\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\Gamma_{\chi}(X-Y)dY=1

from Lemma
1.35
and thus
{\text{Op}(\chi,1)}=\operatorname{Id}
.
We infer that for
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\langle{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(Y)\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}u,{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}v\rangle dY,

so that with any
\nu>0
,

|\langle{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}|\\
\leq\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\nu\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}+\nu^{-1}\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\chi(\cdot-Y))}v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\bigr)dY\\
=\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\nu\langle{\text{Op}(\chi,1)}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\nu^{-1}\langle{\text{Op}(\chi,1)}v,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr)\\
=\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\nu\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}+\nu^{-1}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\bigr),

and taking the infimum of the right-hand-side with respect to
\nu
,
we obtain

|\langle{\text{Op}(\chi,a)}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}|\leq\|a\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

proving
(
1.2.133
).
To prove (
1.2.134
),
it is enough to prove the last statement in the theorem
which follows immediately from (
1.2.125
), (
1.2.126
) since each operator
\Sigma_{Y}
is non-negative.
The proof of the theorem is complete.∎

It is nice to have examples of non-negative quantizations, but somehow more importantly, it is crucial to relate these quantizations to the mainstream quantization, that is to the Weyl quantization. This is what we do in the next theorem, dealing with semi-classical symbols.

Theorem 1.40

(Sharp Gårding Inequality)
.

Let
a
be a function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]
such that
a(x,\xi,h)
is smooth for all
h\in(0,1]
and such that

(1.2.135)

\forall(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\times\mathbb{N}^{n},\quad\sup_{(x,\xi,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]}|(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}a)(x,\xi,h)|h^{-|\beta|}<+{\infty}.

Let us assume that the function
a
is valued in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Then, there exists a constant
C
such that

(1.2.136)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}+Ch\geq 0.

Proof.

We have given a proof of this result in Remark
1.38
but with a different definition for a semi-classical symbol (see (
1.2.130
)).
Starting with our definition above in (
1.2.135
), we define

(1.2.137)

b(x,\xi,h)=a(h^{1/2}x,h^{-1/2}\xi,h),

and we see that
b
satisfies the estimates (
1.2.130
) and is a non-negative function so that, applying Remark
1.38
, we can find a constant
C
such that

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}+Ch\geq 0.

We note now that Segal’s formula (
1.2.73
) applied to the symplectic mapping

(x,\xi)\mapsto(h^{1/2}x,h^{-1/2}\xi),

shows that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}
is unitarily equivalent to
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
, providing the sought result.
∎

N.B
.

Several versions of the above theorem can be found in the literature,
in particular Theorem 18.1.14 in
[
24
]
. The first proof of this result was given in 1966 by L. Hörmander in
[
21
]
for scalar-valued symbols
and a proof for systems was given by
P. Lax & L. Nirenberg in
[
28
]
on the same year.
Far-reaching refinements of that inequality were given by C. Fefferman & D.H. Phong,
who proved in
[
9
]
in 1978 that, under the same assumption as in Theorem
1.40
for scalar-valued symbols, they obtain the much stronger

(1.2.138)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}+Ch^{2}\geq 0.

A thorough discussion of these questions is given in Section 18.6 of
[
24
]
and in Section 2.5 of
[
33
]
(see also
[
1
]
).

1.3.
Examples

1.3.1.
Hermite functions

We can easily calculate the Wigner distribution of Hermite functions and since the Wigner distributions respect tensor products as partial Fourier transforms, it is enough to do so in one dimension.
With
\psi_{k}
given in (
1.2.127
), the Wigner distribution
\mathcal{W}(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})
appears as the Weyl symbol of
\mathbb{P}_{k;1}=\mathbb{P}_{k}
as defined in (
9.1.32
).
We find that the Weyl symbol of
\mathbb{P}_{0;n}
, following (
9.3.3
), is

2^{n}e^{-2\pi(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}.

More generally, the paper
[
27
]
provides in one dimension

(1.3.1)

\mathcal{W}(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})(x,\xi)=(-1)^{k}2e^{-2\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}L_{k}\bigl(4\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})\bigr),

where
L_{k}
is the standard Laguerre polynomial with degree
k
(see (
9.4.1
)). As a result, the Weyl symbol of
\mathbb{P}_{k;n}
is equal to
\pi_{k,n}(x,\xi)
with

(1.3.2)

\pi_{k,n}(x,\xi)=(-1)^{k}2^{n}e^{-2\pi(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},|\alpha|=k}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}L_{\alpha_{j}}\bigl(4\pi(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr).

Note that the leading term in the polynomial
(-1)^{k}L_{k}(t)
is
t^{k}/k!
and this implies that the set

\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathcal{W}(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})(x,\xi)<0\}

where
\mathcal{W}(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})
is given by (
1.3.1
) is a relatively compact
open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
:
Indeed we have

W(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})(X)=2e^{-2\pi|X|^{2}}\Bigl\{\frac{(4\pi|X|^{2})^{k}}{k!}\Bigr\}\underbrace{\Bigl(1+\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-1}a_{l}(4\pi|X|^{2})^{-(k-l)}\Bigr)}_{\geq 1/2\text{ for $|X|\geq R_{0}$}}

which implies that

\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2},|X|\geq\max(R_{0},1)\}\subset\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2},W(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})(X)>0\}

and thus
\{W(\psi_{k},\psi_{k})(X)\leq 0\}\subset\{|X|<\max(R_{0},1)\}.

1.3.2.
One-sided exponentials

Let us define for
a>0
,

(1.3.3)

f_{a}(t)=H(t)a^{1/2}e^{-at/2}.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=aH(x)\int_{|z|\leq 2x}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}e^{-\frac{a}{2}(x+z/2)}e^{-\frac{a}{2}(x-z/2)}dz

\displaystyle=aH(x)e^{-xa}\int_{|z|\leq 2x}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}dz

\displaystyle=2aH(x)e^{-xa}\int_{0}^{2x}\cos\left(z2\pi\xi\right)dz

(1.3.4)

\displaystyle=aH(x)e^{-xa}\frac{\sin\left(4\pi x\xi\right)}{\pi\xi}.

We can check

\iint\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\frac{a}{\pi}\int_{x=0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-ax}\int\frac{\sin\left(4\pi x\xi\right)}{\xi}d\xi dx=1=\|f_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

and since

(1.3.5)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin^{2}t}{t^{2}}dt=\pi,

we verify (see Lemma
1.29
and (
1.1.6
)),

\iint\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)^{2}dxd\xi=\frac{a^{2}}{\pi^{2}}\int_{x=0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-2ax}\int\frac{\sin^{2}\left(4\pi x\xi\right)}{\xi^{2}}d\xi dx=1=\|f_{a}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{4}.

On the other hand, the ambiguity function
\mathcal{A}(f_{a},f_{a})
is the inverse Fourier transform of
\mathcal{W}
and we have

\mathcal{A}(f_{a},f_{a})(\eta,y)=\frac{a}{\pi}\iint H(x)e^{-x(a-2i\pi\eta)}\frac{\sin\xi}{\xi}e^{2i\pi\frac{y}{4\pi x}\xi}dxd\xi\\
=a\int_{|y|/2}^{+{\infty}}e^{-x(a-2i\pi\eta)}dx=\frac{ae^{-\frac{1}{2}|y|(a-2i\pi\eta)}}{a-2i\pi\eta},

which corresponds to Formula (9) in
[
17
]

noting that with our notations, we have

\mathcal{A}(f,f)(\eta,y)=\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(f,f)(y,-\eta),

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}(f,f)
is the normalization chosen in
[
17
]
.
Going back to the Wigner distribution, that simple example is interesting since we have

\bigl\{(x,\xi),\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)<0\bigr\}=\cup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\bigl\{(x,\xi)\in(0,+{\infty})\times\mathbb{R}^{*},\ \frac{k}{2}+\frac{1}{4}<x|\xi|<\frac{k}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bigr\},

and we see that the Lebesgue measure of

E_{k}=\bigl\{(x,\xi)\in(0,+{\infty})\times\mathbb{R}^{*},\ \frac{k}{2}+\frac{1}{4}<x|\xi|<\frac{k}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\bigr\},

is infinite since

|E_{k}|=2\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{dx}{4x}=+{\infty}.

Moreover the function

\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)
does not belong to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
since

\iint H(x)e^{-xa}\left|\frac{\sin\left(4\pi x\xi\right)}{\pi\xi}\right|dxd\xi\geq\iint_{(0,+{\infty})^{2}}e^{-xa}\left|\frac{\sin\eta}{\pi\eta}\right|dxd\eta=+{\infty}.

As a consequence, we have,
using the notation for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,

(1.3.6)

\alpha_{\pm}=\max(\pm\alpha,0),

(1.3.7)

\iint\bigl(\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)\bigr)_{+}dxd\xi=\iint\bigl(\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)\bigr)_{-}dxd\xi=+{\infty},

since the real-valued function
\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})

does not belong to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and is such that

\iint\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\|f_{a}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1.

We shall see in Section
6.4

several important consequences of that phenomenon for the quantization of the indicatrix of some subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such as

(1.3.8)

E_{\pm}=\bigl\{(x,\xi),\pm\mathcal{W}(f_{a},f_{a})(x,\xi)>0\bigr\}.

1.3.3.
Box functions

We start with

(1.3.9)

\beta_{0}(t)=\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(t),

for which a straightforward calculation gives

(1.3.10)

\mathcal{W}(\beta_{0},\beta_{0})(x,\xi)=\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(x)\frac{\sin\bigl(2\pi(1-2|x|)\xi\bigr)}{\pi\xi}.

More generally for real parameters
a\leq b
, defining

\beta=(b-a)^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{[a,b]}(x)e^{2i\pi\omega x},\quad\text{we find}\\
\mathcal{W}(\beta,\beta)(x,\xi)=[(b-a)\pi(\xi-\omega)]^{-1}\Bigl(\mathbf{1}_{[a,\frac{a+b}{2}]}(x)\sin[4\pi(\xi-\omega)(x-a)]\\
+\mathbf{1}_{[\frac{a+b}{2},b]}(x)\sin[4\pi(\xi-\omega)(b-x)]\Bigr).

Checking now

(1.3.11)

\beta_{1}(t)=\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]}(t)\operatorname{sign}t,

we find after a simple (but this time a bit tedious) calculation

(1.3.12)

\mathcal{W}(\beta_{1},\beta_{1})(x,\xi)=\mathbf{1}\bigl(|x|\leq\frac{1}{4}\bigr)\ \frac{2\sin(4\pi|x|\xi)-\sin\bigl(2\pi(1-2|x|)\xi\bigr)}{\pi\xi}\\
+\mathbf{1}\bigl(\frac{1}{4}\leq|x|\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigr)\ \frac{\sin\bigl(2\pi(1-2|x|)\xi\bigr)}{\pi\xi}.

1.4.
Integrals of the Wigner distribution on subsets of the phase space

Lemma 1.41
.

Let
E
be a measurable subset with finite Lebesgue measure
of the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
\mathbf{1}_{E}
be the indicator function of the set
E
. Then the operator with Weyl symbol
\mathbf{1}_{E}
is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and for any
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

(1.4.1)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iint_{E}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi.

Proof.

It follows immediately from (
1.2.3
) and (
1.2.8
).
∎

Remark 1.42
.

A consequence of the above formula is that a spectral analysis of the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}

would display interesting extremalization properties for the right-hand-side of
(
1.4.1
);
for instance if

\lambda_{-}=\inf\bigl(\text{spectrum}({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})})\bigr),\quad\lambda_{+}=\sup\bigl(\text{spectrum}({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})})\bigr),

we obtain that for
u
normalized in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

(1.4.2)

\lambda_{-}\leq\iint_{E}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\lambda_{+}.

In particular, if
\lambda_{-}
is an eigenvalue related to a normalized eigenfunction
u_{-}
,
(resp. if
\lambda_{+}
is an eigenvalue related to a normalized eigenfunction
u_{+}
),
we get for all
u
normalized in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(1.4.3)

\iint_{E}\mathcal{W}(u_{-},u_{-})(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\iint_{E}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\\
\text{resp.}\leq\iint_{E}\mathcal{W}(u_{+},u_{+})(x,\xi)dxd\xi.

We shall see below several examples where the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with an
E
having infinite Lebesgue measure. We may note in particular that

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}})}=\operatorname{Id},

and for a given non-zero linear form
L(x,\xi)
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and

(1.4.4)

E=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},L(x,\xi)\in J\},\quad\text{where $J$ is a subset of $\mathbb{R}$},

we may find affine symplectic coordinates
(y,\eta)

on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
L(x,\xi)=y_{1}
, implying with (
1.2.73
) that

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
is unitarily equivalent to the orthogonal projection

u\mapsto u(y)\mathbf{1}_{J}(y_{1}).

Although in that case, the quantization of the indicatrix of
E
given by (
1.4.4
)
is trivial,
we shall see below that in many cases, including some rather explicit ones,
the Weyl quantization of the rough Hamiltonian
\mathbf{1}_{E}(x,\xi)

could be far from a projection and may have a rather complicated spectrum
with a supremum which could be strictly larger than 1 and an infimum which could be negative.

In some sense, although we have the trivial identity

\mathbf{1}_{E}(x,\xi)^{2}=\mathbf{1}_{E}(x,\xi)
, we shall see that the quantization process by the Weyl formula is destroying that property;
to understand integrals of the Wigner distribution on subsets of the phase space,
Formula (
1.4.1
) forces us to consider the Weyl quantization
of the function
\mathbf{1}_{E}(x,\xi)
and the Heisenberg Uncertainty Principle shows that non-commutation properties
are governing operators
and these properties are of course distorting the classical identities
satisfied by classical Hamiltonians.

We must point out as well
that we do not have here at our disposal a semi-classical version
of our quantization which could ensure some bridge between classical properties and operator-theoretic results as it is the case for the quantization of nice smooth semi-classical symbols depending on a small parameter
h
such as a
C^{{\infty}}

function
a(x,\xi,h)
satisfying (
1.2.135
).
In particular for a symbol
a
satisfying (
1.2.135
),
we have the following result: if for all
(x,\xi,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]
we have

a(x,\xi,h)\leq 1
, then there exists a semi-norm
C
of the symbol
a
such that

(1.4.5)

\operatorname{Id}-{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}+Ch^{2}\geq 0\qquad\text{i.e.}\quad{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\leq\operatorname{Id}+Ch^{2},

an inequality following from the Fefferman-Phong Inequality
(cf. (
1.2.138
))
which implies as well the following lemma.

Lemma 1.43
.

Let
a
be a semi-classical symbol of order 0, i.e. a smooth function satisfying
(
1.2.135
)
such that for all
(x,\xi,h)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(0,1]
we have

0\leq a(x,\xi,h)\leq 1.

Then there exists a semi-norm
C
of the symbol
a
such that

-Ch^{2}\leq{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}\leq\operatorname{Id}+Ch^{2}.

2.
Quantization of radial functions and Mehler’s formula

This section and the following are essentially based upon the author’s paper
[
36
]
.

2.1.
Basic formulas in one dimension

In this section,
we work in one dimension and consider a function
F
in the Schwartz class of
\mathbb{R}
.
We want to calculate somewhat explicitly the Weyl quantization of
F(x^{2}+\xi^{2})

and also extend that computation to the case where
F
is merely
L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
.
We have, say for
F
in the Wiener algebra
\mathscr{W}(\mathbb{R})=

Fourier
\bigl(L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\bigr)
,

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(x^{2}+\xi^{2})})}d\tau,

as an absolutely converging integral of a function defined on
\mathbb{R}
(equipped with the Lebesgue measure)
valued in
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))

(bounded endomorphisms of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
).
In fact applying Mehler’s Formula (
9.3.2
),
we find

\underbrace{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(x^{2}+\xi^{2})})}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{operator with Weyl symbol}\\
e^{2i\pi\tau(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}\end{subarray}}=\cos(\arctan\tau)\underbrace{e^{2i\pi(\arctan\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exponential }e^{iM},\\
\text{ with $M$ self-adjoint operator}\\
=2\pi(\arctan\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}\end{subarray}},

so that, using the spectral decomposition (
9.1.32
)
of the Harmonic Oscillator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))},

we get

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\sum_{k\geq 0}e^{2i(\arctan\tau)(k+\frac{1}{2})}\mathbb{P}_{k}\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)e^{2i(k+\frac{1}{2})\arctan\tau}\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}\mathbb{P}_{k},

where the use of Fubini theorem is justified by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{F}(\tau)|\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}<+{\infty},\quad\mathbb{P}_{k}\geq 0,\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{k}=\operatorname{Id}.

We have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)e^{2i(k+\frac{1}{2})\arctan\tau}\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\bigl(\cos(\arctan\tau\bigr)+i\sin(\arctan\tau)\bigr)^{2k+1}\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}},

and, using Section
9.8.1
, we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)e^{2i(k+\frac{1}{2})\arctan\tau}\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\bigl(1+i\tau\bigr)^{2k+1}\frac{d\tau}{(1+\tau^{2})^{k+1}}.

We have proven the following lemma.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
F
be a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}

such that

\hat{F}
is locally integrable and such that

(2.1.1)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{F}(\tau)|\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}<+{\infty}.

Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}
has the spectral decomposition

(2.1.2)

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\hat{F}(\tau)(1+i\tau)^{2k+1}}{(1+\tau^{2})^{k+1}}d\tau\ \mathbb{P}_{k}

(2.1.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\hat{F}(\tau)(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+1}}d\tau\ \mathbb{P}_{k},

where the orthogonal projections
\mathbb{P}_{k}
are defined in (
9.1.32
).

2.2.
Higher dimensional questions

We work now in
n
dimensions and consider a function
F
in the Schwartz class of
\mathbb{R}
.
We want to calculate somewhat explicitly the Weyl quantization of
F\bigl(\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr)
, where the
\mu_{j}
are positive parameters, denoted by

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F(\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}))\bigr)},\quad q_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}),

and also extend that computation to the case where
F
is merely
L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
.
We have, say for
F
in the Wiener algebra
\mathscr{W}(\mathbb{R})=

Fourier
\bigl(L^{1}(\mathbb{R})\bigr)
,

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\bigr)}d\tau,

as an absolutely converging integral of a function defined on
\mathbb{R}
(equipped with the Lebesgue measure)
valued in
\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))

(bounded endomorphisms of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
).
In fact applying Mehler’s Formula (
9.3.2
),
we find by tensorisation,

(2.2.1)

\underbrace{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\bigr)}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{operator with Weyl symbol}\\
e^{2i\pi\tau q_{\mu}(x,\xi)}\end{subarray}}=\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\cos(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}))\underbrace{e^{2i\pi(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{exponential }e^{iM_{j}},\\
\text{ with $M_{j}$ self-adjoint operator}\\
=2\pi(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\end{subarray}},

so that, using the spectral decomposition (
9.1.36
)
of the Harmonic Oscillator
we get

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F(q_{\mu}(x,\xi))\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{2i(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}))(\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2})}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{j}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}{d\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{2i(\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2})\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}{d\tau}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where the use of Fubini theorem is justified by

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{F}(\tau)|\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}<+{\infty},\quad\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}\geq 0,\sum_{\alpha}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}=\operatorname{Id}.

We have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{2i(\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2})\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}d\tau\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\bigl(\cos(\arctan(\mu_{j}\tau)\bigr)+i\sin(\arctan(\mu_{j}\tau))\bigr)^{2\alpha_{j}+1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}d\tau

and, using Section
9.8.1
, we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{2i(\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2})\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}d\tau\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{2\alpha_{j}+1}}{(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2}}}d\tau.

We have proven the following lemma.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
F
be a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}

such that

\hat{F}
is locally integrable and such that

(2.2.2)

\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{F}(\tau)|\frac{d\tau}{\sqrt{1+\tau^{2}}}<+{\infty}.

Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F(\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}))\bigr)}
has the spectral decomposition

(2.2.3)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr)\bigr)}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{2\alpha_{j}+1}}{(1+\tau^{2}\mu_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}d\tau\ \mathbb{P}_{\alpha}\\
=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}d\tau\ \mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is the rank-one orthogonal projection onto
\Psi_{\alpha}
given by
(
9.1.33
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
F
be as in Lemma
2.3
and let us assume that all the
\mu_{j}
are equal to
\mu
(positive).
Then

(2.2.4)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(\mu\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr)\bigr)}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\frac{(1+i\tau\mu)^{k}}{(1-i\tau\mu)^{k+n}}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

with

(2.2.5)

\mathbb{P}_{k;n}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\\
|\alpha|=k\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is the rank-one orthogonal projection onto
\Psi_{\alpha}
given by
(
9.1.33
).

Proof.

With all the
\mu_{j}
equal to
\mu>0
, we find

\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}=\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu)^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu)^{\alpha_{j}+1}}=\frac{(1+i\tau\mu)^{|\alpha|}}{(1-i\tau\mu)^{|\alpha|+n}},

which depends only on
|\alpha|
, so that applying the previous lemma gives

\Bigl(F\bigl(\mu\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr)\Bigr)^{w}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\frac{(1+i\tau\mu)^{k}}{(1-i\tau\mu)^{k+n}}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

giving the sought result.
∎

3.
Conics with eccentricity smaller than 1

3.1.
Indicatrix of a disc

Let us assume now that, with some
a\geq 0
,

F=\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{a}{2\pi},\frac{a}{2\pi}]},\text{
so that
}\quad F(x^{2}+\xi^{2})=\mathbf{1}_{\{{2\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})\leq a}\}}.

According to Section
9.8.3
,
we have

\hat{F}(\tau)=\frac{\sin a\tau}{\pi\tau},

so that (
2.1.1
) holds true. We find in this case,

(3.1.1)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}=\sum_{k\geq 0}F_{k}(a)\mathbb{P}_{k},\quad F_{k}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin a\tau}{\pi\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+1}}d\tau,

so that (note that
F_{k}(a)
is real-valued since
F
is real-valued and thus the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(F(x^{2}+\xi^{2}))}
is self-adjoint), and
for
a>0
,
using the result (
9.8.4
) of the Appendix page
9.8.2
,
we obtain

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\cos a\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+1}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\left\{\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+1}}+\frac{(1-i\tau)^{k}}{(1+i\tau)^{k+1}}\right\}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\left\{\frac{i^{k}(\tau-i)^{k}}{(-i)^{k+1}(\tau+i)^{k+1}}+\frac{(-i)^{k}(\tau+i)^{k}}{i^{k+1}(\tau-i)^{k+1}}\right\}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2i\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\left\{-\frac{(\tau-i)^{k}}{(\tau+i)^{k+1}}+\frac{(\tau+i)^{k}}{(\tau-i)^{k+1}}\right\}d\tau.

We shall now calculate explicitly both integrals above: let
1<R
be given and let us consider the closed path

(3.1.2)

\gamma_{R}=[-R,R]\cup\underbrace{\{Re^{i\theta}\}_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi}}_{\gamma_{2;R}}.

Figure 1.
\gamma_{R}=[-R,R]\cup\{Re^{i\theta}\}_{0\leq\theta\leq\pi}

We have

\frac{1}{2i\pi}\int_{\gamma_{R}}e^{ia\tau}\left\{-\frac{(\tau-i)^{k}}{(\tau+i)^{k+1}}+\frac{(\tau+i)^{k}}{(\tau-i)^{k+1}}\right\}d\tau=\text{\tt Res}(e^{ia\tau}\frac{(\tau+i)^{k}}{(\tau-i)^{k+1}};i)\\
=\frac{1}{k!}(\frac{d}{d\tau})^{k}\bigl\{e^{ia\tau}(\tau+i)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\tau=i},

and we note that, for
a>0
,

\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{\gamma_{2;R}}e^{ia\tau}\left\{-\frac{(\tau-i)^{k}}{(\tau+i)^{k+1}}+\frac{(\tau+i)^{k}}{(\tau-i)^{k+1}}\right\}d\tau=0,

since for
R\geq 2
,

\int_{0}^{\pi}|e^{iaRe^{i\theta}}|\left|-\frac{(Re^{i\theta}-i)^{k}}{(Re^{i\theta}+i)^{k+1}}+\frac{(Re^{i\theta}+i)^{k}}{(Re^{i\theta}-i)^{k+1}}\right||iRe^{i\theta}|d\theta\\
\leq\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-aR\sin\theta}\left|-\frac{(e^{i\theta}-iR^{-1})^{k}}{(e^{i\theta}+iR^{-1})^{k+1}}+\frac{(e^{i\theta}+iR^{-1})^{k}}{(e^{i\theta}-iR^{-1})^{k+1}}\right|d\theta\\
\leq\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-aR\sin\theta}d\theta\sup_{0\leq\rho\leq 1/2}\left\{\frac{(1+\rho)^{k}}{(1-\rho)^{k+1}}+\frac{(1+\rho)^{k}}{(1-\rho)^{k+1}}\right\}.

For
a>0
, we obtain
\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-aR\sin\theta}d\theta=0
by dominated convergence.
As a result, we get

F^{\prime}_{k}(a)=(-1)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}(\frac{d}{d\tau})^{k}\bigl\{e^{ia\tau}(\tau+i)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\tau=i}=(-1)^{k}\frac{1}{k!}(\frac{d}{\frac{i}{a}d\epsilon})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-a-\epsilon}(i+i\frac{\epsilon}{a}+i)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0},

that is

F^{\prime}_{k}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}e^{-a}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-\epsilon}(2a+\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}.

We note that
F^{\prime}_{k}
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
as the product of
e^{-a}
by a polynomial.
We have also that

(3.1.3)

\lim_{a\rightarrow+{\infty}}F_{k}(a)=1\quad\text{(see the Appendix page \ref {sub555}),}

and this yields

F_{k}(a)=1+\int_{+{\infty}}^{a}F^{\prime}_{k}(b)db=1-\int_{a}^{+{\infty}}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}e^{-b}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-\epsilon}(2b+\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}db,

so that

(3.1.4)

F_{k}(a)=1-e^{-a}P_{k}(a),

with

(3.1.5)

P_{k}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2\epsilon}(a+t+\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}dt\\
=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2\epsilon-2t}(a+t+\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}dt\\
=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(a+t)^{k}\bigr\}dt.

We see that
P_{k}
is a polynomial with leading monomial
\frac{2^{k}a^{k}}{k!}

(by a direct computation)
and
P_{k}(0)=1

(since
0=F_{k}(0)=1-P_{k}(0)
)
and moreover,
using Laguerre polynomials (see e.g. (
9.4.1
) in our Section
9.4
), we obtain

(3.1.6)

\displaystyle P_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}e^{2t+2a}(\frac{d}{2dt})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2t-2a}(2a+2t)^{k}\bigr\}dt

(3.1.7)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k}(2t+2a)dt,

and this gives in particular

(3.1.8)

P^{\prime}_{k}(a)=(-1)^{k}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}2L^{\prime}_{k}(2t+2a)dt\\
=(-1)^{k}\Bigl\{[e^{-t}L_{k}(2t+2a)]_{t=0}^{t=+{\infty}}+\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k}(2t+2a)dt\Bigr\}\\
=(-1)^{k+1}L_{k}(2a)+P_{k}(a).

Moreover we have from (
3.1.5
), for
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{k}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2t}k(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}\frac{d}{dt}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}k(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\Bigl\{\bigl[e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}k(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}\bigr]^{t=+{\infty}}_{t=0}-\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}k(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}dt\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}_{|t=0}+\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}e^{2t+2a}(\frac{d}{2dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t-2a}(2a+2t)^{k-1}\bigr\}_{|t=0}

\displaystyle\hskip 150.0pt+\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(a+t)^{k-1}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}L_{k-1}(2a)+P_{k-1}(a),

so that

(3.1.9)

\forall k\geq 1,\quad P^{\prime}_{k}(a)=(-1)^{k-1}L_{k-1}(2a)+P_{k-1}(a)=(-1)^{k+1}L_{k}(2a)+P_{k}(a).

This implies for
N\geq 1,

\sum_{1\leq k\leq N}P_{k}(a)-\sum_{1\leq k\leq N}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N-1}P_{k}(a)+\sum_{0\leq k\leq N-1}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a),

yielding

P_{N}(a)-\underbrace{P_{0}(a)}_{=1=L_{0}(a)}=\sum_{1\leq k\leq N}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)+\sum_{0\leq k\leq N-1}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a),

and

(3.1.10)

P_{N}(a)=\sum_{0\leq k\leq N}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)+\sum_{0\leq k\leq N-1}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a).

Note that the previous formula holds as well for
N=0
, since
P_{0}=1=L_{0}
.

Although the function
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni a\mapsto F_{k}(a)
has no monotonicity properties, we prove below that

\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni a\mapsto P_{k}(a)
is indeed increasing. For that purpose, let us use (
3.1.9
),
which implies

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k-1}L_{k-1}(2a)+P_{k-1}(a),\quad k\geq 1,

\displaystyle P_{k-1}(a)

\displaystyle=P_{k-2}(a)+(-1)^{k-2}L_{k-2}(2a)+(-1)^{k-1}L_{k-1}(2a),\quad k\geq 2,

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=2(-1)^{k-1}L_{k-1}(2a)+(-1)^{k-2}L_{k-2}(2a)+P_{k-2}(a),\quad k\geq 2.

We claim that that for
k\geq 1
,

(3.1.11)

P^{\prime}_{k}(a)=2\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-1}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a).

That property holds for
k=1
since
P_{1}(a)=1+2a
: we check

P^{\prime}_{1}(a)=2.

Moreover we have

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{k+1}(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)+P_{k}(a)\qquad\text{\footnotesize(from the first equation in \eqref{lagk-1})}

(using (
3.1.10
))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)+\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a)+\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-1}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a),\quad\text{\tt qed.}

As a byproduct we find from (
9.4.3
)

(3.1.12)

\forall a\geq 0,\quad P^{\prime}_{k}(a)\geq 0,

which implies that for
a\geq 0
,
P_{k}(a)\geq P_{k}(0)=1
.
We have proven the following

Lemma 3.1
.

The polynomial
P_{k}(a)=e^{a}\bigl(1-F_{k}(a)\bigr)
is increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
P_{k}(0)=1
.

Let us take a look at the first
P_{k}
: we have

\displaystyle P_{0}(a)

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle P_{1}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a,

\displaystyle P_{2}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a^{2},

\displaystyle P_{3}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a-2a^{2}+\frac{4a^{3}}{3},

\displaystyle P_{4}(a)

\displaystyle=1+4a^{2}-\frac{8a^{3}}{3}+\frac{2a^{4}}{3},

\displaystyle P_{5}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a-4a^{2}+\frac{16a^{3}}{3}-2a^{4}+\frac{4a^{5}}{15},

\displaystyle P_{6}(a)

\displaystyle=1+6a^{2}-8a^{3}+\frac{14a^{4}}{3}-\frac{16a^{5}}{15}+\frac{4a^{6}}{45},

\displaystyle P_{7}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a-6a^{2}+12a^{3}-\frac{26a^{4}}{3}+\frac{44a^{5}}{15}-\frac{4a^{6}}{9}+\frac{8a^{7}}{315},

\displaystyle P_{8}(a)

\displaystyle=1+8a^{2}-16a^{3}+\frac{44a^{4}}{3}-\frac{32a^{5}}{5}+\frac{64a^{6}}{45}-\frac{16a^{7}}{105}+\frac{2a^{8}}{315},

\displaystyle P_{9}(a)

\displaystyle=1+2a-8a^{2}+\frac{64a^{3}}{3}-\frac{68a^{4}}{3}+\frac{184a^{5}}{15}-\frac{32a^{6}}{9}+\frac{176a^{7}}{315}-\frac{2a^{8}}{45}+\frac{4a^{9}}{2835},

\displaystyle P_{10}(a)

\displaystyle{\text{\small$=1+10a^{2}-\frac{80a^{3}}{3}+\frac{100a^{4}}{3}-\frac{64a^{5}}{3}+\frac{344a^{6}}{45}-\frac{496a^{7}}{315}+\frac{58a^{8}}{315}-\frac{32a^{9}}{2835}+\frac{4a^{10}}{14175}$}},

\displaystyle P_{11}(a)

=1+2a-10a^{2}+\frac{100a^{3}}{3}-\frac{140a^{4}}{3}+\frac{104a^{5}}{3}-\frac{664a^{6}}{45}+\frac{1184a^{7}}{315}-\frac{26a^{8}}{45}

\displaystyle\hskip 240.0pt{\text{\small$+\frac{148a^{9}}{2835}-\frac{4a^{10}}{1575}+\frac{8a^{11}}{155925}$}},

\displaystyle P_{12}(a)

=1+12a^{2}-40a^{3}+\frac{190a^{4}}{3}-\frac{160a^{5}}{3}+\frac{1184a^{6}}{45}-\frac{2512a^{7}}{315}+\frac{478a^{8}}{315}-\frac{512a^{9}}{2835}

+\frac{184a^{10}}{14175}-\frac{16a^{11}}{31185}+\frac{4a^{12}}{467775}.

We note as well that

(3.1.13)

P_{k}(x)=\sum_{0\leq m\leq k}\frac{x^{m}}{m!}\sum_{m\leq l\leq k}2^{l}(-1)^{k-l}\binom{k}{l},

since from (
3.1.5
),

\displaystyle P_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(a+t)^{k}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\sum_{0\leq m\leq k}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}\frac{(-2)^{k-m}}{(k-m)!}\frac{k!}{(k-m)!m!}(a+t)^{k-m}dt

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\sum_{0\leq m\leq k}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}\frac{(-2)^{k-m}}{(k-m)!}\frac{k!}{(k-m)!m!}\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-m}a^{l}t^{k-l-m}\binom{k-m}{l}dt

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq m\leq k\\
0\leq l\leq k-m\end{subarray}}\frac{(-2)^{k-m}}{(k-m)!}\frac{k!}{(k-m)!m!}a^{l}(k-l-m)!\binom{k-m}{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq l+m\leq k\end{subarray}}\frac{(-1)^{m}2^{k-m}}{(k-m)!}\frac{k!}{m!}a^{l}\frac{1}{l!}=\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}\frac{a^{l}}{l!}\sum_{l\leq m^{\prime}\leq k}{(-1)^{k-m^{\prime}}2^{m^{\prime}}}\binom{k}{m^{\prime}},\quad\text{\tt qed.}

Lemma 3.2
.

With the polynomial
P_{k}
defined by (
3.1.7
), we have

(3.1.14)

\begin{cases}P_{k}(a)&=2\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-1}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a)+(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a),\\
P^{\prime}_{k}(a)&=2\sum_{0\leq l\leq k-1}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2a).\end{cases}

Proof.

We may use the already proven (
3.1.10
), (
3.1.11
), but we may also prove this directly by
induction on
k
.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
F_{k}
be given by (
3.1.4
) with
P_{k}
defined by (
3.1.5
). We have

(3.1.15)

\displaystyle F_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=1-e^{-a}P_{k}(a)\leq 1-e^{-a}=F_{0}(a)\quad\text{for $a\geq 0$},

(3.1.16)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k}(a)

\displaystyle=e^{-a}\bigl(P_{k}(a)-P^{\prime}_{k}(a)\bigr)=e^{-a}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a),

(3.1.17)

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k}(0)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k},\quad\lim_{a\rightarrow+{\infty}}F^{\prime}_{k}(a)=0_{+},\quad F_{k}(0)=0,\lim_{a\rightarrow+{\infty}}F_{k}(a)=1_{-}.

Proof.

We use (
3.1.4
), (
3.1.11
) and (
3.1.10
) for the three first equalities, Lemma
3.1
for the first inequality.
The fourth equality follows from
L_{k}(0)=1
,
while the fifth is due to the fact that the leading monomial of
(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2a)
is
2^{k}a^{k}/k!
. The two last equalities are a consequence of the first line.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The zeroes of
F^{\prime}_{k}
on the positive half-line are the positive zeroes of the Laguerre polynomial
L_{k}

divided by
2
.
When
k
is even (resp. odd)
the function
F_{k}
is positive increasing (resp. negative decreasing) near
0
, then oscillates
with changes of monotonicity at each
a
such that
L_{k}(2a)=0
and when
2a
is larger than the largest zero of
L_{k}
, the function
F_{k}
is increasing, smaller than 1, with limit 1 at infinity.

Typically we have
F_{2l}(0)=0,F^{\prime}_{2l}(0)=+1,

(3.1.18)

\quad 0<a_{1,2l}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{2l-1,2l}<a_{2l,2l}\quad\text{the zeroes of $L_{2l}(2a)$,}

F_{2l}
vanishes simply at
b_{0}=0
and at
b_{j}\in(a_{j},a_{j+1})
for
1\leq j\leq 2l-1
, also at
b_{2l}>a_{2l}
:
2l+1
zeroes
with a positive (resp. negative)
derivative at
b_{0},b_{2},\dots,b_{2l}

(resp. at
b_{1},b_{3},\dots,b_{2l-1}
).

Moreover, we have
F_{2l+1}(0)=0,F^{\prime}_{2l+1}(0)=-1,

(3.1.19)

\quad 0<a_{1,2l+1}<a_{2,2l+1}<\dots<a_{2l,2l+1}<a_{2l+1,2l+1},\text{the zeroes of $L_{2l+1}(2a)$,}

F_{2l+1}
vanishes simply at
b_{0}=0
and at
b_{j}\in(a_{j},a_{j+1})
for
1\leq j\leq 2l
, also at
b_{2l+1}>a_{2l+1}
:
2l+2
zeroes
with a positive (resp. negative)
derivative at
b_{1},b_{3},\dots,b_{2l+1}

(resp. at
b_{0},b_{2},\dots,b_{2l}
).

We note as well that a consequence of the previous remark is that

(3.1.20)

\displaystyle\min_{a\geq 0}F_{2l}(a)=\min_{1\leq j\leq l}\{F_{2l}(a_{2j,2l})\},

(3.1.21)

\displaystyle\min_{a\geq 0}F_{2l+1}(a)=\min_{0\leq j\leq l}\{F_{2l+1}(a_{2j+1,2l+1})\},

where
(a_{p,k})_{1\leq p\leq k}
are defined in (
3.1.18
), (
3.1.19
).

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
a\geq 0
be given and let

(3.1.22)

D_{a}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x^{2}+\xi^{2}\leq\frac{a}{2\pi}\}.

Then we have

(3.1.23)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{D_{a}})}=\sum_{k\geq 0}F_{k}(a)\mathbb{P}_{k}\leq 1-e^{-a}.

Proof.

An immediate consequence of (
3.1.1
) and (
3.1.15
).
Note that the inequality in the above theorem is due to P. Flandrin in
[
13
]

(see also the related references
[
20
]
,
[
14
]
).
∎

Curves.

Let us display some curves of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\ni a\mapsto F_{k}(a)=1-e^{-a}P_{k}(a)
.

F_{5},F_{6}
.

Figure 3.
Functions
F_{k}

3.2.
Indicatrix of an Euclidean ball

The following result displays an explicit spectral decomposition on the Hermite basis for the Weyl quantization of the characteristic function of Euclidean balls.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
a\geq 0
be given and let

(3.2.1)

\mathcal{Q}_{a,n}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}\{{2\pi(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})\leq a}\})},

be the Weyl quantization of the characteristic function of the Euclidean ball of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with center
0
and radius
\sqrt{a/(2\pi)}
.
Then we have

(3.2.2)

\mathcal{Q}_{a,n}=\sum_{k\geq 0}F_{k;n}(a)\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

with

\mathbb{P}_{k;n}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},|\alpha|=k}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\Psi_{\alpha}

(defined in (
9.1.33
)), with
|\alpha|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\alpha_{j}=k

and

(3.2.3)

F_{k;n}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin a\tau}{\pi\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+n}}d\tau.

The spectral decomposition of the previous theorem
allows a simple recovery of the result of the article
[
39
]
by E. Lieb
and Y. Ostrover.

Theorem 3.7
.

Let
a\geq 0,\mathcal{Q}_{a,n},F_{k;n}
be defined above.
Then we have

(3.2.4)

F_{k;n}(a)\leq 1-\frac{1}{\Gamma(n)}\int_{a}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}t^{n-1}dt=1-\frac{\Gamma(n,a)}{\Gamma(n)},

and thus we have

(3.2.5)

\mathcal{Q}_{a,n}\leq 1-\frac{\Gamma(n,a)}{\Gamma(n)},

where the incomplete Gamma function
\Gamma(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined in (
9.8.8
).

Proof of Theorems
3.6
and
3.7
.

We use the results of (the previous) Section
3.1
:
Let us assume now that, with some
a\geq 0
,

F=\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{a}{2\pi},\frac{a}{2\pi}]},\text{
so that
}\quad F(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})=\mathbf{1}\{{2\pi(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})\leq a}\}.

According to Section
9.8.3
,
we have

\hat{F}(\tau)=\frac{\sin a\tau}{\pi\tau},

so that (
2.1.1
) holds true. We find in this case,
following the results of Lemma
2.3
,

(3.2.6)

\displaystyle\bigl(F(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})\bigr)^{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}F_{k;n}(a)\mathbb{P}_{k;n},\quad\mathbb{P}_{k;n}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},|\alpha|=k}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

(3.2.7)

\displaystyle F_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin a\tau}{\pi\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+n}}d\tau,

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\Psi_{\alpha}

(defined in (
9.1.33
)), with
|\alpha|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\alpha_{j}=k
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We postpone the proof of Theorem
3.7
until after settling a couple of lemmas.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
(k,n)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{*}
. With
F_{k;n}(a)
given by (
3.2.7
), we have

(3.2.8)

\displaystyle F_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=1-e^{-a}P_{k,n}(a),\quad\text{where $P_{k;n}$ is the polynomial}

(3.2.9)

\displaystyle P_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(t+a)^{n-1}\Bigl\{e^{s}(\frac{d}{ds})^{n+k-1}\bigl[s^{k}e^{-s}\bigr]\Bigr\}_{|s=2t+2a}dt,

(3.2.10)

\displaystyle P_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}(t+a)^{n-1}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{n+k-1}\bigl\{(t+a)^{k}e^{-2t}\bigr\}dt.

Proof of the lemma.

The lemma holds true for
n=1
from Proposition
3.3
.
We have for
a>0
,
n\geq 2
,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\cos a\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+n}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+n}}d\tau+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\frac{(1-i\tau)^{k}}{(1+i\tau)^{k+n}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2i\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\frac{i^{k}(\tau-i)^{k}}{(-i)^{k+n}(\tau+i)^{k+n}}d\tau+\frac{i}{2i\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{ia\tau}\frac{(-i)^{k}(\tau+i)^{k}}{i^{k+n}(\tau-i)^{k+n}}d\tau,

so that

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=i^{1-n}(-1)^{k}\text{\tt Res}\bigl(e^{ia\tau}\frac{(\tau+i)^{k}}{(\tau-i)^{k+n}};i\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{1-n}(-1)^{k}}{(k+n-1)!}(\frac{d}{d\tau})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{ia\tau}(\tau+i)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\tau=i}

and thus

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{1-n}(-1)^{k}}{(k+n-1)!}(\frac{d}{\frac{i}{a}d\epsilon})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-a-\epsilon}(i+i\frac{\epsilon}{a}+i)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{i^{1-n}(-1)^{k}a^{n-1}}{i^{n-1}(k+n-1)!}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-a-\epsilon}(2a+\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}

\displaystyle=e^{a}\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}a^{n-1}}{(k+n-1)!}(\frac{d}{2d\epsilon})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-2a-2\epsilon}(2a+2\epsilon)^{k}\bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0},

that is

\displaystyle F^{\prime}_{k;n}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!}e^{t}t^{n-1}(\frac{d}{ds})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-s}s^{k}\bigr\}_{|s=2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!2^{n-1}}e^{t}t^{n-1}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}t^{k}\bigr\}.

We have also that
\lim_{a\rightarrow+{\infty}}F_{k;n}(a)=1
(following the arguments of Section
3.1
) and this yields

\displaystyle F_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!2^{n-1}}\int_{a}^{+{\infty}}e^{t}t^{n-1}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}t^{k}\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=1-e^{-a}\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{(k+n-1)!2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}(t+a)^{n-1}e^{t}(\frac{d}{dt})^{k+n-1}\bigl\{e^{-2t}(t+a)^{k}\bigr\}dt,

concluding the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Let us go back to Formula (
3.2.9
), written as

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}\Bigl\{\frac{(2t+2a)^{n-1}}{(k+n-1)!}(\frac{d}{d\epsilon}-1)^{n+k-1}\bigl[(\epsilon+2t+2a)^{k}\bigr]\Bigr\}_{|\epsilon=0}dt=

(3.2.11)

\displaystyle P_{k;n}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2t+2a)dt,

where the generalized Laguerre polynomial
L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}

is defined by (
9.4.6
) (note that
1-n+k+n-1=k
which not negative).

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
P_{k;n}
be the polynomial defined in Lemma
3.8
(and thus in (
3.2.11
)). Then we have

(3.2.12)

\displaystyle P_{k;n}(X)-P^{\prime}_{k;n}(X)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2X),\quad P_{k;n}(0)=1,

(3.2.13)

\displaystyle\text{for $n\geq 2$},

\displaystyle\quad P^{\prime}_{k;n}=P_{k;n-1}.

Proof.

From (
3.2.11
), we find

(3.2.14)

P^{\prime}_{k;n}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}2(L_{k+n-1}^{1-n})^{\prime}(2t+2a)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}\Bigl\{\bigl[e^{-t}(L_{k+n-1}^{1-n})(2t+2a)\bigr]_{t=0}^{t=+{\infty}}+\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2t+2a)dt\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n}}{2^{n-1}}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2a)+P_{k;n}(a),

and since
0=F_{k;n}(0)=1-P_{k;n}(0)
, this proves (
3.2.12
).
Using now (
3.2.11
) and (
9.4.8
), we find that

\displaystyle P_{k;n}(a)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n}}{2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{d}{dt}\bigl\{e^{-t}\bigr\}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2t+2a)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n}}{2^{n-1}}\Bigl\{\bigl[e^{-t}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2t+2a)\bigr]_{t=0}^{t=+{\infty}}-\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}2(L_{k+n-1}^{1-n})^{\prime}(2t+2a)dt\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k+n}}{2^{n-1}}\Bigl\{-L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2a)+\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}2(L_{k+n-2}^{2-n})(2t+2a)dt\Bigr\}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2a)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k;n}(a)-P^{\prime}_{k;n}(a)\\
\text{from \eqref{3212}}\end{subarray}}+\underbrace{\frac{(-1)^{k+n-2}}{2^{n-2}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k+n-2}^{2-n}(2t+2a)dt}_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k;n-1}(a)\\
\text{from \eqref{3211}}\end{subarray}},

so that for
n\geq 2
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain (
3.2.13
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
k,n,P_{k;n}
be as in Lemma
3.9
. Then we have

(3.2.15)

\forall j\in\llbracket 0\mkern 1.5mu..\mkern 1.5mun-1\rrbracket,\quad\left(\frac{d}{dX}\right)^{j}P_{k;n}=P_{k;n-j}.

Moreover, for all
a\geq 0
and all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(3.2.16)

P_{k;n}(a)\geq P_{0;n}(a)=\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(t+a)^{n-1}dt=e^{a}\frac{\Gamma(n,a)}{\Gamma(n)}.

Proof.

Formula (
3.2.15
) follows immediately by induction from (
3.2.13
) since the latter is proving (
3.2.15
) for
j=1,n\geq 2,k\in\mathbb{N}.

Assuming that (
3.2.15
) holds true for some
1\leq j<n
, all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
we have

P_{k;n}^{(j)}=P_{k,n-j}

and if
j+1<n
, we obtain from (
3.2.13
) that

P_{k,n-j-1}=P^{\prime}_{k,n-j}=P_{k;n}^{(j+1)},

proving (
3.2.15
). The property (
3.2.16
)
holds true for
n=1
.
From (
3.2.13
) and
P_{k;n+1}(0)=1
, we find that

P_{k;n+1}(a)=1+\int_{0}^{a}P_{k;n}(s)ds

and assuming that (
3.2.16
) holds true for
n
, we obtain for
a\geq 0
,

\displaystyle P_{k;n+1}(a)

\displaystyle\geq 1+\int_{0}^{a}\frac{1}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(t+s)^{n-1}dtds

\displaystyle=1+\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}\Bigl[\frac{(t+s)^{n}}{n!}\Bigr]^{s=a}_{s=0}dt

\displaystyle=1+\frac{1}{n!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}\bigl((t+a)^{n}-t^{n}\bigr)dt=\frac{1}{n!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(t+a)^{n}dt,

completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

We can now prove Theorem
3.7
: since

F_{k;n}(a)=1-e^{-a}P_{k;n}(a)

the estimate (
3.2.15
) implies indeed

F_{k;n}(a)\leq\frac{\Gamma(n,a)}{\Gamma(n)},

concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

Our methods of proof in one and more dimensions are quite similar:

•

Using Mehler’s Formula,
we diagonalize in the Hermite basis the quantization of the indicatrix of the Euclidean ball

D_{a;n}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},2\pi\bigl(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\bigr)\leq a\}.

•

Once we get the diagonalization

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{D_{a;n}})}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}F_{k;n}(a)\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

we study explicitly the functions
F_{k;n}
and prove that

F_{k;n}(a)=1-e^{-a}P_{k;n}(a),

where
P_{k;n}
is a polynomial given in terms of the generalized Laguerre polynomials

P_{k;n}(a)=\frac{(-1)^{k+n-1}}{2^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}L_{k+n-1}^{1-n}(2t+2a)dt.

•

Following the Flandrin paper
[
13
]
,
we use Feldheim inequality in
[
12
]
to tackle the case
n=1
, and next we use an induction on
n
, made possible by the relationship between the standard and the generalized Laguerre polynomials.
It is interesting to note that the functions
F_{k;n}

have no monotonicity properties:
with value 0 at 0, they have an oscillatory behavior for
a\leq a_{k,n}
and for
a
large enough, increase monotonically to 1 (see for instance Figures
2
and

3

in the 1D case); the inequality
F_{k;n}(a)\leq 1-e^{-a}
holds true for all
a\geq 0
in all dimensions. On the other hand the polynomials
P_{k;n}
are increasing and larger than 1 on the positive half-line.

The key ingredients are thus Mehler’s formula and Feldheim inequality, but it should be pointed out that the arguments proving Feldheim inequality
(Formula (6.8) and Theorem 12) in the R. Askey & G. Gasper’s article
[
2
]
are also based upon a version of Mehler’s Formula
which appears thus as the basic result for our investigation.
The paper
[
39
]
by E. Lieb
and Y. Ostrover has a slightly different line of arguments and takes advantage of symmetry properties of the sphere. We shall go back to this
in a situation where the symmetry is absent, such as for some general ellipsoids.

3.3.
Ellipsoids in the phase space

3.3.1.
Preliminaries

We provide below a couple of remarks on ellipsoids in higher dimensions.
Let us first recall a particular case of Theorem 21.5.3 in
[
24
]
.

Theorem 3.12

(Symplectic reduction of quadratic forms)
.

Let
q
be a positive-definite quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the canonical symplectic form (
1.2.24
). Then there exists
S
in the symplectic group
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})

of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}

and
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n}

positive such that for all
X=(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(3.3.1)

q(SX)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}).

Note that an interesting consequence of this theorem is that, considering a general ellipsoid in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
(with center of gravity at
0
),

\mathbb{E}=\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},q(X)\leq 1\}

where
q
is a positive definite quadratic form, we are able to find symplectic coordinates
such that
q
is given by (
3.3.1
).
Note however that no further simplification is possible and that the
\mu_{j}
are symplectic invariants of
\mathbb{E}
.
Note that the volume of
\mathbb{E}
is given by

|\mathbb{E}|_{2n}=\frac{\pi^{n}}{n!{\mu_{1}\dots\mu_{n}}}.

3.3.2.
Spectral decomposition for the quantization of the characteristic function of the ellipsoid

Let
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
be positive numbers.
We consider the ellipsoid
E(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
given by

(3.3.2)

E(a)=E(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n},2\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}}{a_{j}}\leq 1\}.

We define on
\mathbb{R}^{n}

the function

F(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\frac{2\pi}{a_{1}}X_{1}+\dots+\frac{2\pi}{a_{n}}X_{n}).

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
a=(a_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
be positive numbers and let
E(a)
be defined by (
3.3.2
). Then we have

(3.3.3)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E(a)})}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}F_{\alpha}(a)\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is defined in (
9.1.36
) and

(3.3.4)

F_{\alpha}(a)=1-K_{\alpha}(a),

with

(3.3.5)

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum t_{j}/a_{j}\geq 1\\
t_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt,

Remark 3.14
.

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
the functions
F_{\alpha},K_{\alpha}
are holomorphic on

(3.3.6)

\mathcal{U}=\{a\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\forall j\in\llbracket 1\mkern 1.5mu..\mkern 1.5mun\rrbracket,\operatorname{Re}a_{j}>0\}.

Indeed let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathcal{U}
; there exists
\rho>0
such that

\forall(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in K,\quad\min_{1\leq j\leq n}\operatorname{Re}a_{j}\geq\rho,

and as a result for
a\in K
, we have for
s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}

|e^{-(a_{1}s_{1}+\dots+a_{n}s_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2a_{j}s_{j})|\leq e^{-\rho(s_{1}+\dots+s_{n})}C_{K,\alpha}(1+|s|)^{|\alpha|},

so that

\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum s_{j}\geq 1\\
s_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\sup_{a\in K}|e^{-(a_{1}s_{1}+\dots+a_{n}s_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2a_{j}s_{j})|ds\\
\leq\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum s_{j}\geq 1\\
s_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-\rho(s_{1}+\dots+s_{n})}C_{K,\alpha}(1+|s|)^{|\alpha|}ds\leq C_{K,\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-\rho\sigma_{n}|s|}(1+|s|)^{|\alpha|}ds<+{\infty}.

Since we have

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum s_{j}\geq 1\\
s_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-(a_{1}s_{1}+\dots+a_{n}s_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2a_{j}s_{j})dsa_{1}\dots a_{n},

this proves the sought holomorphy.

Proof of the theorem.

We have

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E(a)})}

\displaystyle=\bigl(F(x_{1}^{2}+\xi_{1}^{2},\dots,x_{n}^{2}+\xi_{n}^{2})\bigr)^{w}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{F}(\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\sum_{j}\tau_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\bigr)}d\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau_{j})^{2\alpha_{j}+1}}{(1+\tau_{j}^{2})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}
is defined in (
9.1.36
).
On the other hand we have

\hat{F}(\tau)=\int e^{-2i\pi\tau\cdot x}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\frac{2\pi}{a_{1}}x_{1}+\dots+\frac{2\pi}{a_{n}}x_{n})dx_{1}\dots dx_{n}\\
=a_{1}\dots a_{n}(2\pi)^{-n}\int e^{-i\sum_{j}\tau_{j}a_{j}y_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum y_{j})dy,

so that, with
M_{k}
defined in (
9.4.4
), using (
9.4.5
), we get

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E(a)})}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\dots a_{n}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-i2\pi\sum_{j}\tau_{j}a_{j}y_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum y_{j})dy\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i2\pi\tau_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i2\pi\tau_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\dots a_{n}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-i2\pi\sum_{j}\tau_{j}a_{j}y_{j}}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum y_{j})dy\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\overline{\hat{G}_{\alpha_{j}}(\tau_{j})}d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=a_{1}\dots a_{n}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum y_{j})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}G_{\alpha_{j}}(a_{j}y_{j})dy\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum t_{j}/a_{j})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}H(t_{j})e^{-t_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

with

(3.3.7)

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(a)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\bigl(1-\mathbf{1}_{[1,+{\infty}]}(\sum t_{j}/a_{j})\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}H(t_{j})e^{-t_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt

\displaystyle=1-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{[1,+{\infty}]}(\sum t_{j}/a_{j})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}H(t_{j})e^{-t_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt,

where we have used that
P_{k;1}(0)=1
(see page
3.1.7
)
,
so that setting

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum t_{j}/a_{j}\geq 1\\
t_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt,

we have

F_{\alpha}(a)=1-K_{\alpha}(a),

concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 3.15
.

We have from (
3.3.7
)

(3.3.8)

F_{\alpha}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\mathbf{1}_{[0,1]}(\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j})\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}H(s_{j})e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2a_{j}s_{j})a_{j}ds,

and since the set

\{s\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n},\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\leq 1\}

is compact,
we obtain that
F_{\alpha}
is an entire function, as well as
K_{\alpha}
which is indeed given by (
3.3.5
) on the open subset
\mathcal{U}
defined in (
3.3.6
).

Lemma 3.16
.

With the notations of Theorem
3.13
, we have with
\mu_{j}=1/a_{j}
,

(3.3.9)

F_{\alpha}(a)=\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{j}\Bigr)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(a_{j}+i\tau)^{\alpha_{j}}}{(a_{j}-i\tau)^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau.

Proof.

Mehler’s formula implies in one dimension that

(3.3.10)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2\pi i\tau(x^{2}+\xi^{2})})}=(1+\tau^{2})^{-1/2}\exp{\bigl[2\pi i(\arctan\tau)(x^{2}+D_{x}^{2})\bigr]},

and a simple tensorisation gives

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2\pi i\tau\sum_{j}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})})}=\prod_{j}(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{-1/2}\exp{\bigl[2\pi i\sum_{j}(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}))(x_{j}^{2}+D_{x_{j}}^{2})\bigr]},

so that we have

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(\sum_{j}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\bigr)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2\pi i\tau\sum_{j}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\bigr)}d\tau

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{j}(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{-1/2}\exp{\bigl[2\pi i\sum_{j}(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}))(x_{j}^{2}+D_{x_{j}}^{2})\bigr]}d\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\left(\prod_{j}(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{-1/2}\exp{\bigl[2i(\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}))(\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2})\bigr]}\right)d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\left(\prod_{j}(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{-1/2}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{2\alpha_{j}+1}}{(1+(\tau\mu_{j})^{2})^{\alpha_{j}+\frac{1}{2}}}\right)d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},

and for
F(t)=\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(2\pi t)
, we find
\hat{F}(\tau)=\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}

and the sought result.
∎

Remark 3.17
.

It is also possible to provide a direct checking for the above lemma, since with the notations (
9.4.4
), (
9.4.5
), we have

\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}=\check{\widehat{G_{\alpha_{j}}}}\bigr(\tau\mu_{j}/(2\pi)\bigl),

and thus

F_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\prod_{j}\check{\widehat{G_{\alpha_{j}}}}\bigr(\tau\mu_{j}/(2\pi)\bigl)d\tau\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\prod_{j}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})H(t_{j})e^{-t_{j}}e^{2\pi i\tau\mu_{j}t_{j}/(2\pi)}dtd\tau\\
=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\prod_{j}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})H(t_{j})e^{-t_{j}}F\bigl(\sum_{j}\mu_{j}t_{j}/2\pi\bigr)dt.

Now since we have

F\bigl(\sum_{j}\mu_{j}t_{j}/2\pi\bigr)=\mathbf{1}_{[-1,1]}(\sum_{j}\mu_{j}t_{j})
,
this fits with the expression of
F_{\alpha}
in Theorem
3.13
.

Remark 3.18
.

Another interesting remark is that the expression (
3.3.9
) depends obviously only on
|\alpha|
and
a=a_{1}=\dots=a_{n}
in the case where all the
a_{j}
are equal:
indeed in that case, we have with
\mu=1/a
,

\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}=\frac{(1+i\tau\mu)^{|\alpha|}}{(1-i\tau\mu)^{|\alpha|+n}},

and this gives another (
a posteriori
) justification of our calculations in the isotropic case
of Section
3.2
.
On the other hand, we get also
the identity

(3.3.11)

F_{0_{\mathbb{N}^{n}}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}\operatorname{Re}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{-1}\Bigr)d\tau,

where the explicit expression (
3.3.13
) is given for the left-hand-side.

Lemma 3.19
.

With the notations of Theorem
3.13
, the function

K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})

is symmetric in the variables

(\alpha_{1},a_{1};\dots;\alpha_{n},a_{n})
, i.e. for a permutation
\pi
of

\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have

(3.3.12)

K_{\alpha_{\pi(1)},\dots,\alpha_{\pi(n)}}(a_{\pi(1)},\dots,a_{\pi(n)})=K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}).

Proof.

Formula (
3.3.5
) yields

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum s_{j}\geq 1\\
s_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\bigl(e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}a_{j}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2a_{j}s_{j})\bigr)\ ds,

and the domain of integration is invariant by permutation of the variables,
entailing the sought result.
∎

Lemma 3.20
.

With the notations of Theorem
3.13
, we have

\displaystyle K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=e^{-a_{n}}P_{\alpha_{n}}(a_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt+\int_{0}^{a_{n}}(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}L_{\alpha_{n}}(2t_{n})e^{-t_{n}}K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}}\bigl(a_{1}(1-t_{n}/a_{n}),\dots,a_{n-1}(1-t_{n}/a_{n})\bigr)dt_{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}P_{\alpha_{n}}(a_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt+\int_{0}^{1}(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}L_{\alpha_{n}}(2a_{n}\theta)e^{-\theta a_{n}}K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}}\bigl(a_{1}(1-\theta),\dots,a_{n-1}(1-\theta)\bigr)d\theta a_{n}.

Proof.

The domain
of integration is the disjoint union

\left\{\frac{t_{1}}{a_{1}}+\dots+\frac{t_{n-1}}{a_{n-1}}\geq 1-\frac{t_{n}}{a_{n}},t_{j}\geq 0,0\leq\frac{t_{n}}{a_{n}}\leq 1\right\}\sqcup\left\{\frac{t_{n}}{a_{n}}>1,t_{j}\geq 0,1\leq j\leq n-1\right\},

so that

\displaystyle K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=e^{-a_{n}}P_{\alpha_{n}}(a_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt+\int_{0}^{a_{n}}(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}L_{\alpha_{n}}(2t_{n})e^{-t_{n}}K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}}\bigl(a_{1}(1-t_{n}/a_{n}),\dots,a_{n-1}(1-t_{n}/a_{n})\bigr)dt_{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}P_{\alpha_{n}}(a_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt+\int_{0}^{1}(-1)^{\alpha_{n}}L_{\alpha_{n}}(2a_{n}\theta)e^{-\theta a_{n}}K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}}\bigl(a_{1}(1-\theta),\dots,a_{n-1}(1-\theta)\bigr)d\theta a_{n},

which is the sought result.
∎

Lemma 3.21
.

With the notations of Theorem
3.13
, we have, assuming that the
(a_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
are positive distinct numbers,

(3.3.13)

K_{0,\dots,0}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}.

Proof.

The latter formula is true for
n=1
since we have
K_{0}(a_{1})=e^{-a_{1}}
.
We have also

\displaystyle K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=e^{-a_{n}}+a_{n}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{n}}K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}}\bigl(a_{1}(1-\theta),\dots,a_{n-1}(1-\theta)\bigr)d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}+a_{n}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{n}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}e^{-a_{j}(1-\theta)}\frac{\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}+a_{n}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{n}}e^{-a_{j}(1-\theta)}d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}e^{-a_{j}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\theta(a_{j}-a_{n})}d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{e^{a_{j}-a_{n}}-1}{a_{j}-a_{n}}

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{e^{-a_{n}}-e^{-a_{j}}}{(a_{j}-a_{n})}

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{n}}\left(1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{1}{(a_{j}-a_{n})}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 94.0pt+\underbrace{\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{e^{-a_{j}}}{(a_{n}-a_{j})}}_{\text{OK}}.

We need to prove that

\left(1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{1}{(a_{j}-a_{n})}\right)=\frac{\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}}{\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-a_{n})}.

that is

\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}=\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-a_{n})\left(1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{1}{(a_{j}-a_{n})}\right),

which is

\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}=\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-a_{n})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}\frac{\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-a_{n})}{(a_{j}-a_{n})},

i.e.

(3.3.14)

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}=\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-a_{n})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a_{n}\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}(a_{k}-a_{n})}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}.

Let us reformulate (
3.3.14
) as an equality between polynomials (to be proven) with

(3.3.15)

\displaystyle\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}(a_{l}-X)+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{X\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}(a_{k}-X)}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}-\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}=0,

and let us assume that the
(a_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n-1}
are distinct and different from
0
. The polynomial
\mathcal{Q}

on the left-hand-side has degree less than
n-1
and we have

\mathcal{Q}(0)=0,\qquad\text{and \quad$\forall j\in\llbracket 1\mkern 1.5mu..\mkern 1.5mun-1\rrbracket,$}

\mathcal{Q}(a_{j})=\frac{a_{j}\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}a_{k}(a_{k}-a_{j})}{\prod_{k\not=j,1\leq k\leq n-1}(a_{k}-a_{j})}-\prod_{1\leq l\leq n-1}a_{l}=0,

so that
\mathcal{Q}
has degree less than
n-1
with
n
distinct roots and this proves the identity (
3.3.15
) when the
(a_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n-1}
are distinct and all different from
0
, proving (
3.3.13
) in that case; of course we may assume that all
a_{j}
are positive and noting from (
3.3.5
) that
K_{\alpha}
is continuous on
({\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}})^{n}
,
we get Formula (
3.3.13
) in all cases where all the
a_{j}
are positive,
concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

With the notations of Theorem
3.13
, we have, assuming

0<a_{1}\leq\dots\leq a_{n},

the inequality

(3.3.16)

K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\geq\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{\prod_{1\leq l<j}a_{l}}{(j-1)!}\geq e^{-\min_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{j}}=\max_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}.

Remark 3.23
.

The above estimate is sharp in the sense that when all the
a_{j}
are equal to the same
a>0
, we have proven in (
3.2.4
) that

K_{0}(a)=\frac{e^{-a}}{(n-1)!}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-s}(s+a)^{n-1}ds=e^{-a}\sum_{0\leq l\leq n-1}\frac{a^{l}}{(n-1-l)!l!}\Gamma(n-l)\\
=e^{-a}\sum_{0\leq l\leq n-1}\frac{a^{l}}{l!}=e^{-a}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{a^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{\prod_{1\leq l<j}a_{l}}{(j-1)!}_{|a_{1}=\dots=a_{n}=a}.

Proof.

The property is true for
n=1
since
K_{0}(a_{1})=e^{-a_{1}}
.
We check the case
n=2
with
a_{1}<a_{2}
, and we find

K_{(0,0)}(a_{1},a_{2})=e^{-a_{1}}+\int_{0}^{a_{1}}e^{-t_{1}}e^{-a_{2}(1-t_{1}/a_{1})}dt_{1}\\
=e^{-a_{1}}+e^{-a_{2}}\frac{e^{a_{2}-a_{1}}-1}{\frac{a_{2}}{a_{1}}-1}=e^{-a_{1}}+e^{-a_{2}}a_{1}\frac{e^{a_{2}-a_{1}}-1}{{a_{2}}-{a_{1}}}\geq e^{-a_{1}}+e^{-a_{2}}a_{1}.

Let us consider for some
n\geq 3
,
0<a_{1}<\dots<a_{n}
and inductively,

\displaystyle K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}

\displaystyle(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{1}}P_{0}(a_{1})+\int_{0}^{a_{1}}e^{-t_{1}}K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}}\bigl(a_{2}(1-t_{1}/a_{1}),\dots,a_{n}(1-t_{1}/a_{1})\bigr)dt_{1}

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{1}}P_{0}(a_{1})+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-a_{1}\theta}K_{0\in\mathbb{N}^{n-1}}\bigl(a_{2}(1-\theta),\dots,a_{n}(1-\theta)\bigr)d\theta

\displaystyle\geq e^{-a_{1}}+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-a_{1}\theta}\sum_{2\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}(1-\theta)}\frac{\prod_{2\leq l<j}a_{l}}{(j-2)!}(1-\theta)^{j-2}d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{1}}+\sum_{2\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\Bigl(\underbrace{a_{1}\prod_{2\leq l<j}a_{l}}_{\prod_{1\leq k<j}a_{k}}\Bigr)\int_{0}^{1}e^{(a_{j}-a_{1})\theta}\frac{1}{(j-2)!}(1-\theta)^{j-2}d\theta

\displaystyle\geq e^{-a_{1}}+\sum_{2\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq k<j}a_{k}\Bigr)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{(j-2)!}(1-\theta)^{j-2}d\theta

\displaystyle=e^{-a_{1}}+\sum_{2\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq k<j}a_{k}\Bigr)\frac{1}{(j-1)!},

concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 3.24
.

The reader may have noticed that it is not obvious on Formula (
3.3.13
)

K_{0,\dots,0}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{\prod_{k\not=j}a_{k}}{\prod_{k\not=j}(a_{k}-a_{j})},

that
K_{0}
is an entire function.
Let us start with taking a look at

\displaystyle K_{0,0}(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-a_{1}}a_{2}}{a_{2}-a_{1}}+\frac{e^{-a_{2}}a_{1}}{a_{1}-a_{2}}=\frac{a_{2}e^{-a_{1}}-a_{1}e^{-a_{2}}}{a_{2}-a_{1}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2}}\frac{a_{2}e^{-\frac{a_{1}}{2}+\frac{a_{2}}{2}}-a_{1}e^{-\frac{a_{2}}{2}+\frac{a_{1}}{2}}}{a_{2}-a_{1}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2}}\frac{a_{2}(\cosh\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2}+\sinh\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})-a_{1}(\cosh\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{2}+\sinh\frac{a_{1}-a_{2}}{2})}{a_{2}-a_{1}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2}}\Bigl[\cosh(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})+\frac{(a_{2}+a_{1})\sinh(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})}{a_{2}-a_{1}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2}}\Bigl[\cosh(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})+\frac{\frac{1}{2}(a_{2}+a_{1})\sinh(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})}{\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2}}\Bigr]

(3.3.17)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{2})}{2}}\Bigl[\cosh(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})+\frac{1}{2}(a_{2}+a_{1})\shc(\frac{a_{2}-a_{1}}{2})\Bigr],

where
\shc
stands for the even entire function defined by

(3.3.18)

\shc t=\frac{\sinh t}{t}.

We have also from Lemma
3.16

(3.3.19)

F_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau,

and defining the function
F_{\alpha}(a,\lambda)
as the absolutely converging integral,

(3.3.20)

F_{\alpha}(a,\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau,\quad F_{\alpha}(a)=F_{\alpha}(a,1),

we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\alpha}}{\partial\lambda}(a,\lambda)=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\cos(\lambda\tau)}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\lambda\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau

\displaystyle\hskip 44.0pt+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\lambda\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{i\lambda\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}+\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau

\displaystyle=i\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\text{Res}\left(e^{i\lambda\tau}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}};\tau=i/\mu_{j}=ia_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=i\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\text{Res}\left(e^{i\lambda\tau}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-i\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}(ia_{j}+\tau)^{\alpha_{j}}}{(i\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}(-ia_{j}+\tau)^{\alpha_{j}+1}};\tau=ia_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i^{n-1}}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\text{Res}\left(e^{i\lambda\tau}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}\frac{a_{j}(ia_{j}+\tau)^{\alpha_{j}}}{(\tau-ia_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}};\tau=ia_{j}\right),

so that assuming that the
a_{j}
are positive and distinct, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial F_{\alpha}}{\partial\lambda}(a,\lambda)=\frac{1}{i^{n-1}}(\prod a_{k})

\displaystyle\times\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}!}\left(\frac{d}{d\tau}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(e^{i\lambda\tau}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}(ia_{j}+\tau)^{\alpha_{j}}\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}(-1)^{\alpha_{k}}\frac{(ia_{k}+\tau)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(\tau-ia_{k})^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\tau=ia_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i^{n-1}}(\prod_{1\leq k\leq n}a_{k})\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 44.0pt\times\left(\frac{d}{id\sigma}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(e^{-\lambda\sigma}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}(ia_{j}+i\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}(-1)^{\alpha_{k}}\frac{(ia_{k}+i\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(i\sigma-ia_{k})^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1+|\alpha|}(\prod_{1\leq k\leq n}a_{k})\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{1}{\alpha_{j}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 44.0pt\times\left(\frac{d}{d\sigma}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(e^{-\lambda\sigma}(a_{j}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(a_{k}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(\sigma-a_{k})^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}

\displaystyle=(\prod_{1\leq k\leq n}a_{k})\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\left(\frac{d}{d\sigma}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(e^{-\lambda\sigma}(a_{j}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(a_{k}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}.

Since
F_{\alpha}(a,+{\infty})=1
, thanks to Lemma
9.7
, we find eventually that

\displaystyle F_{\alpha}(a)=F_{\alpha}(a,1)=\int_{+{\infty}}^{1}\frac{\partial F_{\alpha}}{\partial\lambda}(a,\lambda)d\lambda+1=1-K_{\alpha}(a),

\displaystyle K_{\alpha}(a)=(\prod_{1\leq k\leq n}a_{k})\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0pt\int^{+{\infty}}_{1}\left(\frac{d}{d\sigma}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(e^{-\lambda\sigma}(a_{j}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(a_{k}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}

\displaystyle\int^{+{\infty}}_{1}e^{-\lambda a_{j}}\!\!\!\left(\frac{d}{d\sigma}-\lambda\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((a_{j}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}a_{j}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(a_{k}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}a_{k}}{(a_{k}-\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}d\lambda.

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\int^{+{\infty}}_{1}e^{-\lambda a_{j}}\left(\frac{d}{d\sigma}-\lambda\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((a_{j}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(a_{k}+\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-\sigma)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|\sigma=a_{j}}d\lambda

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\int^{+{\infty}}_{a_{j}}\!\!\!e^{-t_{j}}

\displaystyle\hskip 32.0pt\left(\frac{d}{da_{j}s}-\frac{t_{j}}{a_{j}}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((a_{j}+a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{a_{k}(a_{k}+a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s=1}{dt_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\int^{+{\infty}}_{a_{j}}e^{-t}\left(\frac{d}{ds}-{t}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((1+s)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{a_{k}(a_{k}+a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s=1}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\int^{+{\infty}}_{a_{j}}e^{-t}\left(\frac{d}{ds}-{1}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((t+s)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{a_{k}(a_{k}+a_{j}s/t)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(a_{k}-a_{j}s/t)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s=t}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\int^{+{\infty}}_{a_{j}}e^{-t}

\displaystyle\hskip 44.0pt\left(\frac{d}{d(s+t)}-{1}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left((t+s)^{\alpha_{j}}\hskip-12.0pt\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{ta_{k}(t(a_{k}-a_{j})+a_{j}(s+t))^{\alpha_{k}}}{(t(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}(s+t))^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s+t=2t}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}

\displaystyle\times\int^{+{\infty}}_{a_{j}}e^{-t}\left(\frac{d}{ds}-{1}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(\frac{s^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{ta_{k}(t(a_{k}-a_{j})+a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}}}{(t(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}s)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s=2t}{dt}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}e^{-a_{j}}\int^{+{\infty}}_{0}e^{-t}

\displaystyle\times\left(\frac{d}{ds}-{1}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\left(\frac{s^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(t+a_{j})a_{k}\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}-a_{j})+a_{j}s\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}}}{\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}s\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\right)_{|s=2t+2a_{j}}{dt}.

We have also to deal with

\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\frac{(t+a_{j})a_{k}\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}-a_{j})+a_{j}s\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}}}{\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}s\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}

and

\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}(2t+2a_{j})\bigr)=a_{j}(a_{k}+a_{j})-2a_{j}^{2}+t(a_{k}-a_{j})=(t+a_{j})(a_{k}-a_{j})

(t+a_{j})(a_{k}+a_{j})-a_{j}s=(t+a_{j})(a_{k}-a_{j})+a_{j}(2t+2a_{j}-s)

so that

(3.3.21)

K_{\alpha}(a)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}}e^{-a_{j}}\int^{+{\infty}}_{0}e^{-t}\\
\times\left(\frac{d}{ds}-{1}\right)^{\alpha_{j}}\!\!\!\biggl(\frac{s^{\alpha_{j}}}{\alpha_{j}!}\!\!\prod_{1\leq k\leq n,k\not=j}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\frac{(t+a_{j})a_{k}\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}+a_{j})+a_{j}(s-2t-2a_{j})\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}}}{\bigl((t+a_{j})(a_{k}-a_{j})-a_{j}(s-2t-2a_{j})\bigr)^{\alpha_{k}+1}}\biggr)_{|s=2t+2a_{j}}\hskip-20.0pt{dt}.

3.4.
A conjecture on integrals of products of Laguerre polynomials

We formulate in this section a conjecture on the behaviour of the functions
K_{\alpha}(a)
;
as displayed in the previous sections, we know several useful elements for the analysis of these functions, including some quite explicit expression. However, in the non-isotropic case, we were not able to prove the estimate
F_{\alpha}(a)\leq 1
, equivalent to
K_{\alpha}(a)\geq 0
, except for the case
\alpha=0
. We are thus reduced to conjectural statements.

Conjecture 3.25
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer and let
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
. For
a=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in(0,+{\infty})^{n}
we define

(3.4.1)

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\\
\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}t_{j}/a_{j}\geq 1\end{subarray}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}(-1)^{\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{j}}(2t_{j})dt,

where
L_{k}
stands for the classical Laguerre polynomial

(3.4.2)

L_{k}(X)=\Bigl(\frac{d}{dX}-1\Bigr)^{k}\frac{X^{k}}{k!}.

Then we conjecture that, assuming
0<a_{1}\leq\dots\leq a_{n}
, we have

(3.4.3)

K_{\alpha}(a)\geq\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}e^{-a_{j}}\frac{\prod_{1\leq l<j}a_{l}}{(j-1)!}.

Remark 3.26
.

A slightly stronger and more symmetrical version of the above conjecture
is that for
n,\alpha,a,K_{\alpha}
as above, we have

(3.4.4)

K_{\alpha}(a)\geq K_{0}(a).

It is indeed stronger since we have proven in Lemma
3.22
that
K_{0}(a)

is greater than the right-hand-side of (
3.4.3
).

Theorem 3.27
.

The previous conjecture is a proven theorem in the following cases.

(1)

When
n=1
.

(2)

For all
n\geq 1
, when all the
a_{j}
are equal.

(3)

For all
n\geq 1
, when
\alpha=0_{\mathbb{N}^{n}}
.

(4)

When
n=2
and
\min(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=0
.

Proof.

\mathbf{(1)}

When
n=1
