, we have proven above (in Proposition
3.3
)
that for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}
,
a>0
,

(3.4.5)

K_{\alpha}(a)=e^{-a}P_{\alpha}(a)\geq e^{-a},

which is indeed (
3.4.4
) in that case.
With the notations of Theorem
3.5

(and in particular where
D_{a}
is defined in
(
3.1.22
))
this implies

(3.4.6)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{D_{a}})}\leq 1-e^{-a},

an inequality due to P. Flandrin in the 1988 paper
[
13
]
.

\mathbf{(2)}
Assuming that all the
a_{j}
are equal to
a>0
,
we have proven in Theorem
3.7
that for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
|\alpha|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\alpha_{j}
,

(3.4.7)

K_{\alpha}(a,\dots,a)\geq\frac{\Gamma(n,a)}{\Gamma(n)}=e^{-a}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{a^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}=K_{0}(a,\dots,a)

since from (
3.3.5
), we have

\displaystyle K_{0}(a,\dots,a)

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sum t_{j}\geq a\\
t_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}t_{n}\geq a\\
t_{j}\geq 0\end{subarray}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n})}dt+\int_{0}^{a}e^{-t_{n}}\int_{\sum t_{j}\geq a-t_{n}}e^{-(t_{1}+\dots+t_{n-1})}dt

(inductively)

\displaystyle=e^{-a}+\int_{0}^{a}e^{-t_{n}}e^{-(a-t_{n})}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{(a-t_{n})^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}dt_{n}

\displaystyle=e^{-a}\Bigl(1+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\frac{a^{j}}{j!}\Bigr)=e^{-a}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{a^{j-1}}{(j-1)!},

proving (
3.4.4
) in that case.
With

(3.4.8)

D_{(a)}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},2\pi\frac{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}}{a}\leq 1\},

this implies that

(3.4.9)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{D_{(a)}})}\leq 1-e^{-a}\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{a^{j-1}}{(j-1)!},

an inequality proven in the 2010 article
[
39
]
by E. Lieb
and Y. Ostrover.

\mathbf{(3)}
When
\alpha=0_{\mathbb{N}^{n}}
, we have proven (
3.4.3
) in Lemma
3.22
.

\mathbf{(4)}
When
n=2
, from the case
n=1
we have

K_{\alpha_{2}}(a_{2})=e^{-a_{2}}P_{\alpha_{2}}(a_{2}),

so that from Lemma
3.20
, we obtain

K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}(a_{1},a_{2})=e^{-a_{1}}P_{\alpha_{1}}(a_{1})+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{1}-(1-\theta)a_{2}}(-1)^{\alpha_{1}}L_{\alpha_{1}}(2\theta a_{1})P_{\alpha_{2}}(a_{2}(1-\theta))d\theta,

and if
\alpha_{1}
=0, it means that

K_{0,\alpha_{2}}(a_{1},a_{2})=e^{-a_{1}}+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{1}-(1-\theta)a_{2}}P_{\alpha_{2}}(a_{2}(1-\theta))d\theta\\
\geq e^{-a_{1}}+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{1}-(1-\theta)a_{2}}d\theta=K_{0,0}(a_{1},a_{2}),

and the reasoning is identical for
\alpha_{2}=0
,
concluding the proof of the theorem.
∎

We are interested in the Weyl quantization of the indicatrix of

(3.4.10)

D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},2\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}}{a_{j}}\leq 1\},\quad a_{j}>0,

and
we have a weaker conjecture.

Conjecture 3.28

(A weak form of Conjecture
3.25
)
.

With

n,\alpha,a,K_{\alpha}
as in Conjecture
3.25
,
we conjecture that

(3.4.11)

K_{\alpha}(a)\geq 0.

Note that Inequality (
3.4.11
) is equivalent to

(3.4.12)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{D_{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}})}\leq 1.

Remark 3.29
.

In the first place, although the second conjecture is much weaker than the first, there is no reason to believe that the weak conjecture should be easier to prove than the first: in particular, in the known cases, it is indeed the proof of the precise statement (
3.4.3
) which leads to (
3.4.11
) and we are not aware of a direct proof of (
3.4.11
), even in one dimension.

A summary of our knowledge on the functions
K_{\alpha}
.
As proven in Remarks
3.14
and
3.15
, the functions
K_{\alpha}
are entire functions given on the open subset (
3.3.6
) by Formula (
3.3.5
) (see also Formula (
3.3.17
)).
Moreover the function
F_{\alpha}(a)=1-K_{\alpha}(a)
can be expressed as a simple integral for
a_{j}>0
,

(3.4.13)

F_{\alpha}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin\tau}{\pi\tau}\Bigl(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{(1+i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}}}{(1-i\tau\mu_{j})^{\alpha_{j}+1}}\Bigr)d\tau,\quad\mu_{j}=\frac{1}{a_{j}},

and we have an explicit expression of the function
K_{\alpha}
as a sum of simple integrals in (
3.3.21
). However, having an explicit expression
does not mean much and for instance, we do have several explicit expressions for the Laguerre
polynomials but Inequality (
9.4.3
) remains very hard work,
requiring a deep understanding of these polynomials.
We have also an induction formula
in Lemma
3.20
.
As a further remark, we have the following

Lemma 3.30
.

Let
n,\alpha,a,K_{\alpha}
as in Conjecture
3.25
. Then we have

(3.4.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{a_{n}\rightarrow+{\infty}}K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1},\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1},a_{n})=K_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n-1}),

(3.4.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{a_{1}\rightarrow{0}_{+}}K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})=1.

Proof.

Formula (
3.3.5
) and Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem imply the first equality (
3.4.14
).
Lemma
3.20
, in which we may swap the variables
a_{1}
and
a_{n}
gives for
a_{1}>0

K_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})=e^{-a_{1}}P_{\alpha_{1}}(a_{1})\\
+a_{1}\int_{0}^{1}e^{-\theta a_{1}}(-1)^{\alpha_{1}}L_{\alpha_{1}}(2a_{1}\theta)K_{\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{n}}\bigl(a_{2}(1-\theta),\dots,a_{n}(1-\theta)\bigr)d\theta,

and since
P_{\alpha_{1}}
is a polynomial such that
P_{\alpha_{1}}(0)=1
, we get (
3.4.15
).
∎

Reasons to believe in the conjecture
.
This is true in one dimension, also in
n
dimensions for spheres and it is a quadratic problem in the sense that ellipsoids are convex subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
characterized by an inequality

\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},p(X)\leq 0\},

where

p
is a polynomial of degree 2 with a positive-definite quadratic part.
We shall see below in this paper that convexity of a set
A
does not guarantee that the quantization

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{A})}
is smaller than 1 as an operator and that Flandrin’s conjecture is not true,
but it is hard to believe that such a phenomenon could occur for ellipsoids.
We must point out a specific feature of anisotropy related to Mehler’s formula (
2.2.1
): if all the
\mu_{j}
are equal to the same
\mu>0
(this is the isotropic case),
then, with
q_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\mu(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})
, we have

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau q_{\mu}(x,\xi)})}=\phi(\tau\mu)e^{2i\arctan(\tau\mu)\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\pi(x_{j}^{2}+D_{j}^{2})},

where
\phi(\tau\mu)
is a scalar quantity. As a consequence, if we quantize
F(q_{\mu}(x,\xi))
, we get

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\phi(\tau\mu)e^{2i\frac{\arctan(\tau\mu)}{\mu}\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu})}}d\tau,

and thus

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}=\widetilde{F}({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu})}),\qquad\widetilde{F}(\lambda)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\phi(\tau\mu)e^{2i\pi\frac{\arctan(\tau\mu)}{\mu}\lambda}d\tau,

and
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}
appears as a function of the self-adjoint operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu})}
.
Following the same route in the anisotropic case, we get,
with

(3.4.16)

\displaystyle q_{\mu}(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}),

(3.4.17)

\displaystyle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{F}(\tau)\phi(\tau\mu)e^{2i\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(\frac{\arctan(\tau\mu_{j}}{\mu_{j}})\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+D_{j}^{2})}d\tau,

and since
\frac{1}{\mu_{j}}\arctan(\tau\mu_{j})
does depend on
\mu_{j}
(and not only on

\tau
), the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(F\bigl(q_{\mu}(x,\xi)\bigr)\bigr)}
is not a function of
the self-adjoint operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu})}
.

As a final comment on the strongest form of the Conjecture (
3.4.4
),
we would say that it could be seen as a property of the Laguerre polynomials, known in the case
n=1
, where it stands as follows:
we define for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the polynomial
P_{k}
by

(3.4.18)

P_{k}(x)=\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{-t}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2x+2t)dt,

and we have
P_{k}(0)=1

from (
9.4.5
). Moreover, we have the inequality (equivalent to (
3.4.4
) for
n=1
)

(3.4.19)

\forall x\geq 0,\quad P_{k}(x)\geq P_{k}(0).

We note that

e^{-x}P_{k}(x)=\int_{x}^{+{\infty}}e^{-s}(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2s)ds,

so that the unique solution
P_{k}

of the Initial Value Problem for the ODE

(3.4.20)

P_{k}(x)-P^{\prime}_{k}(x)=(-1)^{k}L_{k}(2x),\quad P_{k}(0)=1,

does satisfy (
3.4.19
).
We note that from Lemma
3.2
, we have

P^{\prime}_{k}(X)=2\sum_{0\leq l<k}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(2X),

so that (
3.4.19
) is a consequence of Feldheim Inequality
(
9.4.3
). Let us reformulate (
3.4.4
), using the polynomials
P_{k}
: for
a_{j}\geq 0
,

(3.4.21)

K_{\alpha}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\\
\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}t_{j}/a_{j}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\Bigl\{-e^{-t_{j}}P_{\alpha_{j}}(t_{j})\Bigr\}dt\\
\geq K_{0}(a)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}\\
\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}t_{j}/a_{j}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\Bigl\{-e^{-t_{j}}\Bigr\}dt,

which is equivalent to

(3.4.22)

\int H\bigl(1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}H(s_{j})\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}\Bigl\{-e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}P_{\alpha_{j}}(a_{j}s_{j})\Bigr\}ds\\
\leq\int H\bigl(1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{j}H(s_{j})e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}ds,

where
H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
(Heaviside function).
This is equivalent to

(3.4.23)

\int H\bigl(1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}H(s_{j})e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}\left(a_{j}-\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}\right)\Bigl\{P_{\alpha_{j}}(a_{j}s_{j})\Bigr\}ds\\
\leq\int H\bigl(1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{j}H(s_{j})e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}ds,

i.e. to

(3.4.24)

\int H\bigl(1-\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}s_{j}\bigr)\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}H(s_{j})e^{-a_{j}s_{j}}\\
\times\left(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}a_{j}-\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\left(a_{j}-\frac{\partial}{\partial s_{j}}\right)\Bigl\{P_{\alpha_{j}}(a_{j}s_{j})\Bigr\}\right)ds\geq 0.

Note that for
n=1
, it means for
a\geq 0
,

\int_{0}^{1}e^{-as}\bigl(a-aP_{k}(as)+aP^{\prime}_{k}(as)\bigr)ds={1-e^{-a}}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\bigl\{e^{-as}P_{k}(as)\bigr\}\\
={1-e^{-a}}+e^{-a}P_{k}(a)-P_{k}(0)=e^{-a}\bigl(P_{k}(a)-1\bigr)\geq 0,

which holds true from (
3.4.19
).

Remark 3.31
.

There are several classical results on products of Laguerre polynomials, in particular the article
[
7
]
,

On some expansions in Laguerre polynomials
by A. Erdélyi
and also the paper
[
40
]
,

Linearization of the products of the generalized Lauricella polynomials and the multivariate Laguerre polynomials via their integral representations

by
Shuoh-Jung Liu,
Shy-Der Lin,
Han-Chun Lu
and H. M. Srivastava.
However it seems that the non-negativity of the polynomials

P_{\alpha;1},P^{\prime}_{\alpha;1}
do not suffice to tackle the conjecture in two dimensions and more.

4.
Parabolas

4.1.
Preliminary remarks

We start with a picture, demonstrating that the epigraph
of a parabola is an increasing union of ellipses.

Figure 4.
The epigraph of a parabola is an increasing union of ellipses.

It is easy to see that the epigraph of a parabola, i.e. the set

\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\xi>x^{2}\}

is a countable increasing union of ellipses
in the sense that

(4.1.1)

\mathcal{P}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\xi>x^{2}\}=\cup_{k\geq 1}\underbrace{\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\xi>x^{2}+k^{-2}\xi^{2}\}}_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}.

Note that for
k\geq 1
we have
\mathcal{E}_{k}\subset\mathcal{E}_{k+1}\subset\mathcal{P}
since

x^{2}+k^{-2}\xi^{2}\geq x^{2}+(k+1)^{-2}\xi^{2}>x^{2},

from the fact that
\xi>0
on
\mathcal{E}_{k}
. Moreover, if
\xi>x^{2}
and
k>{\xi}/{\sqrt{\xi-x^{2}}}
, we get
(x,\xi)\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Remark 4.1
.

The ellipse
\mathcal{E}_{k}
is symplectically equivalent to a circle with area
\frac{\pi k^{3}}{4}

since

x^{2}+k^{-2}\xi^{2}-\xi=x^{2}+k^{-2}\bigl(\xi-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\bigr)^{2}-\frac{k^{2}}{4}=(\lambda^{-1}y)^{2}+k^{-2}\bigl(\lambda\eta-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\bigr)^{2}-\frac{k^{2}}{4}\\
=\lambda^{-2}y^{2}+\lambda^{2}k^{-2}\bigl(\eta-\frac{k^{2}}{2\lambda}\bigr)^{2}-\frac{k^{2}}{4},

so that choosing
\lambda
such that
\lambda^{-2}=\lambda^{2}k^{-2}
, e.g.
\lambda=\sqrt{k}
, we get

x^{2}+k^{-2}\xi^{2}-\xi=k^{-1}\bigl(y^{2}+(\eta-\frac{k^{2}}{2\lambda})^{2}\bigr)-\frac{k^{2}}{4},

and

\mathcal{E}_{k}=\{(y,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},y^{2}+\zeta^{2}<\frac{k^{3}}{4}\},

where
(y,\zeta)
are the affine symplectic coordinates

y=xk^{1/2},\quad\zeta=\xi k^{-1/2}-\frac{k^{3/2}}{2}.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

and with
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}_{k}
defined by (
4.1.1
), we have

\iint_{\xi>x^{2}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\lim_{k\rightarrow+{\infty}}\iint_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, we may apply the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem and (
4.1.1
) to obtain the equality in the lemma. On the other hand Theorem
3.5
and Remark
4.1
imply

\iint_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{k}})}u,u\rangle\leq(1-e^{-\frac{\pi k^{3}}{2}})\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

and the sought result.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

Moreover, Theorem
3.5
and the expression of
F_{0}(a)=1-e^{-a}
imply that with
\psi_{0}
defined in (
9.1.31
), we have

\iint_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{k}})}\psi_{0},\psi_{0}\rangle=\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}(1-e^{-\pi k^{3}/3}),

so that from Lemma
4.2
, we have

\iint_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\|\psi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

entailing

(4.1.2)

\sup_{\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}),\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=1}\iint_{\mathcal{P}}\mathcal{W}(\phi,\phi)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=1.

Remark 4.4
.

We want to study the operator with Weyl symbol
H(\xi-x^{2})
(
H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the Heaviside function) and since
\xi-x^{2}
is a polynomial with degree less than 2,
see from (
1.2.5
)
that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(H(\xi-x^{2})\bigr)}
commutes with
D_{x}-x^{2}=e^{2\pi ix^{3}/3}D_{x}e^{-2\pi ix^{3}/3}
, and the latter has (continuous) spectrum
\mathbb{R}
:
we expect thus that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(H(\xi-x^{2})\bigr)}
should have continuous spectrum and be conjugated to a Fourier multiplier.

4.2.
Calculation of the kernel

The Weyl symbol of the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}
is

H(\xi-x^{2}),

(
\mathcal{P}
is defined in (
4.1.1
),

H
is the Heaviside function
H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
),
corresponding to the distribution kernel

k_{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)

obtained from Proposition
1.9
by (we use freely integrals meaning only Fourier transform in the distributional sense),

k_{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)=\int e^{2i\pi(x-y)\xi}H(\xi-(\frac{x+y}{2})^{2})d\xi=\int e^{2i\pi(x-y)(\xi+(\frac{x+y}{2})^{2})}H(\xi)d\xi\\
=e^{2i\pi(x-y)(\frac{x+y}{2})^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi(y-x)}\bigr)\\
=\frac{\delta_{0}(y-x)}{2}+\frac{e^{2i\pi(x-y)(\frac{x+y}{2})^{2}}}{2i\pi(y-x)}.

We have

4(x-y)(\frac{x+y}{2})^{2}=(x^{2}-y^{2})(x+y)=x^{3}-y^{3}+x^{2}y-y^{2}x\\
=\frac{4}{3}(x^{3}-y^{3})+\frac{1}{3}(y-x)^{3},

so that

(4.2.1)

k_{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}x^{3}}\left(\frac{\delta_{0}(y-x)}{2}+\frac{e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\frac{1}{3}(y-x)^{3}}}{2i\pi(y-x)}\right)e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}y^{3}},

and the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}
is unitarily equivalent to the operator with kernel

(4.2.2)

\tilde{k}(x,y)=\frac{\delta_{0}(y-x)}{2}+\frac{e^{i\frac{\pi}{6}(y-x)^{3}}}{2i\pi(y-x)}.

We have proven the following result.

Lemma 4.5
.

The operator with Weyl symbol
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x,\xi)\mapsto\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(\xi-x^{2})
has the distribution kernel

k_{\mathcal{P}}(x,y)=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}x^{3}}\left(\frac{\delta_{0}(y-x)}{2}+\frac{e^{i\frac{\pi}{6}(y-x)^{3}}}{2i\pi(y-x)}\right)e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}y^{3}},

and is thus unitarily equivalent to

(4.2.3)

\frac{\operatorname{Id}}{2}+\text{convolution with\hskip 10.22217pt}\frac{ie^{-i\pi t^{3}/6}}{2\pi}\textrm{\rm pv}\frac{1}{t}.

Lemma 4.6
.

The distribution
\frac{ie^{-i\pi t^{3}/6}}{2\pi}\textrm{\rm pv}\frac{1}{t}

has the Fourier transform

(4.2.4)

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\frac{\sin(2\pi as\tau+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds,\quad a=(2/\pi)^{1/3}.

The operator (
4.2.3
) is the Fourier multiplier
\omega(D_{t})
with

(4.2.5)

\omega(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(s\eta+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds\right),\quad\eta=2^{4/3}\pi^{2/3}\tau.

Proof.

We calculate in the distribution sense (
t=as,a=(2/\pi)^{1/3}
),

\int e^{-2i\pi t\tau}i\frac{e^{-i\pi t^{3}/6}}{2\pi t}dt=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int e^{-2i\pi as\tau}\frac{e^{-i\pi a^{3}s^{3}/6}}{s}ds=\frac{i}{2\pi}\int\frac{(-i)\sin(\frac{s^{3}}{3}+2\pi as\tau)}{s}ds\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\frac{\sin(2\pi as\tau+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds,

so that
with
\eta=2\pi a\tau
, we get

\omega(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(s\eta+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds\right)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(1-F(\eta)\bigr)=G(\eta),

proving the lemma.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

We have, with
\eta=2^{4/3}\pi^{2/3}\tau
,

(4.2.6)

\displaystyle\omega(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(s\eta+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds\right)=G(\eta),\quad\omega(0)=\frac{2}{3}=G(0),

(4.2.7)

\displaystyle G^{\prime}(\eta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cos(s\eta+\frac{s^{3}}{3})ds=\operatorname{Re}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp{i(s\eta+\frac{s^{3}}{3})}ds=\operatorname{\tt A\!i}(\eta),

(4.2.8)

\displaystyle G(\eta)=\frac{2}{3}+\int_{0}^{\eta}\operatorname{\tt A\!i}(\xi)d\xi,

where
\operatorname{\tt A\!i}
is the Airy function defined as the inverse Fourier transform of
t\mapsto e^{i(2\pi t)^{3}/3}
.

Proof.

We have

(4.2.9)

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(\frac{s^{3}}{3})}{s}ds=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(\sigma)}{3^{1/3}\sigma^{1/3}}3^{1/3}\frac{1}{3}\sigma^{-2/3}d\sigma\\
=\frac{1}{3\pi}\int_{-{\infty}}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin\sigma}{\sigma}d\sigma=\frac{1}{3},

proving (
4.2.6
).
We have also

G(\eta)=\frac{1}{2}+\operatorname{Im}\Bigl\{\text{Inverse Fourier Transform}\big\{y\mapsto e^{i(2\pi y)^{3}/3}\text{pv}(\frac{1}{2\pi y})\bigr\}\Bigr\},

and thus

G^{\prime}(\eta)=\operatorname{Im}\Bigl\{\text{Inverse Fourier Transform}\big\{y\mapsto e^{i(2\pi y)^{3}/3}i\bigr\}\Bigr\}\\
=\operatorname{Im}\left(\int e^{2i\pi y\eta}e^{i(2\pi y)^{3}/3}idy\right)=\operatorname{Im}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int e^{it\eta}e^{it^{3}/3}idt\right)=\operatorname{\tt A\!i}(\eta),

which is (
4.2.7
), implying (
4.2.8
).
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

With
G
defined in Lemma
4.7
, we get that
G
is an entire function, real-valued on the real line such that

(4.2.10)

\lim_{\eta\rightarrow+{\infty}}G(\eta)=1,\quad\lim_{\eta\rightarrow-{\infty}}G(\eta)=0,

and moreover with
\eta_{0}
the largest zero of the Airy function (
\eta_{0}\approx-2.33811
), the function
G
has an absolute minimum at
\eta_{0}
with
G(\eta_{0})\approx-0.274352
,

(4.2.11)

\forall\eta\in\mathbb{R},\quad G(\eta_{0})\leq G(\eta)<1.

Proof.

The first statements follow from Lemma
4.7
and (
4.2.10
) is implied by
(
4.2.8
)
and (
9.7.34
), (
9.7.38
).
The strict inequality in (
4.2.11
) follows for
\eta\geq 0
from (
4.2.7
) since
\operatorname{\tt A\!i}
is positive on
[0,+{\infty})
so that
G
is strictly increasing there from
G(0)=2/3
to
G(+{\infty})=1
.
The other statements are proven in Section
9.7
of the Appendix.
∎

4.3.
The main result

Collecting the results of Lemmas
4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
,
4.8
and of Section
9.7
in the Appendix,
we have proven the following theorem.

Theorem 4.9
.

Let
H(\xi-x^{2})=\mathbf{1}\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\xi\geq x^{2}\}
be the indicatrix
of the epigraph of the parabola with equation
\xi=x^{2}
. Then the operator with Weyl symbol
H(\xi-x^{2})
is unitary equivalent to the Fourier multiplier

G(2^{4/3}\pi^{2/3}\tau)
where

(4.3.1)

G(\eta)=\frac{2}{3}+\int_{0}^{\eta}\operatorname{\tt A\!i}(\xi)d\xi=\int_{-{\infty}}^{\eta}\operatorname{\tt A\!i}(\xi)d\xi,\qquad\text{($\operatorname{\tt A\!i}$ is the Airy function)}.

The function
G
is entire on
\mathbb{C}
, real valued on the real line and such that

G(\mathbb{R})=[G(\eta_{0}),1),

where
\eta_{0}
is the largest zero of the Airy function

(4.3.2)

we have
\eta_{0}\approx-2.338107410,\qquad G(\eta_{0})\approx-0.2743520591
.

The operator with Weyl symbol
H(\xi-x^{2})
is self-adjoint bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with norm
1
,
with spectrum equal to
[G(\eta_{0}),1]
(continuous spectrum) and

(4.3.3)

\forall u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad G(\eta_{0})\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\iint_{\xi\geq x^{2}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Figure 5.
The function
G
. More details on
G
are given in our Appendix
9.7
.

4.4.
Paraboloids, a conjecture

We are interested now in multi-dimensional versions of the previous results, namely, we would like to find a bound for integrals of the Wigner distribution on paraboloids of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
for
n\geq 2
.
Let us start with recalling Theorem 21.5.3 in
[
24
]
, a version of which was given in our Theorem
3.12
in the positive-definite case.

4.4.1.
On non-negative quadratic forms

Theorem 4.10

(Symplectic reduction of quadratic forms, Theorem 21.5.3 in
[
24
]
)
.

Let
q
be a non-negative quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
equipped with the canonical symplectic form (
1.2.24
). Then there exists
S
in the symplectic group
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})

of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

r\in\{0,\dots,n\}
,

\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{r}

positive,
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
such that
r+s\leq n
,

so that for all
X=(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

(4.4.1)

q(SX)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})+\sum_{r+1\leq j\leq r+s}x_{j}^{2}.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
be equipped with the canonical
symplectic form (
1.2.24
).
Let
q
be a non-negative quadratic form on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with rank
2n-1
and
T
be a non-zero vector in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
q(\sigma T)=0
.
A paraboloid
\mathcal{P}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with vertex
0
and shape
(q,T)

is defined by

(4.4.2)

\mathcal{P}=\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},q(X)\leq[X,T]\}.

A paraboloid
\mathcal{Q}
with vertex
m\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and shape
(q,T)

is defined as

(4.4.3)

\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{P}+m,

where
\mathcal{P}
is a paraboloid with vertex
0
and shape
(q,T)
.

Remark 4.12
.

We can find some symplectic coordinates such that

q(X)-[X,T]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})+\sum_{r+1\leq j\leq r+s}x_{j}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(x_{j}\tau_{j}-\xi_{j}t_{j}),

with
2r+s=2n-1
.
We can get rid of the linear terms
x_{j}\tau_{j}-\xi_{j}t_{j}

when
1\leq j\leq r
by writing

\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})+x_{j}\tau_{j}-\xi_{j}t_{j}=\mu_{j}\bigl(x_{j}+\frac{\tau_{j}}{2\mu_{j}}\bigr)^{2}+\mu_{j}\bigl(\xi_{j}-\frac{t_{j}}{2\mu_{j}}\bigr)^{2}-\frac{1}{4\mu_{j}}(t_{j}^{2}+\tau_{j}^{2}),

and also of
x_{j}\tau_{j}
for
r+1\leq j\leq r+s
, since

x_{j}^{2}+x_{j}\tau_{j}=(x_{j}+\frac{\tau_{j}}{2})^{2}-\frac{\tau_{j}^{2}}{4}.

We are left with using affine symplectic coordinates
(y,\eta)
so that

q(X)-[X,T]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}\mu_{j}(y_{j}^{2}+\eta_{j}^{2})+\sum_{r+1\leq j\leq r+s}y_{j}^{2}\\
-\sum_{r+1\leq j\leq r+s}\eta_{j}t_{j}+\sum_{r+s+1\leq j\leq n}(y_{j}\tau_{j}-\eta_{j}t_{j})-a.

Since we have
2r+s=2n-1
, we get
r+s+1=2n-r
: we cannot have

r+s+1\leq n

since it would imply that
2n-r\leq n
and thus
r\geq n
, which is incompatible with

2r+s=2n-1,r,s\geq 0.

We get then that

s=2l+1,r=n-1-l

and since
r+s\leq n,1\leq s
, we have
l=0
,

s=1
,
r=n-1
,
and

q(X)-[X,T]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\mu_{j}(y_{j}^{2}+\eta_{j}^{2})+y_{n}^{2}-\eta_{n}t_{n}-a,

and
t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
.
With
y_{n}=t^{1/3}\tilde{y}_{n},\eta_{n}=t^{-1/3}\tilde{\eta}_{n}
,
we get

q(X)-[X,T]=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\mu_{j}(y_{j}^{2}+\eta_{j}^{2})+t^{2/3}(\tilde{y}_{n}^{2}-\tilde{\eta}_{n}-at^{-2/3}),

and the inequality
q(X)-[X,T]\leq 0
is equivalent to

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}t^{-2/3}\mu_{j}(y_{j}^{2}+\eta_{j}^{2})+\tilde{y}_{n}^{2}\leq\tilde{\eta}_{n}+at^{-2/3}.

We can thus assume
ab initio
that our paraboloid is given by the inequality

(4.4.4)

\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}\nu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})+x_{n}^{2}\leq\xi_{n}.

4.4.2.
On the kernel for the paraboloid

We shall consider the paraboloid

(4.4.5)

\mathcal{P}_{n}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\ x_{n}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})\leq\xi_{n}\}.

We have, with
X^{\prime}=(x^{\prime};\xi^{\prime})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1};\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n-1})
,

\displaystyle P={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(H(\xi_{n}-x_{n}^{2}-|X^{\prime}|^{2})\bigr)}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{H}(\tau){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(\xi_{n}-x_{n}^{2})})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{-2i\pi\tau|X^{\prime}|^{2}})}d\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{H}(\tau)\mathbb{P}_{k;n-1}\otimes{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(\xi_{n}-x_{n}^{2})})}e^{-i(\arctan\tau)(2k+n-1)}(1+\tau^{2})^{-\frac{(n-1)}{2}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Id}

\displaystyle{\hskip 15.0pt}{\hskip 15.0pt}+\frac{1}{2i\pi}\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{k;n-1}\otimes\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(\xi_{n}-x_{n}^{2})})}\frac{1}{\tau}\Bigl(\frac{1-i\tau}{(1+\tau^{2})^{1/2}}\Bigr)^{2k+{n-1}}(1+\tau^{2})^{-\frac{(n-1)}{2}}d\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Id}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{k;n-1}\otimes\int_{\mathbb{R}}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{2i\pi\tau(\xi_{n}-x_{n}^{2})})}\frac{(1-i\tau)^{k}}{i\pi\tau(1+i\tau)^{k+n-1}}d\tau.

Let
k(x_{n},y_{n})
be the kernel of the operator in the integral: we have

k(x_{n},y_{n})=e^{\frac{2i\pi}{3}(x_{n}^{3}-y_{n}^{3})}e^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}(x_{n}-y_{n})^{3}}\frac{i}{\pi(x_{n}-y_{n})}\frac{(1+i(x_{n}-y_{n}))^{k}}{(1-i(x_{n}-y_{n}))^{k+n-1}}.

As a result, we find that
P
is unitarily equivalent to
\tilde{P}
, with

(4.4.6)

2\tilde{P}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{k;n-1}\otimes\bigl(I_{n}+\text{convolution with }\frac{ie^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}x_{n}^{3}}}{\pi x_{n}}\frac{(1+ix_{n})^{k}}{(1-ix_{n})^{k+n-1}}\bigr).

We define

(4.4.7)

\omega_{k,n-1}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}+\int\frac{ie^{-\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}}{2\pi t}\frac{(1+it)^{k}}{(1-it)^{k+n-1}}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt\\
=\frac{1}{2}+\int\frac{e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}}{2i\pi t}\frac{(1-it)^{k}}{(1+it)^{k+n-1}}e^{2i\pi t\tau}dt,

and we get that

(4.4.8)

\tilde{P}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\mathbb{P}_{k;n-1}\otimes\omega_{k,n-1}(D_{x_{n}}).

We note that for
n=1
, the sum is reduced to
k=0
with
\mathbb{P}_{0;0}=I
,
so that we recover Formula (
4.2.6
) with
\omega_{0,0}=\omega
.
We find also that

(4.4.9)

\omega_{k,n-1}^{\prime}(\tau)=\int e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}\frac{(1-it)^{k}}{(1+it)^{k+n-1}}e^{2i\pi t\tau}dt,

in the sense that the inverse Fourier transform
of
t\mapsto e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}\frac{(1-it)^{k}}{(1+it)^{k+n-1}}
is the distribution derivative of
\omega_{k,n-1}
.
Going back to the normalization of
Lemma
4.7
, we have, with
\eta=2^{4/3}\pi^{2/3}\tau
,

(4.4.10)

\displaystyle G_{k,n-1}(\eta)

\displaystyle=\omega_{k,n-1}(\tau),

\displaystyle G_{k,n-1}^{\prime}(\eta)

\displaystyle=2^{-4/3}\pi^{-2/3}\int e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}\frac{(1-it)^{k}}{(1+it)^{k+n-1}}e^{2^{-\frac{1}{3}}i\pi^{\frac{1}{3}}t\eta}dt,

(4.4.11)

\displaystyle\underbrace{=}_{t=\pi^{-\frac{1}{3}}2^{\frac{1}{3}}s}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int e^{\frac{is^{3}}{3}}\frac{(1-i\pi^{-1/3}2^{1/3}s)^{k}}{(1+i\pi^{-1/3}2^{1/3}s)^{k+n-1}}e^{is\eta}ds:=A_{k,n-1}(\eta).

We have
A_{0,0}=\operatorname{\tt A\!i}
and
A_{k,n-1}
is an entire function, real-valued on the real line;
we have

G_{k,n-1}(\eta)=\int_{-\infty}^{\eta}A_{k,n-1}(\xi)d\xi,\quad G_{k,n-1}(+{\infty})=1.

Remark 4.13
.

We claim that
the asymptotic properties of the functions
A_{k,n-1}
are analogous to the properties of the standard Airy function and we have indeed from (
4.4.9
),

(4.4.12)

\omega_{k,n-1}^{\prime}(\tau)=(1-iD)^{k}(1+iD)^{-k-n+1}\mathcal{F}^{-1}(e^{\frac{i\pi}{6}t^{3}}).

We claim as well that

-\frac{1}{2}<\inf_{k\geq 0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}}G(\eta)<0,\quad\sup_{k\geq 0,\eta\in\mathbb{R}}G(\eta)=1,

so that
\tilde{P}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

(4.4.13)

\int_{\xi_{n}\geq x_{n}^{2}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq n-1}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}.

5.
Conics with eccentricity greater than 1

We want to consider now integrals of the Wigner distribution on “hyperbolic”
convex subsets of the plane such as

(5.0.1)

\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\xi\geq\sigma,x\geq 0\},

where
\sigma
is a non-negative parameter.
It is convenient to start with the limit-case where
\sigma=0
and

\mathcal{C}_{0}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0\}

(we will label
\mathcal{C}_{0}
as the
quarter-plane
).
The indicator function of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is
H(x)H(\xi)
where
H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is the Heaviside function.

Acknowledgements.

The author is grateful to Thomas Duyckaerts for sharp comments on a first version of this section.

N.B
.

The reader will see a great similarity between our calculations below in this section and the J.G. Wood & A.J. Bracken paper
[
55
]
(see also
[
4
]
).
This article is
very important for the problem at stake – Integrating the Wigner distribution on subsets of the phase space – and was a wealthy source of information for us, although as a mathematician,
the author has a quite rigid relationship with calculations, and feels the need to justify formal manipulations;
for instance, we may point out that the test functions used in
[
55
]
are homogeneous distributions of type

x_{\pm}^{-\frac{1}{2}+i\omega},\quad\omega\in\mathbb{R},

which are not in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
(not even in
L^{2}_{\textrm{loc}}
),
a situation which raises some difficulties, first when you try to normalize in
L^{2}
these test functions and also when trying to give a non-formal meaning to their images under the operator with Weyl symbol
H(x)H(\xi)
, images which are not clearly defined.
In our joint paper
[
6
]

with B. Delourme and T. Duyckaerts, proving that Flandrin’s conjecture is not true, we followed numerical arguments which were quite apart from
the arguments of
[
55
]
.
However, in this article, we do follow many of the arguments of
[
55
]
,
along with avoiding formal calculations.

5.1.
The quarter-plane,
a counterexample to Flandrin’s conjecture

5.1.1.
Preliminaries

We study in this section the operator

(5.1.1)

A_{0}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}

where
H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
,
that is the Weyl quantization of the characteristic function of the first quarter of the plane.

Lemma 5.1
.

The operator
A_{0}
given by (
5.1.1
) is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

Since the Weyl symbol of
A_{0}
is real-valued,
A_{0}
is formally self-adjoint and it is enough to prove that
A_{0}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let us start with recalling the classical formulas

(5.1.2)

\hat{H}(t)=\frac{\delta_{0}(t)}{2}+\frac{1}{2i\pi}\text{\rm pv}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right),\quad\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}=\frac{1}{i\pi}\text{\rm pv}\left(\frac{1}{t}\right),

useful below.
The kernel
13
13

13

There is no difficulty at defining the product
S\bigl((x+y)/2\bigr)T(x-y)

for
S,T
tempered distributions on the real line since we may use the tensor product
with

\langle S(\frac{x+y}{2})T(x-y),\Phi(x,y)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=\langle S(x_{1})\otimes T(x_{2}),\Phi(x_{1}+\frac{x_{2}}{2},x_{1}-\frac{x_{2}}{2})\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

However, we shall not use directly Formula (
5.1.3
), since want to avoid formal manipulation involving for instance meaningless products such as
H(x)H(y)k_{0}(x,y)
.
We refer the reader to footnote
14
on page
14
and to Remark
5.2
for more details on this matter.

of
A_{0}

is

(5.1.3)

k_{0}(x,y)=H(x+y)\hat{H}(y-x)=H(x+y)\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{y-x}\Bigr).

For
\lambda>0
, we define

A_{0,\lambda}=\left(H(x)\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}(\xi)\right)^{w},

whose distribution-kernel is the
L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
function

k_{0,\lambda}(x,y)=H(x+y)e^{i\pi(x-y)\lambda}\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)\lambda)}{\pi(x-y)}.

We can thus notice that

(5.1.4)

k_{0,\lambda}(x,y)=\overbracket{H(x)H(y)e^{i\pi(x-y)\lambda}\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)\lambda)}{\pi(x-y)}}^{k_{0,\lambda}^{\flat}(x,y)}\\
+\underbracket{H(x+y)\bigl(H(-x)H(y)+H(x)H(-y)\bigr)\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)\lambda)}{\pi(x-y)}e^{i\pi(x-y)\lambda}}_{k_{0,\lambda}^{\sharp}(x,y)},

and the operator with distribution-kernel
k_{0,\lambda}^{\flat}
is

H{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}(\xi))}H,\text{\ that is\quad$H\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}(D)H$,}

where
H
stands for the operator of multiplication
by the Heaviside function
H
.
On the other hand, the operator with distribution kernel
k_{0,\lambda}^{\sharp}

is such that

|k_{0,\lambda}^{\sharp}(x,y)|\leq H(x+y)\frac{H(-x)H(y)+H(x)H(-y)}{\pi|x-y|}\\
=H(x+y)\frac{H(-x)H(y)}{\pi(y-x)}+H(x+y)\frac{H(x)H(-y)}{\pi(x-y)}.

According to
Proposition
9.30
in our Appendix,
the Hardy operator and the modified Hardy operators are bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

and
we obtain
that, for
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, with
H=H(x),\check{H}=H(-x)
,

(5.1.5)

\left|\iint H(x)\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}(\xi)W(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\right|\\
\leq\|H\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{2}\|H\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{2}\|\check{H}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

so that

(5.1.6)

|\langle A_{0}\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})}|\\
=\Bigl|\iint H(x)H(\xi)\overbracket{W(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)}^{\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}dxd\xi\Bigr|=\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}}\left|\iint H(x)\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}(\xi)W(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\right|\\
\leq\|H\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{2}\|H\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{2}\|\check{H}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|H\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

yielding the
L^{2}
-boundedness of the operator
A_{0}
, and this concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

That cumbersome detour with the operator
A_{0,\lambda}
is useful to ensure that the operator
A
is indeed bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The kernel
k_{0}

of
A_{0}
is a distribution of order 1 and the product
H(x)H(y)k_{0}(x,y)
is not a priori
meaningful, even when
k
is a Radon measure
14
14

14

Even a wave-front-set approach, which would allow the product

H(x)\text{pv}(1/(y-x))
, does not offer a meaning for the product

H(x)H(y)\text{pv}(1/(y-x))
since the wave-front-set
of
\text{pv}(1/(y-x))
is located on the conormal of the first diagonal (i.e.
\{(x,x;\xi,-\xi)\}_{x\in\mathbb{R},\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{*}}
),
whereas the wave-front set at
(0,0)
of
H(x)H(y)
contains all directions and in particular is antipodal to the conormal of the diagonal at
(0,0)
.

. However with the proven
L^{2}
-boundedness of
A_{0}
, the products of operators

HA_{0}H
,

\check{H}A_{0}H
,

HA_{0}\check{H}
,
\check{H}A_{0}\check{H}

make sense and for instance we may approximate in the strong-operator-topology the operator

HA_{0}H
by the operator

\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)A\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon),

where
\chi
is a smooth function supported in
[1,+{\infty})
and equal to
1
on
[2,+{\infty})
.
We have indeed

HAH=\bigl(H-\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)\bigr)AH+\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)A\bigl(H-\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)\bigr)+\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)A\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon),

so that for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

HAHu=\lim_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}}\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)A\chi(\cdot/\varepsilon)u.

The operator with kernel

H(x+y)\chi(x/\varepsilon)\chi(y/\varepsilon)\textrm{\rm pv}\frac{1}{i\pi(y-x)}=\chi(x/\varepsilon)\chi(y/\varepsilon)\text{\rm pv}\frac{1}{i\pi(y-x)},

converges strongly towards the operator

H(\operatorname{sign}D)H
.

Proposition 5.3
.

Let
A_{0}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}
be
the operator
with Weyl symbol

H(x)H(\xi)
, a priori sending
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
into

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
. Then
A_{0}
can be uniquely extended to a self-adjoint bounded operator
on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

with

(5.1.7)

\|A_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq\frac{1+\sqrt{2}}{2}\approx 1.207

N.B
.

The bound above can be significantly improved (see Proposition

5.30

for optimal bounds)
and moreover we will show below that the spectrum of
A_{0}
actually intersects
(1,+{\infty})
.
In fact it is easier to start with the information that
A_{0}
is indeed bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

The
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-boundedness of
A_{0}
is given by Lemma
5.1
.
We are left with proving the bound (
5.1.7
): we note that (
5.1.6
)
implies

|\langle A_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}+\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

proving the proposition, since the eigenvalues of the quadratic form
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{1}^{2}+x_{1}x_{2}

are
(1\pm\sqrt{2})/2
.
∎

We can do much better and actually diagonalize the operator
A_{0}
, using as in Proposition
9.30
logarithmic coordinates on each half-line.
We state a lemma on “diagonal” terms whose proof is already given above.

Lemma 5.4

(Diagonal terms)
.

Let
A_{0}
be the operator with Weyl symbol
H(x)H(\xi)
.
With
H
standing as well for the operator of multiplication by
H(x)
, we have

(5.1.8)

\displaystyle HA_{0}H=HH(D)H=H\frac{(\operatorname{Id}+\operatorname{sign}D)}{2}H.

Lemma 5.5

(Off-diagonal terms)
.

Let
B_{0}=2\operatorname{Re}\check{H}A_{0}H=\check{H}A_{0}H+HA_{0}\check{H}
.
Then we have for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

(5.1.9)

|\langle B_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Proof of the Lemma.

For
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that
0\notin\operatorname{supp}u
, we define for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.1.10)

\phi_{1}(t)=u(e^{t})e^{t/2},\quad\phi_{2}(t)=u(-e^{t})e^{t/2},

so that

(5.1.11)

\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},\quad\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\|\phi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

We have

\displaystyle\langle B_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\iint\frac{H(x+y)\bigl(\check{H}(x)H(y)+H(x)\check{H}(y)\bigr)}{2i\pi(y-x)}u(y)\bar{u}(x)dydx

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt=\iint\frac{H(-e^{s}+e^{t})e^{\frac{s+t}{2}}}{2i\pi(e^{t}+e^{s})}\phi_{1}(t)\bar{\phi}_{2}(s)dsdt-\iint\frac{H(e^{s}-e^{t})e^{\frac{s+t}{2}}}{2i\pi(e^{t}+e^{s})}\phi_{2}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dsdt

\displaystyle\hskip 22.0pt=\iint\frac{H(t-s)}{4i\pi\cosh(\frac{s-t}{2})}\phi_{1}(t)\bar{\phi}_{2}(s)dsdt-\iint\frac{H(s-t)}{4i\pi\cosh(\frac{s-t}{2})}\phi_{2}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dsdt

so that

(5.1.12)

\displaystyle\langle B_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle\tilde{S}_{0}\ast\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle S_{0}\ast\phi_{2},\phi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

(5.1.13)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{0}(t)=\frac{\check{H}(t)}{4i\pi\cosh(t/2)},\quad S_{0}(t)=\frac{iH(t)}{4\pi\cosh(t/2)}.

We calculate

\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{dt}{4\pi\cosh(t/2)}=\frac{1}{2\pi}[\arctan(\sinh(t/2))]_{0}^{+{\infty}}=\frac{1}{4}=\int_{-{\infty}}^{0}\frac{dt}{4\pi\cosh(t/2)},

so that

(5.1.14)

|\langle B_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi_{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{2}\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

proving the estimate of the lemma for
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that
0\notin\operatorname{supp}u
.
We use now that we already know that
B_{0}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
: let
u
be a function in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and let
(\phi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence
15
15

15

Such a sequence is easy to find: a first step is to find a sequence
(\tilde{\phi}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in the Schwartz space converging in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
towards
u
, then consider with a given
\omega\in C^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
such that
\omega(t)=0
for
|t|\leq 1
and
\omega(t)=1
for
|t|\geq 2
,

\phi_{k}(x)=\omega(kx)\tilde{\phi}_{k}(x).
in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that each
\phi_{k}
vanishes in a neighborhood of 0 so that
\lim_{k}\phi_{k}=u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
We find
that

|\langle B_{0}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq|\langle B_{0}(u-\phi_{k}),u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|+|\langle B_{0}\phi_{k},u-\phi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|+|\langle B_{0}\phi_{k},\phi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\\
\leq\|B_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\bigl(\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\|H\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

providing readily the result of the lemma since the multiplication by
H
and
\check{H}
are bounded operators on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
∎

Remark 5.6
.

The estimate (
5.1.9
) and Lemma
5.4
are already improving
(
5.1.7
),
since the eigenvalues of the quadratic form
\mathbb{R}^{2}\ni(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto x_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}

are
(2\pm\sqrt{5})/4
, so that the right-hand-side of (
5.1.7
) can be replaced by

(2+\sqrt{5})/4\approx 1.059
. Anyhow, we shall provide below a diagonalization of
A_{0}
and optimal bounds.

N.B
.

We shall be a little faster in the sequel on the “cumbersome” detours to avoid formal multiplication of kernels by Heaviside functions but the reader should keep in mind that it is an important point to secure
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
-boundedness
before
any further manipulation of the kernels.

5.1.2.
An isometric isomorphism

Remark 5.7
.

The mapping
\Psi
defined by

(5.1.15)

\begin{matrix}\Psi:\ L^{2}(\mathbb{R})&\longrightarrow&L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})\\
u&\mapsto&\Bigl((Hu)(e^{t})e^{t/2},(\check{H}u)(-e^{t})e^{t/2}\Bigr)\end{matrix}

is an isometric isomorphism of Hilbert spaces:
indeed we have

\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u(e^{t})|^{2}e^{t}dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u(-e^{t})|^{2}e^{t}dt.

Moreover if
(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
, we may define for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{*}

u(x)=H(x)\phi_{1}(\ln x)x^{-1/2}+\check{H}(x)\phi_{2}(\ln|x|)|x|^{-1/2},

and we have

\Psi(u)(t)=\bigl(\phi_{1}(t),\phi_{2}(t)\bigr).

Remark 5.8
.

Using Lemma
5.4
and Notations (
5.1.10
)
we see that

\displaystyle\langle HA_{0}Hu,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\iint\frac{1}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{e^{(s+t)/2}}{e^{t}-e^{s}}\phi_{1}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dsdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\iint\frac{1}{4i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{\sinh(\frac{t-s}{2})}\phi_{1}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dsdt

(5.1.16)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\tau)|^{2}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{0}(\tau)\bigr)d\tau,

with

(5.1.17)

T_{0}(t)=\frac{t}{4\sinh(t/2)}\text{pv}\frac{i}{\pi t}.

We have

(5.1.18)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{0}=\operatorname{sign}\ast\rho_{0},\quad\text{with\quad}\rho_{0}(\tau)=\int\frac{t}{4\sinh(t/2)}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt,

and we note that the function
\rho_{0}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
as the Fourier transform of a function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
Also we have

\int\rho_{0}(\tau)d\tau=\hat{\rho}_{0}(0)=\frac{1}{2},

and this yields with

\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{0}\right\}=2\rho_{0}

(which follows from (
5.1.18
))
and

(5.1.19)

\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{0}(\tau)=1-\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}2\rho_{0}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

since

\frac{d}{d\tau}\left\{\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{0}+\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}2\rho_{0}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\right\}=0\quad\text{and\quad}\lim_{\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}}(\operatorname{sign}\ast\rho_{0})(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
A_{0}
be the operator with Weyl symbol
H(x)H(\xi)
.
The operator
A_{0}
is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so that we may define,
with
\Psi
defined in (
5.1.15
),

(5.1.20)

\widetilde{A}_{0}=\Psi A_{0}\Psi^{-1}.

The operator
\widetilde{A}_{0}
is the Fourier multiplier on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
given by the matrix

(5.1.21)

\mathcal{M}_{0}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{0}(\tau)&\hat{S}_{0}(\tau)\\
\overline{\hat{S}_{0}(\tau)}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
T_{0},S_{0}
are defined respectively in
(
5.1.17
),
(
5.1.13
).
In particular we have
with
\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

(5.1.22)

\langle\widetilde{A}_{0}\Phi,\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2i\pi t\tau}\langle\mathcal{M}_{0}(\tau)\hat{\Phi}(\tau),\hat{\Phi}(\tau)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau.

Remark 5.10
.

As a consequence of Theorem
5.9
, we find that the spectrum of the self-adjoint bounded operator
A_{0}
is the closure of the set of eigenvalues of the matrices
\mathcal{M}_{0}(\tau)
when
\tau
runs on the real line.

Proof.

The proof follows readily from Remarks
5.7
,
5.8
and Lemmas

5.4
,

5.5
.
∎

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a
2\times 2
Hermitian matrix

\mathcal{N}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}&a_{12}\\
\overline{a_{12}}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Then the eigenvalues
\lambda_{-}\leq\lambda_{+}
of
\mathcal{N}
are such that

(5.1.23)

\lambda_{-}<0<1<\lambda_{+},

if and only if

(5.1.24)

a_{12}\not=0\text{ \hskip 10.22217ptand \hskip 10.22217pt}|a_{12}|^{2}>1-a_{11}.

Proof.

The characteristic polynomial of
\mathcal{N}

is
p(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}-a_{11}\lambda-|a_{12}|^{2}
and since
a_{11}
is real-valued,
has two real roots
\lambda_{-}\leq\lambda_{+}
. If (
5.1.24
) holds true, the roots are distinct and

p(0)=-|a_{12}|^{2}<0,\quad p(1)=1-a_{11}-|a_{12}|^{2}<0,

implying (
5.1.23
). Conversely, if (
5.1.23
) is satisfied, then
p(0),p(1)
are both negative, implying (
5.1.24
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.12
.

Let us define for
\omega\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.1.25)

I(\omega)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(t\omega)}{\cosh(t/2)}dt.

Then we have

(5.1.26)

I(\omega)=\frac{1}{4\pi\omega}+O(\omega^{-3}),\quad|\omega|\rightarrow+{\infty}.

Proof.

Indeed we have for
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
,

I(\omega)=-\frac{1}{4\pi\omega}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\frac{d}{dt}\cos(t\omega)}{\cosh(t/2)}dt=\frac{1}{4\pi\omega}\Bigl(1-\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos(t\omega)}{(\cosh(t/2))^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\sinh(t/2)dt\Bigr)\\
=\frac{1}{4\pi\omega}\bigl(1+g(\omega)\bigr),

with

\displaystyle g(\omega)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}{\frac{d}{\omega dt}\{\sin(t\omega)\}}{\operatorname{sech}(t/2)}\frac{1}{2}\tanh(t/2)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\omega}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\sin(t\omega)\frac{d}{dt}\bigl\{{\operatorname{sech}(t/2)}\tanh(t/2)\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\omega^{2}}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{d}{dt}\bigl\{\cos(t\omega)\bigr\}\frac{d}{dt}\bigl\{{\operatorname{sech}(t/2)}\tanh(t/2)\bigr\}dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\omega^{2}}\left\{\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\cos(t\omega)\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}\bigl\{{\operatorname{sech}(t/2)}\tanh(t/2)\bigr\}dt+\frac{1}{2}\right\}=O(\omega^{-2}),

proving the lemma.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

The matrix
\mathcal{M}_{0}(\tau)
defined in (
5.1.21
) is equal to

(5.1.27)

\mathcal{M}_{0}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(\tau)&a_{12}(\tau)\\
\overline{a_{12}(\tau)}&0\end{pmatrix}

with

(5.1.28)

1-a_{11}(\tau)=\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}2\rho_{0}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},\quad a_{12}(\tau)=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{1}{\cosh(t/2)}e^{-2i\pi\tau t}dt.

We have

(5.1.29)

\displaystyle 1-a_{11}(\tau)

\displaystyle=O(\tau^{-N})\quad\text{for any $N$ when $\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}$},

(5.1.30)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}(a_{12}(\tau))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}\tau}+O(\tau^{-3})\quad\text{when $\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}$.}

Proof.

Formulas (
5.1.27
), (
5.1.28
) follow from Theorem
5.9
,
(
5.1.19
) and (
5.1.13
).
The estimates (
5.1.29
) follow from the fact that
\rho_{0}
belongs to the Schwartz class
and (
5.1.30
) is a reformulation of Lemma
5.12
.
∎

Theorem 5.14
.

Let
A_{0}
be the operator with Weyl symbol
H(x)H(\xi)
, where
H
is the Heaviside function. Then
A_{0}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(5.1.31)

\inf\bigl(\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{0})\bigr)<0<1<\sup\bigl(\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{0})\bigr).

Proof.

Using Remark
5.10
and Proposition
5.13

we find that for
\tau
large enough, Conditions
(
5.1.24
) are satisfied, proving readily
(
5.1.31
).
∎

Corollary 5.15

(A counterexample to Flandrin’s conjecture)
.

There exists a function
\phi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
norm equal to 1 such that

(5.1.32)

\iint_{x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>1.

There exists
a>0
such that

\iint_{0\leq x\leq a,0\leq\xi\leq a}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>1.

Remark 5.16
.

On page 2178 of
[
13
]
,
we find the sentence

“it is conjectured that

(5.1.33)

\forall u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\iint_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

is true for any convex domain
\mathcal{C}
”
,
a quite mild commitment for the validity of (
5.1.33
),
although that statement was referred to later on as
Flandrin’s conjecture

in the literature.
The second part of the above corollary is providing a disproof of that conjecture based upon an “abstract” argument used in the proof of Theorem
5.14
; the result of that corollary was already known
via a numerical analysis argument
after our joint work
[
6
]
with B. Delourme and T. Duyckaerts.

Proof.

From Theorem
5.14
, we find
u_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle.

Let
\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
: we have

|\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle-\langle A_{0}\psi,\psi\rangle|=|\langle A_{0}(u_{0}-\psi),u_{0}\rangle+\langle A_{0}\psi,u_{0}-\psi\rangle|\\
\leq\|A_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\|u_{0}-\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigl(\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr),

and thus if
(\psi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is a sequence of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
converging towards
u_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we get

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle\\
\leq\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle+\underbrace{\|A_{0}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\|u_{0}-\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigl(\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr)}_{=\sigma_{k},\text{ goes to 0 when $k\rightarrow+{\infty}$.}}.

There exists
k_{0}\geq 1
such that for
k\geq k_{0}
, we have

0\leq\sigma_{k}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle-\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\bigr)=\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},\quad\varepsilon_{0}>0.

We obtain that for
k\geq k_{0}
,

\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle\leq\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},

and thus

\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=\underbrace{\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}-\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}}_{=\theta_{k},\text{ goes to 0 when $k\rightarrow+{\infty}$}}+\|u_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\\
=\theta_{k}+\langle A_{0}u_{0},u_{0}\rangle-\varepsilon_{0}\leq\theta_{k}+\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle+\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}\\
=\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle+\theta_{k}-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}.

Choosing now
k\geq k_{0}
and
k
large enough to have
\theta_{k}<\varepsilon_{0}/4
,
we get

\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\leq\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4}<\langle A_{0}\psi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle,

and since for
\tilde{\phi}=\psi_{k}
, the Wigner distribution
\mathcal{W}(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\phi})
belongs to

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
,
we have

\|\tilde{\phi}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}<\langle A_{0}\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\phi}\rangle=\iint H(x)H(\xi)\mathcal{W}(\tilde{\phi},\tilde{\phi})(x,\xi)dxd\xi,

and noting that this strict inequality above implies that
\tilde{\phi}\not=0
, we may set
\phi_{0}=\tilde{\phi}/\|\tilde{\phi}\|
and get
the first statement in the corollary.

N.B
.

The proof above is complicated by the fact that the identity

\langle a^{w}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}a(x,\xi)\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi,

is valid a priori for
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(and in that case
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
), but could be meaningless as a Lebesgue integral even for
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, since we shall have
\mathcal{W}(u,u)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
but not in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

(we shall see in Section
6
that generically the Wigner distribution of a pulse
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
does
not
belong to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
).

Since
\mathcal{W}(\phi,\phi)
belongs to the Schwartz space of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem provides the last statement in the Corollary.
∎

N.B
.

The reader will notice that the results
of the incoming Section
5.2
in the special case
\sigma=0

imply the results of Section
5.1
, which could be then erased, say at the second reading.
However, as far as the first – and maybe only – reading is concerned, we checked that most of the computational arguments in the next section are much more involved
and it seemed worth while to the author to avoid unnecessary complications
for the disproof of Flandrin’s conjecture
via the quarter-plane example and set apart the more involved examples of the hyperbolic regions tackled in Section
5.2
.

5.2.
Hyperbolic regions

We consider in this section the (
5.0.1
)
set
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}
with a non-negative
\sigma
.

5.2.1.
A preliminary observation

We want to consider the operator
A_{\sigma}

with Weyl symbol
H(x)H(x\xi-\sigma)

and as in Section
5.1.1
, we would like to secure the fact that
A_{\sigma}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Claim 5.17
.

For all
\sigma\geq 0
the operator
A_{\sigma}
is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof of the Claim.

Let us choose

(5.2.1)

\text{$\chi_{0}\in C^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])$ with }\begin{cases}\text{$\chi_{0}(t)=0$, for $t\leq 1$,}\\
\text{$\chi_{0}(t)=1$, for $t\geq 2$.}\end{cases}

For
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

(5.2.2)

\langle(A_{0}-A_{\sigma})\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})}=\iint H(x)H(\xi)H(\sigma-x\xi)\underbrace{\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)}_{\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}dxd\xi\\
=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}}\iint\chi_{0}(x/\epsilon)H(\xi)H(\sigma-x\xi)\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)dxd\xi.

The kernel
k_{\sigma,\epsilon}
of the operator with Weyl symbol
\chi_{0}(x/\epsilon)H(\xi)H(\sigma-x\xi)

is

(5.2.3)

\ell_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x,y)=\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2\epsilon}\bigr)e^{2i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{\pi(x-y)},

and we have

\displaystyle\iint\ell_{\sigma,\epsilon}(x,y)

\displaystyle\phi(y)\bar{\psi}(x)dydx=\iint\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2\epsilon}\bigr)e^{2i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{\pi(x-y)}\phi(y)\bar{\psi}(x)dxdy

\displaystyle=\iint\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)e^{2i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{\pi\epsilon(x-y)}\phi(\epsilon y)\bar{\psi}(\epsilon x)\epsilon^{2}dxdy

(5.2.4)

\displaystyle=\iint\underbrace{\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)e^{2i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{\pi(x-y)}}_{m_{\sigma}(x,y)}\underbrace{\phi(\epsilon y)\epsilon^{1/2}}_{\phi_{\epsilon}(y)}\underbrace{\bar{\psi}(\epsilon x)\epsilon^{1/2}}_{\bar{\psi}_{\epsilon}(x)}dydx.

We note that, assuming as we may that
\sigma>0
,

(5.2.5)

|m_{\sigma}(x,y)H(x)H(y)|=\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\left|\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y}}\right|\frac{2\sigma H(x)H(y)}{x+y}\\
\leq\frac{2\sigma H(x)H(y)}{x+y},

and

(5.2.6)

|m_{\sigma}(x,y)\check{H}(x)H(y)|=\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\left|\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{{\pi(x-y)}}\right|{\check{H}(x)H(y)}\\
\leq\frac{\check{H}(x)H(y)}{\pi(y-x)},

as well as

(5.2.7)

|m_{\sigma}(x,y)\check{H}(y)H(x)|=\chi_{0}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\left|\frac{\sin(\frac{2\pi\sigma(x-y)}{x+y})}{{\pi(x-y)}}\right|{\check{H}(y)H(x)}\\
\leq\frac{\check{H}(y)H(x)}{\pi(x-y)}.

As a consequence, since we have also

m_{\sigma}(x,y)\check{H}(x)\check{H}(y)\equiv 0,

the inequalities (
5.2.5
), (
5.2.6
), (
5.2.7
),
the identities
(
5.2.4
), (
5.2.2
) and
Proposition
9.30

imply that

|\langle(A_{0}-A_{\sigma})\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{*}(\mathbb{R}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq 2\pi\sigma\underbrace{\|H\phi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}_{\|H\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\|H\psi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\underbrace{\|\check{H}\phi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}_{\|\check{H}\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}}\|H\psi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
+\|H\phi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\check{H}\psi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

proving that

A_{0}-A_{\sigma}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
; with Proposition
5.3
, this implies that

A_{\sigma}
is also bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, proving the claim.
∎

N.B
.

With that important piece of information in Claim
5.17
, we shall be less strict in our manipulations of the kernels and accept below some abuse of language in these matters.

The Weyl quantization of
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}}
has the kernel

(5.2.8)

k_{\sigma}(x,y)=H(x+y)e^{4i\pi\sigma(\frac{x-y}{x+y})}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{y-x}\Bigr),

a formula to be compared to (
5.1.3
).
Using the Schwartz function
\phi_{0}
of Corollary
5.15
, we get from the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem that for
\sigma
small enough,

(5.2.9)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}})}\phi_{0},\phi_{0}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\iint_{x\xi\geq\sigma,x>0}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>1.

However, this argument does not work for large positive
\sigma
and we must go back to a direct calculation.

5.2.2.
Diagonal terms

Denoting by
A_{\sigma}
the operator with kernel (
5.2.8
)
(and Weyl symbol
H(x\xi-\sigma)H(x)
),
we find that for

u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
u_{+}=Hu
,
we have

\displaystyle\langle A_{\sigma}Hu,Hu\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{4i\pi\sigma(\frac{x-y}{x+y})}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{y-x}\Bigr)u_{+}(y)\bar{u}_{+}(x)dydx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|u_{+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{4i\pi\sigma(\frac{e^{s}-e^{t}}{e^{s}+e^{t}})}\frac{1}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{e^{t}-e^{s}}u_{+}(e^{t})\bar{u}_{+}(e^{s})e^{s+t}dsdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|u_{+}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{s-t}{2})}\frac{1}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{e^{(s+t)/2}}{e^{t}-e^{s}}\phi_{1}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dsdt,

with

(5.2.10)

\phi_{1}(t)=u_{+}(e^{t})e^{t/2},\qquad\text{so that
$\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\|u_{+}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}.$}

We get

\langle A_{\sigma}Hu,Hu\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\frac{1}{2}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})}+\frac{1}{4i\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{s-t}{2})}}{\sinh(\frac{t-s}{2})}\phi(t)\bar{\phi}(s)dsdt,

and noting that

\sinh x=xC(x),

with
C
even such that
1/C\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
we find

\displaystyle\langle A_{\sigma}Hu,Hu\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{s-t}{2})}}{(s-t)C(\frac{s-t}{2})}\phi(t)\bar{\phi}(s)dsdt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\|\phi_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle T_{\sigma}\ast\phi_{1},\phi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

(5.2.11)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\hat{\phi}_{1}(\tau)|^{2}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)\bigr)d\tau,

with

(5.2.12)

T_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{1}{4}\frac{te^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}\text{pv}{\frac{i}{\pi t}}.

We note that

(5.2.13)

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\operatorname{sign}\ast\rho_{\sigma},\quad\text{with}

(5.2.14)

\displaystyle\rho_{\sigma}(\tau)=\frac{1}{4}\int\frac{te^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt,\quad\rho_{\sigma}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}),

since the function
\mathbb{R}\ni t\mapsto\frac{te^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}
belongs to the Schwartz space
16
16

16

Indeed, the iterated derivatives of
\tanh
are polynomials of
\tanh
(check this by induction on the order of derivatives) and thus bounded on the real line; since the function
t\mapsto t/\sinh(t/2)
belongs to the Schwartz space, this proves that the above product is in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
.
.
Note also that the function
\rho_{\sigma}
is real-valued on the real line.
This entails that

(5.2.15)

\frac{d}{d\tau}\Bigl\{\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}\Bigr\}=2\rho_{\sigma},

and since

\rho_{\sigma}(\tau)=\frac{1}{4}\mathcal{F}\Bigl\{t\mapsto\frac{te^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(t/2)}}{\sinh({t}/2)}\Bigr\},\quad\text{implying }\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\rho_{\sigma}(\tau)d\tau=\frac{1}{2},

we get that

(5.2.16)

\lim_{\tau\rightarrow\pm{\infty}}\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\pm\frac{1}{2}.

This yields that

(5.2.17)

\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)-1=\int_{+{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}=-1+\int_{-{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

where the last equality follows from (
5.2.16
): indeed we have for
\tau>0
, from (
5.2.15
),

(5.2.18)

\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)-1=\int_{+{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}=-1+\int_{-{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime},

and for
\tau<0
,

\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\int_{-{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}=1+\int_{+{\infty}}^{\tau}2\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}.

We note that

(5.2.19)

\forall N\in\mathbb{N},\quad\sup_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}|\tau|^{N}\left|\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)-H(\tau)\right|<+{\infty}.

Indeed for
\tau>0
, we have, using
\rho_{\sigma}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,

\left|\tau^{N}\int_{+{\infty}}^{\tau}\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\right|\leq\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})|{\tau^{\prime}}^{N}d\tau^{\prime}\leq\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})|{\tau^{\prime}}^{N}d\tau<+{\infty}.

Also, for
\tau<0
,
we have

\left|\tau^{N}\int_{-{\infty}}^{\tau}\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau^{\prime}\right|\leq\int_{-{\infty}}^{\tau}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})||\tau^{\prime}|^{N}d\tau^{\prime}\leq\int_{-{\infty}}^{0}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})||\tau^{\prime}|^{N}d\tau<+{\infty}.

This means that the Fourier multiplier
\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)
is somehow “exponentially close” to
H(\tau)
for large values of
|\tau|
and in particular close to 1 for large positive values of
\tau
.
We have also

(5.2.20)

\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2i\pi\tau t}\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\\
=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt.

The next lemma provides more precise estimates than (
5.2.19
).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\tau>0,\sigma\geq 0
.
Defining
a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)
as given by (
5.2.12
), we have

(5.2.21)

|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|\leq 2e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}}e^{4\pi\sigma}.

Proof.

Using (
5.2.18
) and Lemma
9.33
, we find that for
\tau>0
,

|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|\leq 2\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})|d\tau^{\prime}\leq 2\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}|\rho_{\sigma}(\tau^{\prime})|d\tau^{\prime}\\
\leq 12e^{4\pi\sigma}\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau^{\prime}}}d\tau^{\prime}=e^{4\pi\sigma}\frac{12}{\pi^{2}}e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}},

entailing the sought result.
∎

5.2.3.
Off-diagonal terms

We want now to check the off-diagonal terms:
we have with
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,

(5.2.22)

\displaystyle u_{+}=Hu,\ u_{-}=\check{H}u,

(5.2.23)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(t)=u_{+}(e^{t})e^{t/2},\quad\phi_{2}(t)=u_{-}(-e^{t})e^{t/2}

\displaystyle\langle A_{\sigma}\check{H}u,Hu\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}

\displaystyle=\iint e^{4i\pi\sigma(\frac{x-y}{x+y})}\frac{H(x+y)\check{H}(y)H(x)}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{y-x}u_{-}(y)\bar{u}_{+}(x)dydx

\displaystyle=\iint e^{4i\pi\sigma(\frac{e^{s}+e^{t}}{e^{s}-e^{t}})}\frac{H(e^{s}-e^{t})}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{-e^{t}-e^{s}}\phi_{2}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)e^{\frac{t+s}{2}}dtds

\displaystyle=\iint e^{4i\pi\sigma\text{coth}(\frac{s-t}{2})}\ \frac{iH(s-t)}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\cosh(\frac{t-s}{2})}\phi_{2}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dtds

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4\pi}\iint e^{4i\pi\sigma\text{coth}(\frac{s-t}{2})}\ {H(s-t)}\frac{1}{\cosh(\frac{s-t}{2})}\phi_{2}(t)\bar{\phi}_{1}(s)dtds

(5.2.24)

\displaystyle=\langle S_{\sigma}\ast\phi_{2},\phi_{1}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

with

(5.2.25)

S_{\sigma}(t)=\frac{i}{4\pi}H(t)\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\text{coth}(\frac{t}{2})}}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})},

and

(5.2.26)

\hat{S}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int H(t)\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\text{coth}(\frac{t}{2})}}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt\\
=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos(4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2)-2\pi t\tau)}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})}dt-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2)-2\pi t\tau)}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})}dt\\
=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2))}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})}dt+\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2))}{\cosh({t}/2)}dt.

Note that from (
5.2.12
), (
5.2.14
), we have

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\frac{i}{4\pi}\int\frac{e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt.

5.2.4.
An isometric isomorphism

Theorem 5.19
.

Let
\sigma\geq 0
be given, let
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}
be the set defined by (
5.0.1
) and let
A_{\sigma}
be the operator with Weyl symbol
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}}
,
(whose kernel is given by (
5.2.8
)).
The operator
A_{\sigma}
is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so that we may define,
with
\Psi
defined in (
5.1.15
),

(5.2.27)

\widetilde{A}_{\sigma}=\Psi A_{\sigma}\Psi^{-1}.

The operator
\widetilde{A}_{\sigma}
is the Fourier multiplier on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
given by the matrix

(5.2.28)

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}+\hat{T}_{\sigma}(\tau)&\hat{S}_{\sigma}(\tau)\\
\overline{\hat{S}_{\sigma}(\tau)}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
T_{\sigma},S_{\sigma}
are defined respectively in (
5.2.12
),
(
5.2.20
),
(
5.2.25
).
In particular we have
with
\Phi=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})
,

(5.2.29)

\langle\widetilde{A}_{\sigma}\Phi,\Phi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C}^{2})}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2i\pi t\tau}\langle\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\tau)\hat{\Phi}(\tau),\hat{\Phi}(\tau)\rangle_{\mathbb{C}^{2}}d\tau.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\text{kernel}(HA_{\sigma}H)

\displaystyle=e^{4i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}H(x)H(y)\hat{H}(y-x),

\displaystyle\text{kernel}(\check{H}A_{\sigma}H+HA_{\sigma}\check{H})

\displaystyle=e^{4i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}H(x+y)\bigl(\check{H}(x)H(y)+H(x)\check{H}(y)\bigr)\frac{1}{2i\pi(y-x)},

\displaystyle\check{H}A_{\sigma}\check{H}

\displaystyle=0.

Proposition
9.30
in our Appendix is readily giving the
L^{2}
-boundedness (and self-adjointness) of
\check{H}A_{\sigma}H+HA_{\sigma}\check{H}
. We find also that
HA_{\sigma}H-\frac{H}{2}
has kernel

e^{4i\pi\sigma\frac{x-y}{x+y}}H(x)H(y)\frac{1}{2i\pi(y-x)},

and thus it is enough to study the operator with kernel

e^{4i\pi\sigma\frac{e^{s}-e^{t}}{e^{s}+e^{t}}}\frac{e^{\frac{s+t}{2}}}{2i\pi(e^{t}-e^{s})}=e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{s-t}{2})}\frac{1}{4i\pi\sinh(\frac{t-s}{2})},

which is a convolution operator by

T_{\sigma}(t)=e^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}\frac{t}{4\sinh(\frac{t}{2})}\text{pv}\frac{i}{\pi t},

given by (
5.2.12
). Formula (
5.2.14
) implies in particular that
\hat{T}_{\sigma}
is bounded (and real-valued) on the real line, entailing eventually the boundedness and self-adjointness of
A_{\sigma}
.
Formulas (
5.2.11
), (
5.2.24
) and (
5.2.25
) are providing (
5.2.29
),
completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.2.5.
The main result on hyperbolic regions

Theorem 5.20
.

Let
\sigma\geq 0
be given and let
A_{\sigma}
be the operator defined in Theorem
5.19
.
Then
A_{\sigma}
is a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(5.2.30)

\inf\bigl(\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{\sigma})\bigr)<0<1<\sup\bigl(\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{\sigma})\bigr).

The spectrum of
A_{\sigma}
is the closure of the set of eigenvalues of
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\tau)
for
\tau
running on the real line.

Remark 5.21
.

It is enough to prove that, with a given
\sigma\geq 0
,
there exists
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\tau)
satisfies (
5.1.24
).

Proof.

We have from (
5.2.28
), (
5.2.20
), (
5.2.26
),

(5.2.31)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt&\cdot&\frac{i}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{e^{{-2i\pi}(t\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\cosh({t}/2)}dt\\
\frac{1}{4i\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\cosh({t}/2)}dt&\cdot&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)&a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)\\
a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)&a_{22}(\tau,\sigma)\end{pmatrix}.

On the other hand we have

(5.2.32)

\overline{a_{12}}=a_{21}=\frac{1}{4i\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\cosh({t}/2)}dt,

so that

(5.2.33)

\operatorname{Re}a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin[{{2\pi}(t\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh({\frac{t}{2}})})]}}{\cosh(\frac{t}{2})}dt.

We note that the function

t\mapsto\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\cosh({t}/2)},

is holomorphic on
\mathbb{C}\backslash i\pi\mathbb{Z}
, with
simple poles at

(2\mathbb{Z}+1)i\pi
(zeroes of
\cosh(t/2)
) and essential singularities at
2\mathbb{Z}i\pi

(zeroes of
\sinh(t/2)
).
We shall need a more explicit quantitative expression for
a_{21}
to obtain a precise asymptotic result which could be compared to the estimate (
5.2.21
).
The next lemma is proven in
[
55
]
; we provide a proof here for the convenience of the reader.

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
\tau>0,\sigma\geq 0
be given and let
a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)
be given by
(
5.2.32
). We have

(5.2.34)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\biggl\{\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}-1}{\sin(t/2)}+\frac{\sinh(t/2)-\sin(t/2)}{\sinh(t/2)\sin(t/2)}\Bigr)dt

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0pt+\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{1-\cos 2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt

\displaystyle\hskip 110.0pt-\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos 2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\biggr\}.

Proof of Lemma
5.22
.

Let
0<\epsilon<\pi/2<\pi<R
be given. We consider the closed path
\gamma_{\epsilon,R}

of
\mathbb{C}\backslash i\pi\mathbb{Z}
with
\text{index}_{\gamma_{\epsilon,R}}(i\pi\mathbb{Z})\equiv 0
,

(5.2.35)

\gamma_{\epsilon,R}=[\epsilon,R]\cup[R,R+i\pi]\cup[R+i\pi,\epsilon+i\pi]\\
\cup\{i\pi+\epsilon e^{i\theta}\}_{0\geq\theta\geq-\pi/2}\cup i[\pi-\epsilon,\epsilon]\cup\{\epsilon e^{i\theta}\}_{\pi/2\geq\theta\geq 0},

and we have

(5.2.36)

\oint_{\gamma_{\epsilon,R}}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(z\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(z/2)})}}{\cosh({z}/2)}dz=0.

We note as well that

(5.2.37)

I_{2}=\oint_{[R,R+i\pi]}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(z\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(z/2)})}}{\cosh({z}/2)}dz=i\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}((R+it)\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(\frac{R+it}{2})})}}{\cosh(\frac{R+it}{2})}dt\\
=ie^{2i\pi R\tau}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{-2\pi t\tau}e^{-4i\pi\sigma\frac{1+e^{-R-it}}{1-e^{-R-it}}}\frac{2dt}{e^{\frac{R+it}{2}}(1+e^{-R-it})},

so that

|I_{2}|\leq 2e^{-R/2}\int_{0}^{\pi}e^{4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}\left(\frac{1+e^{-R-it}}{1-e^{-R-it}}\right)}\frac{dt}{|1-e^{-R}|},

and since

\operatorname{Im}\left(\frac{1+e^{-R-it}}{1-e^{-R-it}}\right)=\operatorname{Im}\frac{(1+e^{-R-it})(1-e^{-R+it})}{|1-e^{-R-it}|^{2}}=\frac{-2e^{-R}\sin t}{|1-e^{-R-it}|^{2}}\leq 0,

we get

(5.2.38)

|I_{2}|\leq e^{-R/2}\frac{2\pi}{1-e^{-R}},\quad\text{where $I_{2}$ is defined in \eqref{I22222}}.

Let us now check
17
17

17

Let us note for future reference the standard formulas

(5.2.39)

\cosh\bigl(\frac{i\pi}{2}+z\bigr)=i\sinh z,\quad\sinh\bigl(\frac{i\pi}{2}+z\bigr)=i\cosh z,\quad\tanh\bigl(\frac{i\pi}{2}+z\bigr)=\operatorname{coth}z.

(5.2.40)

I_{4}=-\int_{-\pi/2}^{0}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}((i\pi+\epsilon e^{i\theta})\tau-{2\sigma}{\operatorname{coth}(\frac{i\pi+\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2})})}}{\cosh\frac{i\pi+\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2}}i\epsilon e^{i\theta}d\theta\\
=-e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\int_{-\pi/2}^{0}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}\left(\epsilon e^{i\theta}\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2})}\right)}}{i\sinh\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2}}i\epsilon e^{i\theta}d\theta,

and since

\left|\frac{e^{{2i\pi}\left(\epsilon e^{i\theta}\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2})}\right)}}{i\sinh\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2}}i\epsilon e^{i\theta}\right|\leq 2\max_{|z|\leq\pi/2}|\frac{z}{\sinh z}|e^{\pi^{2}\tau}e^{4\pi\sigma\sup_{|z|\leq\pi/4}\left|\frac{\sinh z}{\cosh z}\right|},

the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem gives

(5.2.41)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}}I_{4}=-\pi e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}.

Defining now

(5.2.42)

I_{6}=-\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(\epsilon e^{i\theta}\tau-{2\sigma}{\operatorname{coth}(\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2})})}}{\cosh\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2}}i\epsilon e^{i\theta}d\theta,

and noting that

\displaystyle 4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}

\displaystyle\operatorname{coth}(\frac{\epsilon e^{i\theta}}{2})=4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}\frac{1+e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}}{1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}}=4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}\frac{(1+e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}})(1-e^{-\epsilon e^{-i\theta}})}{|1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}\frac{e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}-e^{-\epsilon e^{-i\theta}}}{|1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}|^{2}}=4\pi\sigma\operatorname{Im}\frac{e^{-\epsilon\cos\theta}(e^{-i\epsilon\sin\theta}-e^{i\epsilon\sin\theta})}{|1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}|^{2}}

\displaystyle=4\pi\sigma e^{-\epsilon\cos\theta}\operatorname{Im}\frac{(-2i)\sin(\epsilon\sin\theta)}{|1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}|^{2}}=-4\pi\sigma e^{-\epsilon\cos\theta}\frac{2\sin(\epsilon\sin\theta)}{|1-e^{-\epsilon e^{i\theta}}|^{2}}\leq 0,

we get that

|I_{6}|\leq\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{e^{-2\pi\epsilon\tau\sin\theta}}{\min_{|z|\leq\pi/4}|\cosh z|}d\theta\epsilon\leq\epsilon\frac{\pi/2}{\min_{|z|\leq\pi/4}|\cosh z|},

entailing

(5.2.43)

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}}I_{6}=0.

With

(5.2.44)

I_{1}=\oint_{[\epsilon,R]}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(z\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(z/2)})}}{\cosh({z}/2)}dz,

we have from (
5.2.32
)

(5.2.45)

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}\\
R\rightarrow+{\infty}\end{subarray}}I_{1}=4i\pi a_{21}.

We define now

\displaystyle I_{5}=-\oint_{[i\epsilon,i(\pi-\epsilon)]}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(z\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(z/2)})}}{\cosh({z}/2)}dz=-\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(it\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(it/2)})}}{\cosh({it}/2)}idt

\displaystyle=-\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{-2\pi t\tau}\frac{e^{\frac{-4i\pi\sigma}{i\tan(t/2)}}}{\cos({t}/2)}idt=-i\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{-2\pi t\tau}\frac{e^{\frac{-4\pi\sigma}{\tan(t/2)}}}{\cos({t}/2)}dt

\displaystyle=-i\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{-2\pi(\pi-s)\tau}\frac{e^{-\frac{4\pi\sigma}{\tan((\pi-s)/2)}}}{\cos({(\pi-s)}/2)}ds=-ie^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{2\pi s\tau}\frac{e^{-\frac{4\pi\sigma\sin(s/2)}{\cos(s/2)}}}{\sin({s}/2)}ds,

so that

(5.2.46)

\displaystyle I_{5}=

\displaystyle-ie^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{2\pi s\tau}\frac{e^{-{4\pi\sigma\tan(s/2)}{}}}{\sin({s}/2)}ds.

We have also

(5.2.47)

I_{3}=\oint_{[R+i\pi,\epsilon+i\pi]}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(z\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh(z/2)})}}{\cosh({z}/2)}dz=-\int_{\epsilon}^{R}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}((t+i\pi)\tau-\frac{2\sigma}{\tanh((t+i\pi)/2)})}}{\cosh({(t+i\pi)}/2)}dt,

so that using Formulas (
5.2.39
), we get

I_{3}=-e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\int_{\epsilon}^{R}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{i\sinh(t/2)}dt,

and

(5.2.48)

I_{3}+I_{5}=ie^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\left(\int_{\epsilon}^{R}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt-\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}e^{2\pi t\tau}\frac{e^{-{4\pi\sigma\tan(t/2)}{}}}{\sin({t}/2)}dt\right)\\
=ie^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\bigg\{\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi-\epsilon}\Bigl(\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}-\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}}{\sin({t}/2)}\Bigr)dt\\
+\int_{\pi-\epsilon}^{R}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\biggr\}.

From (
5.2.36
), (
5.2.35
), (
5.2.37
), (
5.2.40
),
(
5.2.42
), (
5.2.44
), (
),
(
5.2.47
), we find that

I_{1}=-I_{2}-(I_{3}+I_{5})-I_{4}-I_{6},

so that taking the limit of both sides
18
18

18

I_{1},I_{2},I_{4},I_{6},I_{3}+I_{5}
do have limits when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+},R\rightarrow+{\infty}
.
when
\epsilon\rightarrow 0_{+},R\rightarrow+{\infty}

we get, thanks to
(
5.2.45
), (
5.2.38
), (
5.2.48
),
(
5.2.41
), (
5.2.43
),

(5.2.49)

4i\pi a_{21}=\\
-ie^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\bigg\{\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}-\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}}{\sin({t}/2)}\Bigr)dt+\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\biggr\}\\
+\pi e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau},

implying that

a_{21}=\\
\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\bigg\{\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(-\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}+\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}}{\sin({t}/2)}\Bigr)dt-\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{e^{{2i\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\biggr\}\\
-\frac{i}{4}e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}

that is

(5.2.50)

a_{21}=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}}{\sin({t}/2)}-\frac{\cos{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}\Bigr)dt\\
-\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\\
-i\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt-\frac{i}{4}e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}\\
-i\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt,

yielding

(5.2.51)

\operatorname{Re}a_{21}=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\Bigl(\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}}{\sin({t}/2)}-\frac{\cos{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}\Bigr)dt\\
-\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt,

completing the proof of Lemma
5.22
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

Formula (
5.2.50
) also yields

\operatorname{Im}a_{12}=-\operatorname{Im}a_{21}=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\Bigl\{\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt+\pi\\
+\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin{{2\pi}(t\tau-{2\sigma}{\tanh(t/2)})}}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\Bigr\},

and since from (
5.2.31
), we have

(5.2.52)

a_{11}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin(2\pi t\tau-4\pi\sigma\tanh(t/2))}{\sinh(t/2)}dt,

this gives

(5.2.53)

\operatorname{Im}a_{12}=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\bigl(2\pi(a_{11}-\frac{1}{2})+\pi\bigr)=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{2}a_{11}.
