To complete the proof of Theorem
5.20
, it will be enough, according to Lemma

5.11
, to prove that, for
\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}
,

|a_{12}|^{2}\gg 1-a_{11}.

To achieve that, we note from (
5.2.53
) that the imaginary part of
a_{12}
is useless and we shall prove simply that

{(\operatorname{Re}a_{12})}^{2}\gg 1-a_{11}.

To get this we are going to use (
5.2.21
) and a precise asymptotic behavior for

{(\operatorname{Re}a_{12})}^{2}
displayed in the next lemma and issued from the explicit formula
(
5.2.34
).

Lemma 5.24
.

Let
\tau\geq 1,\sigma\geq 0
be given and let
a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)
be given by
(
5.2.32
). We have then

(5.2.54)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{8\pi^{3}\tau}-\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}.

Proof of the lemma.

Since for
t\geq 0
we have

\sinh(t/2)-\sin(t/2)\geq 0
,
we get from (
5.2.34
),

(5.2.55)

\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\biggl\{\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}-1}{\sin(t/2)}dt-\int_{\pi}^{+{\infty}}\frac{1}{\sinh(t/2)}dt\biggr\}\\
=\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{e^{2\pi(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))}-1}{\sin(t/2)}dt-\frac{e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}}{2\pi}\ln\bigl(\operatorname{coth}\frac{\pi}{4}\bigr).

Let us define

(5.2.56)

\omega=2\pi\tau,\quad\kappa=2\pi\sigma,\quad\nu=\kappa^{1/2}\omega^{-1/2},\quad\phi_{\nu}(s)=s-\nu^{2}\tan s.

We have

2\pi\bigl(t\tau-2\sigma\tan(t/2))=2\pi\tau\bigl(t-2\nu^{2}\tan(t/2)\bigr)=4\pi\tau\bigl(\frac{t}{2}-\nu^{2}\tan\frac{t}{2}\bigr)=2\omega\phi_{\nu}(t/2).

We have thus

(5.2.57)

\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{e^{2\omega\phi_{\nu}(s)}-1}{\sin s}ds-\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{2\pi}\underbrace{\ln\bigl(\operatorname{coth}\frac{\pi}{4}\bigr)}_{\approx 0.421908}.

Defining

(5.2.58)

\psi_{\nu}(\omega)=\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{e^{2\omega\phi_{\nu}(s)}-1}{\sin s}ds,

we can use (
5.2.56
), (
5.2.57
) and (
9.6.22
) to get
whenever
\tau>0
,

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{\pi^{2}\tau}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{4\tau}\Bigr)-e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau},

so that for
\tau\geq 1
we find

(5.2.59)

\displaystyle 2\pi\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{4\pi^{2}\tau}-e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau},

yielding the lemma.
∎

We eventually go back to the proof of Theorem
5.20
: let
\sigma>0
be given.
From Lemma
5.24
and (
5.2.21
),
we have for
\tau\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|\leq 2e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}}e^{4\pi\sigma},

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{8\pi^{3}\tau}-\frac{1}{2\pi}e^{-2\pi^{2}\tau}=\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{8\pi^{3}\tau}\Bigl(1-\frac{4\pi^{2}\tau e^{8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{e^{2\pi^{2}\tau}}\Bigr).

This entails that for
\tau\geq\tau_{0}(\sigma)
, we have

(5.2.60)

\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)\geq\frac{e^{-8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{16\pi^{3}\tau},

and thus
a_{21}\not=0
and

(5.2.61)

|a_{21}(\sigma,\tau)|^{2}\geq\frac{e^{-16\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{2^{8}\pi^{6}\tau^{2}}>|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|,

where the last inequality above holds true (thanks to (
5.2.21
)) whenever

2e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}}e^{4\pi\sigma}<\frac{e^{-16\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{2^{8}\pi^{6}\tau^{2}},

which is indeed true for
\tau\geq\tau_{1}(\sigma)
.
As a result for
\tau\geq\max(4\sigma,4,\tau_{0}(\sigma),\tau_{1}(\sigma))
,
we obtain that (
5.2.61
) is satisfied so that Remark
5.21

implies the result of Theorem
5.20
, completing our proof.
∎

Remark 5.25
.

The functions
\tau_{0}(\sigma),\tau_{1}(\sigma)
can be determined rather easily, the first one by the condition

\tau\geq\tau_{0}(\sigma)\Longrightarrow\frac{4\pi^{2}\tau e^{8\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{e^{2\pi^{2}\tau}}\leq\frac{1}{2},

whereas the second one should satisfy

\tau\geq\tau_{1}(\sigma)\Longrightarrow e^{4\pi\sigma}2^{9}\pi^{6}\tau^{2}e^{16\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}<e^{{\pi^{2}\tau}}.

5.3.
Comments and further results

5.3.1.
Qualitative explanations on the various computations

We would like to go back to our proofs that

(5.3.1)

|a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)|^{2}\gg|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|,\quad\tau\rightarrow+{\infty},

which is our key argument via Lemma
5.11
and give a couple of qualitative explanations which may enlighten the calculations. It is of course much simpler to begin with the case
\sigma=0
: in that case, according to Proposition
5.13
and (
5.1.18
),
we have

(5.3.2)

1-a_{11}(\tau,0)=\int_{\tau}^{+{\infty}}2\rho_{0}(\tau^{\prime})d\tau,\quad 2\rho_{0}(\tau)=\int\underbrace{\left(\frac{t/2}{\sinh(t/2)}\right)}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=f_{0}(t),\ \text{$f_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})$}\\
\text{holomorphic }\\
\text{on $|\operatorname{Im}t|<2\pi.$}\end{subarray}}e^{-2i\pi t\tau}ds,

so that

2\rho_{0}(\tau)=\hat{f}_{0}(\tau).

We get thus readily that
\rho_{0}
belongs to the Schwartz space, as the Fourier transform
of a function in the Schwartz space and this implies in particular that
1-a_{11}(\tau,0)
has fast decay towards 0 when
\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}
, as proven in Proposition
5.13
.
We note also that (
5.2.53
)
gives

\operatorname{Im}a_{12}(\tau,0)^{2}={e^{-4\pi^{2}\tau}}a_{11}(\tau,0)^{2}/4,

and since the limit of
a_{11}
is 1, we do not expect any help from the imaginary part of
a_{12}
to proving (
5.3.1
).
Turning our attention to
\operatorname{Re}a_{12}
in (
5.1.30
),
we have,

(5.3.3)

4\pi\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,0)=\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\sin{(2\pi t\tau)}}{\cosh({t}/2)}dt,

which is the sine-Fourier transform of the function
t\mapsto H(t)\operatorname{sech}(t/2)=g_{0}(t)
, which has a singularity at
t=0
: as a consequence, thanks to Lemma
9.1
, the Fourier transform

\widehat{g_{0}}
cannot be rapidly decreasing, cannot even belong to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
(that would imply that
g_{0}
is continuous). Moreover the sine-Fourier transform above is the Fourier transform of the odd part of
g_{0}
,
g_{\text{odd}}(t)=\operatorname{sech}(t/2)\operatorname{sign}t
, which is also singular at 0,
thus
\widehat{g_{\text{odd}}}
cannot be rapidly decreasing and is an odd function,
which is enough to prove, without more calculations, that (
5.3.1
) holds true.
In Section
5.1
, we used a more explicit argument, with providing an equivalent of (
5.3.3
) equal to
1/(2\pi\tau)
near
+{\infty}
.
Summing-up, (
5.3.1
) in the case
\sigma=0

follows from the existence of a singularity of the function
g_{0}
above, which is discontinuous at 0.

Let us now take a look at the case
\sigma>0
, which turns out to be more
computationally involved.
We have from (
5.2.32
)

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle 4\pi i{a_{21}}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}H(t)\operatorname{sech}(t/2)e^{-i4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2)}e^{2i\pi t\tau}dt=\check{\widehat{g_{\sigma}}}(\tau),

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle g_{\sigma}(t)

\displaystyle=H(t)\operatorname{sech}(t/2)e^{-i4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(t/2)}.

The single discontinuity at
t=0
of
g_{\sigma}
when
\sigma>0
is much wilder than for
\sigma=0
: in the latter case, we had only a jump discontinuity with different limits on both sides,
whereas when
\sigma>0
, we have an essential discontinuity with an oscillatory behaviour in
(-1,+1)
when
t\rightarrow 0_{+}
for the real and imaginary parts of
a_{12}
.
However,
g_{\sigma}
belongs to all
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),p\in[1,+{\infty}]
, so that its Fourier transform
belongs to
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}),p\in[2,+{\infty}]
:
we expect then that both sides of (
5.3.1
) have limit
0
for
\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}

and we must prove that

1-a_{11}
decays much faster than
a_{12}
.
Looking at a slightly simplified model and using the notations (
5.2.56
),
we define for
\omega,\nu
positive, a function
\alpha
presumably close to
4\pi ia_{21}
,
given by

(5.3.6)

\alpha(\omega,\nu)=\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{i2\omega\mu_{\nu}(s)}\operatorname{sech}(s)ds,\quad\mu_{\nu}(s)=s-\frac{\nu^{2}}{s},\quad\mu^{\prime}_{\nu}(s)=1+\frac{\nu^{2}}{s^{2}}.

Trying our hand with the stationary phase method, we look at

\alpha(\omega,\nu)=\frac{1}{2i\omega}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{e^{i2\omega\mu_{\nu}(s)}\right\}\frac{\operatorname{sech}(s)}{\mu^{\prime}_{\nu}(s)}ds\\
=\frac{1}{2i\omega}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{e^{i2\omega\mu_{\nu}(s)}\right\}\frac{s^{2}\operatorname{sech}(s)}{s^{2}+\nu^{2}}ds\\
=\frac{i}{2\omega}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}e^{i2\omega\mu_{\nu}(s)}\frac{d}{ds}\left\{\frac{s^{2}\operatorname{sech}(s)}{s^{2}+\nu^{2}}\right\},

since the boundary term vanishes.
Iterating that computation shows that
\alpha(\omega,\nu)=O_{\sigma}(\omega^{-N})
for all
N

when
\omega\rightarrow+{\infty}
,
meaning that the information of fast decay for
1-a_{11}
will not suffice to get (
5.3.1
).
Also, it is worth noticing that no fast decay of the function
\alpha

occurs when
\omega\rightarrow-{\infty}
, otherwise Lemma
9.1
would give smoothness for
the function
s\mapsto e^{-2i\kappa/s}H(s)\operatorname{sech}s
:
in fact we see also that for
\sigma>0
,
\tau=-\lambda
,
\lambda>0
, we have

2\pi i{a_{21}}(-\lambda,\sigma)=\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\operatorname{sech}(s)e^{-i4\pi\sigma\operatorname{coth}(s)}e^{-4i\pi s\lambda}ds,

and the phase function is

\tilde{\mu}(s)=-4i\pi(s\lambda+\sigma\operatorname{coth}(s))

and we have

\frac{d}{ds}\bigl\{s\lambda+\sigma\operatorname{coth}(s)\bigr\}=\lambda-\frac{\sigma(1-\tanh^{2}s)}{\tanh^{2}s}=\frac{(\lambda+\sigma)\tanh^{2}s-\sigma}{\tanh^{2}s},

which does vanish at
\tanh s=\sigma/(\lambda+\sigma)
.
As a result we could say that, for
\sigma>0
,
the
C^{{\infty}}

wave-front-set (see e.g. Section 8.1 in
[
23
]
)
of the function
g_{\sigma}
is reduced to
\{0\}\times(-{\infty},0)
.
It turns out that we can show that the Gevrey-2 wave-front-set of
g_{\sigma}

is

\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{*}
,
and it is expressed via the lowerbound estimate (
5.2.54
);
the route that we took for proving this was an explicit calculation of
\operatorname{Re}a_{12}
,
following the paper
[
55
]
.
Finally the upper bound (
5.2.21
) can be improved as

(5.3.7)

|1-a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)|\leq C_{\sigma,\epsilon}e^{-{(\pi-\epsilon)2\pi\tau}},\quad\epsilon>0,

and is expressing the fact the the function

t\mapsto\frac{te^{4i\pi\sigma\tanh(\frac{t}{2})}}{\sinh(t/2)}
is analytic on the real line,
with a radius of convergence on the real line bounded below by
\pi

(cf. Proposition
9.2
).

5.3.2.
More results and examples:
\ell^{p}
balls, corners

For
a,\phi_{0}
like in Corollary
5.15
,
defining

\Omega_{p}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},|x-\frac{a}{2}|^{p}+|\xi-\frac{a}{2}|^{p}<\bigl(\frac{a}{2}\bigr)^{p}\},

since
\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we get

\lim_{p\rightarrow+{\infty}}\iint_{\Omega_{p}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint_{[0,a]^{2}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

proving that the spectrum of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{p}})}
intersects
(1,+{\infty})
for
p
large enough,
showing that a counterexample to Flandrin’s conjecture can be a convex analytic open bounded set.
Moreover, defining

Q_{a}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},|x|+|\xi|\leq a/\sqrt{2}\},

we note that
Q_{a}
is obtained by rotation and translation of
[0,a]^{2}

so that
we can find
\phi_{1}
in the Schwartz space such that

\iint_{Q_{a}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Since we have

\lim_{p\rightarrow 1}\hskip-13.0pt\iint_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|^{p}+|\xi|^{p}\leq(a/\sqrt{2})^{p}\end{subarray}}\hskip-12.0pt\mathcal{W}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint_{Q_{a}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

we get that for
p-1
small enough we have

(5.3.8)

\iint_{\begin{subarray}{c}|x|^{p}+|\xi|^{p}\leq(a/\sqrt{2})^{p}\end{subarray}}\hskip-12.0pt\mathcal{W}(\phi_{1},\phi_{1})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

proving that
\ell^{p}
balls are counterexamples to Flandrin’s conjecture for
p-1
or
1/p
small enough.

Convex affine cones with aperture strictly less than
\pi
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
are translations and rotations of

(5.3.9)

\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash(\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\{0\}),\ \arg(x+i\xi)\in(0,\theta_{0})\},\quad\text{for some $\theta_{0}\in(0,\pi)$.}

The vertex of
\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
and its rotations is defined as 0
and the vertex of the translation of vector
T_{0}
of

\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
is defined as
T_{0}
.
We note that all
convex affine cones with aperture strictly less than
\pi
are symplectically equivalent in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
since
\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
is symplectically equivalent to (the interior of)
the quarter plane
\Sigma_{\pi/2}
:
indeed let
\theta_{0}
be in
(0,\pi)
; the symplectic matrix
M_{\theta_{0}}
defined by

M_{\theta_{0}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-\operatorname{cotan}\theta_{0}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},

is such that

M_{\theta_{0}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad M_{\theta_{0}}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta_{0}\\
\sin\theta_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\sin\theta_{0}\end{pmatrix},

proving that

M_{\theta_{0}}\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}=\Sigma_{\pi/2}.

The next result follows from Theorem 1.3 in
[
6
]

and
shows that many counterexamples to
Flandrin’s conjecture can be be obtained.

Theorem 5.26
.

Let
K
be a subset of the closure of a convex affine cone with aperture strictly less than
\pi

and vertex
X_{0}
such that
K
contains a neighborhood of the vertex in the cone
19
19

19

We shall say that the set
K
has a corner.
. Then there exists

\lambda>0
such that, with

K_{\lambda}=X_{0}+\lambda(K-X_{0}),

there exists
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(5.3.10)

\displaystyle\iint_{K_{\lambda}}\mathcal{W}(\phi,\phi)(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

N.B
.

Note that (
5.3.10
) implies that
\phi
is not the zero function. Also, taking
K
convex produces another counterexample to Flandrin’s conjecture since
K_{\lambda}
will be then convex, but
we do not need that assumption to proving the result.

Proof.

There is no loss of generality at assuming
X_{0}=0
and

[0,\rho_{0}]^{2}\subset K\subset\overline{\Sigma}_{\pi/2},\quad\rho_{0}>0.

Using Corollary
5.15
, we find
\phi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
(so that

\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
) such that

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}}\iint_{K_{\lambda}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint_{{\Sigma}_{\pi/2}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

implying for
\lambda
large enough that

\iint_{K_{\lambda}}\mathcal{W}(\phi_{0},\phi_{0})(x,\xi)dxd\xi>\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2},

which is the sought result.
∎

5.4.
Numerics

Definition 5.27
.

Let
\sigma\geq 0
be given.
With the
2\times 2
Hermitian matrix
\mathcal{M}_{\sigma}
given by (
5.2.31
),
we define for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,

(5.4.1)

\displaystyle\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)\!+\!\sqrt{a_{11}^{2}(\tau,\sigma)+4|a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)|^{2}}\right),

(5.4.2)

\displaystyle\lambda_{-}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)\!-\!\sqrt{a_{11}^{2}(\tau,\sigma)+4|a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)|^{2}}\right).

Remark 5.28
.

According to (
5.2.53
), we have

(5.4.3)

\displaystyle\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)\!+\!\sqrt{a_{11}^{2}(\tau,\sigma)\bigl(1+e^{-4\pi^{2}\tau}\bigr)+4{(\operatorname{Re}a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)})^{2}}\right),

(5.4.4)

\displaystyle\lambda_{-}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)-\sqrt{a_{11}^{2}(\tau,\sigma)\bigl(1+e^{-4\pi^{2}\tau}\bigr)+4{(\operatorname{Re}a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)})^{2}}\right),

so that the knowledge of
a_{11}
and
\operatorname{Re}{a_{12}}
suffices for expressing
\lambda_{\pm}
.

An immediate consequence of Theorem
5.20
is

Theorem 5.29
.

Let
\sigma\geq 0
be given and let
A_{\sigma}
be the self-adjoint operator bounded in

defined in Theorem
5.20
. With the notations of Definition
5.27
,
we have

(5.4.5)

\displaystyle M_{\sigma}:=\sup\{\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{\sigma})\}

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma),

(5.4.6)

\displaystyle m_{\sigma}:=\inf\{\text{\rm spectrum}(A_{\sigma})\}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\lambda_{-}(\tau,\sigma).

Moreover for all
\sigma\geq 0
we have

(5.4.7)

m_{\sigma}<0<1<M_{\sigma}.

,

), (

).

.

We have from (

a_{11}(\tau,0)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-4\pi^{2}\tau}},\quad\operatorname{Re}a_{12}(\tau,0)=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\sin(2\pi t\tau)\operatorname{sech}(t/2)dt,

5.4.2.
On hyperbolic regions

We want now to tackle the case
\sigma>0
.
In order to use the expressions
(
9.6.37
), (
5.2.34
)
respectively for
a_{11}
and
a_{12}
,
we need first to evaluate the residue term in (
9.6.37
).
The mapping
z\mapsto\tanh z
is a biholomorphism of neighborhoods of
0
in the complex plane, so that
we have for
z
near the origin,

(5.4.10)

\displaystyle\zeta=\tanh z,\ d\zeta=(1-\zeta^{2})dz,\quad z=\operatorname{arcth}\zeta=\frac{1}{2}\ln\left(\frac{1+\zeta}{1-\zeta}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{e^{2i\omega z-2i\kappa\operatorname{coth}z}}{\cosh z}dz=\left(\frac{1+\zeta}{1-\zeta}\right)^{i\omega}e^{-2i\frac{\kappa}{\zeta}}\ \frac{2}{\left(\frac{1+\zeta}{1-\zeta}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\frac{1-\zeta}{1+\zeta}\right)^{1/2}}\frac{d\zeta}{(1-\zeta^{2})}

(5.4.11)

\displaystyle\hskip 88.0pt=(1+\zeta)^{-\frac{1}{2}+i\omega}(1-\zeta)^{-\frac{1}{2}-i\omega}e^{-2i\frac{\kappa}{\zeta}}d\zeta,

so that

(5.4.12)

\text{\tt Res}\left(\frac{e^{2i\omega z-2i\kappa\operatorname{coth}z}}{\cosh z},0\right)=\text{\tt Res}\left((1+\zeta)^{-\frac{1}{2}+i\omega}(1-\zeta)^{-\frac{1}{2}-i\omega}e^{-2i\frac{\kappa}{\zeta}},0\right).

Proposition 5.31
.

Let
\sigma\geq 0
be given.
Then for any
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
,
using the notations,

\omega=2\pi\tau
,
\kappa=2\pi\sigma
,
we have,
for any
\rho\in(0,1)
,

(5.4.13)

a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-2\pi\omega}}\\
+\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{1+e^{-2\pi\omega}}\frac{\rho}{2\pi}\operatorname{Im}\left\{\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\exp\left(i\omega\operatorname{Log}\Bigl(\frac{1+\rho e^{i\theta}}{1-\rho e^{i\theta}}\Bigr)\right)\frac{e^{-\frac{2i\kappa e^{-i\theta}}{\rho}}e^{i\theta}}{\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}e^{2i\theta}}}\ d\theta\right\}.

(5.4.14)

\operatorname{Re}a_{21}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{2\pi}\biggl\{2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{e^{(s\omega-\kappa\tan s)}\sinh(s\omega-\kappa\tan s)}{\sin s}ds\\
+\ln\bigl(\operatorname{coth}\frac{\pi}{4}\bigr)+2\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin^{2}(s\omega-\kappa\tanh s)}{\sinh s}ds-\int_{\pi/2}^{+{\infty}}\frac{\cos 2(s\omega-\kappa\tanh s)}{\sinh s}ds\biggr\},

(5.4.15)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\pi\omega}}{2}a_{11}(\tau,\sigma).\hskip 33.0pt

Proof.

Formula (
5.4.13
) follows from (
5.4.12
) and (
9.6.37
)
whereas (
5.4.14
)
is (
5.2.34
) after a change of variable
t=2s
,
where the second integral term inside the brackets is evaluated (cf. Lemma
9.31
); Formula
(
5.4.15
) is a reminder of (
5.2.53
).
∎

N.B
.

Our choice for
\rho
in the numerical calculations of (
5.4.13
) is
\rho=3/4
,
which is a good compromise between using a value of
\rho
clearly away from
1
(to avoid singularities coming from small denominators in the
\operatorname{Log}
term) and minimize the oscillations and size
coming from the term
\exp(-2i\kappa\rho^{-1}e^{-i\theta})
; note that the modulus of the latter is

\exp(-2\kappa\rho^{-1}\sin\theta),

which is a smooth function of
\rho
(flat at 0) when
\theta\in[0,\pi]
, but is unbounded for
\rho\rightarrow 0_{+}
when
\theta\in(-\pi,0)
. There is no surprise here since although the residue does not depend on the choice of
\rho\in(0,1)
, we cannot get the value of that residue by letting
\rho
go to 0 because of the part of the path in the lower half-plane.
The argument of
\exp(-2i\kappa\rho^{-1}e^{-i\theta})
is

-2\kappa\rho^{-1}\cos\theta

and taking
\rho
too small would be devastating for the calculations
because of the strong oscillations triggered by the term

\exp(-2i\kappa\rho^{-1}\cos\theta)

all over the circle.
Of course for the evaluation of

\operatorname{Log}\Bigl(\frac{1+\rho e^{i\theta}}{1-\rho e^{i\theta}}\Bigr)

is easier for
\rho
small, but we have to take into account the constraints in that direction mentioned above.

Remark 5.32
.

It seems easier numerically for the evaluation of
a_{11}

to use (
5.4.13
)
rather than any other expression
(see e.g. Lemma
5.18
, (
5.2.31
), (
9.6.23
)).
However the following formula could be interesting, theoretically and numerically:
recalling that
\sinc x=\frac{\sin x}{x}
,
we have from (
5.2.31
)

(5.4.16)

a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2\omega}{\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\sinc(2\omega s)\frac{s}{\sinh s}\cos(2\kappa\tanh s)ds\\
-\frac{2\kappa}{\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\sinc(2\kappa s)\frac{1}{\cosh s}\cos(2\omega s)ds,

but it turns out that numerical calculations involving (
5.4.16
)
seem to be less reliable than the methods using (
5.4.13
).

We can also take a look at the following curves.

Figure 8.

Functions
\lambda_{+}(\tau,\kappa/2\pi)
with
\kappa=1,2,3
: their maxima are strictly greater than 1.

Remark 5.33
.

In the above figure, in order to put the three curves on the same picture, we have used three different logarithmic scales on the vertical axis, namely we have drawn

\tau\mapsto 1+\alpha_{j}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{j})\bigr),\quad 1\leq j\leq 3,\sigma_{j}=j/2\pi,\alpha_{1}=20,\alpha_{2}=100,\alpha_{3}=500.

Of course we have

1+\alpha_{j}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{j})\bigr)>1\Longleftrightarrow\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{j})\bigr)>0\Longleftrightarrow\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{j})>1,

so that the piece of curves in Figure
8
which are above 1 are indeed
corresponding to curves of
\tau\mapsto\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{j})
which go strictly above the threshold 1. We have also

\displaystyle\max_{\tau}\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{1})

\displaystyle\approx 1+55{\scriptstyle\times}10^{-5}{\hskip 15.0pt}\text{ at $\tau\approx 0.402030$, }

\displaystyle\max_{\tau}\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{2})

\displaystyle\approx 1+8{\scriptstyle\times}10^{-5}{\hskip 15.0pt}\text{ at $\tau\approx 0.613262$,}

\displaystyle\max_{\tau}\lambda_{+}(\tau,\sigma_{3})

\displaystyle\approx 1+10^{-5}{\hskip 15.0pt}\text{ at $\tau\approx 0.854746$.}

We are glad to have a theoretical proof of Theorem
5.20

since the numerical analysis of cases where
\sigma
is large, say larger than 10, seem to be very difficult to achieve,
at least through a standard use of
Mathematica
.
The reason for that is quite clear since using our Lemma
5.11
, we did study the function

\beta
defined by

(5.4.17)

\beta(\tau,\sigma)=|a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)|^{2}+a_{11}(\tau,\sigma)-1,

and proved that for each
\sigma\geq 0
there exists
T_{0}(\sigma)
such that for all
\tau\geq T_{0}(\sigma)
we have
\beta(\tau,\sigma)>0
and
a_{12}(\tau,\sigma)\not=0
.
Thanks to Lemma
5.18
and (
5.2.60
) we knew that for
\tau\geq T_{0}(\sigma)
, we had

|1-a_{11}|\leq 2e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}}e^{4\pi\sigma}\ll\frac{e^{-16\pi\sqrt{\tau}\sqrt{\sigma}}}{2^{8}\pi^{6}\tau^{2}}\leq(\operatorname{Re}a_{21})^{2}\leq|a_{12}|^{2},

where the second inequality
\ll
is in fact comparing for
\sigma
fixed two exponential decays. The numerical analysis of that inequality is certainly quite difficult when
\sigma
and
\tau
are large since both sides are converging to zero quite fast for
\sigma
fixed and
\tau\rightarrow+{\infty}
;
of course taking the logarithm of both sides
looks quite reasonable, but in practice does not seem really easy numerically.
When
\sigma=0
, the situation is much better,
since we had to compare (cf. Subsection
5.3.1
) an exponential decay

|1-a_{11}|\leq 2e^{-{\pi^{2}\tau}}

to a polynomial decay

|\operatorname{Re}a_{12}|^{2}\sim\frac{1}{2^{6}\pi^{4}\tau^{2}},\quad\tau\rightarrow+{\infty},

and this could be an
a posteriori
explanation for which our numerical argument in
[
6
]

worked smoothly to disprove Flandrin’s conjecture.
So to pick-up the quarter-plane ((
5.0.1
) with
\sigma=0
)
to produce a counterexample to that conjecture was indeed a very wise choice:
if you choose instead
\mathcal{C}_{\sigma}
for
\sigma
large, our Theorem
5.20
shows that it is also a counterexample to Flandrin’s conjecture
20
20

20

As a convex subset of the plane on which the integral of the Wigner distribution of some normalized pulse is
>1
.
, but we have a theoretical proof for that Theorem and if we were depending on a numerical analysis, it is quite likely that checking numerically the positivity of the function
\beta
defined in (
5.4.17
)
could be rather difficult, even say for
\sigma=10
.

6.
Unboundedness is Baire generic

In this section we show that for plenty of subsets
E
of the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
is not bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Acknowledgements.

The author is grateful to H.G. Feichtinger and K. Gröchenig for sharp comments on a first version of this section.

6.1.
Preliminaries

6.1.1.
Prolegomena

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and let
\mathcal{W}(u,u),

\mathcal{W}(v,v),
be their Wigner distributions.
Then
we have

\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)-\mathcal{W}(v,v)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq\|u-v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigl(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr).

As a consequence if a sequence
(u_{k})
is converging in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then the sequence
(\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u_{k}))
converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
towards
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
.

Proof.

We have by sesquilinearity

\mathcal{W}(u,u)-\mathcal{W}(v,v)=\mathcal{W}(u-v,u)+\mathcal{W}(v,u-v),

so that

\|\mathcal{W}(u,u)-\mathcal{W}(v,v)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq\|\mathcal{W}(u-v,u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}+\|\mathcal{W}(v,u-v)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\\
\underbracket{\hskip 3.0pt=\hskip 3.0pt}_{\eqref{norm}}\|u-v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigl(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr),

proving the lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
(u_{k})
be a converging sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with limit
u
.
Let us assume that there exists
C_{0}\geq 0
such that

(6.1.1)

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\quad\iint|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u_{k})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq C_{0}.

Then we have

\iint|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq C_{0}.

Proof.

Let
R>0
be given. We check

\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)-\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u_{k})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\\
\leq\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u-u_{k},u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi+\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u-u_{k})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\\
\leq\sqrt{|\mathbb{B}^{2n}|R^{2n}}\bigl(\|\mathcal{W}(u-u_{k},u)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}+\|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u-u_{k})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\bigr)\\
=\sqrt{|\mathbb{B}^{2n}|R^{2n}}\|u-u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigl(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr),

and thus

\displaystyle\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u_{k})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi

\displaystyle\hskip 155.0pt+\sqrt{|\mathbb{B}^{2n}|R^{2n}}\|u-u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigl(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 75.0pt\leq C_{0}+\sqrt{|\mathbb{B}^{2n}|R^{2n}}\|u-u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigl(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr),

implying for all
R>0
,

\iint_{|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2}\leq R^{2}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq C_{0},

and thus the sought result.
∎

6.1.2.
An explicit construction

We just calculate

\mathcal{W}(v_{0},v_{0})
for

(6.1.2)

v_{0}=\mathbf{1}_{[-1/2,1/2]}.

Remark 6.3
.

When
u
is supported in a closed convex set
J
, we have in the integral (
1.1.6
)
defining
\mathcal{W}
,

x\pm\frac{z}{2}\in J\Longrightarrow x\in J,

so that

\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{W}(u,u)\subset J\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(v_{0},v_{0})(x,\xi)=\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}-1/2\leq x+z/2\leq 1/2\\
-1/2\leq x-z/2\leq 1/2\end{subarray}}e^{2i\pi z\xi}dz,

and the integration domain is

-\min(1-2x,1+2x)=\max(-1-2x,2x-1)\leq z\leq\min(1-2x,1+2x),

which is empty unless
1-2x,1+2x\geq 0
i.e.
x\in[-1/2,+1/2]
, and moreover we have the equivalence

1-2x\leq 1+2x\Longleftrightarrow x\geq 0,

so that

(6.1.3)

\mathcal{W}(v_{0},v_{0})(x,\xi)=H(x)\int_{-(1-2x)}^{1-2x}e^{2i\pi z\xi}dz+H(-x)\int_{-(1+2x)}^{1+2x}e^{2i\pi z\xi}dz\\
=H(x)\frac{e^{2i\pi\xi(1-2x)}-e^{-2i\pi\xi(1-2x)}}{2i\pi\xi}+H(-x)\frac{e^{2i\pi\xi(1+2x)}-e^{-2i\pi\xi(1+2x)}}{2i\pi\xi}\\
=\mathbf{1}_{[0,1/2]}(x)\frac{\sin(2\pi\xi(1-2x))}{\pi\xi}+\mathbf{1}_{[-1/2,0]}\frac{\sin(2\pi\xi(1+2x))}{\pi\xi}.

More generally for
a,b,\omega
real numbers with
a<b

and

(6.1.4)

u_{a,b,\omega}(x)=(b-a)^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{[a,b]}(x)e^{2i\pi\omega x},

we have

(6.1.5)

\mathcal{W}(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})(x,\xi)\\
=\frac{\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{[a,\frac{a+b}{2}]}(x)\sin[4\pi(\xi-\omega)(x-a)]+\mathbf{1}_{[\frac{a+b}{2},b]}(x)\sin[4\pi(\xi-\omega)(b-x)]\bigr)}{(b-a)\pi(\xi-\omega)}.

We check now, using (
6.1.3
), for
N>0
,

\displaystyle\iint|\mathcal{W}(v_{0},v_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0\leq x\leq 1/4}\int_{0}^{N}\left|\frac{\sin(2\pi\xi(1-2x))}{\pi\xi}\right|d\xi dx

\displaystyle=\int_{0\leq x\leq 1/4}\int_{0}^{N2\pi(1-2x)}\left|\frac{\sin\eta}{\pi\eta}\right|d\eta dx

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0\leq x\leq 1/4}\int_{0}^{N\pi}\left|\frac{\sin\eta}{\pi\eta}\right|d\eta dx=\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{N\pi}\left|\frac{\sin\eta}{\pi\eta}\right|d\eta,

so that

(6.1.6)

\iint|\mathcal{W}(v_{0},v_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi=+\infty.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
a,b,\omega
be real numbers with
a<b
and let us define
u_{a,b,\omega}
by
(
6.1.4
).
Then we have

(6.1.7)

\iint\left|\mathcal{W}(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})(x,\xi)\right|dxd\xi=+{\infty}.

N.B
.

Since
u_{a,b,\omega}
is a normalized
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
function,
we also have from (
1.1.8
), (
1.1.12
) that the real-valued
W(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})

does satisfy

\displaystyle\int\left|\int{W(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})(x,\xi)}dx\right|d\xi=\int\left|\int{W(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})(x,\xi)}d\xi\right|dx

\displaystyle\hskip 285.0pt=\|u_{a,b,\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}=1,

\displaystyle\hskip 55.0pt\iint{W(u_{a,b,\omega},u_{a,b,\omega})(x,\xi)}^{2}dxd\xi=\|u_{a,b,\omega}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{4}=1.

We shall see in the next sections that most of the time in the Baire Category sense, we have for
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\iint|W(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi=+{\infty}.

Proof.

The proof is already given above for
v_{0}=u_{-1/2,1/2,0}
. Moreover we have with

\alpha=\frac{1}{b-a},\quad\beta=\frac{b+a}{2(a-b)},

the formula

v_{0}(y)=e^{-2i\pi\omega(y-\beta)\alpha^{-1}}u_{a,b,\omega}\bigl((y-\beta)\alpha^{-1}\bigr)\alpha^{-1/2},

so that

u_{a,b,\omega}=\mathcal{M}v_{0}
,
where
\mathcal{M}
belongs to the group
Mp_{a}(n)
.
(cf. Section
1.2.1
) and the covariance property (
1.2.74
) shows that the already proven (
6.1.6
) implies (
6.1.7
).
∎

6.2.
Modulation spaces

In this section, we use the Feichtinger algebra
M^{1}
, introduced in
[
10
]

(the terminology
Feichtinger algebra
goes back to the book
[
44
]
). The survey article
[
26
]
by M.S. Jakobsen
is a good source for recent developments of the theory
as well as Chapter 12 in the K. Gröchenig’s book
[
16
]
.
We refer the reader to the paper
[
18
]
by K. Gröchenig & M. Leinert as well as to
J. Sjöstrand’s article
[
48
]
for the use of modulation spaces to proving a non-commutative Wiener lemma.

6.2.1.
Preliminary lemmas

The following lemmas in this subsection are well-known (see e.g. Theorem 11.2.5 in
[
16
]
). However we provide a proof for the self-containedness of our survey.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\phi_{0}
be a non-zero function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For

u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
the following properties are equivalent:

(i)

u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

(iii)

\forall N\in\mathbb{N},\quad\sup_{X\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})(X)|(1+|X|)^{N}<+{\infty}.

Proof.

Let us assume (i) holds true; with
\Omega(u,\phi_{0})
defined in (
1.1.1
),
we find that
\Omega(u,\phi_{0})
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, thus as well as its partial Fourier transform
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})
, proving (ii).
We have obviously that (ii) implies (iii). Let us now assume that (iii) holds true.
Using (
1.1.7
), we find

u(x_{1})\bar{\phi}_{0}(x_{2})=\int\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)e^{2i\pi(x_{1}-x_{2})\xi}d\xi,

and thus

u(x_{1})\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)e^{2i\pi(x_{1}-x_{2})\xi}\phi_{0}(x_{2})d\xi dx_{2}\\
=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})(y,\xi)e^{4i\pi(x_{1}-y)\xi}\phi_{0}(2y-x_{1})d\xi dy2^{n},

so that the latter equality, the fact that
\phi_{0}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

imply (i)
by differentiation under the integral sign, concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\phi_{0},\phi_{1}
be non-zero functions in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
Then
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{1})
belongs as well to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
1.30
applied to

u_{0}=u,u_{1}=u_{2}=\phi_{0},u_{3}=\phi_{1}
, we have

\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{1})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),

since
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi_{0})
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
as well as
\mathcal{W}(\check{\phi}_{0},\phi_{1})
.
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The following properties are equivalent.

(i)

For all
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

(ii)

For a non-zero
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have
\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

(iii)

\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

Proof.

We have obviously (i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii) and, conversely, Lemma
6.6
yields
(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i).
Assuming (i) and using Lemma
1.30

with
u_{0}=u_{3}=u
,
u_{1}=u_{2}=\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we get

\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}^{2}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(X)|\leq 2^{n}\bigl(|\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)|\ast|\mathcal{W}(\check{\phi},u)|\bigr)(X),

so that choosing a non-zero
\phi
in the Schwartz space,
we obtain (iii).
Conversely, assuming (iii) and using again Lemma
1.30

with
u_{0}=u_{2}=u
,
u_{3}=\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
u_{1}=\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we find

(6.2.1)

|\langle\psi,u\rangle_{L^{2}}||\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)(X)|\leq 2^{n}\bigl(\underbrace{|\mathcal{W}(u,u)|}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\ast|\underbrace{\mathcal{W}(\check{\psi},\phi)}_{\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}|\bigr)(X).

Assuming as we may
u\not=0
, we can choose
\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\langle\psi,u\rangle_{L^{2}}\not=0
,
so that (
6.2.1
) implies (i).
∎

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Then we have the
inversion formula
,

(6.2.2)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u_{1},u_{2}))}u_{3}=\langle u_{3},u_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}u_{1}.

Proof.

It is an immediate consequence of Lemma
1.29
.
∎

6.2.2.
The space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

Definition 6.9
.

The space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is defined as the set of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that, for all
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
According to Lemma
6.7
,
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is also the set of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
as well as the
the set of
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that, for a non-zero
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
\psi_{0}
be the standard fundamental state of the Harmonic Oscillator

\pi(D_{x}^{2}+x^{2})

given by

(6.2.3)

\psi_{0}(x)=2^{n/4}e^{-\pi|x|^{2}}.

Then
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni u\mapsto\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}
is a norm on
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let
\psi
be a non-zero function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
: then

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni u\mapsto\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}

is a norm on
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
equivalent to the previous norm.

Proof.

The homogeneity and triangle inequality are immediate, let us check the separation: let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{W}(u,\psi)=0
. Then we have

0=\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u,\psi))}\psi,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\psi\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

proving the sought result. Let
\psi
be a non-zero function in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
;
according to Lemma
1.30
applied to

u_{0}=u,u_{1}=u_{2}=\psi_{0},u_{3}=\psi
,
we find

(6.2.4)

|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi)(X)|\leq 2^{n}\bigl(|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})|\ast|\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\psi)|\bigr)(X),

so that we have

(6.2.5)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq 2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\psi)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

(6.2.6)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq 2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(\psi,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

proving the equivalence of norms.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

The space

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, equipped with the equivalent norms of Proposition
6.10
,
is a Banach space.
The space
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is dense in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a Cauchy sequence in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
:
it means that

(\mathcal{W}(u_{k},\psi_{0}))_{k\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,
thus such that

(6.2.7)

\lim_{k}\mathcal{W}(u_{k},\psi_{0})=U\quad\text{in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})$.}

On the other hand, from Lemma
1.29
,
we have

(6.2.8)

u_{k}-u_{l}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(\mathcal{W}(u_{k}-u_{l},\psi_{0})\bigr)}\psi_{0},

so that

\|u_{k}-u_{l}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(\mathcal{W}(u_{k}-u_{l},\psi_{0})\bigr)}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\\
\underbrace{\leq}_{\text{cf. \eqref{norm01}}}2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(u_{k}-u_{l},\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

implying that

(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, thus converging towards a function
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Since from (
1.1.8
), we have

\|\mathcal{W}(u_{k}-u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|u_{k}-u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

we obtain as well that

(6.2.9)

\lim_{k}\mathcal{W}(u_{k},\psi_{0})=\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\quad\text{in $L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})$},

and this implies along with (
6.2.7
) that
U=\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})
in
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
As a result, we have
\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, so that
u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and

\lim_{k}\mathcal{W}(u_{k},\psi_{0})=\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\quad\text{in $L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})$},

entailing convergence towards
u
for the sequence
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the sought completeness.
We are left with the density question and we start with a calculation.

Claim 6.12
.

With the phase symmetry
\sigma_{y,\eta}
given by (
1.2.9
) and
\psi_{0}
by
(
6.2.3
) we have for
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

(6.2.10)

\mathcal{W}(\sigma_{Y}\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(X)=2^{n}e^{-2\pi|X-Y|^{2}}e^{-4i\pi[X,Y]},

where the symplectic form is given in (
1.2.24
).

Proof of the Claim.

We have indeed

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(\sigma_{y,\eta}\psi_{0},

\displaystyle\psi_{0})(x,\xi)=\int\bigl(\sigma_{y,\eta}\psi_{0}\bigr)(x+\frac{z}{2})\psi_{0}(x-\frac{z}{2})e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}dz

\displaystyle=\int\psi_{0}(2y-x-\frac{z}{2})e^{4i\pi\eta\cdot(x+\frac{z}{2}-y)}\psi_{0}(x-\frac{z}{2})e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}dz

\displaystyle=2^{n/2}\int e^{-\pi\left(|2y-x-\frac{z}{2}|^{2}+|x-\frac{z}{2}|^{2}\right)}e^{2i\pi z\cdot(\eta-\xi)}dze^{4i\pi\eta\cdot(x-y)}

\displaystyle=2^{n/2}e^{4i\pi\eta\cdot(x-y)}\int e^{-\frac{\pi}{2}\left(|2y-z|^{2}+|2(y-x)|^{2}\right)}e^{2i\pi z\cdot(\eta-\xi)}dz

\displaystyle=2^{n/2}e^{4i\pi\eta\cdot(x-y)}e^{-2\pi|y-x|^{2}}e^{4i\pi y\cdot(\eta-\xi)}2^{n/2}e^{-2\pi|\eta-\xi|^{2}},

which is the sought formula.
∎

Let
u
be a function in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. For
\varepsilon>0
we define

u_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(Y)e^{-\varepsilon|Y|^{2}}2^{n}(\sigma_{Y}\psi_{0})(x)dY,

and we have

\mathcal{W}(u_{\varepsilon},\psi_{0})(X)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(Y)e^{-\varepsilon|Y|^{2}}2^{n}\mathcal{W}(\sigma_{Y}\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(X)dY,

so that Lemma

6.5
and (
6.2.10
) imply readily that
u_{\varepsilon}
belongs to the Schwartz space.
Moreover we have
u={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0}))}\psi_{0},

from Lemma
6.8
and thus

\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(X)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(Y)2^{n}\mathcal{W}(\sigma_{Y}\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(X)dY,

so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u_{\varepsilon},\psi_{0})(X)-\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(X)|dX\\
\leq 2^{n}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\underbrace{|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(Y)||\mathcal{W}(\sigma_{Y}\psi_{0},\psi_{0})(X)|}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{4n})\text{ from \eqref{648poi}}\text{ and $u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\bigl(1-e^{-\varepsilon|Y|^{2}}\bigr)}_{\in[0,1]}dYdX.

The Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem shows that the integral above tends to
0
when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0_{+}
,
proving the convergence in
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of the sequence
(u_{\varepsilon})
, which completes the proof of the density.
∎

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be an element of the metaplectic group
Mp(n)
(Definition
1.17
). Then
\mathcal{M}
is an isomorphism of
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we have for
u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(6.2.11)

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}u,\mathcal{M}\phi)=\mathcal{W}(u,\phi)\circ S^{-1},

where
\mathcal{M}
is in the fiber of the symplectic transformation
S
.
In particular, the space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

is invariant by the Fourier transformation and partial Fourier transformations, by the rescaling (
1.2.51
),
by the transformations (
1.2.50
),
(
1.2.52
) and also
by the phase translations (
1.2.76
)
and phase symmetries (
1.2.9
).

Proof.

Formula (
6.2.11
) follows readily from
(
1.2.74
) and if
u
belongs to
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
we find that

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}u,\underbrace{\mathcal{M}\psi_{0}}_{\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})})=\underbrace{\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\circ S^{-1},

and since
\det S=1
, we have

\|\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}u,\mathcal{M}\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

implying that

\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{M}u,\mathcal{M}\psi_{0})

belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
so that, thanks to Definition
6.9
,
we get that
\mathcal{M}u
belongs to
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
The same properties are true for
\mathcal{M}^{-1}
.
∎

Remark 6.14
.

From Definition
6.9
, we see that, for
u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\mathcal{W}(u,u)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),

and this implies, thanks to Theorem
1.28
, that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Moreover
we have

\mathcal{F}\bigl(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\bigr)\subset M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

since for
u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we have

\mathcal{W}(\hat{u},\psi_{0})=\mathcal{W}(\hat{u},\hat{\psi}_{0})

and thanks to (
6.2.11
),

\|\mathcal{W}(\hat{u},\hat{\psi}_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

As a consequence we find

\mathcal{F}\bigl(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\bigr)\subset M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathcal{F}^{2}\mathcal{C}(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))=\mathcal{F}^{2}(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\subset\mathcal{F}\bigl(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\bigr),

and consequently

(6.2.12)

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathcal{F}(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\subset\mathcal{F}(L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\subset C_{(0)}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

where the latter inclusion is due to the Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma
with

C_{(0)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
standing for space of
continuous functions
with limit
0
at infinity.
Moreover, for
u\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\psi_{0}
given by (
6.2.3
), we get from
(
1.1.7
),

(6.2.13)

u(x_{1})\bar{\psi}_{0}(x_{2})=\int\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2},\xi)e^{2i\pi(x_{1}-x_{2})\cdot\xi}d\xi,

so that

u(x_{1})=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(y,\eta)e^{4i\pi(x_{1}-y)\cdot\eta}\bar{\psi}_{0}(2y-x_{1})dyd\eta 2^{n},

implying

(6.2.14)

\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}2^{\frac{5n}{4}},

and similarly for
p\in[1,+{\infty}]
,

(6.2.15)

\|u\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}2^{\frac{5n}{4}}p^{-\frac{n}{2p}},

yielding the continuous injection of
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
into
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Theorem 6.15
.

The space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach algebra for convolution and for pointwise multiplication.

Proof.

Let
u,v\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
; then the convolution
u\ast v
makes sense and belongs to all
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

for
p\in[1,+{\infty}]
, since we have
u\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. We calculate

\mathcal{W}(u\ast v,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u(y)\mathcal{W}(\tau_{y}v,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)dy,\quad(\tau_{y}v)(x)=v(x-y),

so that

\|\mathcal{W}(u\ast v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(y)|\|\mathcal{W}(\tau_{y}v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}dy,

and since we have

\mathcal{W}(\tau_{y}v,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)=\mathcal{W}(v,\tau_{y}\psi_{0})(x,\xi)e^{-4i\pi y\cdot\xi},

we get

(6.2.16)

\|\mathcal{W}(u\ast v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(y)|\|\mathcal{W}(v,\tau_{y}\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}dy,

so that using (
6.2.5
),
we obtain

\|\mathcal{W}(u\ast v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u(y)|2^{n}\|\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\tau_{y}\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}dy\|\mathcal{W}(v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

We can check now that

\mathcal{W}(\psi_{0},\tau_{y}\psi_{0})(x,\xi)=2^{n}e^{-2\pi\left(\xi^{2}+(x-\frac{y}{2})^{2}\right)}e^{2i\pi\xi y},

so that

(6.2.17)

\|\mathcal{W}(u\ast v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq 2^{n}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\mathcal{W}(v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\\
\underbrace{\leq}_{\eqref{6414mm}}2^{\frac{9n}{4}}\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|\mathcal{W}(v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

proving that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach algebra for convolution
when equipped with the norm

(6.2.18)

N(u)=2^{\frac{9n}{4}}\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}.

On the other hand, for
u,v\in M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, the pointwise product
u\cdot v
makes sense and belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(since both functions are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
) and we have

u\cdot v=\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}(\hat{u}\ast\hat{v}),

so that

\mathcal{W}(u\cdot v,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)=\mathcal{W}\bigl(\mathcal{C}\mathcal{F}(\hat{u}\ast\hat{v}),\psi_{0}\bigr)(x,\xi)=\mathcal{W}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(\hat{u}\ast\hat{v}),\check{\psi}_{0}\bigr)(-x,-\xi),

and since
\psi_{0}=\hat{\psi}_{0}
is also even, we get

\|\mathcal{W}(u\cdot v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=\|\mathcal{W}\bigl(\mathcal{F}(\hat{u}\ast\hat{v}),\mathcal{F}\psi_{0}\bigr)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\underbrace{=}_{\text{cf. }\eqref{segal+}}\|\mathcal{W}\bigl(\hat{u}\ast\hat{v},\psi_{0}\bigr)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\\
\underbrace{\leq}_{\eqref{6417jj}}2^{\frac{9n}{4}}\|\mathcal{W}(\hat{u},\hat{\psi}_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|\mathcal{W}(\hat{v},\hat{\psi}_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\\
=2^{\frac{9n}{4}}\|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\|\mathcal{W}(v,\psi_{0})\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

proving as well that

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a Banach algebra for pointwise multiplication with the norm (
6.2.18
).
∎

6.3.
Most pulses give rise to non-integrable Wigner distribution

In the sequel,
n
is an integer
\geq 1
.

Lemma 6.16
.

We have with
\psi_{0}
given by (
6.2.3
),

(6.3.1)

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi<+{\infty}\}.

Then
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

is an
F_{\sigma}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with empty interior.

Proof.

We have
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\cup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\Phi_{N}
with

\Phi_{N}=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq N\}.

The set
\Phi_{N}
is a closed subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
since if
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is a
sequence in
\Phi_{N}

which
converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with limit
u
, we get for
R\geq 0
,

\iint_{|(x,\xi)|\leq R}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\\
\leq\iint_{|(x,\xi)|\leq R}|\mathcal{W}(u-u_{k},\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi+\iint_{|(x,\xi)|\leq R}|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\\
\leq\|u-u_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}(|\mathbb{B}^{2n}|R^{2n})^{1/2}+N,

implying

\iint_{|(x,\xi)|\leq R}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq N,
and this for any
R
, so that we obtain

u\in\Phi_{N}
.
The interior of
\Phi_{N}
is empty, since if it were not the case, as
\Phi_{N}
is also convex and symmetric,
0
would be an interior point of
\Phi_{N}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and we would find
\rho_{0}>0
such that

\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\rho_{0}\Longrightarrow\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq N,

and thus for any non-zero
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we would have

\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,\psi_{0})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{-1}\rho_{0}\leq N\quad\text{and thus}\quad\|u\|_{M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq N\rho_{0}^{-1}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

implying as well

{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}={M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

which is untrue,
thanks to the examples of Section
6.1.2
,
e.g.
(
6.1.6
),
and this
proves that the interior of
\Phi_{N}
is actually empty.
Now the Baire Category Theorem implies that the
F_{\sigma}
set
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with empty interior.
∎

Let us give another decomposition of the space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Lemma 6.17
.

According to Lemma
6.7
,
we have

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi<+{\infty}\}.

Then defining

(6.3.2)

\mathcal{F}_{N}=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq N\},

each
\mathcal{F}_{N}
is a closed subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with empty interior.

Proof.

We have

\mathscr{F}=M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\cup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{N}
.
The set
\mathcal{F}_{N}
is a closed subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
since if
(u_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is a sequence in
\mathcal{F}_{N}
which converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with limit
u
, we have

\forall k\geq 1,\quad\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\mathcal{W}(u_{k},u_{k})(x,\xi)|dxd\xi\leq N,

so that we may apply Lemma
6.2
with
C_{0}=N
,
and readily get that
u
belongs to
\mathcal{F}_{N}
.
We have also that
\text{interior}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left(\mathcal{F}_{N}\right)\subset\text{interior}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\left(M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\right)=\emptyset
.
∎

Theorem 6.18
.

Defining

(6.3.3)

\mathscr{G}=\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi=+{\infty}\}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\backslash M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

we obtain that the set
\mathscr{G}
is a dense
G_{\delta}
subset of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

It follows immediately from Lemma
6.17

and formula

\bigl\{\mathring{A}\bigr\}^{c}=\overline{A^{c}},

yielding for
\mathcal{F}_{N}
defined in (
6.3.2
),

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\bigl\{\text{interior}({\cup_{\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{N})}\bigr\}^{c}=\overline{\cap_{\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{F}_{N}^{c}}.

∎

Remark 6.19
.

It is interesting to note that the space
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is not reflexive,
as it can be identified to
\ell^{1}
via Wilson bases, but it is a dual space.
It turns out that both properties are linked to the fact that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is an
F_{\sigma}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

as proven by Lemmas
6.16
and
6.17
:
if
\mathbb{X}
is a reflexive Banach space continuously included in a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
,
it is always an
F_{\sigma}
of
\mathbb{H}
, since we may write

\mathbb{X}=\cup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}N\mathbf{B}_{\mathbb{X}},

where
\mathbf{B}_{\mathbb{X}}

is the closed unit ball of
\mathbb{X}
and
N\mathbf{B}_{\mathbb{X}}
is
\mathbb{H}
-closed
since it is weakly compact (for the topology
\sigma(\mathbb{H},\mathbb{H})
);
we cannot use that abstract argument in the case of the non-reflexive

M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
so we produced a direct elementary proof above.
Also it can be proven that
if
\mathbb{X}
is a Banach space continuously included in a Hilbert space
\mathbb{H}
,
so that
\mathbb{X}
is an
F_{\sigma}
of
\mathbb{H}
, then
\mathbb{X}
must have a predual.
As a result, the fact that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
has a predual
appears as a consequence of the fact that
M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

is an
F_{\sigma}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

6.4.
Consequences on integrals of the Wigner distribution

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\mathscr{G}
be defined in (
6.3.3
) and let
u\in\mathscr{G}
. Then the positive and negative part of the real-valued
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
are such that

(6.4.1)

\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=+{\infty}.

Proof.

For
h\in(0,1]
, we define the symbol

(6.4.2)

a(x,\xi,h)=e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})},

and we see that it is a semi-classical symbol in the sense (
1.2.108
).
Let us start a
reductio ad absurdum
and assume that

\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)dxd\xi<+{\infty},

(which implies since
u\in\mathcal{G}
,

\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=+{\infty}
).
We note that

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a(x,\xi,h))}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\iint\underbrace{a(x,\xi,h)}_{\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\underbrace{\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)}_{\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}dxd\xi,

and thanks to Theorem
1.31
we have also

\sup_{h\in(0,1]}|\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a(x,\xi,h))}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}|\leq\sigma_{n}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},

so that

\iint{e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)}dxd\xi+\iint{e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi\\
=\iint{e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi,

and thus with
\theta_{h}\in[-1,1]
, we have

(6.4.3)

\theta_{h}\sigma_{n}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\iint{e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi\\
=\iint{e^{-h(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi.

Choosing
h=1/m,m\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we note that

{e^{-\frac{1}{m}(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}\leq{e^{-\frac{1}{m+1}(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}.

From the Beppo-Levi Theorem (see e.g. Theorem 1.6.1 in
[
34
]
) we get that

\lim_{m\rightarrow+{\infty}}\iint{e^{-\frac{1}{m}(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}}{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi=\iint{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi=+{\infty}.

However the left-hand-side of (
6.4.3
) is bounded above by

\sigma_{n}\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\iint{\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)}dxd\xi,\quad\text{which is finite,}

triggering a contradiction.
We may now study the case where

\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)dxd\xi<+{\infty},\quad\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=+{\infty}.

The identity (
6.4.3
) still holds true
with a left-hand-side going to
+{\infty}
when
h
goes to 0 whereas the right-hand side is bounded.
This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

N.B
.

A shorter
heuristic
argument would be that the identity
\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}
and

\iint|\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)|dxd\xi=+{\infty}
should imply the lemma, but the former integral is not absolutely converging, so that argument fails to be completely convincing
since we need to give a meaning to the first integral.

Theorem 6.21
.

Defining

\mathscr{G}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\backslash M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(cf. (
6.3.3
))
we find that the set
\mathscr{G}
is a dense
G_{\delta}
set in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and for all

u\in\mathscr{G}
, we have
21
21

21

Note that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is real-valued.

(6.4.4)

\displaystyle\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{+}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint\mathcal{W}(u,u)_{-}(x,\xi)dxd\xi=+{\infty},

Defining
22
22

22

Thanks to Theorem
1.26
, the function
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is a continuous function, so it makes sense to consider its pointwise values.

(6.4.5)

E_{\pm}(u)=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},\pm\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)>0\},

we have for all
u\in\mathscr{G}
,

(6.4.6)

\iint_{E_{\pm}(u)}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\pm{\infty},

and both sets
E_{\pm}(u)
are open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with infinite Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

The first statements follow from Theorem

6.18
and Lemma
6.20
. As far as (
6.4.6
) is concerned, we note that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)>0

(resp.
<0
) on
E_{+}(u)
(resp.
E_{-}(u)
), so that
Theorem
6.18
implies (
6.4.6
).
Moreover

E_{\pm}(u)
are open subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
since, thanks to Theorem
1.26
,
the function
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is continuous; also, both subsets have infinite Lebesgue measure from
(
6.4.4
) since
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
.
∎

Remark 6.22
.

There are many other interesting properties and generalizations of the space
M^{1}

and in particular a close link between the Bargmann transform, the Fock spaces and modulation spaces:
we refer the reader to Remark 5 on page 243 in Section 11.4 of
[
16
]
,
to our Section
1.2.8
in this article and to Section 2.4 of

[
33
]
.

Remark 6.23
.

As a consequence of the previous theorem, we could say that for any
generic

u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

(i.e. any
u\in\mathscr{G}=L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\backslash M^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
), we can find open sets
E_{+},E_{-}
such that the real-valued
\pm\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is positive on
E_{\pm}
and

\iint_{E_{\pm}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi=\pm{\infty}.

We shall see in the next section some results on polygons in the plane and for instance, we shall be able to prove that there exists a “universal number”

\mu_{3}^{+}>1
such that
for any triangle
23
23

23

We define a triangle as the intersection of three half-planes, which includes of course the convex envelope of three points, but also the set with infinite area

\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,x+\xi\geq\lambda\}
for some
\lambda>0
.

\mathcal{T}
in the plane, we have

(6.4.7)

\forall u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}),\quad\iint_{\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)dxd\xi\leq\mu_{3}^{+}\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}.

Note in particular that we will show that (
6.4.7
) holds true regardless of the area of the triangle (which could be infinite according to our definition of a triangle in our footnote
23
).
Although that type of result may look pretty weak, it gets enhanced by Theorem
6.21

which proves that no triangle in the plane could be a set
E_{+}(u)
(cf. (
6.4.5
)) for a generic
u
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

7.
Convex polygons of the plane

7.1.
Convex Cones

We have seen in Proposition
5.30
and Theorem
5.20

that the self-adjoint bounded operator with Weyl symbol

H(x)H(\xi)
does satisfy

(7.1.1)

\mu_{2}^{-}=m_{0}=\lambda_{\text{min}}\bigl({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\bigr)\leq{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\\
\leq\lambda_{\text{max}}\bigl({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\bigr)=M_{0}=\mu_{2}^{+},

(7.1.2)

\displaystyle[\mu_{2}^{-},\mu_{2}^{+}]=\text{spectrum}\left({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\right),

with

(7.1.3)

\mu_{2}^{-}\approx-0.155939843191243,\qquad\mu_{2}^{+}\approx 1.00767997007003.

This result is true as well for the characteristic function of any convex cone (which is not a half-plane nor the full plane) in the plane since we can map it to the quarter plane by a transformation in
\text{Sl}(2,\mathbb{R})=\text{Sp}(1,\mathbb{R})
.
On the other hand a concave cone is the complement of a convex cone and the diagonalization offered by Theorem
5.19
proves that the spectrum of the Weyl quantization of the indicatrix of a concave cone is

1-\text{Spectrum}\left({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\right).

We may sum-up the situation by the following theorem.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
\Sigma_{\theta}
be a convex cone in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with aperture
\theta\in[0,2\pi]
(cf. (
5.3.9
))
and let
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
be the self-adjoint bounded operator with the indicator function of
\Sigma_{\theta}
as a Weyl symbol.

(1)

If
\theta=0
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}=0
.

(2)

If
\theta\in(0,\pi)
, the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
is unitarily equivalent to
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}
, thus with spectrum

[\mu_{2}^{-},\mu_{2}^{+}]
with
\mu_{2}^{-}<0<1<\mu_{2}^{+}

as given in Theorem
5.20
.

(3)

If
\theta=\pi
,
\Sigma_{\pi}
is a half-space and
\mathcal{A}_{\pi}
is a proper orthogonal projection, thus with spectrum
\{0,1\}
.

(4)

If
\theta\in(\pi,2\pi)
,
\Sigma_{\theta}
is a concave cone and the operator
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
is unitarily equivalent to
\operatorname{Id}-{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}
, thus with spectrum
[1-\mu_{2}^{+},1-\mu_{2}^{-}]
.
24
24

24

So that we have in particular,
from (2), the inequalities

1-\mu_{2}^{+}<0<1<1-\mu_{2}^{-}
.

(5)

If
\theta=2\pi
, we have
\mathcal{A}_{2\pi}=\operatorname{Id}
.

Remark 7.2
.

It is only in the trivial cases
\theta\in\{0,\pi,2\pi\}
that

A_{\theta}
is an orthogonal projection. These cases are also characterized (among cones) by the fact that the spectrum of
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
is included in
[0,1]
.

Remark 7.3
.

It is interesting to remark that
all operators
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
for
\theta\in(0,\pi)
are unitarily equivalent and thus with constant spectrum
[\mu_{2}^{-},\mu_{2}^{+}]
as given in Theorem
5.20
. Nevertheless the sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{\theta})_{0<\theta<\pi}
is weakly converging to the orthogonal projection
\mathcal{A}_{\pi}
whose spectrum is
\{0,1\}
:
indeed for
\phi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
,
\psi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
, we have

\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\iint_{\Sigma_{\theta}}\underbrace{\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)}_{\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}(x,\xi)dxd\xi,

and thus the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem implies that

(7.1.4)

\lim_{\theta\rightarrow\pi_{-}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

On the other hand for
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and sequences
(\phi_{k})_{k\geq 1},(\psi_{k})_{k\geq 1}

in
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
with respective limits
u,v

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
we have

\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}(u-\phi_{k}),v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi_{k},v-\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

so that

\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\\
=\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}(u-\phi_{k}),v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi_{k},v-\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},\\
-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}(u-\phi_{k}),v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}\phi_{k},v-\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}\phi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

implying

|\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\\
\leq(\mu_{2}^{+}+1)\bigl(\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|v-\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr)\\
+|\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}\phi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}\phi_{k},\psi_{k}\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|,

and thus, using (
7.1.4
), we get

\limsup_{\theta\rightarrow 0_{+}}|\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}-\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\\
\leq(\mu_{2}^{+}+1)\bigl(\|u-\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|v-\psi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|\phi_{k}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\bigr).

Taking now the infimum with respect to
k
of the right-hand-side in the above inequality,
we obtain indeed the weak convergence

(7.1.5)

\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0_{+}}\langle\mathcal{A}_{\theta}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\langle\mathcal{A}_{\pi}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

Of course we cannot have strong convergence of the bounded self-adjoint
\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
towards (the bounded self-adjoint)
A_{\pi}
because of their respective spectra
and the same lines can be written on the weak limit
0
when
\theta\rightarrow 0_{+}
of

\mathcal{A}_{\theta}
.

7.2.
Triangles

We may consider general “triangles” in the plane that we define as

(7.2.1)

\mathcal{T}_{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}=\bigl\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},L_{j}(x,\xi)\geq c_{j},j\in\{1,2,3\}\bigr\},

c_{j}
are real numbers and
L_{j}
are linear forms. To avoid degenerate situations, we shall assume that

(7.2.2)

\text{for $j\not=k$},\quad dL_{j}\wedge dL_{k}\not=0,\quad|\mathcal{T}_{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}|>0\text{ \quad and $\mathcal{T}_{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}$ is not a cone}.

Note that this includes standard triangles (convex envelope of three non-colinear points) but also sets with infinite area such as

(7.2.3)

\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,x+\xi\geq\lambda\},\quad\text{where $\lambda$ is a positive parameter.}

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
L_{1}(x,\xi)-c_{1}=x,L_{2}(x,\xi)-c_{2}=\xi
,
so that

\mathcal{T}_{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,ax+b\xi\geq\nu\},

where
a,b,\lambda
are real parameters with
a\not=0,b\not=0
from the assumption (
7.2.2
); using the symplectic mapping

(x,\xi)\mapsto(\mu x,\xi/\mu)
with
\mu=\sqrt{|b/a|}
, we see that the condition
ax+b\xi\geq\nu
becomes

x\operatorname{sign}a+\xi\operatorname{sign}b\geq\lambda=\nu/\sqrt{|ab|}\quad\text{i.e\quad}\begin{cases}x+\xi&\geq\tilde{\nu},\\
x-\xi&\geq\tilde{\nu},\\
-x+\xi&\geq\tilde{\nu},\\
-x-\xi&\geq\tilde{\nu}.\end{cases}

The first case requires
\tilde{\nu}>0
and the other cases

\tilde{\nu}<0
. The only case with finite area is the fourth case

(7.2.4)

\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,x+\xi\leq\lambda\}\ \text{ triangle with area $\lambda^{2}/2$, $\lambda>0$.}

The second case is

(7.2.5)

\mathcal{T}_{2,\lambda}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,x-\xi\geq-\lambda\},\quad\lambda>0,

The third case is

(7.2.6)

\mathcal{T}_{3,\lambda}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,\xi-x\geq-\lambda\},\quad\lambda>0,

and the first case is

(7.2.7)

\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},x\geq 0,\xi\geq 0,\xi+x\geq\lambda\},\quad\lambda>0.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}
be a triangle with finite non-zero area in the plane given by (
7.2.4
),
where
\lambda
is a positive parameter.
Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}
is unitarily equivalent to the operator with kernel

(7.2.8)

\tilde{k}_{4,\lambda}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\frac{\sin\bigl(\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})\bigr)}{\pi(x-y)}.

The operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}
is self-adjoint and bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so that

(7.2.9)

\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu_{2}^{+}+\sqrt{1+(\mu_{2}^{+})^{2}}\right):=\tilde{\mu}_{3},

where
\mu_{2}^{+}
is given in (
7.1.1
).

Proof.

The kernel
k_{4,\lambda}
of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}
is such that

k_{4,\lambda}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\int_{0}^{\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2}}e^{2i\pi(x-y)\xi}d\xi\\
=\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\frac{\bigl(e^{2i\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})}-1\bigr)}{2i\pi(x-y)}\\
=e^{i\pi(\lambda x-\frac{x^{2}}{2})}\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2}))}{\pi(x-y)}e^{-i\pi(\lambda y-\frac{y^{2}}{2})},

proving (
7.2.8
).
We note now that the kernel of the operator with Weyl symbol
H(\xi)H(\lambda-\xi-x)

is

(7.2.10)

\ell_{\lambda}(x,y)=e^{i\pi(\lambda x-\frac{x^{2}}{2})}H(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2}))}{\pi(x-y)}e^{-i\pi(\lambda y-\frac{y^{2}}{2})},

and that

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(\xi)H(\lambda-\xi-x))}

is unitarily equivalent to the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}
as given by Theorem
7.1
.
We get then

(7.2.11)

k_{4,\lambda}(x,y)=H(x+y)\ell_{\lambda}(x,y)=H(x)\ell_{\lambda}(x,y)H(y)\\
+H(x+y)\bigl(H(x)\check{H}(y)+\check{H}(x)H(y)\bigr)H(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})\\
\times\frac{\sin(\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2}))}{\pi(x-y)}\times e^{i\pi(\lambda x-\frac{x^{2}}{2})}e^{-i\pi(\lambda y-\frac{y^{2}}{2})},

and we have thus

(7.2.12)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}=H{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(H(\xi)H(\lambda-\xi-x)\bigr)}H+\Omega_{\lambda},

where the kernel
\omega_{\lambda}(x,y)
of the operator
\Omega_{\lambda}
verifies

|\omega_{\lambda}(x,y)|\leq\frac{H(x+y)\bigl(H(x)\check{H}(y)+\check{H}(x)H(y)\bigr)}{\pi|x-y|}\\
=\frac{H(x+y)\bigl(H(x)\check{H}(y)+\check{H}(x)H(y)\bigr)}{\pi(|x|+|y|)}.

We obtain thanks to Proposition

9.30
[2] that

(7.2.13)

\iint|\omega_{\lambda}(x,y)||u(y)||u(x)|dydx\leq\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}.

As a result, we find that

|\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{4,\lambda}})}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq\mu_{2}^{+}\|Hu\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

proving (
7.2.9
).
∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}
be a triangle with infinite area in the plane given by (
7.2.7
),
where
\lambda
is a positive parameter.
Then the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}
is unitarily equivalent to the operator with kernel

(7.2.14)

\tilde{k}_{1,\lambda}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{[0,\lambda]}\bigl(\frac{x+y}{2}\bigr)\frac{\sin\bigl(\pi(x-y)(\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})\bigr)}{\pi(x-y)}.

The operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}
is self-adjoint and bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
so that

(7.2.15)

\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\mu_{2}^{+}+\sqrt{\frac{1}{4}+(\mu_{2}^{+})^{2}}\right)\approx 1.066294188078,

where
\mu_{2}^{+}
is given in (
7.1.1
).

Proof.

The kernel
k_{1,\lambda}

of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}
is such that

\displaystyle k_{1,\lambda}(x,y)

\displaystyle=H(x+y)e^{2i\pi(x-y)\max(0,\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi(y-x)}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=H(x)\frac{1}{2}\delta_{0}(x-y)H(y)+H(x)\frac{e^{2i\pi(x-y)\max(0,\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})}}{2i\pi(y-x)}H(y)

\displaystyle\hskip 33.0pt+H(x+y)\bigl(H(x)\check{H}(y)+\check{H}(x)H(y)\bigr)\frac{e^{2i\pi(x-y)\max(0,\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})}}{2i\pi(y-x)}.

We note that the kernel of the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x+\xi-\lambda)H(\xi))}
is

\ell_{1}(x,y)=e^{2i\pi(x-y)\max(0,\lambda-\frac{x+y}{2})}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\delta_{0}(y-x)+\frac{1}{i\pi(y-x)}\Bigr),

so that

(7.2.16)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}=H\underbrace{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x+\xi-\lambda)H(\xi))}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{unitarily equivalent to}\\
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}\end{subarray}}H+\Omega_{1,\lambda},

where the kernel
\omega_{1,\lambda}

of the operator
\Omega_{1,\lambda}
is such that

|\omega_{1,\lambda}(x,y)|\leq H(x+y)\frac{\bigl(H(x)\check{H}(y)+\check{H}(x)H(y)\bigr)}{2\pi(|x|+|y|)},

and, thanks to Proposition

9.30
[2], we get from (
7.2.16
) that

|\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}_{1,\lambda}})}u,u\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\leq\mu_{2}^{+}\|Hu\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}+\frac{1}{2}\|\check{H}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\|Hu\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

which gives (
7.2.15
).
∎

We leave for the reader to check the two other cases (
7.2.5
), (
7.2.6
),
which are very similar as well as the degenerate cases excluded by (
7.2.2
), which are in fact easier to tackle.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\mathscr{T}=\bigl\{\mathcal{T}_{L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}}^{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}\bigr\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{j}\in\mathbb{R},\ L_{j}\\
\text{linear form on $\mathbb{R}^{2}$}\end{subarray}}
be the set of triangles of

For all

, self-adjoint and
we have

(7.2.17)

1.007680\approx\mu_{2}^{+}=\sup_{\mathcal{C}\text{ cone}}\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}})}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\\
\leq\mu_{3}^{+}=\sup_{\mathcal{T}\text{ triangle}}\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{T}})}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{3}\approx 1.213668.

N.B
.

The

) (resp. (

),

)) they are the union of two cones (resp. one cone) with a strip

.
What matters most in the above statement is the effective explicit bound.
Our result does not give an explicit value for
\mu_{3}^{+}
and it is quite likely that the bound given by
\tilde{\mu}_{3}
is way too large.

Proof.

The second inequality is proven in Propositions
7.4
&

and the first inequality is a consequence of Theorem
5.26
.
∎

Remark 7.7
.

This implies that for any

7.3.
Convex Polygons

We want to tackle now the general case of a convex polygon in the plane. We consider

L_{1},\dots,L_{N},

to be
N
linear forms of
x,\xi
(
L_{j}(x,\xi)=a_{j}\xi-\alpha_{j}x=[(x,\xi);(a_{j},\alpha_{j})]
) and
c_{1},\dots,c_{N}
some real constants.
We consider the convex polygon

(7.3.1)

\mathcal{P}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\ \forall j\in\{1,\dots,N\},L_{j}(x,\xi)-c_{j}\geq 0\},

so that

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(x,\xi)=\prod_{1\leq j\leq N}H\bigl(L_{j}(x,\xi)-c_{j}\bigr).

Definition 7.8
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
L_{1},\dots L_{N}
be linear forms on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and let
c_{1},\dots,c_{N}
be real numbers. The polygon with
N
sides
\mathcal{P}^{c_{1},\dots,c_{N}}_{L_{1},\dots,L_{N}}
is defined by (
7.3.1
). We shall denote by
\mathscr{P}_{N}
the set of all polygons with
N
sides.

N.B
.

Since we may take some
L_{j}=0
in (
7.3.1
), we see that
\mathscr{P}_{N}\subset\mathscr{P}_{N+1}
.

Note as above that it includes some convex subsets of the plane with infinite area such as (
7.2.3
).

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
\mathscr{P}_{N}
be the set of convex polygons with
N
sides of the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We define

(7.3.2)

\mu_{N}^{+}=\sup_{\mathcal{P}\in\mathscr{P}_{N}}\|{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))}

Then
\mu_{2}^{+}
is given by Theorem
5.20
and

(7.3.3)

\forall N\geq 3,\quad\mu_{N}^{+}\leq\sqrt{N/2}.

Proof.

Using an affine symplectic transformation, we may assume that
L_{N}(x,\xi)-c_{N}=x
,
so that

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}}(x,\xi)=H(x)\prod_{1\leq j\leq N-1}H\bigl(a_{j}\xi-\alpha_{j}x-c_{j}\bigr).

and the kernel of the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}
is

k_{N}(x,y)=H(x+y)\int e^{2i\pi(x-y)\xi}\prod_{1\leq j\leq N-1}H\bigl(a_{j}\xi-\alpha_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})-c_{j}\bigr)d\xi.

As a result, we have

k_{N}(x,y)=H(x+y)k_{N-1}(x,y),

where
k_{N-1}
is the kernel of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{N-1}})}
,
where

\mathcal{P}_{N-1}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\forall j\in\{1,\dots,N-1\},L_{j}(x,\xi)-c_{j}\geq 0\}.

We may assume inductively that for any convex polygon
\mathcal{P}_{k}
with
k\leq N-1
sides,
there exist
\mu_{k}^{+}
such that

(7.3.4)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}})}\leq\mu_{k}^{+},

where
\mu_{k}^{+}
depends only on
k
and not on the area of the polygon, a fact already proven
for
k=1,2,3
.
We note that with
A_{N}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{N}})}
,
we have with
H
standing for the operator of multiplication by
H(x)
,

HA_{N}H=HA_{N-1}H,\quad A_{N-1}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{N-1}})},

since the kernel of
HA_{N}H
is

H(x)H(y)k_{N}(x,y)=H(x+y)H(x)H(y)k_{N-1}(x,y)=H(x)H(y)k_{N-1}(x,y).

Also we have, with
\check{H}(x)=H(-x)
, that

\check{H}A_{N}\check{H}=0,

since the kernel of that operator is

\check{H}(x)\check{H}(y)H(x+y)k_{N-1}(x,y)=0.

We have thus

(7.3.5)

A_{N}=HA_{N-1}H+2\operatorname{Re}\check{H}A_{N}H,

and the kernel of
2\operatorname{Re}\check{H}A_{N}H
is

(7.3.6)

\omega_{N}(x,y)=H(x+y)\bigl(\check{H}(x)H(y)+\check{H}(y)H(x)\bigr)k_{N-1}(x,y).

We calculate now

(7.3.7)

k_{N-1}(x,y)=\int e^{2i\pi(x-y)\xi}\prod_{1\leq j\leq N-1}H\bigl(a_{j}\xi-\alpha_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})-c_{j}\bigr)d\xi.

We check first the
j

such that
a_{j}=0
(and thus
\alpha_{j}\not=0
)
25
25

25

In this induction proof, we may assume that all the linear forms
L_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq N
are different from 0, otherwise we may use the induction hypothesis.
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that this happens for

1\leq j<N_{0}
so that with some interval
J
of the real line,

\tilde{\alpha}_{j}=\alpha_{j}/a_{j},\tilde{c}_{j}=c_{j}/a_{j}
,

k_{N-1}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{J}(\frac{x+y}{2})\int e^{2i\pi(x-y)\xi}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{0}\leq j\leq N-1\\
a_{j}>0\end{subarray}}H\bigl(\xi-\tilde{\alpha}_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})-\tilde{c}_{j}\bigr)\\
\times\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{0}\leq j\leq N-1\\
a_{j}<0\end{subarray}}\check{H}\bigl(\xi-\tilde{\alpha}_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})-\tilde{c}_{j}\bigr)d\xi.

We note that the integration domain is

\psi(\frac{x+y}{2})=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{0}\leq j\leq N-1\\
a_{j}>0\end{subarray}}\bigl(\tilde{\alpha}_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})+\tilde{c}_{j}\bigr)\leq\xi\leq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{0}\leq j\leq N-1\\
a_{j}<0\end{subarray}}\tilde{\alpha}_{j}(\frac{x+y}{2})+\tilde{c}_{j}=-\phi(\frac{x+y}{2}),

with
\phi,\psi
convex piecewise affine functions;
since
\phi+\psi
is also a convex function,
we get the – convex – constraint
(\phi+\psi)((x+y)/2)\leq 0
, so that
(x+y)/2
must belong to a subinterval
\tilde{J}
of the interval
J
.
As a result we get that

\displaystyle k_{N-1}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{J}}(\frac{x+y}{2})\frac{e^{-2i\pi(x-y)\phi(\frac{x+y}{2})}-e^{2i\pi(x-y)\psi(\frac{x+y}{2})}}{2i\pi(x-y)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{J}}(\frac{x+y}{2})e^{{-i\pi(x-y)(\phi-\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})}}\frac{e^{-i\pi(x-y)(\phi+\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})}-e^{i\pi(x-y)(\phi+\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})}}{2i\pi(x-y)}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{J}}(\frac{x+y}{2})e^{{-i\pi(x-y)(\phi-\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})}}\frac{\sin\bigl(\pi(x-y)(\phi+\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})\bigr)}{\pi(y-x)},

and thus the kernel of
2\operatorname{Re}\check{H}A_{N}H
is

\omega_{N}(x,y)=H(x+y)\bigl(\check{H}(x)H(y)+\check{H}(y)H(x)\bigr)\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{J}}(\frac{x+y}{2})\\
\times e^{{-i\pi(x-y)(\phi-\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})}}\frac{\sin\bigl(\pi(x-y)(\phi+\psi)(\frac{x+y}{2})\bigr)}{\pi(y-x)},

so that, thanks to Proposition
9.30
[2],

(7.3.8)

2\operatorname{Re}\langle\check{H}A_{N}Hu,u\rangle\leq\|Hu\|\|\check{H}u\|,

and with (
7.3.5
),

\langle A_{N}u,u\rangle\leq\mu_{N-1}^{+}\|Hu\|^{2}+\|Hu\|\|\check{H}u\|,

we get

(7.3.9)

\mu_{N}^{+}\leq\frac{\mu_{N-1}^{+}+\sqrt{(\mu_{N-1}^{+})^{2}+1}}{2},

which implies that

(7.3.10)

\forall N\geq 3,\quad\mu_{N}^{+}\leq\sqrt{N/2},

since it is true for
N=3
and
26
26

26

Indeed we have

\mu_{3}^{+}\leq\tilde{\mu}_{3}<1.2137<1.2247\approx\sqrt{3/2}
.
if we assume that it is true for some
N\geq 3
,
we get
