\mu_{N+1}^{+}\leq\frac{\mu_{N}^{+}+\sqrt{(\mu_{N}^{+})^{2}+1}}{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\sqrt{\frac{N}{2}}+\sqrt{\frac{N+2}{2}}\bigr)\leq\sqrt{\frac{N+1}{2}},

where the latter inequality follows from the concavity of the square-root function since we have for a concave function
F
,

\frac{1}{2}\frac{N}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{N+2}{2}=\frac{N+1}{2}\text{\quad and thus \quad}\frac{1}{2}F\bigl(\frac{N}{2}\bigr)+\frac{1}{2}F\bigl(\frac{N+2}{2}\bigr)\leq F\bigl(\frac{N+1}{2}\bigr).

The proof of Theorem
7.9
is complete.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

The above result is weak by its dependence on the number of sides, but it should be pointed out that it is independent of the area of the polygon (which could be infinite). Another general comment is concerned with convexity: although Flandrin’s conjecture is not true,
there is still something special about convex subsets of the phase space and it is in particular interesting that an essentially explicit calculation of the kernel of the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}

is tractable when
\mathcal{P}
is a polygon with
N
sides of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

7.4.
Symbols supported in a half-space

Theorem 7.11
.

\mathbf{[1]}
Let
A
be a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

such that its Weyl symbol
a(x,\xi)
is supported in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
.
Then with
\check{H}
standing for the orthogonal projection onto

(7.4.1)

\{u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \operatorname{supp}u\subset\mathbb{R}_{-}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\},

we have

\check{H}A\check{H}=0.

\mathbf{[2]}
Let
A
be as above; if
A
is a non-negative operator, then with
H=I-\check{H}
, we have

\check{H}A=A\check{H}=0,\quad A=HAH,

N.B
.

We have seen explicit examples of bounded self-adjoint operators such that the Weyl symbol is supported in
x\geq 0
but for which
\check{H}AH\not=0
: the quarter-plane operator (see Section

5.1
)
has the Weyl symbol
H(x)H(\xi)
, the kernel of

\check{H}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}H\quad\text{ is }\quad\check{H}(x)H(y)H(x+y)\frac{1}{2i\pi}\text{pv}\frac{1}{y-x},

which is not the zero distribution and, according to the above result, this alone implies that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H(x)H(\xi))}
cannot be non-negative.

Proof.

Let us prove first that

\check{H}A\check{H}=0
;
let
\phi,\psi\in C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

\operatorname{supp}\phi\cup\operatorname{supp}\psi\subset(-{\infty},0)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}.

Since the Wigner distribution
\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)

belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

and is given by the integral

\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)(x,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{\psi}(x-\frac{z}{2})e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}dz,

we infer right away
27
27

27

In the integrand, we must have,

x_{1}+\frac{z_{1}}{2}\leq-\epsilon_{0}<0,x_{1}-\frac{z_{1}}{2}\leq-\epsilon_{1}<0
and thus
x_{1}\leq-(\epsilon_{0}+\epsilon_{1})/2
that
\operatorname{supp}\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)\subset(-{\infty},0)\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
. We know also that

\langle A\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle A\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle a,\mathcal{W}(\phi,\psi)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})}=0.

As a result, the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
bounded operator
\check{H}A\check{H}
is such that,
for
u,v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\phi,\psi
as above,

\langle\check{H}A\check{H}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle\check{H}A\check{H}\check{H}u,\check{H}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
=\langle\check{H}A\check{H}(\check{H}u-\phi),\check{H}v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\langle\check{H}A\check{H}\phi,\check{H}v-\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\underbrace{\langle\check{H}A\check{H}\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}_{\langle A\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0},

so that

|\langle\check{H}A\check{H}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}|\leq\|A\|_{\mathcal{B}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))}\Bigl(\|\check{H}u-\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|v\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\\
+\|\check{H}v-\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Bigr).

Using now that the set
\{\phi\in C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\operatorname{supp}\phi\subset(-{\infty},0)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\}

is dense
28
28

28

Let
\chi_{0}
be a function satisfying (
5.2.1
)
and let
w
be in the set (
7.4.2
). Let
(\phi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be a sequence in
C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

converging in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

towards
w
;
the function defined by

\tilde{\phi}_{k}(x)=\chi_{0}(-kx_{1})\phi_{k}(x),

belongs to
C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
is supported in
(-{\infty},-1/k]\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, and that sequence converges in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

towards
w
since

\|\tilde{\phi}_{k}-w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\underbrace{\|\chi_{0}(-kx_{1})\bigl(\phi_{k}(x)-w(x)\bigr)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}_{\leq\|\phi_{k}-w\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\rightarrow\ 0\ \text{when $k\rightarrow+{\infty}$.}}+\|(\chi_{0}(-kx_{1})-1)w(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

and

\|(\chi_{0}(-kx_{1})-1)w(x)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}^{2}\leq\int\mathbf{1}\bigl\{-\frac{2}{k}\leq x_{1}\leq 0\bigr\}|w(x)|^{2}dx

which has also limit 0 when
k
goes to
+\infty

by the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem.

in

(7.4.2)

\{w\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \operatorname{supp}w\subset(-{\infty},0]\times\mathbb{R}^{n-1}\},

we obtain that

\langle\check{H}A\check{H}u,v\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0
and the first result.
Let us assume that the operator
A
is non-negative.
We have

A=B^{2},\quad B=B^{*}\text{ bounded self-adjoint.}

It implies with
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
norms and dot-products,

\displaystyle\langle Au,u\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle HAHu,u\rangle+2\operatorname{Re}\langle\check{H}AHu,\check{H}u\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle HBBHu,u\rangle+2\operatorname{Re}\langle\check{H}BBHu,\check{H}u\rangle

\displaystyle=\|BHu\|^{2}+2\operatorname{Re}\langle BHu,B\check{H}u\rangle

\displaystyle=\|BHu+B\check{H}u\|^{2}-\|B\check{H}u\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\|Bu\|^{2}-\|B\check{H}u\|^{2}=\langle Au,u\rangle-\|B\check{H}u\|^{2},

and thus

B\check{H}=0
, so that
\check{H}B=0
and thus
\check{H}B^{2}=\check{H}A=0=A\check{H}
,
so that
\check{H}AH=0=HA\check{H}
,
and

A=HAH,

concluding the proof of [2].
∎

Corollary 7.12
.

Let
A
be a bounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that its Weyl symbol is supported in
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n-1}
and
such that
\operatorname{Re}(\check{H}AH)\not=0
, then the spectrum of
A
intersects
(-{\infty},0)
.

Proof.

We have from
[1]
in the previous
theorem,

A=(H+\check{H})A(H+\check{H})=HAH+2\operatorname{Re}HA\check{H},

and from
[2]
, if
A
were non-negative, we would have
A\check{H}=0
and
\operatorname{Re}HA\check{H}=0
,
contradicting the assumption.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a compact convex body of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, we may use the fact (see e.g.
[
45
]
) that

\mathcal{C}=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathfrak{H}_{j}\ \text{closed half-spaces}\\
\text{containing $K$}\end{subarray}}\mathfrak{H}_{j}.

Then of course
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}})}

is a bounded self-adjoint
operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and if
\mathfrak{H}_{j}
is defined by

\mathfrak{H}_{j}=\{(x,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{2},L_{j}(x,\xi)\geq c_{j}\},

where
L_{j}
is a linear form on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
c_{j}
a real constant, we obtain with the symplectic covariance of the Weyl calculus, setting

\mathcal{H}_{j}(x,\xi)=H(L_{j}(x,\xi)-c_{j}),

that for all
\mathfrak{H}_{j}
closed half-spaces containing
K
, we have

(7.4.3)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{K})}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H_{j})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{K})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H_{j})}+2\operatorname{Re}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\check{H}_{j})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{K})}{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(H_{j})},

where
\check{H}(x,\xi)=H(-L_{j}(x,\xi)+c_{j})
.

8.
Open questions & Conjectures

In this section we review the rather long list of conjectures formulated in the text and we try to classify their statements by rating their respective interest, relevance and difficulty.
We should keep in mind that the study of
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
for a subset
E
of the phase space is highly correlated to some particular set of special functions related to
E
:
Hermite functions and Laguerre polynomials for ellipses,
Airy functions for parabolas,
homogeneous distributions for hyperbolas and so on. It is quite likely
that the “shape” of
E
will determine the type of special functions to be studied
to getting a diagonalization of the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
.

8.1.
Anisotropic Ellipsoids & Paraboloids

Conjecture 8.1
.

Let
E
be an ellipsoid in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
equipped with its canonical symplectic structure. Then the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
(which is obvious from (
1.2.8
)) and we have

(8.1.1)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}\leq\operatorname{Id}.

A sharp version of this result was proven for
n=1

in the 1988 P. Flandrin’s article
[
13
]
, and was improved to an isotropic higher dimensional setting in the
paper
[
39
]
by E. Lieb
and Y. Ostrover. Without isotropy, it remains a conjecture. As described in more details in
Section
3.4
, it can be reformulated as a problem on Laguerre polynomials.
That conjecture is a very natural one and it would be quite surprising that a counterexample to (
8.1.1
) could occur from an anisotropic ellipsoid
29
29

29

We mean by anisotropic ellipsoid a set of type (
3.3.2
) where
0<a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{n}.
.
We introduced in Section
4.4
a conjecture on anisotropic paraboloids
directly related to Conjecture
8.1
.

Conjecture 8.2
.

Let
E
be an anisotropic paraboloid in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
equipped with its canonical symplectic structure. Then the operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}
is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and we have

(8.1.2)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{E})}\leq\operatorname{Id}.

In terms of special functions, it is related to a property of Airy-type functions. As a contrast with ellipses, we do not expect (
8.1.2
) to leave any room for improvement
whereas (
8.1.1
) can certainly be improved with its right-hand-side replaced by a smaller operator as in (
3.2.5
).

8.2.
Balls for the
\ell^{p}
norm

We have seen in Section
5.3.2
that the quantization of the indicatrix of a
\ell^{p}

ball could have a spectrum intersecting
(1,+{\infty})
when
p\not=2
.
More generally one could raise the following question.

Question 8.3
.

Let
p\in[1,+{\infty}]
,
p\not=2
and let
\mathbb{B}^{2n}_{p}
be the unit
\ell^{p}
ball in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. For
\lambda>0
,
we define the operator

(8.2.1)

P_{n,p,\lambda}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\lambda\mathbb{B}^{2n}_{p}})}.

Is it possible to say something on the spectrum of the operator
P_{n,p,\lambda}
,
even in a two-dimensional phase space (
n=1
)?
Is there an asymptotic behaviour for the upper bound of the spectrum of

P_{n,p,\lambda}

when
\lambda
goes to
+\infty
?

8.3.
On generic pulses in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

We have seen that the set
\mathcal{G}
defined in (
6.3.3
)
is generic in the Baire category sense, but our explicit examples were quite
simplistic.

Question 8.4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be defined in (
6.3.3
). Does there exist
u\in\mathcal{G}
such that
the set
E_{+}(u)

(defined in (
6.4.5
))
is connected?

8.4.
On convex bodies

Conjecture 8.5
.

For
N\geq 2
, we define

(8.4.1)

\mu_{N}^{+}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\mathcal{P}$ convex bounded}\\
\text{ polygon with $N$ sides}\end{subarray}}\text{\rm Spectrum}\left({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{P}})}\right).

Then the sequence
(\mu_{N}^{+})_{N\geq 2}
is increasing
30
30

30

According to our Definition
7.8
of the set
\mathscr{P}_{N}
of polygons with
N
sides is increasing with respect to
N
.

and there exists
\alpha>0

such that

(8.4.2)

\forall N\geq 2,\quad\mu_{N}^{+}\leq\alpha\ln N.

N.B
.

Theorem
7.9
is a small step in this direction.

A stronger version of Conjecture
8.5
would be

Conjecture 8.6
.

We define

(8.4.3)

\mu^{+}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$\mathcal{C}$ convex }\\
\text{bounded}\end{subarray}}\text{\rm Spectrum}\left({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}})}\right).

Then we have
\mu^{+}<+{\infty}
.

The invalid Flandrin’s conjecture was
\mu^{+}=1
and we know now that
\mu_{+}\geq\mu_{2}^{+}>1
as given
by (
7.1.3
).

Question 8.7
.

There is a diagonalization of the quantization of the indicator function of Ellipsoids, Paraboloids and Hyperbolic regions. Is there a non-quadratic example of diagonalization?

Question 8.8
.

The value of
\mu_{2}^{+}
is known explicitly, but for
\mu_{3}^{+}
, we have only the upperbound
\tilde{\mu}_{3}
as given by Theorem
7.6
. Is it possible to determine explicitly the value of
\mu_{3}^{+}
,
either by answering Question
8.7
, or via another argument?

Conjecture 8.9
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a proper closed convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with positive Lebesgue measure
such that
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}})}
is bounded self-adjoint on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})

(that assumption is useless if Conjecture
8.6
is proven) with a spectrum included in
[0,1]
. Then
\mathcal{C}
is the strip
[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}
, up to an affine symplectic map.

All the explicitly avalaible examples are compatible with that conjecture (see also Remark
7.2
)
and the second part of Theorem
7.11
is also an indication in that direction. It would be nice in that instance to reach a spectral characterization of a subset modulo the affine symplectic group.

9.
Appendix

9.1.
Fourier transform, Weyl quantization, Harmonic Oscillator

9.1.1.
Fourier transform.

We use in this paper the following normalization for the Fourier transform and inversion formula: for
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(9.1.1)

\hat{u}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{-2i\pi x\cdot\xi}u(x)dx,\quad u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2i\pi x\cdot\xi}\hat{u}(\xi)d\xi,

a formula that can be extended to
u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
with defining the distribution
\hat{u}
by the duality bracket

(9.1.2)

\langle\hat{u},\phi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle u,\hat{\phi}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Checking (
9.1.1
) for
u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is then easy, that is

(9.1.3)

\check{\hat{\hat{u}}}=u,

where the distribution
\check{u}
is defined by

(9.1.4)

\langle\check{u},\phi\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle u,\check{\phi}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\text{with}\ \check{\phi}(x)=\phi(-x).

It is useful to notice that for
u\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

(9.1.5)

\check{\hat{u}}=\hat{\check{u}}.

Using (
1.2.9
) and denoting the Fourier transformation
by
\mathcal{F}
, (
9.1.3
) and
(
9.1.5
) read

(9.1.6)

\sigma_{0}\mathcal{F}^{2}=\operatorname{Id},\quad[\mathcal{F},\sigma_{0}]=0,\quad{\text{so that}\quad\mathcal{F}^{*}=\mathcal{F}^{-1}=\sigma_{0}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}\sigma_{0}.}

This normalization yields simple formulas for the Fourier transform of Gaussian functions: for
A
a real-valued symmetric positive definite
n\times n
matrix, we define the function
v_{A}
in the Schwartz space by

(9.1.7)

v_{A}(x)=e^{-\pi\langle Ax,x\rangle},\quad\text{and we have }\quad\widehat{v_{A}}(\xi)=(\det A)^{-1/2}e^{-\pi\langle A^{-1}\xi,\xi\rangle}.

Similarly when
B
is
a real-valued symmetric non-singular
n\times n
matrix, the function
w_{B}

defined by

w_{B}(x)=e^{i\pi\langle Bx,x\rangle}

is in
L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and thus a tempered distribution and we have

(9.1.8)

\widehat{w_{B}}(\xi)=|\det B|^{-1/2}e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sign}B}e^{-i\pi\langle B^{-1}\xi,\xi\rangle},

where
\operatorname{sign}B

stands for the
signature of
B
that is, with
\mathtt{E}
the set of eigenvalues of
B

(which are real and non-zero),

(9.1.9)

\operatorname{sign}B=\underbrace{\operatorname{Card}(\mathtt{E}\cap\mathbb{R}_{+})}_{\nu_{+}(B)}-\underbrace{\operatorname{Card}(\mathtt{E}\cap\mathbb{R}_{-})}_{\nu_{-}(B)=\operatorname{index}{(B)}}.

The integer
\nu_{-}(B)
is called the
index
of
B
, noted
\operatorname{index}{(B)}
; Formula
(
9.1.8
) can be written as

(9.1.10)

e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\left(e^{i\pi\langle Bx,x\rangle}\right)=i^{-{\operatorname{index}{B}}}|\det B|^{-1/2}e^{-i\pi\langle B^{-1}\xi,\xi\rangle},

since
\nu_{+}+\nu_{-}=n
(as
B
is non-singular),

e^{\frac{i\pi n}{4}}e^{-\frac{i\pi\nu_{-}}{2}}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}(\nu_{+}+\nu_{-}-2\nu_{-})}=e^{\frac{i\pi}{4}\operatorname{sign}(B)}.

We note also that

(9.1.11)

\operatorname{sign}(\det B)=(-1)^{\operatorname{index}{B}},

so that

\left(i^{-{\operatorname{index}{B}}}|\det B|^{-1/2}\right)^{2}=(-1)^{\nu_{-}}|\det B|^{-1}=\operatorname{sign}(\det B)|\det B|^{-1}=(\det B)^{-1},

and thus the prefactor
i^{-{\operatorname{index}{B}}}|\det B|^{-1/2}
in the rhs of (
9.1.10
) is a square root of

1/\det B
.

With

H
standing for the characteristic function of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
we have

\displaystyle 1=H+\check{H},\quad\delta_{0}=\hat{H}+\hat{\check{H}},

\displaystyle D\operatorname{sign}=\frac{\delta_{0}}{i\pi},\quad\widehat{D\operatorname{sign}}=\frac{1}{i\pi},\quad\xi\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}=\frac{1}{i\pi},\quad\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}=\frac{1}{i\pi}\text{pv}{\frac{1}{\xi}},\quad\text{\footnotesize(principal value)}

the latter formula following from the fact that

\xi\bigl(\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}-\text{pv}\frac{1}{i\pi\xi}\bigr)=0,\text{\quad which implies\quad}\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}-\text{pv}\frac{1}{i\pi\xi}=c\delta_{0}=0,

since
\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}-\frac{1}{i\pi\xi}
is odd.
We infer from that

\displaystyle\hat{H}-\widehat{\check{H}}=\widehat{\operatorname{sign}}=\text{pv}\frac{1}{i\pi\xi},

and

(9.1.12)

\hat{H}=\frac{\delta_{0}}{2}+\text{pv}\frac{1}{2i\pi\xi}.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
T
be a compactly supported distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

(9.1.13)

\forall N\in\mathbb{N},\quad\langle\xi\rangle^{N}\hat{T}(\xi)\quad\text{is bounded, with $\langle\xi\rangle=\sqrt{1+|\xi|^{2}}$.}

Then
T
is a
C^{{\infty}}
function.

Proof.

Note that
\hat{T}
is an entire function, as the Fourier transform of a compactly supported distribution. Moreover, from (
9.1.13
) with
N=n+1
, we get that
\hat{T}
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and thus
T
is a continuous function. Moreover,
we have for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

(D_{x}^{\alpha}T)(x)=\int e^{2i\pi x\cdot\xi}\underbrace{\xi^{\alpha}\hat{T}(\xi)}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}d\xi,

so that
T
is a
C^{{\infty}}
function.
∎

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
\rho>0

and let
f
be an holomorphic function on a neighborhood of
\{z\in\mathbb{C},|\operatorname{Im}z|\leq\rho\}

such that

(9.1.14)

\displaystyle\forall y\in[-\rho,\rho],\ \int|f(x+iy)|dx<+{\infty},

(9.1.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{|y|\leq\rho}|f(\pm R+iy)|dy=0.

Then we have

(9.1.16)

\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R},\quad|\hat{f}(\xi)|\leq Ce^{-2\pi\rho|\xi|},

with

C=\max(C_{+},C_{-}),\ C_{\pm}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x\pm i\rho)|dx.

Conversely, if
f
is a bounded measurable function such that
\hat{f}(\xi)
is
O(e^{-2\pi r|\xi|})
for some
r>0
, then

f
is holomorphic on
\{z\in\mathbb{C},|\operatorname{Im}z|<r\}
.

Proof.

If
f
is holomorphic
near
\{z\in\mathbb{C},|\operatorname{Im}z|\leq\rho\}
,
satisfies (
9.1.14
)
and
(
9.1.15
), then Cauchy’s formula
shows that for
|y|\leq\rho
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle e^{-2i\pi(x+iy)\xi}f(x+iy)dx=e^{2\pi y\xi}\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{-R}^{R}e^{-2i\pi x\xi}f(x+iy)dx

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{[-R+iy,R+iy]}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}f(z)dz

\displaystyle=\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{[-R+iy,-R]\cup[-R,R]\cup[R,R+iy]}e^{-2i\pi z\xi}f(z)dz

\displaystyle=\hat{f}(\xi)+\lim_{R\rightarrow+{\infty}}\left(\int_{0}^{y}e^{-2i\pi(R+it)\xi}f(R+it)idt-\int_{0}^{y}e^{-2i\pi(-R+it)\xi}f(-R+it)idt\right).

We have for
|y|\leq\rho
,

\Bigl|\int_{0}^{y}e^{-2i\pi(\pm R+it)\xi}f(\pm R+it)idt\Bigr|\leq\int_{|t|\leq\rho}|f(\pm R+it)|dt\ e^{2\pi\rho|\xi|},

which goes to 0 when
R
goes to
+{\infty}
, thanks to (
9.1.15
), so that for all
y\in[-\rho,\rho]
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-2i\pi(x+iy)\xi}f(x+iy)dx=\hat{f}(\xi),

which implies for
y=-\rho\operatorname{sign}\xi
(taken as 0, if
\xi=0
)

|\hat{f}(\xi)|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(x\mp i\rho)|dx\ e^{-2\pi\rho|\xi|}\underbrace{\leq}_{\text{from \eqref{firstc}}}Ce^{-2\pi\rho|\xi|},

proving the first part of the proposition.
Let us consider now a function
f
in
L^{{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\hat{f}(\xi)
is
O(e^{-2\pi r|\xi|})
for some
r>0
, and let
\rho\in(0,r)
.
We have

f(x)=\int e^{2i\pi x\xi}\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi

and for
|y|\leq\rho
, we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2\pi|y||\xi|}|\hat{f}(\xi)|d\xi<+{\infty},

so that

f
is holomorphic on
\{z\in\mathbb{C},|\operatorname{Im}z|<r\}
with

f(x+iy)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{2i\pi(x+iy)\xi}\hat{f}(\xi)d\xi,

concluding the proof.
∎

9.1.2.
Weyl quantization

Let
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
. We define the operator
{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}
, continuous from

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

into
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, given by the formula

(9.1.17)

({\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u)(x)=\iint e^{2i\pi(x-y)\cdot\xi}a(\frac{x+y}{2},\xi)u(y)dyd\xi,

to be understood weakly as

(9.1.18)

\langle{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)}u,\bar{v}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle a,\mathcal{W}(u,v)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

where the so-called Wigner function
\mathcal{W}(u,v)
is defined for
u,v\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
by

(9.1.19)

\mathcal{W}(u,v)(x,\xi)=\int e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}u(x+\frac{z}{2})\bar{v}(x-\frac{z}{2})dz.

We note that the sesquilinear mapping

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\ni(u,v)\mapsto\mathcal{W}(u,v)\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

is continuous so that the above bracket of duality

\langle a,\mathcal{W}(u,v)\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

makes sense.
We note as well that a temperate distribution
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
gets quantized
by a continuous operator
a^{w}
from

\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

into

\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Also, we find that
\mathcal{W}(u,u)
is real-valued since

\overline{\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi)}=\int e^{2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\bar{u}(x+\frac{z}{2})u(x-\frac{z}{2})dz\\
=\int e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}\bar{u}(x-\frac{z}{2})u(x+\frac{z}{2})dz=\mathcal{W}(u,u)(x,\xi).

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
a
be a tempered distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and let
b
be a polynomial of degree
d
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Then we have

(9.1.20)

\displaystyle a\sharp b

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq k\leq d}\omega_{k}(a,b),\quad\text{with}

(9.1.21)

\displaystyle\omega_{k}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(4i\pi)^{k}}\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=k}\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}a)(x,\xi)(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}b)(x,\xi),

(9.1.22)

\displaystyle\omega_{k}(b,a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\omega_{k}(a,b).

The Weyl symbol of the commutator
[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)},{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}]
is

(9.1.23)

c(a,b)=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq k\leq d\\
k\ \text{\rm odd}\end{subarray}}\omega_{k}(a,b).

If the degree of
b
is smaller than 2, we have

(9.1.24)

c(a,b)=2\omega_{1}(a,b)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left\{a,b\right\},

and if
a
is a function of
b
, the commutator
[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)},{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(b)}]=0
.

Remark 9.4
.

In particular if
q(x,\xi)
is a quadratic polynomial and

a(x,\xi)=H\bigl(1-q(x,\xi)\bigr),

is the characteristic function of the set
\{(x,\xi),q(x,\xi)\leq 1\}
,
then we have

(9.1.25)

\bigl[{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(a)},{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q)}\bigr]=0.

Proof.

Applying (
1.2.4
), (
1.2.5
), we obtain that this lemma follows from
(
9.1.22
), that we check now:

\displaystyle(4i\pi)^{k}\omega_{k}(a,b)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=k}\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}a)(x,\xi)(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}b)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=k}\frac{(-1)^{|\alpha|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a)(x,\xi)(\partial_{x}^{\beta}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}b)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=k}\frac{(-1)^{k-|\beta|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}a)(x,\xi)(\partial_{x}^{\beta}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}b)(x,\xi)=(-1)^{k}(4i\pi)^{k}\omega_{k}(b,a),

which is the sought result.∎

Remark 9.5
.

We can note that Formula (
1.2.100
) is non-local in the sense that for
a,b\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
with disjoint supports, although all
\omega_{k}(a,b)
(given by (
9.1.21
)) are identically 0, the function
a\sharp b
(which belongs to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
) is different from 0;
let us give an example. Let
\chi_{0}\in C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
with support
[-1+\epsilon_{0},1-\epsilon_{0}]
with
\epsilon_{0}\in(0,1)

and let us consider in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

a(x,\xi)=\chi_{0}(x)e^{-\pi\xi^{2}},\quad b(x,\xi)=\chi_{0}(x-2)e^{-\pi\xi^{2}},

so that
a,b
both belong to
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and

\operatorname{supp}a=[-1+\epsilon_{0},1-\epsilon_{0}]\times\mathbb{R},\quad\operatorname{supp}b=[1+\epsilon_{0},3-\epsilon_{0}]\times\mathbb{R},

so that the supports are disjoint and all
\omega_{k}(a,b)
are identically vanishing.
We check now

\displaystyle(a\sharp b)(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=4\iint\hskip-5.0pt\iint\chi_{0}(y)e^{-\pi\eta^{2}}\chi_{0}(z-2)e^{-\pi\zeta^{2}}e^{-4i\pi(\xi-\eta)(x-z)}e^{4i\pi(x-y)(\xi-\zeta)}dyd\eta dzd\zeta

\displaystyle=4\iint\chi_{0}(y)\chi_{0}(z-2)e^{-4\pi(x-z)^{2}}e^{-4\pi(x-y)^{2}}e^{4i\pi\xi(z-x+x-y)}dydz

\displaystyle=4\Bigl(\int\chi_{0}(y)e^{-4i\pi\xi y}e^{-4\pi(x-y)^{2}}dy\Bigr)\Bigl(\int\chi_{0}(z)e^{4i\pi\xi z}e^{-4\pi(x-2-z)^{2}}dz\Bigr),

so that

(a\sharp b)(0,0)=4\underbracket{\Bigl(\int\chi_{0}(y)e^{-4\pi y^{2}}dy\Bigr)}_{>0}\underbracket{\Bigl(\int\chi_{0}(z)e^{-4\pi(2+z)^{2}}dz\Bigr)}_{>0}>0.

9.1.3.
Some explicit computations

We may also calculate with

(9.1.26)

u_{a}(x)=(2a)^{1/4}e^{-\pi ax^{2}},a>0,

(9.1.27)

\mathcal{W}(u_{a},u_{a})(x,\xi)=(2a)^{1/2}\int e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}e^{-\pi a|x-\frac{z}{2}|^{2}}e^{-\pi a|x+\frac{z}{2}|^{2}}dz\\
=(2a)^{1/2}\int e^{-2i\pi z\cdot\xi}e^{-2\pi ax^{2}}e^{-\pi az^{2}/2}dz=(2a)^{1/2}e^{-2\pi ax^{2}}2^{1/2}a^{-1/2}e^{-\pi\frac{2}{a}\xi^{2}}\\
=2e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})},

which is also a Gaussian function on the phase space (and positive function).
The calculation of
\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})(x,\xi)
is interesting since we have

4\pi^{2}\langle D_{x}b^{w}D_{x}u_{a},\bar{u}_{a}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

=\langle b^{w}u^{\prime}_{a},\bar{u}^{\prime}_{a}\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\langle b,\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})\rangle_{\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})},

and for
b(x,\xi)
real-valued we have

\xi\sharp b\sharp\xi=\bigl(\xi b+\frac{b^{\prime}_{x}}{4i\pi}\bigr)\sharp\xi=\xi^{2}b+\frac{b^{\prime}_{x}\xi}{4i\pi}-\frac{\partial_{x}}{4i\pi}\bigl(\xi b+\frac{b^{\prime}_{x}}{4i\pi}\bigr)=\xi^{2}b+\frac{b^{\prime\prime}_{xx}}{16\pi^{2}},

so that

4\pi^{2}\iint 2e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})}\bigl(\xi^{2}b+\frac{b^{\prime\prime}_{xx}}{16\pi^{2}}\bigr)dxd\xi=\langle b,\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})\rangle,

proving that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})(x,\xi)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})}4\pi^{2}\xi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}2\partial_{x}^{2}\bigl(e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})}\bigr)

\displaystyle=2e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})}\bigl(4\pi^{2}\xi^{2}+\frac{1}{4}((-4\pi ax)^{2}-4\pi a)\bigl)

\displaystyle=8\pi^{2}e^{-2\pi(ax^{2}+a^{-1}\xi^{2})}a\bigl(a^{-1}\xi^{2}+ax^{2}-\frac{1}{4\pi}\bigr).

We obtain that the function
\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})
is negative on

a^{-1}\xi^{2}+ax^{2}<\frac{1}{4\pi},

which has area
1/4
.
We may note as well for consistency that for
u_{a}
given by (
9.1.26
),
we have

u^{\prime}_{a}=(2a)^{1/4}(-2\pi ax)e^{-\pi ax^{2}},\quad\|u^{\prime}_{a}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\pi a,

and

\displaystyle\iint\mathcal{W}(u^{\prime}_{a},u^{\prime}_{a})(x,\xi)dxd\xi

\displaystyle=8\pi^{2}a\iint e^{-2\pi(y^{2}+\eta^{2})}(y^{2}+\eta^{2}-\frac{1}{4\pi})dyd\eta

\displaystyle=\frac{8\pi^{2}a}{8\pi}=\pi a=\|u^{\prime}_{a}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

For
\lambda>0
and
a\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, we define

(9.1.28)

a_{\lambda}(x,\xi)=a(\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda\xi),

and we find that

(9.1.29)

\displaystyle(a_{\lambda})^{w}=U_{\lambda}^{*}a^{w}U_{\lambda},

(9.1.30)

\displaystyle\text{for $f\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})$, }(U_{\lambda}f)(x)=f(\lambda x)\lambda^{n/2},\quad U_{\lambda}^{*}=U_{\lambda^{-1}}=(U_{\lambda})^{-1}.

We note that the above formula is a particular case of
Segal’s Formula
(see e.g. Theorem 2.1.2 in
[
33
]
).

9.1.4.
The Harmonic Oscillator

The Harmonic oscillator
\mathcal{H}_{n}
in
n
dimensions is defined as the operator with Weyl symbol

\pi(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})
and thus from (
9.1.29
), we find that

\mathcal{H}=U_{\sqrt{2\pi}}\ \frac{1}{2}\bigl(|x|^{2}+4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{2}\bigr)^{w}U_{\sqrt{2\pi}}^{*}=U_{\sqrt{2\pi}}\ \frac{1}{2}\bigl(-\Delta+|x|^{2}\bigr)U_{\sqrt{2\pi}}^{*}.

We shall define in one dimension the Hermite function of level
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
by

(9.1.31)

\psi_{k}(x)=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{2^{k}\sqrt{k!}}2^{1/4}e^{\pi x^{2}}\left(\frac{d}{\sqrt{\pi}dx}\right)^{k}(e^{-2\pi x^{2}}),

and we find that
(\psi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Hilbertian orthonormal basis on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
The one-dimensional harmonic oscillator can be written as

(9.1.32)

\mathcal{H}_{1}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(\frac{1}{2}+k)\mathbb{P}_{k},

where
\mathbb{P}_{k}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\psi_{k}
.

In
n
dimensions, we consider a multi-index
(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n})=\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}

and we define on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, using the one-dimensional (
9.1.31
),

(9.1.33)

\Psi_{\alpha}(x)=\prod_{1\leq j\leq n}\psi_{\alpha_{j}}(x_{j}),\quad\mathcal{E}_{k}=\text{Vect}\bigl\{\Psi_{\alpha}\bigr\}_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},|\alpha|=k},\quad|\alpha|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\alpha_{j}.

We note that

(9.1.34)

the dimension of
\mathcal{E}_{k,n}
is
\binom{k+n-1}{n-1}

and that (
9.1.32
) holds with

\mathbb{P}_{k;n}
standing for the orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{E}_{k,n}
;
the lowest eigenvalue of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is
n/2
and the corresponding eigenspace is one-dimensional
in all dimensions, although in two and more dimensions, the eigenspaces corresponding to the eigenvalue

\frac{n}{2}+k,k\geq 1
are multi-dimensional with dimension
\binom{k+n-1}{n-1}
.
The
n
-dimensional harmonic oscillator can be written as

(9.1.35)

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(\frac{n}{2}+k)\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

where
\mathbb{P}_{k;n}
stands for the orthogonal projection
onto
\mathcal{E}_{k,n}
defined above.
We have in particular

(9.1.36)

\mathbb{P}_{k;n}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},|\alpha|=k}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha},\quad\text{where $\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}$ is the orthogonal projection onto $\Psi_{\alpha}$.}

9.1.5.
On the spectrum of the anisotropic harmonic oscillator

The standard
n
-dimensional harmonic oscillator is the operator

\mathcal{H}_{n}=\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}(D_{j}^{2}+x_{j}^{2}),\text{ $D_{j}=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{x_{j}}$},

and its spectral decomposition is

\mathcal{H}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(\frac{n}{2}+k)\mathbb{P}_{k;n},\quad\mathbb{P}_{k;n}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n},\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{n}=k}\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{n}},

where
\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{j}}
stands for the orthogonal projection onto the one-dimensional Hermite function with level
\alpha_{j}
. Now let us consider for
{\mu}=({\mu}_{1},\dots,{\mu}_{n})
with
\mu_{j}>0
,
the operator

(9.1.37)

\mathcal{H}_{(\mu)}=\pi\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mu}_{j}(D_{j}^{2}+x_{j}^{2})=\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(q_{\mu}(x,\xi))},

with

(9.1.38)

q_{\mu}(x,\xi)=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}\mu_{j}(x_{j}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2}).

With the notation
|{\mu}|=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mu}_{j}
and
{\mu}\cdot\alpha=\sum_{1\leq j\leq n}{\mu}_{j}\alpha_{j}
, we have

(9.1.39)

\mathcal{H}_{(\mu)}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}(\frac{|{\mu}|}{2}+{\mu}\cdot\alpha)\underbrace{(\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mathbb{P}_{\alpha_{n}})}_{\mathbb{P}_{\alpha}},

so that the eigenspaces are the same as for
\mathcal{H}_{n}
but the arithmetic properties of
{\mu}
make possible that all eigenvalues
(\frac{|{\mu}|}{2}+{\mu}\cdot\alpha)
are simple. For instance for

n=2,0<\mu_{1}<\mu_{2},\ \frac{\mu_{2}}{\mu_{1}}\notin\mathbb{Q},

if
\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is such that

\mu_{1}\beta_{1}+\mu_{2}\beta_{2}=0,

this implies that
\beta=0
and thus that all the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{H}_{(\mu)}
are simple.

Remark 9.6
.

If
0<\mu_{1}\leq\dots\leq\mu_{n}
and if for all
j\in[2,n]
we have
\mu_{j}/\mu_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
, we then have for
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,

\alpha\cdot\mu=\mu_{1}\underbrace{\Bigl(\alpha_{1}+\sum_{2\leq j\leq n}\frac{\alpha_{j}\mu_{j}}{\mu_{1}}\Bigr)}_{\beta_{1}}=\beta\cdot\mu,\quad\beta=(\beta_{1},0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}.

Sinus cardinal
.
It is a classical result of Distribution Theory that the weak limit when
\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}

of the Sinus Cardinal
\frac{\sin(\lambda x)}{x}
is
\pi\delta_{0}
, where
\delta_{0}
is the Dirac mass at 0, but we wish to extend that result to more general test functions.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
f
be a function in
L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
such that

\int_{|\tau|\geq 1}\frac{|f(\tau)|}{|\tau|}d\tau<+{\infty}\quad\text{and $\exists a\in\mathbb{C}$ so that\hskip 10.22217pt}\int_{|\tau|\leq 1}\frac{|f(\tau)-a|}{|\tau|}d\tau<+{\infty}.

Then we have

(9.1.40)

\lim_{\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}f(\tau)d\tau=a.

N.B
.

In particular if
f
is an Hölderian function such that
f(\tau)/\tau\in L^{1}(\{|\tau|\geq 1\})
we get that the left-hand-side of (
9.1.40
) equals
f(0)
.

Proof.

Let
\chi_{0}
be a function in
C^{{\infty}}_{\textit{c}}(\mathbb{R})
equal to 1 near the origin and let us define
\chi_{1}=1-\chi_{0}
. We have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}f(\tau)d\tau=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi}\underbrace{\frac{\bigl(f(\tau)-a\bigr)}{\tau}\chi_{0}(\tau)}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}d\tau+a\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}\chi_{0}(\tau)d\tau\\
+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi}\underbrace{f(\tau)\tau^{-1}\chi_{1}(\tau)}_{\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}d\tau,

so that the limit when
\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}
of the first and the third integral is zero, thanks to the Riemann-Lebesgue Lemma. We note also that

\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}=\widehat{\mathbf{1}_{[-\frac{\lambda}{2\pi},\frac{\lambda}{2\pi}]}}(\tau),

and applying Plancherel’s Formula to the second integral yields

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\sin(\lambda\tau)}{\pi\tau}\chi_{0}(\tau)d\tau=\int_{|t|\leq\lambda/(2\pi)}\widehat{\chi_{0}}(t)dt,

whose limit when
\lambda\rightarrow+{\infty}
is

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widehat{\chi_{0}}(t)dt=\chi_{0}(0)=1
, thanks to the Lebesgue Dominated Convergence Theorem, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

9.2.
Further properties of the metaplectic group

9.2.1.
Another set of generators for the metaplectic group

Definition 9.1
.

Let
P,L,Q
be
n\times n
real matrices such that
P=P^{*},Q=Q^{*}
and
\det L\not=0
.
We define the operator
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
by the formula

(9.2.1)

(\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}u)(x)=e^{-i\pi n/4}(\det L)^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{i\pi\{\langle Px,x\rangle-2\langle Lx,y\rangle+\langle Qy,y\rangle\}}u(y)dy.

N.B
.

In that definition,
(\det L)^{1/2}
stands for a choice of a square root of the real number
\det L
, that is
\pm\sqrt{\det L}
if
\det L>0
and
\pm i\sqrt{-\det L}
if
\det L<0
.

With
m(L)\in\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
defined by (
1.2.54
) we shall also define

(9.2.2)

(\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}u)(x)=e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}e^{\frac{i\pi m(L)}{2}}|\det L|^{1/2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{i\pi\{\langle Px,x\rangle-2\langle Lx,y\rangle+\langle Qy,y\rangle\}}u(y)dy.

Proposition 9.8
.

The operator
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
given in Definition
9.1
is an automorphism of
\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and of
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which is a unitary operator on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
belonging to the metaplectic group
(cf. Definition
1.17
). Moreover the metaplectic group is generated by the set
\{\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}P=P^{*},Q=Q^{*}\\
\det L\not=0\end{subarray}}
.

Proof.

Using the Notation (
1.2.48
) and (
1.2.58
), we see that
31
31

31

We note that
m(B)+n\in\{m(-B),m(-B)+2\}
modulo 4:
indeed we have modulo 4

\begin{cases}\text{for $n$ even,}&\underbracket{\{0,2\}}_{\det B>0}+n=\underbracket{\{0,2\}}_{\det(-B)>0},\qquad\underbracket{\{1,3\}}_{\det B<0}+n=\underbracket{\{1,3\}}_{\det(-B)<0},\\
\text{for $n$ odd,}&\underbracket{\{0,2\}}_{\det B>0}+n=\underbracket{\{1,3\}}_{\det(-B)<0},\qquad\underbracket{\{1,3\}}_{\det B<0}+n=\underbracket{\{0,2\}}_{\det(-B)>0}.\end{cases}

We have also

m(L)-n\in\{m(-L),m(-L)+2\}

since we know already (from the above in that footnote)
that
m(L)-n\in\{m(-L),m(-L)+2\}-2n
,
which gives

m(L)-n\in\{m(-L),m(-L)+2\}
for
n
even; for
n=2l+1
odd we get
the same result since

m(L)-n\in\{m(-L),m(-L)+2\}-4l-2=\{m(-L)-2,m(-L)\}=\{m(-L)+2,m(-L)\}.

(9.2.3)

M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}=\mathcal{M}_{A,-B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)+n\}}\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4},\quad\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}=M_{P,-L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)-n\}}\left(\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}\right)^{-1},

and (
1.2.67
) imply that the set
\{\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}\}
is included in
Mp(n)

(second formula in (
9.2.3
))
whereas the fact that

(9.2.4)

\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}=\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}},

the first formula in (
9.2.3
) and Definition
1.17
imply that
Mp(n)
is generated by the
set
\{\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}\}
, proving the Proposition.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

From (
9.2.3
), we deduce, noting that
m(I_{n})\in\{0,2\},m(-I_{n})\in\{n,n+2\}
,

(9.2.5)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle=M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{2\}}=\mathcal{M}_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n+2\}}\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}},\qquad\text{so that}

(9.2.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)+2\}}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}=\mathcal{M}_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n+2\}}\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}.

Lemma 9.10
.

With the homomorphism
\Psi
defined in (
1.2.71
) and defining

(9.2.7)

\Lambda_{P,L,Q}=\Psi(\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}),

we find that

(9.2.8)

\Lambda_{P,L,Q}=\begin{pmatrix}L^{-1}Q&L^{-1}\\
PL^{-1}Q-L^{*}&PL^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

Indeed, from the second formula in (
9.2.3
), (
1.2.60
), (
1.2.46
) and (
1.2.72
) we get that

\Lambda_{P,L,Q}=\Xi_{P,-L,Q}\ \Xi_{-I_{n},2^{1/2}I_{n},-I_{n}}^{-2}=\begin{pmatrix}-L^{-1}&L^{-1}Q\\
-PL^{-1}&-L^{*}+PL^{-1}Q\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix},

providing the sought result.
∎

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
P_{j},L_{j},Q_{j},j=1,2
be as in Definition
9.1
and let us assume that

(9.2.9)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}=e^{i\phi}\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\quad\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

Then we have

(9.2.10)

P_{1}+Q_{2}=Q_{1}+P_{2}=0,\quad L_{2}=-L_{1}^{*},\qquad e^{i\phi}\in\{\pm 1\}.

Proof.

The assumption (
9.2.9
) implies that both sides of the equality belong to
Mp(n)
and

\Lambda_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\Lambda_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}=\Psi\bigl(e^{i\phi}\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigr)=I_{2n},

where the last equality follows from the fact that
e^{i\phi}\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
commutes with every operator

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(L_{Y})}
given in Lemma
1.21
.
We have thus

\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}&L_{1}^{-1}\\
P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}-L_{1}^{*}&P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}&L_{2}^{-1}\\
P_{2}L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}-L_{2}^{*}&P_{2}L_{2}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix},

so that

first line
\times
second column:

\displaystyle L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}L_{2}^{-1}+L_{1}^{-1}P_{2}L_{2}^{-1}=0\Longrightarrow Q_{1}+P_{2}=0,

second line
\times
first column:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}(P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}-L_{1}^{*})L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}+P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}(P_{2}L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}-L_{2}^{*})=0,

second line
\times
second column:

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}-L_{1}^{*})L_{2}^{-1}+P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}P_{2}L_{2}^{-1}=I_{n},

which gives

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,1}(P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}-L_{1}^{*})L_{2}^{-1}+P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}\underbracket{P_{2}}_{-Q_{1}}L_{2}^{-1}=I_{n}\Longrightarrow-L_{1}^{*}L_{2}^{-1}=I_{n}\Longrightarrow L_{2}=-L_{1}^{*},

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0.8,0,0}P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}Q_{1}L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}\underbrace{-L_{1}^{*}L_{2}^{-1}}_{I_{n}}Q_{2}+P_{1}L_{1}^{-1}\underbracket{P_{2}}_{-Q_{1}}L_{2}^{-1}Q_{2}-P_{1}\underbrace{L_{1}^{-1}L_{2}^{*}}_{-I_{n}}=0\Longrightarrow P_{1}+Q_{2}=0,

providing the sought formulas in (
9.2.10
), except for the last one.
Let
\kappa_{j}
be the kernel of
\mathcal{M}_{P_{j},L_{j},Q_{j}}
and let
\kappa=\kappa_{1}\circ\kappa_{2}

be the kernel of the composition (in the lhs of (
9.2.9
)).
We have consequently

\displaystyle\kappa(x,y)

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det L_{2})^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}\int e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}-2L_{1}x\cdot z+Q_{1}z^{2}+P_{2}z^{2}-2L_{2}z\cdot y+Q_{2}y^{2}\}}dz

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det(-L_{1}^{*}))^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}-P_{1}y^{2}\}}\int e^{-2i\pi\{z\cdot(L_{1}x+L_{2}^{*}y)\}}dz

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det(-L_{1}^{*}))^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}-P_{1}y^{2}\}}\delta_{0}\bigl(L_{1}x+L_{2}^{*}y\bigr)

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det(-L_{1}^{*}))^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}-P_{1}y^{2}\}}\delta_{0}\bigl(x-y\bigr)|\det L_{1}|^{-1},

entailing

e^{i\phi}\delta_{0}(x-y)\underbracket{=}_{\eqref{926926}}\kappa(x,y)=e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{1}^{*})+n\right)}\delta_{0}(x-y)e^{-i\pi n/2}=e^{i\pi m(L_{1})}\delta_{0}(x-y),

proving that
e^{i\phi}=e^{i\pi m(L_{1})}\in\{\pm 1\}
. The proof of the lemma is complete.
∎

Claim 9.12
.

Let
P,L,Q
be as in Definition
9.1
. Then we have

(9.2.11)

(\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}})^{-1}=\mathcal{M}_{-Q,-L^{*},-P}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n-m(L)\}},

and moreover
n-m(L)\in\{m(-L^{*}),m(-L^{*})+2\}
modulo 4.

Indeed, calculating the kernel
\kappa
of
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}\mathcal{M}_{-Q,-L^{*},-P}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n-m(L)\}}
,
we get

\kappa(x,y)=e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}(m(L)+n-m(L)-n)}|\det L|\int e^{i\pi\{Px^{2}-2Lx\cdot z+Qz^{2}-Qz^{2}+2L^{*}z\cdot y-Py^{2}\}}dz\\
=|\det L|e^{i\pi\{Px^{2}-Py^{2}\}}\delta_{0}(Lx-Ly)=\delta_{0}(x-y),

so that

\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(L)\}}\mathcal{M}_{-Q,-L^{*},-P}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n-m(L)\}}=\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and since

\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
is unitary, this proves (
9.2.11
).
The last assertion is equivalent to

m(L)\in\{n-m(-L^{*}),n-m(-L^{*})-2\}.

Since the latter
set is equal to
\{-m(L),-m(L)-2\}
and the mapping

\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}\ni x\mapsto-x\in\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z},

leaves invariant the sets
\{0,2\},\{1,3\}
,
we obtain the sought result,
concluding the proof of the claim.∎

Proposition 9.13
.

Let
P_{j},L_{j},Q_{j},j=1,2
be as in Definition
9.1
and let us assume that

(9.2.12)

\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})\not=0.

Then there exist
P,L,Q,
as in Definition
9.1
such that

(9.2.13)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{m(L_{1})\}}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{m(L_{2})\}}}=\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\{m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-\operatorname{index}{(Q_{1}+P_{2})\}}}}.

More precisely, we have

(9.2.14)

\displaystyle P=P_{1}-L_{1}^{*}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1},\qquad Q=Q_{2}-L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{2}^{*},

(9.2.15)

\displaystyle L=L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1}.

Moreover we have

(9.2.16)

m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-\operatorname{index}{(Q_{1}+P_{2})}\in\{m(L),m(L)+2\}\ \mod 4.

Proof.

The kernel
\kappa
of
\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}
is

\displaystyle\kappa(x,y)

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det L_{2})^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}\int e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}-2L_{1}x\cdot z+Q_{1}z^{2}+P_{2}z^{2}-2L_{2}z\cdot y+Q_{2}y^{2}\}}dz

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det L_{2})^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}+Q_{2}y^{2}\}}\int e^{-2i\pi(L_{1}x+L_{2}^{*}y)\cdot z}e^{i\pi(Q_{1}+P_{2})z^{2}}dz

\displaystyle=(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det L_{2})^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}e^{i\pi\{P_{1}x^{2}+Q_{2}y^{2}\}}e^{-i\pi(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}(L_{1}x+L_{2}^{*}y)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 195.0pt\times|\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})|^{-1/2}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\operatorname{sign}(Q_{1}+P_{2})},

according to Formula (
9.1.8
) (see also (
9.1.10
)), noting that the matrix
Q_{1}+P_{2}
is real symmetric and non-singular.
As a result, we have

\kappa(x,y)=e^{i\pi\left\{\left(P_{1}-L_{1}^{*}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1}\right)x^{2}+\left(Q_{2}-L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{2}^{*}y^{2}\right)\right\}}e^{-2i\pi\left\{L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1}x\cdot y\right\}}\\
\times(\det L_{1})^{1/2}(\det L_{2})^{1/2}e^{-i\pi n/2}|\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})|^{-1/2}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\operatorname{sign}(Q_{1}+P_{2})}.

We note that, with
\mathtt{E_{12}}
standing for the eigenvalues of
Q_{1}+P_{2}
,

\displaystyle\nu_{+}=\operatorname{Card}(\mathtt{E_{12}}\cap\mathbb{R}_{+}),\qquad\nu_{-}=\operatorname{Card}(\mathtt{E_{12}}\cap\mathbb{R}_{-})=\operatorname{index}{(Q_{1}+P_{2})},

implying that the kernel
\kappa
is given by

(9.2.17)

\kappa(x,y)=e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-n+\frac{1}{2}(\nu_{+}-\nu_{-})\right)}|\det L|^{1/2}e^{i\pi\{Px^{2}-2Lx\cdot y+Qy^{2}\}},

with

(9.2.18)

\displaystyle P=P_{1}-L_{1}^{*}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1},\qquad Q=Q_{2}-L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{2}^{*},

(9.2.19)

\displaystyle L=L_{2}(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}L_{1}.

Checking the unit factor in front of the rhs of (
9.2.17
), we note that
\nu_{+}+\nu_{-}=n
since
Q_{1}+P_{2}

is non-singular and we get

e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-n+\frac{1}{2}(\nu_{+}-\nu_{-})\right)}=e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-\frac{n}{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\nu_{+}-\nu_{-})\right)}\\
=e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-\nu_{-}\right)}.

We have also, since
\operatorname{index}{(Q_{1}+P_{2})}=\operatorname{index}{(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1}}
,

\displaystyle\left(e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}\left(m(L_{1})+m(L_{2})-\nu_{-}\right)}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(\det L_{1})\operatorname{sign}(\det L_{2})(-1)^{\nu_{-}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(\det L_{1})\operatorname{sign}(\det L_{2})\operatorname{sign}(\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{-1})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{sign}(\det L),

entailing that

\kappa(x,y)=e^{-\frac{i\pi n}{4}}(\det L)^{1/2}e^{i\pi\{Px^{2}-2Lx\cdot y+Qy^{2}\}},

concluding the proof of the proposition.
∎

Lemma 9.14
.

Let
P_{j},L_{j},Q_{j},j=1,2,3
be as in Definition
9.1
. Then there exist

(P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}),(P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})
as in Definition
9.1
such that

(9.2.20)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}}\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

If
\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})\not=0
, Lemma
9.13
implies that
\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}}
so that (
9.2.20
) is satisfied with
(P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime})=(P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}).
We may thus assume in the sequel that
\det(Q_{1}+P_{2})=0
. Then the kernel of
Q_{1}+P_{2}
is of dimension
r\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
; let us define
J_{r}
as the orthogonal projection onto

\ker(Q_{1}+P_{2})
.

Claim 9.15
.

The matrix
J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}
is positive definite (thus invertible).

Indeed, if
J_{r}x+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}x=0
, we obtain by taking the dot-product with
x
that

\|J_{r}x\|^{2}+\|(Q_{1}+P_{2})x\|^{2}=0\Longrightarrow x\in\ker(Q_{1}+P_{2}),J_{r}x=0\Longrightarrow x=0.

This matrix is also non-negative, proving the claim.∎

Let us define the real
n\times n
symmetric matrix

(9.2.21)

P=\mu L_{2}\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]^{-1}L_{2}^{*}-Q_{2},

where
\mu
is a positive parameter to be chosen later;
we note that
P+Q_{2}

is invertible.
Also
we have

(9.2.22)

L_{2}^{*}\bigl(Q_{2}+P\bigr)^{-1}L_{2}-(Q_{1}+P_{2})=\mu^{-1}\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]-(Q_{1}+P_{2}),

which is invertible if
\mu
(is different from 0 and) does not meet the spectrum of
Q_{1}+P_{2}
.
32
32

32

The symmetric matrices
Q_{1}+P_{2}
and
J_{r}
can be diagonalized simultaneously so that the invertibility of

\mu^{-1}\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]-(Q_{1}+P_{2})
is equivalent to

\mu\not=0,\quad\mu^{-1}\lambda_{j}^{2}\not=\lambda_{j}\text{ i.e. }\mu\not=\lambda_{j},\text{ where the $\lambda_{j}$ are the non-zero eigenvalues of $Q_{1}+P_{2}$.}

We have also

P-P_{3}=\mu L_{2}\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]^{-1}L_{2}^{*}-(Q_{2}+P_{3})\\
=L_{2}\Bigl\{\mu\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]^{-1}-L_{2}^{-1}(Q_{2}+P_{3})L_{2}^{*-1}\Bigr\}L_{2}^{*},

which is invertible for
\mu
large enough.
33
33

33

Indeed the eigenvalues of

\bigl[J_{r}+(Q_{1}+P_{2})^{2}\bigr]^{-1}

are
1
and
\lambda_{j}^{-2}
where the
\lambda_{j}
are the non-zero eigenvalues of
Q_{1}+P_{2}
.
To secure the invertibility of
P-P_{3}
, it is thus enough to have

\min(\mu,\mu\lambda_{j}^{-2})>\|L_{2}^{-1}(Q_{2}+P_{3})L_{2}^{*-1}\|,

where the
\lambda_{j}
are the non-zero eigenvalues of
Q_{1}+P_{2}
.

Eventually, defining

(9.2.23)

\lambda_{0}=\max(\operatorname{Spectrum}|Q_{2}+P_{1}|),

the condition

(9.2.24)

\mu>\max\bigl\{\lambda_{0},\|L_{2}^{-1}(Q_{2}+P_{3})L_{2}^{*-1}\|,\|L_{2}^{-1}(Q_{2}+P_{3})L_{2}^{*-1}\|\lambda_{0}^{2}\bigr\},

implies that, with
P
given by (
9.2.21
), we obtain that

(9.2.25)

\text{the matrices }P+Q_{2},\ Q_{1}+P_{2}-L_{2}^{*}\bigl(Q_{2}+P\bigr)^{-1}L_{2},\ P-P_{3}\ \text{are invertible.}

Using now Lemma
9.13
and the first property in (
9.2.25
), we get that
we can find
\tilde{P},\tilde{L},\tilde{Q}
as in Definition
9.1
such that

(9.2.26)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}=\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{P},\tilde{L},\tilde{Q}},

with (thanks to (
9.2.18
)),

(9.2.27)

\tilde{P}=P_{2}-L_{2}^{*}(Q_{2}+P)^{-1}L_{2}.

We check now

(9.2.28)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}=\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{P},\tilde{L},\tilde{Q}},

and we note that

Q_{1}+\tilde{P}=Q_{1}+P_{2}-L_{2}^{*}(Q_{2}+P)^{-1}L_{2}\quad\text{is invertible,}

thanks to the second property in (
9.2.25
) so that, from Lemma
9.13
, we can find

P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}
as in Definition
9.1
such that

(9.2.29)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{\tilde{P},\tilde{L},\tilde{Q}}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}},

and this yields

(9.2.30)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}}.

Finally, we check

\underbracket{\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}^{-1}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}=\mathcal{M}_{0,-I_{n},-P}\\
\text{cf. Claim \ref {claim.911}}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}}=\mathcal{M}_{0,-I_{n},-P}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}},

and since
-P+P_{3}
is invertible
(thanks to the third property in (
9.2.25
)), we obtain, using once again Lemma
9.13
,
that
we can find
P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}
as in Definition
9.1
such that

(9.2.31)

\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}}=\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}}.

Gathering the information above, we find that

(9.2.32)

\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}}\\
=\underbracket{\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}}_{\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime},L^{\prime},Q^{\prime}},\ \eqref{9224}}\underbracket{\mathcal{M}_{P,I_{n},0}^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{P_{3},L_{3},Q_{3}}}_{\mathcal{M}_{P^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime},Q^{\prime\prime}},\ \eqref{9225}},

which ends the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proposition 9.16
.

The metaplectic group
Mp(n)
is equal to the set

(9.2.33)

\left\{\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{j}=P_{j}^{*},Q_{j}=Q_{j}^{*}\\
\det L_{j}\not=0\end{subarray}}

In other words, every metaplectic operator of
Mp(n)
is the product of two operators of type

\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
as given by Definition
9.1
.

Proof.

From Proposition
9.8
, the metaplectic group is generated by the
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
and since the inverse of
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
is
\mathcal{M}_{-Q,-L^{*},-P}
, thanks to Claim
9.12
, it is enough to check the products
\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\dots\mathcal{M}_{P_{N},L_{N},Q_{N}}
for
N\geq 3
.
Lemma
9.14
is tackling the case
N=3
and a trivial recurrence on
N
provides the result of the proposition.
∎

Theorem 9.17
.

Let
M
be an element of
Mp(n)
such that
M=e^{i\phi}\operatorname{Id}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})},\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

Then
e^{i\phi}
belongs to the set
\{-1,1\}
. In other words, the intersection of the metaplectic group with the unit circle (identified to the unitary operators in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
defined by the mappings
v\mapsto zv
where
z\in\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset\mathbb{C}
)
is reduced to the set
\{-1,1\}
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
9.16
, the result follows from Lemma
9.11
.
∎

We may go back to the description given by Proposition
1.15
and Definition
1.17
.

Proposition 9.18
.

The metaplectic group
Mp(n)
is equal to the set

(9.2.34)

\left\{M_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}M_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{j}=A_{j}^{*},C_{j}=C_{j}^{*}\\
\det B_{j}\not=0\end{subarray}},

where the operators
M_{A,B,C}
are defined in
Proposition
1.15
.

Proof.

Let
M
be in
Mp(n)
. We have

M=(M_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}})^{\pm 1}\dots(M_{A_{N},B_{N},C_{N}})^{\pm 1}\\
\underbracket{=}_{\eqref{linkme}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1},-B_{1},C_{1}}e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\right)^{\pm 1}\dots\left(\mathcal{M}_{A_{N},-B_{N},C_{N}}e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\right)^{\pm 1}\\
=\left(\mathcal{M}_{A_{1},-B_{1},C_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}\right)^{\pm 1}\dots\left(\mathcal{M}_{A_{N},-B_{N},C_{N}}\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}\right)^{\pm 1},

and since from Claim
9.12
, we have
\mathcal{M}_{A,B,C}^{-1}=\mathcal{M}_{-C,-B^{*},-A}
,
we find that
M
is in fact a product of
2N
terms of type
\mathcal{M}_{P,L,Q}
,
and thanks to Proposition
9.16
, we get

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}=\underbrace{\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}}_{M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}}}\underbrace{\left(e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}}_{\left(\mathcal{M}_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},-P_{2}}e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}\right)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}}\left(M_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},-P_{2}}\right)^{-1}=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}}\left(M_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},0}M_{0,I_{n},-P_{2}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}}M_{0,I_{n},P_{2}}\left(M_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},0}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}+P_{2}}\left(M_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},0}\right)^{-1}\qquad\text{\tiny(cf. Formula \eqref{54gvrs})}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}+P_{2}}M_{A^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime\prime},0}\quad\text{\tiny(cf. Lemma \ref {lem.784512} below in the next subsection)},

proving the Proposition.
∎

9.2.2.
On some subgroups of the metaplectic group

We have seen in (
1.2.36
), (
1.2.34
) some equivalent conditions for a matrix

(9.2.35)

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{where $P,Q,R,S$ are $n\times n$ real matrices,}

to be symplectic.
We note here that when
\Xi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
, we have

(9.2.36)

\Xi^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}S^{*}&-Q^{*}\\
-R^{*}&P^{*}\end{pmatrix},

as it is easily checked from (
1.2.36
), (
1.2.34
).
When
\det P\not=0
, we proved that
\Xi=\Xi_{A,B,C}
as defined in (
1.2.30
).
Also from (
9.2.36
), we get
that if
\det S\not=0
we have

\Xi^{-1}=\Xi_{A,B,C},

so that

(9.2.37)

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B&0\\
0&B^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
-A&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}.

Some other properties of the same type are available when
\det Q
or
\det R
are different from
0
. Indeed we have for
\Xi\in Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
and
\sigma
given by (
1.2.26
),

(9.2.38)

\Xi\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}-Q&P\\
-S&R\end{pmatrix}\underbracket{=}_{\text{if $\det Q\not=0$}}\Xi_{A,B,C},

so that

(9.2.39)

\Xi=-\Xi_{A,B,C}\sigma=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
A&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B^{-1}&0\\
0&B^{*}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&-C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

If we have
\det R\not=0
, using the two first equalities in (
9.2.38
), we get that

(\Xi\sigma)^{-1}=\Xi_{A,B,C}

which gives

(9.2.40)

\Xi=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&C\\
0&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B&0\\
0&B^{*-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n}&0\\
-A&I_{n}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}.

However, it is indeed possible when
n\geq 2
to have a symplectic matrix in
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
in the form (
9.2.35
) such that all the blocks are singular,
as shown in the following remark.

Remark 9.19
.

The
4\times 4
matrix

\begin{pmatrix}\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}-1&0\\
0&0\end{matrix}&\begin{matrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{matrix}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
R&S\end{pmatrix}

belongs to
Sp(2,\mathbb{R})

although all the block
2\times 2

matrices
P,Q,R,S,

are singular (with rank 1).

Lemma 9.20
.

With
M_{A,B,C}
defined in Proposition
1.15
, the sets

(9.2.41)

\mathcal{L}=\{M_{A,B,0}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=A^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}},\qquad\mathcal{R}=\{M_{0,B,C}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}C=C^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}},

are subgroups of the metaplectic group (cf. Definition
1.17
).

Proof.

Indeed
\mathcal{L}
contains the identity of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and we have for
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\displaystyle M_{A_{1},B_{1},0}M_{A_{2},B_{2},0}^{-1}v=M_{A_{1},B_{1},0}\bigl\{M_{0,B_{2}^{-1},0}\{e^{-i\pi A_{2}x^{2}}v(x)\}\bigr\}

\displaystyle{\hskip 15.0pt}=M_{A_{1},B_{1},0}\bigl\{e^{-i\pi B_{2}^{*-1}A_{2}B_{2}^{-1}x^{2}}v(B_{2}^{-1}x)\bigr\}(\det{B_{2}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle{\hskip 15.0pt}=e^{i\pi A_{1}x^{2}}e^{-i\pi B_{1}^{*}B_{2}^{*-1}A_{2}B_{2}^{-1}B_{1}x^{2}}v(B_{2}^{-1}B_{1}x)(\det{B_{1}})^{1/2}(\det{B_{2}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle{\hskip 15.0pt}=e^{i\pi(A_{1}-B_{1}^{*}B_{2}^{*-1}A_{2}B_{2}^{-1}B_{1})x^{2}}v(B_{2}^{-1}B_{1}x)(\det{B_{1}})^{1/2}(\det{B_{2}})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle{\hskip 15.0pt}=(M_{A_{1}-B_{1}^{*}B_{2}^{*-1}A_{2}B_{2}^{-1}B_{1},B_{2}^{-1}B_{1},0}v)(x),

so that
M_{A_{1},B_{1},0}M_{A_{2},B_{2},0}^{-1}
belongs to the set
\mathcal{L}
in (
9.2.41
), proving that

\mathcal{L}

is indeed a subgroup of the metaplectic group.
We note also that the bijective mapping

(9.2.42)

\mathcal{L}\ni M\mapsto F^{*}MF\in\mathcal{R},

(
F
stands for the Fourier transformation) sends
\mathcal{L}
onto
\mathcal{R}

since we have

(9.2.43)

F^{*}M_{A,B,0}F=F^{*}M_{A,I_{n},0}FF^{*}M_{0,B,0}F=M_{0,I_{n},A}M_{0,B^{*-1},0}\\
=M_{0,B^{*-1},B^{*-1}AB^{-1}}.

Moreover the mapping (
9.2.42
) is obviously one-to-one and is also onto
since, given
B_{1}\in Gl(n,\mathbb{R})
and
C_{1}
a symmetric
n\times n
matrix,
we see from (
9.2.43
) that

F^{*}M_{B_{1}^{-1}C_{1}B_{1}^{*-1},B_{1}^{*-1},0}F=M_{0,B_{1},C_{1}}.

The mapping (
9.2.42
) also extends to a group isomorphism of
Mp(n)
, proving the lemma.
∎

Remark 9.21
.

We may note that

(M_{A_{1},B_{1},0}M_{A_{2},B_{2},0}v)(x)=e^{i\pi A_{1}x^{2}}(M_{A_{2},B_{2},0}v)(B_{1}x)(\det B_{1})^{1/2}\\
=e^{i\pi(A_{1}+B^{*}_{1}A_{2}B_{1})x^{2}}v(B_{2}B_{1}x)(\det B_{1})^{1/2}(\det B_{2})^{1/2}=(M_{A_{1}+B_{1}^{*}A_{2}B_{1},B_{2}B_{1},0}v)(x),

so that the internal binary operation
\star
can be defined on the set
\{(A,B)\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=A^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}}

as

(9.2.44)

(A_{1},B_{1})\star(A_{2},B_{2})=(A_{1}+B_{1}^{*}A_{2}B_{1},B_{2}B_{1}),

for which the identity is
(0,I_{n})
and the inverse

(9.2.45)

(A,B)^{-1}=(-B^{*-1}AB^{-1},B^{-1}).

Remark 9.22
.

A consequence of Lemma
9.20

is,
with
\Psi
defined in (
1.2.71
), that

\{\Psi(M_{A,B,0})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=A^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}}=\{\Xi_{A,B,0}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A=A^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}},\quad\{\Psi(M_{0,B,C})\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}C=C^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}}=\{\Xi_{0,B,C}\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}C=C^{*}\\
\det B\not=0\end{subarray}},

are subgroups of the symplectic group
Sp(n,\mathbb{R})
.

Proposition 9.23
.

The metaplectic group
Mp(n)
is equal to the set

(9.2.46)

\left\{M_{A_{1},B_{1},C_{1}}M_{A_{2},B_{2},C_{2}}\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}A_{j}=A_{j}^{*},C_{j}=C_{j}^{*}\\
\det B_{j}\not=0\end{subarray}}

In other words, every metaplectic operator of
Mp(n)
is the product of two operators of type

M_{A,B,C}
as given by Proposition
1.15
.

Proof.

Let
M\in Mp(n)
;
using Proposition
9.16
,
we may assume that

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{P_{1},L_{1},Q_{1}}\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}\left(\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}\right)^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}

(
9.2.3
)

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{P_{2},L_{2},Q_{2}}^{-1}\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}\right)^{-1}

(Claim
9.12
)

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}}\left(\mathcal{M}_{-Q_{2},-L_{2}^{*},-P_{2}}\mathcal{F}e^{-i\pi n/4}\right)^{-1}

(
9.2.3
), (
1.2.53
)

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}}M_{-Q_{2},L_{2}^{*},-P_{2}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}}\bigl(M_{-Q_{2},L_{2}^{*},0}M_{0,I_{n},-P_{2}}\bigr)^{-1}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}}M_{0,I_{n},P_{2}}M_{-Q_{2},L_{2}^{*},0}^{-1}

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},0}M_{0,I_{n},Q_{1}+P_{2}}M_{-Q_{2},L_{2}^{*},0}^{-1}=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}+P_{2}}M_{-Q_{2},L_{2}^{*},0}^{-1}

(using Lemma
9.20
)

\displaystyle=M_{P_{1},-L_{1},Q_{1}+P_{2}}M_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime},0},

proving the sought result.
∎

Remark 9.24
.

We have used two different sets of generators of the metaplectic group. First the set
\mathscr{G}_{1}=\big\{M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}\bigr\}
given by (
1.2.56
) which is somewhat natural, also allowing us to recover the operator

e^{-i\pi n/4}\mathcal{F}
where the phase factor appears via Formula (
1.2.60
).
The Identity appears clearly as
M_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}
, but the inverse of

M_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}
cannot always be expressed within
\mathscr{G}_{1}
.

Also we have the set

\mathscr{G}_{2}=\big\{\mathcal{M}_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}\bigr\}
given in Definition
9.1
,
which incorporates a phase prefactor
e^{-i\pi n/4}
, looking a priori rather arbitrary but of course necessary for the sequel (this prefactor is also suggested by (
1.2.60
));
here to express the identity, we need to write it as

\mathcal{M}_{0,I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{0\}}\mathcal{M}_{0,-I_{n},0}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{n\}}
, but the inverse of

\mathcal{M}_{A,B,C}^{\scriptscriptstyle\{m(B)\}}

is easily obtained by Claim
9.12
within

\mathscr{G}_{2}
.
Certainly the description given by
\mathscr{G}_{2}

is much better, in particular because the calculations leading to
Lemma
9.11
and Proposition
9.13

are rather easy as well as the proof of Lemma
9.14
;
a statement analogous to Proposition
9.16
for
\mathscr{G}_{1}
is true (cf. Proposition
9.18
),
but its proof is quite indirect and relies heavily on the results for
\mathscr{G}_{2}
.

9.3.
Mehler’s formula

We provide here a couple of statements related to the so-called Mehler’s formula, appearing as particular cases of L. Hörmander’s study in
[
22
]

(see also the more recent K. Pravda-Starov’ article
[
42
]
).
In the general framework, we consider a complex-valued quadratic form
Q

on the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
\operatorname{Re}Q\leq 0
: we want to quantize the Gaussian
function (here
X
stands for
(x,\xi)
)

\mathbf{a}(X)=e^{\langle QX,X\rangle},

and to relate the operator with Weyl symbol
\mathbf{a}
to the operator

\exp\left\{{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(\langle QX,X\rangle)}\right\}.

Lemma 9.25
.

For
\operatorname{Re}t\geq 0
,
t\notin i\pi(2\mathbb{Z}+1)
, we have in
n
dimensions,

(9.3.1)

\bigl(\cosh(t/2)\bigr)^{n}\exp-t\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{-2\tanh(\frac{t}{2})\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}\bigr)}.

In particular, for
t=-2is,s\in\mathbb{R}
,
s\notin\frac{\pi}{2}(1+2\mathbb{Z})
, we have in
n
dimensions

(9.3.2)

(\cos s)^{n}\exp\bigl(2i\pi s{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tan s(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)}.

Lemma 9.26
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C},\ \operatorname{Re}z\geq 0
, we have in
n
dimensions

(9.3.3)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\Bigl(\exp-\bigl(2z\pi\bigl(|\xi|^{2}+|x|^{2}\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)}=\frac{1}{(1+z)^{n}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\Big(\frac{1-z}{1+z}\Big)^{k}\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

where
\mathbb{P}_{k;n}
is defined in Section
9.1.4
and the equality holds between

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-bounded operators.

We provide first a proof of a particular case of the results of
[
22
]
.

Lemma 9.27
.

For
\operatorname{Re}t\geq 0
,
t\notin i\pi(2\mathbb{Z}+1)
, we have in
n
dimensions,

(9.3.4)

\bigl(\cosh(t/2)\bigr)^{n}\exp-t\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{-2\tanh(\frac{t}{2})\pi(x^{2}+\xi^{2})}\bigr)}.

Proof.

By tensorisation, it is enough to prove that formula for
n=1
, which we assume from now on.
We define

L=\xi+ix,\quad\bar{L}=\xi-ix,\quad M(t)=\beta(t){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{-\alpha(t)\pi L\bar{L}})},

where
\alpha,\beta
are smooth functions of
t
to be chosen below.
Assuming
\beta(0)=1,\alpha(0)=0,
we find that
M(0)=\operatorname{Id}
and

\dot{M}+\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|L|^{2})}M={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\Bigl(\dot{\beta}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}-\beta\dot{\alpha}\pi|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}+\pi(|L|^{2})\sharp\beta e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigr)}.

We have from (
1.2.5
), since
\partial_{x}\partial_{\xi}|L|^{2}=0
,

\displaystyle|L|^{2}\sharp e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}

\displaystyle=|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}+\frac{1}{4i\pi}\overbrace{\left\{|L|^{2},e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\right\}}^{=0}

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt+\frac{1}{(4i\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(\partial_{\xi}^{2}(|L|^{2})\partial_{x}^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}+\partial_{x}^{2}(|L|^{2})\partial_{\xi}^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle=|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 25.0pt+\frac{1}{(4i\pi)^{2}}\frac{1}{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigl(2\bigl((-2\alpha\pi x)^{2}-2\alpha\pi\bigr)+2\bigl((-2\alpha\pi\xi)^{2}-2\alpha\pi\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigl(1-\frac{4\alpha^{2}\pi^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\Bigr)+\frac{\alpha\pi}{4\pi^{2}}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}},

so that

\dot{M}+\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|L|^{2})}M\\
={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\Bigl(\dot{\beta}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}-\beta\dot{\alpha}\pi|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}+\pi\beta|L|^{2}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigl(1-\frac{4\alpha^{2}\pi^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\Bigr)+\frac{\alpha\pi\beta}{4\pi}e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigr)}\\
={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\Bigl(e^{-\alpha\pi|L|^{2}}\Bigl\{|L|^{2}\bigl(-\pi\dot{\alpha}\beta+\pi\beta(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4})\bigr)+\dot{\beta}+\frac{\alpha\beta}{4}\Bigr\}\Bigr)}.

We solve now

\dot{\alpha}=1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{4},\ \alpha(0)=0\Longleftrightarrow\alpha(t)=2\tanh(t/2),

and

4\dot{\beta}+\alpha\beta=0,\beta(0)=1\Longleftrightarrow\beta(t)=\frac{1}{\cosh(t/2)}.

We obtain that

\dot{M}+\pi{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|L|^{2})}M=0,\ M(0)=\operatorname{Id},

and this implies

\beta(t){\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(e^{-\alpha(t)\pi L\bar{L}})}=M(t)=\exp{-t\pi(|L|^{2})^{w}},

which proves (
9.3.4
).
∎

In particular, for
t=-2is,s\in\mathbb{R}
,
s\notin\frac{\pi}{2}(1+2\mathbb{Z})
, we have in
n
dimensions

(9.3.5)

(\cos s)^{n}\exp\bigl(2i\pi s{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tan s(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)}.

Lemma 9.28
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C},\ \operatorname{Re}z\geq 0
, we have in
n
dimensions

(9.3.6)

{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(\exp-\bigl(2z\pi(|\xi|^{2}+|x|^{2})\bigr)\bigr)}=\frac{1}{(1+z)^{n}}\sum_{k\geq 0}\Big(\frac{1-z}{1+z}\Big)^{k}\mathbb{P}_{k;n},

where
\mathbb{P}_{k;n}
is defined in Section
9.1.4
and the equality holds between

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
-bounded operators.

Proof.

Starting from (
9.3.5
), we get for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
, in
n
dimensions,

(\cos(\arctan\tau))^{n}\exp\bigl(2i\pi\arctan\tau{\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)},

so that using the spectral decomposition of the (
n
-dimensional)
Harmonic Oscillator and (
9.8.2
), we get

(1+\tau^{2})^{-n/2}\sum_{k\geq 0}e^{2i(\arctan\tau)(k+\frac{n}{2})}\mathbb{P}_{k;n}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)},

which implies

(1+\tau^{2})^{-n/2}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{2k+n}}{(1+\tau^{2})^{k+\frac{n}{2}}}\mathbb{P}_{k;n}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)},

entailing

\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(1+i\tau)^{k}}{(1-i\tau)^{k+n}}\mathbb{P}_{k;n}={\text{\rm Op}_{\text{\rm w}}\bigl(e^{2i\pi\tau(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})}\bigr)},

proving the lemma by analytic continuation
(we may refer the reader as well to
[
50
]
(pp. 204-205)
and note that for any
z\in\mathbb{C},\ \operatorname{Re}z\geq 0
, we have
|\frac{1-z}{1+z}|\leq 1

).
∎

9.4.
Laguerre polynomials

9.4.1.
Classical Laguerre polynomials

The Laguerre polynomials
\{L_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}

are defined by

(9.4.1)

L_{k}(x)=\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}\frac{(-1)^{l}}{l!}\binom{k}{l}x^{l}=e^{x}\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{k}\bigl\{x^{k}e^{-x}\bigr\}=\left(\frac{d}{dx}-1\right)^{k}\bigl\{\frac{x^{k}}{k!}\bigr\},

and we have

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle=1,

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=-X+1,

\displaystyle L_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(X^{2}-4X+2),

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(-X^{3}+9X^{2}-18X+6),

\displaystyle L_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24}(X^{4}-16X^{3}+72X^{2}-96X+24),

\displaystyle L_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{120}(-X^{5}+25X^{4}-200X^{3}+600X^{2}-600X+120),

\displaystyle L_{6}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{720}\left(X^{6}-36X^{5}+450X^{4}-2400X^{3}+5400X^{2}-4320X+720\right),

\displaystyle L_{7}

\displaystyle=\frac{-X^{7}+49X^{6}-882X^{5}+7350X^{4}-29400X^{3}+52920X^{2}-35280X+5040}{5040}.

We get also easily from the above definition that

(9.4.2)

L^{\prime}_{k+1}=L^{\prime}_{k}-L_{k},

since with
T=d/dX-1

L^{\prime}_{k}-L_{k}=TL_{k}=T^{k+1}(\frac{X^{k}}{k!})=T^{k+1}(\frac{d}{dX}\frac{X^{k+1}}{(k+1)!})=\frac{d}{dX}L_{k+1}.

Formula (6.8) and Theorem 12 in the R. Askey & G. Gasper’s article
[
2
]
provide
the inequalities

(9.4.3)

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\forall x\geq 0,\quad\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}(-1)^{l}L_{l}(x)\geq 0.

This result follows as well from Formula (73) in the 1940 paper
[
12
]
by E. Feldheim.

Let us calculate the Fourier transform of the Laguerre polynomials:
we have

L_{k}(x)=\left(\frac{d}{dx}-1\right)^{k}\bigl\{\frac{x^{k}}{k!}\bigr\},

so that

\displaystyle\widehat{L_{k}}(\xi)=(2i\pi\xi-1)^{k}\left(\frac{-1}{2i\pi}\right)^{k}\frac{\delta_{0}^{(k)}}{k!}=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}(\xi-\frac{1}{2i\pi})^{k}\delta_{0}^{(k)}(\xi).

As a result, defining for
k\in\mathbb{N},t\in\mathbb{R}
,

(9.4.4)

M_{k}(t)=(-1)^{k}H(t)e^{-t}L_{k}(2t),\quad H=\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}},

we find, using the homogeneity of degree
-k-1
of
\delta_{0}^{(k)}
,

\displaystyle\widehat{M_{k}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\bigl(\frac{\tau}{2}-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\bigl)^{k}\delta_{0}^{(k)}(\frac{\tau}{2})\ast\frac{(-1)^{k}}{1+2i\pi\tau}

\displaystyle={(-1)^{k}}{}(\frac{d}{d\sigma})^{k}\left\{\frac{(\sigma-\frac{1}{i\pi})^{k}/k!}{1+2i\pi(\tau-\sigma)}\right\}_{|\sigma=0}

\displaystyle\widehat{M_{k}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}{(-1)^{k}}{}\binom{k}{l}\frac{(\sigma-\frac{1}{i\pi})^{k-l}}{(k-l)!}\frac{(k-l)!(2i\pi)^{k-l}}{\bigl(1+2i\pi(\tau-\sigma)\bigr)^{1+k-l}}_{|\sigma=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l}{(-1)^{k}}{}\binom{k}{l}{}\frac{(-2)^{k-l}}{\bigl(1+2i\pi\tau\bigr)^{1+k-l}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(1+2i\pi\tau)}\sum_{l}\binom{k}{l}{}\frac{(-2)^{k-l}}{\bigl(1+2i\pi\tau\bigr)^{k-l}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(1+2i\pi\tau)}\left(1-\frac{2}{\bigl(1+2i\pi\tau\bigr)}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(1+2i\pi\tau)}\left(\frac{-1+2i\pi\tau}{1+2i\pi\tau}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1+2i\pi\tau)}\left(\frac{1-2i\pi\tau}{1+2i\pi\tau}\right)^{k}

so that

(9.4.5)

\widehat{M_{k}}(\tau)=\frac{(1-2i\pi\tau)^{k}}{(1+2i\pi\tau)^{k+1}}=\frac{(1-2i\pi\tau)^{2k+1}}{(1+4\pi^{2}\tau^{2})^{k+1}}.

9.4.2.
Generalized Laguerre polynomials

Let
\alpha
be a complex number
and let
k
be a non-negative integer such that
\alpha+k\notin(-\mathbb{N}^{*})
. We define the generalized Laguerre polynomial
L_{k}^{\alpha}
by

(9.4.6)

L_{k}^{\alpha}(x)=x^{-\alpha}e^{x}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{k}\bigl\{e^{-x}\frac{x^{k+\alpha}}{k!}\bigr\}=x^{-\alpha}\left(\frac{d}{dx}-1\right)^{k}\bigl\{\frac{x^{k+\alpha}}{k!}\bigr\}.

We note that
L_{k}^{\alpha}
is indeed a polynomial with degree
k
with the formula

\displaystyle L_{k}^{\alpha}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k_{1}+k_{2}=k}\frac{1}{k!}\binom{k}{k_{1}}(-1)^{k_{2}}\Gamma(k+\alpha+1)\frac{x^{k-k_{1}}}{\Gamma(k+\alpha+1-k_{1})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq k_{1}\leq k}\frac{(-1)^{k_{2}}}{k_{1}!(k-k_{1})!}\Gamma(k+\alpha+1)\frac{x^{k-k_{1}}}{\Gamma(k+\alpha+1-k_{1})}

(9.4.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq l\leq k}\binom{k+\alpha}{k-l}\frac{(-1)^{l}x^{l}}{l!}.

N.B
.

We recall that the function
1/\Gamma
is an entire function with simple zeroes at
-\mathbb{N}
. As a result to make sense for the binomial coefficient

\binom{k+\alpha}{k-l}=\frac{\Gamma(k+\alpha+1)}{(k-l)!\Gamma(l+\alpha+1)},

we need to make sure that
k+\alpha+1\notin-\mathbb{N}
, i.e.
\alpha\notin-\mathbb{N}^{*}-k
.

Lemma 9.29
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(-\mathbb{N}^{*})

and let
k
be a non-negative integer. For
\alpha=0
, we have
L_{k}^{\alpha}=L_{k}
, where
L_{k}
is the classical Laguerre polynomial defined in (
9.4.1
). Moreover we have for
l\leq k
,

(9.4.8)

\left(\frac{d}{dX}\right)^{l}L_{k}^{\alpha}=(-1)^{l}L_{k-l}^{\alpha+l}.

Proof.

Indeed,
we have from (
9.4.7
)

\left(\frac{d}{dX}\right)^{l}L_{k}^{\alpha}=(-1)^{l}\sum_{l\leq m\leq k}\binom{k+\alpha}{k-m}\frac{(-1)^{m-l}X^{m-l}}{(m-l)!}\\
=(-1)^{l}\sum_{0\leq r\leq k-l}\binom{k-l+\alpha+l}{k-r-l}\frac{(-1)^{r}X^{r}}{r!}=(-1)^{l}L_{k-l}^{\alpha+l},

proving the sought formula.
∎

9.5.
Singular integrals

Proposition 9.30
.

\mathbf{[1]}
The (Hardy) operator with distribution kernel

\frac{H(x)H(y)}{\pi(x+y)}

is self-adjoint bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with spectrum
[0,1]
and thus norm 1.

\mathbf{[2]}
The (modified Hardy) operators with respective distribution kernels

H(x-y)\frac{H(x)H(y)}{\pi(x+y)},\quad H(y-x)\frac{H(x)H(y)}{\pi(x+y)},

are bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with norm
1/2
.

Proof.

Let us prove
\mathbf{[1]}
: for
\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, we define for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{\phi}(t)=\phi(e^{t})e^{t/2}
, and
we have to check the kernel

\frac{e^{t/2}e^{s/2}}{\pi(e^{t}+e^{s})}=\frac{1}{\pi(e^{(t-s)/2}+e^{-(t-s)/2})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\operatorname{sech}\bigl(\frac{t-s}{2}\bigr),

which is a convolution kernel.
Using now the classical formula

(9.5.1)

\int e^{-2i\pi x\xi}\operatorname{sech}xdx=\pi\operatorname{sech}(\pi^{2}\xi),

we get that

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int\operatorname{sech}(\frac{t}{2})e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt=\operatorname{sech}(\pi^{2}2\tau),

a smooth function whose range is
(0,1]
, proving the first part of the proposition.
To obtain
\mathbf{[2]}
,
we observe with the notations
\phi(t)=u(e^{t})e^{t/2}
,
\psi(s)=v(e^{s})e^{s/2}
that we have to check

\iint H(s-t)\frac{e^{t/2}e^{s/2}}{\pi(e^{t}+e^{s})}\phi(t)\bar{\psi}(s)dtds\\
=\iint\frac{H(s-t)}{\pi(e^{(t-s)/2}+e^{-(t-s)/2})}\phi(t)\bar{\psi}(s)dtds=\langle R\ast\phi,\psi\rangle_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})},

with

(9.5.2)

R(t)=\frac{H(t)}{2\pi\cosh(t/2)},\quad\hat{R}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\operatorname{sech}(t/2)e^{-2i\pi t\tau}dt,

so that
34
34

34

We recall that
\frac{d}{ds}\arctan(\sinh s)=\operatorname{sech}s
.

(9.5.3)

|\hat{R}(\tau)|\leq\hat{R}(0)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{+{\infty}}\operatorname{sech}(t/2)dt=\frac{1}{2},

yielding the sought result.
∎

9.6.
On some auxiliary functions

9.6.1.
A preliminary quadrature

Lemma 9.31
.

We have

(9.6.1)

\int_{0}^{\pi/2}(\operatorname{csc}s-\operatorname{csch}s)ds=\int_{\pi/2}^{+{\infty}}\operatorname{csch}sds=\operatorname{Log}(\operatorname{coth}\frac{\pi}{4}),

with
\csc s=1/\sin s,\operatorname{csch}s=1/\sinh s
.

Proof.

Note that the function
\displaystyle[0,\pi/2]\ni s\mapsto\frac{\sinh s-\sin s}{\sinh s\sin s},
is continuous.
Moreover, we have

\int\frac{ds}{\sin s}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{1-\cos s}{1+\cos s}\bigr)\ \text{\quad and \ }\int\frac{ds}{\sinh s}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{\cosh s-1}{\cosh s+1}\bigr),

so that

\int_{\epsilon}^{\pi/2}(\operatorname{csc}s-\operatorname{csch}s)ds=\frac{1}{2}\left[\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{1-\cos s}{1+\cos s}\bigr)\right]^{\pi/2}_{\epsilon}-\left[\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{\cosh s-1}{\cosh s+1}\bigr)\right]^{\pi/2}_{\epsilon}\\
=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\Bigl(\underbrace{\bigl(\frac{1+\cos\epsilon}{1-\cos\epsilon}\bigr)\bigl(\frac{\cosh\epsilon-1}{\cosh\epsilon+1}\bigr)}_{=\frac{(2+O(\epsilon^{2}))(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}+O(\epsilon^{4}))}{(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}+O(\epsilon^{4}))(2+O(\epsilon^{2}))}\rightarrow 1\text{ for $\epsilon\rightarrow 0$}}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{\cosh\frac{\pi}{2}+1}{\cosh\frac{\pi}{2}-1}\bigr),

so that we obtain

\int_{0}^{\pi/2}(\operatorname{csc}s-\operatorname{csch}s)ds=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{e^{\pi/2}+e^{-\pi/2}+2}{e^{\pi/2}+e^{-\pi/2}-2}\bigr)=\operatorname{Log}\frac{\cosh(\pi/4)}{\sinh(\pi/4)},

which is the first result.
Also we have

\int_{\pi/2}^{+{\infty}}\operatorname{csch}sds=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Log}\bigl(\frac{\cosh(\pi/2)+1}{\cosh(\pi/2)-1}\bigr),

yielding the second result.
∎

9.6.2.
Study of the function
\rho_{\sigma}

We study in this section the real-valued Schwartz function
\rho_{\sigma}
given in (
5.2.14
). Using the notations

(9.6.2)

\omega=2\pi\tau,\quad\kappa=2\pi\sigma,\quad\nu=\sqrt{\kappa/\omega},

we have

(9.6.3)

\rho_{\sigma}(\tau)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s}{\sinh s}e^{2i\omega(s-\nu^{2}\tanh s)}ds=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{s}{\sinh s}\cos\bigl(2\omega(s-\nu^{2}\tanh s)\bigr)ds.

Defining the holomorphic function
F
by

(9.6.4)

F(z)=\frac{z}{\sinh z}e^{2i\omega(z-\nu^{2}\tanh z)},

we see that
F
has simple poles at
i\pi\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and essential singularities at

i\pi(\frac{1}{2}+\mathbb{Z})
.
We already know that the function
\rho_{\sigma}
belongs to the Schwartz space, but we want to prove a more precise exponential decay.
We start with the calculation of

(9.6.5)
